## Assignment 1 — Substance

You are a member of the staff of the Judiciary Committee in the House of Representative. The chairwoman of the committee is considering a proposal that would repeal 28 U.S.C. § 1367(b) and make stylistic conforming changes (such as removing subsection 1367(a)'s reference to subsection (b) and re-lettering subsections (c)-(e)). No other change to section 1367 is contemplated. The chairwoman requests that you draft a short memorandum addressing the following two questions:

1. What would be the legal and practical effect of the change?

2. Given the historical development of federal court jurisdiction and the current state of the world, is this change a good or bad idea?

## 2007-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-01-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

Repealing 1367(b) could possibly end such bizarre cases as *Guaranteed Systems, Inc. v. American National Can Co.* 1367(b) attempted to codify much of the case law that occurred in cases like *Kroger*. It sought to prevent plaintiffs from circumventing *Strawbridge*'s requirement of complete diversity. 1367(b), however, produced a number of odd results, many of which are noted by Professor Freer on page 304 of the casebook. Plaintiffs become disadvantaged in a number of cases, particularly when counterclaims are brought against them and they become de facto defendants. As cases like *Guaranteed Systems* show, even though the plaintiffs are defending against counterclaims, they are still treated as plaintiffs as far as 1367(b) is concerned.

Repealing 1367(b) would have a number of interesting results in diversity cases. In cases like *Aldinger* and *Kroger*, the Court emphasized that it was not enough that Article III gave the courts constitutional power to hear related claims; they needed additional statutory authority to exercise that jurisdiction. This is what 1367 sought to do. Subsection (a) gave the courts the ability to use supplement jurisdiction to the limits of Article III. The only exception to this power was section (b). In diversity cases the "same case or controversy" requirement was not the only hurdle parties had to cross in order to use supplemental jurisdiction. Plaintiffs also had to follow complete diversity in almost all cases.

Repealing subsection (b) would make subsection (a) the only requirement necessary to exercise supplemental jurisdiction and would essentially repeal *Strawbridge* and would make much of the case law prior to 1367 invalid since the statute was meant to codify such decisions. As long as the additional party was considered part of the same case or controversy, supplemental jurisdiction would be allowed. Presumably some courts would follow the definition given in *Gibbs* to this concept. However, even the decision in *Gibbs* would be put in doubt as some could argue that 1367(a) was the only definition Congress wanted to give to the idea of "same case or controversy."

However, 1367(c) would still remain. Subsection (c) could be seen as either replacing *Gibbs* or working in conjunction with it. Either way, the ability to keep cases in the federal system would become much more discretionary for district court judges. Cases like *Guaranteed Systems* may not be given supplemental jurisdiction by district court judges. However they may rely more on subsection (c) (and possibly on *Gibbs*) in order to dismiss such cases on jurisdictional grounds. District courts might be able to hear more of the cases they want as a result.

There is no doubt, however, that the elimination of subsection (b) would cause many more cases to end up in federal court. This might have positive effects of relieving some of the burden on the overloaded state systems. It would also increase efficiency as claims that are part of the same case or controversy would be kept together instead of being split between the two systems. Similar cases would not have to be conducted twice. Moreover, the system would be more fair in its treatment of plaintiffs who had counterclaims brought against them, yet were left powerless in federal court under the current version of 1367.

On the other hand, this would cause many more state cases to be heard in front of federal judges. This leads to the larger discussion of diversity jurisdiction. Many critique diversity jurisdiction as a violation of federalism since legitimate state issues are dealt with by federal courts. Moreover, diversity jurisdiction limits the ability of state law to develop through case law. An increased scope of supplemental jurisdiction would only magnify problems such as these.

## 2007-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-01-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

MEMORANDUM

To: Madam Chairwoman, House Judiciary Committee
From: Industrious staff member
Re: Proposal to repeal 28 U.S.C. § 1367(b)
Date: 9/1/2007

## I. The Legal and Practical Effects of Repealing 28 U.S.C. § 1367(b)

Section 1367 codified the concept of pendent or ancillary jurisdiction, providing the statutory support for such jurisdiction that the courts seemed to require in *Aldinger*, *Kroger*, and *Finley*. Subsection (a) grants federal jurisdiction over supplemental claims that "form part of the same case or controversy", while subsection (b), by limiting a plaintiff's ability to file claims against non-diverse parties, codifies *Kroger*'s defense of *Strawbridge v. Curtiss*'s requirement of complete diversity.

If subsection (b) is repealed, jurisdiction up to the limits of Article III of the Constitution would be permissible, subject only to the discretion of the federal courts under 1367(c) to refuse to hear supplemental claims, and their discretion under § 1441(c) to remand entire cases that were removed from state court if questions of state law predominate.

There is nothing in the language of section 1367(c) suggesting that it is merely a codification of common law. Rather, it sets forth strict benchmarks that must be met if a federal judge refuses to hear a supplemental claim. In *Executive Software*, the Ninth Circuit concluded that a district court erred in remanding pendant claims under section 1367(c) when it failed to explain its reason for doing so. The court also held that only the statutory grounds provided in subsection (c), and not the more flexible considerations of *Gibbs*, provide a permissible foundation for remanding state claims.

The most significant practical effect of repealing 28 U.S.C. § 1367(b) would be an increase in the number of cases removed to federal court. At least one study shows lower win rates for plaintiffs in removed cases. Because it is alleged that federal courts are more 'defendant-friendly,' we may see a rise in the percentage of cases won by defendants.

## II. Change is Needed, but the Current Proposal is Too Radical

Commentators have expressed dissatisfaction with the language of section 1367(b) since its inception. Professor Freer criticized the apparent paradox that exists under section 1367(b) whereby plaintiffs are unable to assert claims against non-diverse parties even when defending against a counter-claim and in federal court through no action of their own. Revision of the current section 1367(b) is desirable. The ideal proposal, however, would amend or replace that subsection, rather than repealing it outright.

There are several arguments to be made in favor of a less restricted version of supplemental jurisdiction. Hearing related claims together promotes efficiency. Furthermore, forcing parties to litigate some claims in state court and some in federal might discourage them from taking advantage of a federal forum. Finally, on questions of state law, litigating in federal court eliminates the risk of prejudice against out-of-state defendants by state court jurors and judges (although discrimination studies have proved inconclusive).

However, allowing jurisdiction over supplemental claims to the limits of the Constitution is probably too extreme. District courts are courts of limited jurisdiction. Limitations of some kind on supplemental jurisdiction are desirable in order to counter-balance the liberal rules of joinder in the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, to prevent 'end runs' around *Strawbridge*'s principle of complete diversity, and to avoid unjustified congestion in the federal courts. Contrary to Professor Shapiro's suggestion that the scope of supplemental jurisdiction should be left to common law development, some legislative guidance is advisable in this area, as the confusion resulting from case law prior to the enactment of section 1367 confirms.

Subsection 1367(b) should be amended rather than repealed, and this revision should occur after further opportunity for empirical study of the relative competencies and caseloads of state and federal courts.

# ASSIGNMENT 2007-assignment-02 — Civil Procedure 2007 — Assignment 2

Source: content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-02.md

## 2007-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

You are an associate in a large law firm. Your (meaning your supervising partner's) client is Charming Lovely Art Posters, Inc. ("CLAP"), incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Boston. CLAP is a startup company attempting to establish itself in the business of manufacturing and distributing (via its Boston store) fine quality, poster-sized reproductions of famous paintings. On July 15, 2007, your client signed a contract with Pablo Picasso's grandson, Harry Picasso, who is a citizen of (and is domiciled in) Massachusetts. The contract provided that your client would pay Mr. Picasso the sum of $20,000.00, and in return, Mr. Picasso would grant CLAP the exclusive right to make and sell poster-sized reproductions of his recent painting, "Eric Cartman in Guernica, Reclining." The relevant portion of the contract provision read: "I, Harry Picasso hereby grant a license of the copyright to my work, 'Eric Cartman in Guernica, Reclining,' to CLAP. In so doing, I intend to grant CLAP (and no others) the exclusive right to produce posters of the image in this painting." Your client informs you that this work of art has become well-known in the New England area, so much so that Yale Law School has devoted a course to analysis of it.

CLAP proceeded to obtain several high-quality photographs of the painting and, in August-September, produced approximately 200 poster-pictures of the work (the cost of this production was approximately $5,000.00). In addition, at the cost of about $10,000.00, CLAP opened a temporary location in Connecticut to sell its posters, including the Picasso work (it has already shipped its products to the Connecticut location), and it expects to generate substantial revenue (it cannot tell how much) from sales of its other posters from persons coming into its store to buy "Eric Cartman.." CLAP plans to sell the "Eric Cartman" posters for $300.00 each. In late September, however, just before starting to sell the poster-pictures, CLAP learned that Mr. Picasso was about to sign a contract with another company called Strange Products And Tastes, Inc. ("SPAT"), incorporated in Maine with its principal place of business in Connecticut. SPAT has only ever sold products or maintained offices in Connecticut. The SPAT deal, if it went through, would allow SPAT to produce posters of above-named painting for sale in SPAT's Connecticut stores.

Your supervising partner wrote a letter to Mr. Picasso protesting this planned second deal. The reply came in the form of a letter from Mr. Picasso's attorney, stating that the July 15 contract granted CLAP the right to sell "Eric Cartman" posters only in Massachusetts, leaving Mr. Picasso free to grant a license for Connecticut sales to SPAT. The attorney further stated that if CLAP attempted to sell "Eric Cartman" posters in Connecticut, Mr. Picasso would sue CLAP for copyright infringement under 17 U.S.C. §§ 502, 504, requesting damages and an injunction under those provisions.

Your supervising partner is contemplating a suit against Mr. Picasso and SPAT for a declaratory judgment allowing CLAP to proceed with sales of the "Eric Cartman" posters in Connecticut. The partner would also like to sue SPAT for tortuous interference with contractual relations under Massachusetts law. Finally, the partner is contemplating a request for a preliminary injunction preventing the consummation of the contemplated Picasso-SPAT deal. Because Mr. Picasso recently gave a large donation to the Massachusetts State Judges Retirement Fund, and because the partner believes that a Connecticut court would be hostile to what it might see as an attempt to limit competition in that state, the partner is only interested in a lawsuit in Massachusetts federal court.

Your client requests that you provide the following:

1. A brief memo (or an outline) of your client's chances of obtaining a preliminary injunction against Mr. Picasso and/or SPAT

2. A draft complaint (a SHORT plain statement, along the lines of the Forms) against Mr. Picasso and SPAT

---

**Word limits:**
- Memo or outline: 750 words
- Complaint: 450 words

**Time limit:** 210 minutes (from the time of downloading the question)

NOTE: You may assume that if your client can prevail against Mr. Picasso, it would also prevail on the substantive merits of its claim against SPAT. In other words, do not spend space in your memo or outline on the substantive, tort-law elements of a tortuous interference claim. Further, all you need to know about 17 U.S.C. §§ 502, 504 is that they provide a cause of action for damages and an injunction for violation of copyright law. You will not receive any credit for researching or knowing anything additional about copyright law.

## 2007-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-02-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Injunctive Relief Against Picasso and SPAT

In order for the court to issue a preliminary injunction, CLAP must be able to show that 1) CLAP will suffer irreparable harm without relief; 2) there's some likelihood of success on the merits; 3) the balance of equities favors CLAP; and 4) an injunction would not be against public policy. CLAP will most likely fail the first two requirements, and therefore, fail to get injunctive relief.

**Irreparable Harm.** It would be hard for CLAP to argue that the consummation of the deal between Picasso and SPAT would lead to irreparable harm. CLAP can be paid for the projected lost profits in the "Eric Cartmen" posters and for increased profits in other posters resulting from increased foot traffic. CLAP could try to claim irreparable harm based on the fact that it would be difficult to calculate total lost profits—particularly profits from foot traffic—with any accuracy, especially since the Connecticut store was to have capitalized on the current public interest in the "Eric Cartmen" painting.

**Success on the Merits.** Bracketing procedural issues, the case against Picasso and SPAT is very strong on the merits. The words of the contract clearly indicate that CLAP has exclusive right to product posters, and there's no mention of a geographical limit. However, there are serious personal and subject matter jurisdiction issues that will likely prevent CLAP from obtaining injunctive relief.

**Personal jurisdiction:** While there's no problem of personal jurisdiction with Picasso—Picasso is a citizen of MA and domiciled in MA—there's a problem with SPAT because SPAT is a citizen of ME and CT and has no continuous or systematic contacts with MA. SPAT has never sold products in MA, doesn't maintain an office in MA, and the subject of the suit only involves SPAT's CT stores. Unless CLAP can convincingly argue that SPAT advertises or solicits in MA, intentionally targets MA, or that sales of its goods in CT causes effects in MA, there's no personal jurisdiction over SPAT.

**Federal Question Jurisdiction.** Under 28 USC § 1331, the claim must arise under federal law and do so as part of the plaintiff's case. CLAP's complaint against Picasso and SPAT is essentially a state breach-of-contracts claim. While it's true that Picasso could sue CLAP in federal court for copyright infringement under 17 U.S.C. 502, 504, CLAP cannot sue first for declaratory judgment in federal court merely in anticipation of a defense to a possible federal claim by Picasso. The only way CLAP can get around this rule is to try to ask for declaratory judgment that it's not a copyright infringement for CLAP to sell its reproductions in CT. Here, it would be important to argue that Picasso can get into federal court himself, even though the facts underlying his dispute is essentially a contracts claim.

**Diversity jurisdiction.** Under 28 USC § 1332, there must be complete diversity and damages must equal or exceed $75K. There's no complete diversity in this case because both CLAP and Picasso are citizens of MA. However, if CLAP were to only sue SPAT (and assuming away personal jurisdiction problems), this case might possibly go forward. If the amount-in-controversy only includes the costs that CLAP has borne so far, the amount's far below the required 75K. However, if it also includes the lost profits in the CT store—and it must appear to a legal certainty that the claim is really for less than the jurisdictional amount to justify dismissal—then there's a chance for diversity jurisdiction. If CLAP only sues SPAT, however, it cannot bring Picasso into the case under supplemental jurisdiction. Under § 1367(b), there's no supplemental jurisdiction where a plaintiff would join a defendant that's not diverse.

**Supplemental Jurisdiction.** If CLAP is somehow allowed to sue under federal question jurisdiction, there could be a problem with supplemental jurisdiction. The tort claim arises from the same nucleus of operative facts. The judge might still decline to exercise jurisdiction because of jury confusion or because the facts mainly indicate state claims. However, the fact that it's not a novel state claim and it doesn't predominate over the case weighs toward granting jurisdiction.

**Public Interest.** The injunction would not disserve the public interest because this is a private contracts dispute with few public implications.

**Balance of Equities:** The balance of equities favors CLAP because CLAP could possibly lose some incalculable amount of profit, whereas SPAT and Picasso's losses are smaller and more contained.

---

## Draft Complaint

DISTRICT COURT OF MASSACHUSETTS
CIVIL ACTION NO. ___________

CHARMING LOVELY ART POSTERS, INC.,
Plaintiff,

vs.

STRANGE PRODUCTS AND TASTES, INC. and HARRY PICASSO,
Defendants.

**COMPLAINT FOR DECLARATORY AND INJUNCTIVE RELIEF**

### Introduction

This is an action for declaratory relief that Charming Lovely Art Posters, Inc. ("CLAP") will not infringe Harry Picasso's copyright under 17 U.S.C. § 502. CLAP also seeks injunctive relief preventing the consummation of the deal between Strange Products and Tastes, Inc. ("SPAT") and Picasso, and damages against SPAT for tortious interference with contractual relations.

### Jurisdiction and Venue

This Court has subject matter jurisdiction over this action pursuant to 28 USC § 1331 because the case arises under federal copyright law. This Court has supplemental jurisdiction under § 1367 over the tortious interference claim.

This Court has authority to grant declaratory relief requested pursuant to § 2201(a).

This Court has in personam jurisdiction over Picasso because Picasso is a citizen of, and domiciled in, Massachusetts. This Court has in personam jurisdiction over SPAT because of the effects its commercial activity has in Massachusetts.

Venue is proper in the District of Massachusetts pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1391 because Picasso resides within this Court's district.

### Parties

Plaintiff CLAP is a corporation incorporated in Delaware and having its principal place of business in Massachusetts.

Defendant SPAT is a corporation incorporated in Maine and having its principal place of business in Connecticut.

Defendant Harry Picasso is citizen of, and domiciled in, Massachusetts.

### Factual Allegations

Plaintiff and Picasso entered into a written contract on July 15, 2007 giving Plaintiff exclusive right to produce posters of the painting "Eric Cartmen in Guernica, Reclining."

In reliance on this contract, Plaintiff proceeded to produce 200 poster-pictures of the work, intending to sell them in a Connecticut store newly opened for this purpose.

In September however, Picasso negotiated a deal with SPAT, to permit SPAT to produce and sell the same "Eric Cartmen" posters in its Connecticut stores.

Picasso then threatened to sue Plaintiff for copyright infringement if Plaintiff proceeded with its plans to sell the "Eric Cartmen" posters in Connecticut.

### Prayer for Relief

Plaintiff respectfully requests that this Court grant him judgment as follows:

Declare that CLAP's intended sale of the "Eric Cartmen" posters in Connecticut is not a violation of 17 U.S.C. § 502, 504.

Issue injunctive relief to prevent the consummation of the proposed deal between SPAT and Picasso regarding the sale of "Eric Cartmen" posters in Connecticut.

Award damages of $75,000 for tortious interference with contractual relations.

## 2007-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-02-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## 1. Preliminary Injunction Memo

Charming Lovely Art Posters, Inc. (CLAP) will probably not be able to obtain emergency relief against Mr. Picasso or against Strange Products And Tastes, Inc. (SPAT). There are four requirements to obtain emergency relief. The party seeking emergency relief must suffer irreparable harm if no injunction issues and be likely to succeed on the merits, the balance of equities must weigh in favor of the party, and the injunction must not be against public policy.

CLAP will probably not suffer irreparable harm if the court does not give CLAP emergency relief. CLAP's only losses are lost profits and costs from the sale of the "Eric Cartman" posters. CLAP will also argue that it will lose profits from the sale of other posters to customers interested in the "Eric Cartman" posters. Although this financial loss may be substantial, CLAP can still recover money damages for these losses. Thus, the harm is not irreparable. CLAP's money damages may be hard to calculate, which could warrant a finding of irreparable harm. As CLAP can probably present information and experts on this issue, however, the court will probably find that CLAP will not suffer irreparable harm if no injunction issues.

If CLAP can succeed on the procedural merits of the claim, then CLAP will likely succeed on the substantive elements of its claims. The primary procedural issues involve whether the court has subject matter jurisdiction and in personam jurisdiction. The federal district court would only have diversity jurisdiction if CLAP only sues SPAT because they are domiciled in different states. 28 U.S.C. 1332. If CLAP sues both SPAT and Picasso, then there would be no complete diversity because CLAP and Picasso are domiciled in Massachusetts. Diversity jurisdiction would also require that the amount in controversy requirement is met and it is not clear that the damages to SPAT would equal or exceed $75,000. The court could find that it has federal question jurisdiction if the declaratory judgment sought is for non-infringement of copyright because the copyright statute is federal law. 28 U.S.C. § 1331. The court could then exercise supplemental jurisdiction over the tortious interference claim because the second claim arises from the same nucleus of operative fact. 28 U.S.C. § 1367; *Gibbs*. This is a tenuous argument, however, because the real claim is a breach of contract claim.

CLAP will also have to demonstrate that the court has personal jurisdiction over Picasso and SPAT. Picasso is a citizen of Massachusetts, so the court has personal jurisdiction over him. SPAT, however, is not domiciled in nor does it have significant contacts with Massachusetts. The only argument for personal jurisdiction over SPAT is that SPAT's activities within other states could be affecting competition and people in Massachusetts. This is also not the strongest argument. If, however, CLAP can overcome these procedural obstacles, CLAP will most likely succeed on the substantive merits because the contract seems to clearly give CLAP the exclusive right to sell the posters.

CLAP probably meets the remaining two requirements, but cannot obtain emergency relief if it fails to meet either of the first two requirements. The balance of equities probably favors CLAP because CLAP stands to lose some possibly large and incalculable amount of profits. SPAT and Picasso's possible losses are probably small relative to CLAP's losses. There is also no indication that public policy would be against granting emergency relief in this case because the dispute seems limited to the parties without major implications for the public. CLAP will probably not be able to obtain emergency relief because it will probably fail to meet two of the four requirements.

---

## 2. Draft Complaint

DISTRICT COURT OF MASSACHUSETTS
CIVIL ACTION NO. ___________

CHARMING LOVELY ART POSTERS, INC.,
Plaintiff,

vs. COMPLAINT FOR DECLARATORY JUDGMENT AND AN EMERGENCY ORDER

STRANGE PRODUCTS AND TASTES, INC. and HARRY PICASSO,
Defendants.

### Introduction

This is an action for declaratory judgment that Charming Lovely Art Posters, Inc. (CLAP) will not infringe Harry Picasso's rights under 17 U.S.C. § 502. CLAP is also suing Strange Products and Tastes, Inc. (SPAT) for tortious interference with contractual relations.

### Jurisdiction and Venue

This Court has subject matter jurisdiction over the action for declaratory judgment under 28 U.S.C. § 1331 because the case arises under federal copyright law. This Court, however, has supplemental jurisdiction over the state law, tortious interference claim under 28 U.S.C. § 1367 because the two claims have a common nucleus of operative fact.

This Court has in personam jurisdiction over Picasso because Picasso is present and domiciled in Massachusetts. This Court has in personam jurisdiction over SPAT because its activities affect Massachusetts entities.

Venue is proper under 28 U.S.C. § 1391 because the Defendant, Picasso, resides within this Court's district.

### Parties

The Plaintiff, CLAP, is a startup company incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Massachusetts.

The Defendant, Harry Picasso, is an adult citizen of the United States domiciled in Massachusetts. The Defendant, SPAT, is a company incorporated in Maine with its principal place of business in Connecticut.

### Facts

The Plaintiff and the Defendant entered into a written contract on July 15, 2007. The relevant portion of the contract stated: "I, Harry Picasso hereby grant a license of the copyright to my work, 'Eric Cartman in Guernica, Reclining,' to Charming Lovely Art Posters. In so doing, I intend to grant Charming Lovely Art Posters (and no others) the exclusive right to produce posters of the image in this painting."

Mr. Picasso has begun negotiating a contract with SPAT to allow SPAT to sell "Eric Cartman" posters in Connecticut. If Picasso signs such a contract, CLAP will be irreparably harmed because CLAP will lose an incalculable amount of revenue from the lost sales of "Eric Cartman" posters and of other posters to customers interested in the "Eric Cartman" posters.

### Prayer for Relief

The Plaintiff respectfully prays that this Court grant the Plaintiff declaratory judgment that its sale of "Eric Cartman" posters in Connecticut would not violate federal copyright law and an emergency order enjoining SPAT from selling "Eric Cartman" posters in Connecticut until the disposition of this action.

The Plaintiff respectfully prays that this Court award the Plaintiff 75,000 dollars in money damages.

# ASSIGNMENT 2007-assignment-03 — Civil Procedure 2007 — Assignment 3

## 2007-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

## Assignment 3 — Substance

You are an associate in the law firm representing Paula Corbin Jones in an appeal from the judge's summary judgment ruling (on page 158 of *The Power of Procedure*). The relevant partner asks you for a memorandum assessing the prospects for an appeal. As guidance only, the partner articulates the following questions (you need answer only those you think relevant): What is the applicable legal standard, given the procedural posture of the case? Did the trial court apply that standard properly in this case? If not, where did it get itself in trouble? Which theories of relief (quid pro quo, hostile workplace, IIED) are most likely prospects for an appellate court reversal, and why? As to the most likely prospects, where is the judge's reasoning strong, and where is it weak? Also, assume that we could somehow get additional evidence into the record at this point (actually this is unlikely, but assume); do you recommend any additional areas of investigation or forms of evidence?

Remember to support factual assertions with appropriate citations to the record (shorthand format, such as "PP pg. 80" would be fine for a citation to interrogatory Jones' interrogatory response on page 80 of *The Power of Procedure*).

**First note:** Don't spend time/space with LRW memo format or fact recitation. Get right to the analysis!

**Second note:** We haven't covered appellate jurisdiction and appellate standards of review yet. Assume that in this appeal, the appellate court would review "de novo," that is, the appellate court would give no deference to the trial judge's ruling and reconsider everything from scratch, conducting the same analysis as did the trial court. This is NOT something you need to discuss in your response.

**Time limit:** 150 minutes from download

**Word limit:** 750 words

## Assignment 3 Logistics and Rules

4. The time limit will appear on the assignment itself. The time clock starts ticking at the moment your downloading of the assignment is complete. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5 . Your response must be typed. If you are worried about your ability to type quickly enough, you may use the full time block to write the assignment out long-hand, then use any length of time to type precisely (including all spelling, punctuation, and other errors) what you wrote. If you choose this option, hand in both the long hand and the typed version.

## 2007-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-03-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

Summary judgment is appropriate when the evidence shows "there is no genuine issue as to any material fact and that the moving party is entitled to a judgment as a matter of law." Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(c). An appeal of Judge Wright's ruling may succeed, particularly since summary judgment is reviewed de novo.

## Quid Pro Quo Sexual Harassment Claim

Jones' strongest argument is that Judge Wright failed to acknowledge the severity of the diminution of her job duties, and that the diminution constitutes a "tangible job detriment." Judge Wright emphasizes that "an alteration of job responsibilities" is insufficient; yet Jones made clear at her deposition that her responsibilities were not altered, they were obliterated: "And I didn't have any work to do. My work had been gone. I was sitting there doing nothing." (Pl.'s Depo. at 14.) The maintenance of Jones' pay cannot hide her job's substantive transformation, and Judge Wright's emphasis that Jones performed data entry before and after the incident (Opinion, p. 159-60) demonstrates that the substantive nature of work is legally significant.

An appeal on these grounds is bolstered by Jones' claim that she was officially demoted. Jones contends that upon returning from maternity leave her job title had changed: "I was not the purchasing agent anymore." (Pl.'s Depo. at 14.) Jones argued that she "might have kept the salary, but I was demoted." Id. at 25. A dispute between two pieces of evidence – in this case Jones' testimony and the AIDC's employment records – renders summary judgment inappropriate. Likewise, Jones' claim that she went down "a grade" in salary (Id. at 7) conflicts with employment records. Although Jones later came close to contradicting herself by saying she "might have kept the [same] salary" after returning from maternity leave (Id. at 14), an appeal could still emphasize this discrepancy between deposition and records. Although an appellate judge may deem that no rational trier of fact would believe uncertain testimony over objective documents, an appeal could stress that facts should be viewed in a light most favorable to the non-moving party in summary judgment proceedings.

That job applications were discouraged is less persuasive. Although Jones claims she applied for jobs and was rebuffed, that testimony was imprecise: she could remember neither the specific jobs applied for nor the specific instances that she discussed her applications with Ms. Pennington. (Id. at 5.) Nonetheless, viewed together with the diminution of work responsibility, there seems to be a decent case that there remain genuine issues of material fact.

## Hostile Workplace Environment

Jones' case on this issue is strong. First, granting summary judgment is questionable considering the use of a "totality of circumstances" test. In contrast to the "tangible job detriment" test for the sexual harassment claim, the totality of the circumstances test carries more leeway. Since there is room in the joints, Judge Wright's decision that no rational trier of fact could find for the plaintiff seems troubling.

Although Judge Wright declares "[n]o single factor is determinative," her opinion concentrates on Jones' job upgrade and salary increase. (Opinion, p. 161.) While these elements should be considered, a wise appeal would argue that this evidence must not overshadow Jones' perceptions of hostility. Nor should Jones' continuation at work or lack of psychological consultation (Id.) be used against her. Doing so would place victims in the impossible position of choosing between risking their jobs by making their frailty apparent, or sacrificing their hostile workplace claim in order to ensure job stability.

Ultimately, the highly interpretive nature of this claim makes an appeal strong. One troubling issue for Jones, though, is proving causation. Perhaps members of the AIDC made Jones' workplace hostile, but demonstrating that those conditions were the result of unwelcome sexual harassment is difficult. The defense suggests an alternative source (Jones' incompetence) for any hostility by highlighting instances where peers criticized her work. (Pl.'s Depo. at 17-18.) In attempting to overcome this hurdle, an appeal should draw attention to Mr. Ferguson's comment that revealed his knowledge of Jones' boyfriend's name. (Id. at 45.) There is little explanation for Ferguson having this knowledge unless some form of retaliation or enduring surveillance against Jones was at play.

## Intentional Infliction of Emotional Distress

Like the totality of the circumstances test, the central issue in IIED cases – deciding if conduct was "utterly intolerable in a civilized community" – seems best suited for jury determination. Furthermore, the court argues that Jones' actions "do not portray someone who experienced emotional distress," but granting summary judgment because of a lack of outward symptoms or because Jones "never missed a day of work following the alleged incident" puts an unfair burden on IIED claimants. (Opinion, p. 163.) The trial court may have unfairly dismissed Dr. Carnes' testimony about Jones' condition. Considering that evidence should be viewed in a light most favorable to the nonmoving party in summary judgment proceedings, the decision to discredit Carnes should be challenged. Even if the President's conduct was not sufficient to qualify as assault (Opinion, P. 163, Para. 1.), the question still remains as to whether the conduct was outrageous.

## 2007-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-03-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

Memorandum
To: Overseeing Partner
From: First Year Associate
Subject: Appeal from Summary Judgment in *Jones v. Clinton*

Applying the *Celotex* standard for summary judgment, to succeed on a motion for summary judgment, the defendant must show that plaintiff presented no plausible evidence as to a material element of each cause of action. If the plaintiff has demonstrated that the record includes sufficient facts that would allow a rational jury to infer the material elements of a cause of action, summary judgment is not appropriate.

The trial judge strayed from this standard because, rather than limit her review to the existence of alleged facts, she entered into a subjective analysis of the quality or believability of the evidence. This analysis should be reserved for the jury.

The quid pro quo and hostile workplace claim are the likely best candidates for success on appeal because Ms. Jones, as noted by the trial judge, did not appear to suffer the sort of "emotional distress so severe in nature that no reasonable person could be expected to endure it…" necessary to the IIED claim. I think the quid pro quo and hostile workplace claims are more likely to succeed because they depend more on the environment and outside influences on Ms. Jones, and less on her subjective reaction.

## Quid Pro Quo Sexual Harassment

The material elements necessary for the quid pro quo claim include (i) that Ms. Jones job was adversely affected, and (ii) her refusal of Mr. Clinton's sexual advances caused the adverse effects. Here, Ms. Jones testimony alleged facts sufficient for a rational jury to infer the veracity of each material element.

In a case involving allegations of licentious conduct, the only likely witnesses are the plaintiff and defendant. Therefore, factual allegations found in the two parties' testimony will often be the only evidence available to a plaintiff to prove her case to a jury. The trial judge found that there was "nothing in plaintiff's… testimony… showing that plaintiff's reaction to Governor Clinton's alleged advances affected tangible aspects of her… terms [or] conditions… of employment." This is just not true. Ms. Jones' testimony alleges that the terms of her employment were adversely altered: her responsibilities were not only reduced but deracinated: She testified that, upon returning to her job following maternity leave, "[She] didn't have any work to do. [Her] work had been gone. [She] was sitting there doing nothing." (Pl.'s Depo. at 14.) She also testified that she was deterred from applying for promotions. (Id. at 5). She also argues that her salary was affected, claiming that her salary was reduced by "a grade." (Id. at 7). Although the employment records also provided in the evidence conflict with this claim, Ms. Jones asserted that she would dispute the contradictory evidence, and this discrepancy should weigh against a grant of summary judgment. (Id.) It is for the jury to decide which of these sources is more dependable. Moreover, Jones provides testimony that her job environment was adversely affected: she was physically isolated, treated coldly, and not given flowers on Secretary's day. (Id. at 17-18). A rational jury could include that the amalgam of these alleged alterations to Ms. Jones' workplace do suffice a detriment suffered by her.

The causation element of this claim is a bit more tenuous, but could still be inferred by a rational jury. The testimony shows that the detriment occurred after her refusal of Gov. Clinton's advances, Gov. Clinton was friends with her boss, and he had the power to exert authority over her boss' actions. Moreover, the plaintiff testifies that when Gov. Clinton's bodyguard propositioned her to meet with the Governor while his wife was away, he was aware of the name of her boyfriend. (Id. at 45). There is little to explain the Mr. Ferguson's detailed knowledge of Ms. Jones' personal life unless the Governor was taking an active interest in Ms. Jones personal affairs. Therefore, evidence in the record indicates the possibility that Gov. Clinton caused the detriment to Ms. Jones work environment. A jury should decide the verity of the claim.

## Hostile Workplace and IIED

The allegations and evidence necessary for the hostile workplace claim to survive summary judgment are almost identical to those necessary for quid pro quo sexual harassment. Rather than evaluating the factual allegations of Ms. Jones, however, the trial judge seems to inappropriately harp on her behavior and her reaction to the hostile environment she alleges that she suffered. (Opinion p. 161-163). Ms. Jones' ability to tolerate a hostile environment is not dispositive of her exposure to the hostile environment. As discussed for the quid pro quo claim, and using the same evidence, a jury could well find that, despite her sang froid in the face of her hostile work environment, she did actually suffer adverse effects because of her refusal of Gov. Clinton's advances.

# ASSIGNMENT 2007-assignment-04 — Civil Procedure 2007 — Assignment 4

Source: content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-04.md

## 2007-assignment-04 · Assignment prompt

## Background

Assume the following background: Invoking its powers under U.S. Const. amends. xiv-xv, on January 2, 2006, Congress passed the Election Improvement Act of 2006 (the "EIA"), Section 1 of which created the United States Election Agency (the "USEA"). Congress designed the USEA to administer certain issues relating to voting rights. Relevant portions of the EIA read as follows:

**Section 2: Remedies**
If the USEA finds a violation of federal election law, the agency shall order anyone responsible, including a state or local governmental entity, to take such action as the USEA deems necessary to prevent future violations of law. In addition, the USEA may direct the responsible person or entity to provide such monetary relief as the USEA shall, in its discretion, deem appropriate.

**Section 3: Review**
Any party aggrieved by a decision of the USEA may seek a declaration of its invalidity by suing the agency in the federal district court having jurisdiction over the situs of the events giving rise to the controversy. The district court shall review all matters of law de novo, but it shall hear no new evidence to decide the dispute, and shall upset agency findings of fact only if such are clearly erroneous.

**Section 4: Procedures**
The USEA shall adjudicate all disputes with the utmost expedition. In keeping with the extraordinary requirement of speedy resolution of election disputes, the USEA shall make its findings of fact and conclusions of law based on a written record submitted by the parties. The record may include affidavits, authenticated documents and other exhibits, interrogatory responses, and deposition transcripts. In no event, however, shall the USEA hear live testimony, nor shall the USEA allow any action filed within it to expand beyond the parties and claims in the original complaint. In all other matters, the USEA may use such procedures as it deems appropriate, including any principles or rules used in federal courts, such as the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

**Section 5: Option To Bypass the Agency**
A person injured by a violation of federal election law may file an action in the USEA against any person or entity responsible, as provided in Sections 1-4. In the alternative, the injured person may seek redress by filing an action in federal district court without first proceeding to the USEA. In the latter event, the court shall have the remedial powers articulated in Section 2, above.

---

In the November 7, 2006 election, voters in various municipalities around the State of Massachusetts complained of long waits at polling booths. In a November 8, 2006 interview with The Boston Globe, Secretary of State William Galvin claimed that his office was not responsible for any delays that might have occurred. According to Secretary Galvin, any problems that might have existed (and the Secretary did not admit anything had gone amiss) were the fault of the Charles Townsend Printing Services, Inc., the printing company the Secretary's office had hired to print the Massachusetts paper ballots. Secretary Galvin stated that he had originally ordered 3,100,000 paper ballots to be printed, but that one month before the election, he had orally increased the order to 4,000,000. In fact, according to Secretary Galvin, Charles Townsend had provided only 3,100,000 ballots. Finally, Secretary Galvin told the Globe reporter that he had already taken steps to reduce the future waiting times, steps including hiring additional poll workers, instituting additional polling stations, and expanding the time during which each polling station would remain open.

On November 14, five citizens of the City of Boston, each an associate of the prestigious Boston law firm of Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan, LLC, filed an action in the USEA against Secretary Galvin (in his official capacity) and Charles Townsend. In subsequent affidavits and deposition testimony, the "Boston Five," as they became known, stated that they had arrived at Polling Place 157 at 9 am, but had had to wait until 5 pm to cast their votes. The Boston Five further averred and testified that because they had been absent from work the whole of November 7, Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan had charged each of them a half day against allotted vacation leave time. As calculated from their salaries, the value of a full day of vacation was $500.00. The Boston Five also submitted a copy of The Boston Globe article containing Secretary Galvin's interview, but provided no other evidence. In terms of relief, the Boston Five requested that the USEA issue an order against Secretary Galvin and Charles Townsend requiring them (i) to assure that no polling place delays occurred in future elections, and (ii) to pay the Boston Five the value of their docked vacation time.

After receiving notice of the action, Secretary of State Galvin filed an answer denying the Boston Five's allegations. The only evidence he submitted, however, was an affidavit from a member of his staff, which stated that she had personally observed operations at Polling Station 157 from 8 am to 6 pm and had seen waits of "30 minutes to two hours but no longer." The Secretary refused to make himself or any members of his staff available for deposition.

After receiving notice of the action, Charles Townsend Printing Services, Inc. filed an answer denying the Boston Five's allegations, demanded a jury trial, and moved for an order compelling the Secretary of State to give a deposition. It further provided affidavits and deposition testimony to the effect that it had received a contract to print 3,100,000 ballots for the 2006 election, that it had complied with that contact, and that it had never received any modification request (oral or written) from Secretary Galvin.

The USEA struck the jury trial demand and denied the motion to compel Secretary Galvin's testimony. On the merits, the USEA found that Charles Townsend had failed to comply with a modified contract to print 4,000,000 ballots, and that this failure had caused the Boston Five to have to wait for eight hours to vote. The USEA held that this delay constituted an undue burden on the constitutionally protected right to vote. The USEA further found that Secretary Galvin was implementing steps to prevent delays on future election days, so it denied the Boston Five's request for an order against future delays. It did, however, order Charles Townsend to pay the Boston Five $250 each as compensation for their docked vacation time. The USEA issued a final judgment to this effect on January 3, 2007. Charles Townsend immediately announced that it would shortly file an action in the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts challenging the USEA's decision.

---

The date is January 5, 2007. You are sole practitioner with an office in Watertown, MA. Two voters there heard about the Boston Five's victory in the USEA. They tell you that they experienced substantial delays when attempting to vote in the November, 2006 election. One voter, a father, says that he had to pay for after-school day care services so as to stay in line at his polling place. The second voter says that her employer docked vacation time from her because of her long absence from work as she waited to vote. Believing that any dispute is likely to end up in federal district court eventually, the two voters want to sue Charles Townsend in district court under Section 5 of the EIA. Accordingly, they voters ask you to address two questions:

1. If they (the two Watertown voters) sued Charles Townsend in federal district court seeking compensation for their losses, what use (if any) could they make of the USEA Boston Five decision?

2. Is Charles Townsend likely to succeed in its own federal district court action against the USEA seeking to overturn the Boston Five decision?

**Time limit:** 3 hours

**Word limit:** 750 words

## 2007-assignment-04 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-04-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Question 1

The Watertown voters would try to employ offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel against Charles Townsend to preclude the company from litigating whether the company breached its contract with the State and whether this failure delayed voting. Drawing on *Parklane*, the Watertown voters could argue that Charles Townsend's breach of a statewide contract made similar future actions foreseeable and gave the company every reason to vigorously contest the Boston Five's allegations. Since the company lost its efforts to contest the issues, the Watertown voters should not suffer the expenses of re-litigation as a penalty, particularly because EIA Section 4 prohibited the USEA panel from allowing the Watertown voters to join the Boston Five action.

However, Charles Townsend would argue that the issue of whether its breach of the ballot contract caused delays for Watertown voters was neither actually litigated nor necessary to a judgment that a ballot shortage caused delays for voters in Boston. Though evidence of Boston might show a contractual breach, it would be insufficient to prove that the shortage caused delays outside of Boston; perhaps the State had enough ballots for Watertown and the delays there were caused by some other mishap. Even assuming a court would consider a theory of statewide causation, Charles Townsend could argue that the trifling damages requested by the Boston Five reduced its incentive for aggressive litigation. The company could also argue based on Restatement § 83 and *Parklane* that the USEA handicapped its defense because 1) the USEA rules prohibited live testimony often critical to a contract dispute and 2) the panel denied a motion to compel its contractual partner even to be deposed. Since such procedures are available in federal court and might have enabled Charles Townsend to win a better result, it would be unfair to take them away by allowing preclusion.

## Question 2

Charles Townsend would challenge the USEA decision as a violation of its Seventh Amendment right to a jury trial. Under *Tull*, a court would 1) compare the Boston Five's claim for relief to causes of action existing at the time of the ratification of the Amendment in 1791 and 2) examine whether the remedy sought sounds more in law or in equity. The first inquiry would be fruitless because notions of a widespread right to vote were unknown to pre-revolutionary English law. In any event, *Tull* made clear that the legal or equitable nature of the remedy is more important. Though the Boston Five sought both damages and an injunction, the mere co-existence of legal and equitable claims does not guarantee the attachment of Seventh Amendment rights. The Boston Five could nevertheless claim that the unique voting rights thread running through both their claims for relief distinguishes those claims from common law tort or contract actions. Moreover, drawing on *Atlas Roofing*, they could argue that the Seventh Amendment does not prevent Congress from creating statutory causes of action to vindicate public rights without the delays and expense of jury trials. Indeed, Congress noted in the EIA that election disputes garner an "extraordinary requirement of speedy resolution."

In response, Charles Townsend could distinguish *Atlas Roofing* because the OSHA proceeding upheld there involved a suit by the United States as sovereign. In contrast, the Boston Five brought the USEA action as private parties. Though the EIA allows the government to commence actions, a judge might be reluctant to conclude that Congress can vitiate the Seventh Amendment merely by creating coincident private and public remedies.

Moreover, Charles Townsend could argue that the Boston Five made claims based on private rather than public rights. While the Boston Five might have been willing to incur delays because they valued voting, their damages could have just as easily been caused by highway construction delaying their commute. Thus, their claim really amounts to tortious interference with contract. In the alternative, the Boston Five's complaint also seems like a claim for contractual breach since Charles Townsend surely contemplated that voters stood as third-party beneficiaries to its ballot contract.

In addition, for the purposes of the second *Tull* inquiry, these tort and contract claims reside at the core of the common law. Under this theory, the Boston Five's equitable complaint seems like a cover to avoid a jury trial, particularly because an order to avoid creating illegal obstacles to voting adds little to existing statutory and constitutional obligations. Though Congress can enact causes of action to vindicate public rights, it cannot eviscerate Seventh Amendment rights in any case remotely related to the exercise of such rights.

## 2007-assignment-04 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-04-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Question 1

The Watertown voters could try to use the USEA decision against Charles Townsend (CT) through offensive nonmutual collateral estoppel (ONMCE). The likelihood of their success, however, is questionable. First, because ONMCE attaches only to issues actually decided, they will be limited by the USEA decision's scope. The voters would clearly want to preclude re-litigation of whether CT caused the delays they experienced. But CT might claim that the issue in the USEA decision – whether CT's breach caused delays in Boston – did not encompass the issue in this case – whether CT's breach caused delays in Watertown. As such, ONMCE would likely be available only for the limited question of whether CT breached their delivery contract.

Second, the voters would have to prove that the USEA decision merits preclusion. Under the Second Restatement, administrative proceedings can be used for ONMCE only if (1) they meet the preclusion requirements for judicial decisions and (2) they "entailed the essential elements of adjudication." For the first, the court will look to the fairness test put forward in *Parklane Hosiery*, the factors of which cut both ways here. The Watertown voters could not have joined the USEA action; § 4 of the EIA prohibited it. On the other hand, CT may not have had incentive for zealous litigation; the Boston Five's damages request was effectively nominal. Although one could argue that CT would have known the USEA suit would have triggered more substantial losses through issue preclusion, that logic is cyclic – the question before the court is precisely whether preclusion attaches. Finally, suit in the district court may offer significant procedural advantages (e.g., the ability to solicit live witness testimony on waiting times, to join responsible third-parties, and to depose the secretary). And this last factor may tilt the balance in CT's favor.

In any event, the USEA proceeding may also fall short on the essential elements question as the procedural disparities just mentioned also indicate that the USEA proceedings may not be of sufficient quality to merit preclusive effect. CT was able to submit depositions and affidavits refuting the case against them, but they were refused the chance to challenge the only evidence of their breach – the secretary's statements. Critically, this might run afoul of § 83(2)(b)'s fair opportunity to rebut provision, or, § 83(2)(e)'s opportunity to obtain evidence requirement. As such, the Restatement appears to counsel against the availability of ONMCE.

## Question 2

To overturn the Boston Five (BF) decision, CT will have to show that the USEA violated their Seventh Amendment jury rights, which will depend mostly on whether they had such a right at all.

Under *Tull*'s two part test, the right exists as it did at common law, which can be determined based on (1) the cause of action and/or (2) the remedy demanded. The cause of action here – a violation of federal election law – seems initially without a clear common law analogue. Pointing to BF's request for lost wages and citing *Granfinanciera*, however, CT could argue that the BF cause was actually simple tortious interference. If so, the right would certainly have attached to BF's common law analogue in tort. But this conclusion seems unlikely. BF's stated claim involved election law violations and the court may reject such a tortured interpretation.

As to remedy, CT could argue that BF's demand for lost wages was a legal remedy, to which jury rights attached at common law. *Atlas Roofing* and *Granfinanciera*, however, cabin this conclusion: because Congress can assign to agencies adjudication of newly created public rights, not only must the remedy be legal, the right asserted must be a private one.

There is, without doubt, a strongly public element to the right in this case. The right is a statutory construct, and it serves to protect the sanctity of elections – a uniquely public concern. Nonetheless, there is also a private side: the right neither "belongs to nor exists against the [] government;" and although *Granfinanciera* does reject the government-as-a-party requirement for public rights, § 5's bypass option implies that individuals, not the public, hold and can pursue the right's vindication. Ultimately, though, Congress did not merely re-cast a common law action in statutory terms. Private interference with voting rights is only actionable pursuant to the EIA's scheme, and may be considered an integral part of that scheme. Therefore, under *Granfinanciera*, the right involved will likely be seen as a public one, to which no jury right can attach.

## 2007-assignment-04 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2007-assignment-04-model-answer-03.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Question 1

This would be nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel. It is fairly clear that an issue at dispute in this case – whether the oral modification was made – was litigated (*Cromwell*) and was decided (*Russell*) in the previous case. Thus, the issue in this case is whether a decision made by a federal administrative agency – in which a second party was prohibited from joining – will be accorded preclusive effect in a federal district court.

As a threshold issue, only decisions of a certain quality are accorded preclusive effect, i.e. if litigants were afforded a "full and fair" opportunity (*Parklane*). Under *Elliott*, an administrative determination will be accorded preclusive effect where the agency is "acting in a judicial capacity" where parties have "adequate opportunity to litigate." Similarly, RJ § 83 suggests preclusion "where an administrative forum has the essential procedural characteristics of a court."

The limited evidentiary procedures in the USEA and, in the Boston Five case, the refusal to order depositions makes a preclusive effect unlikely. The *Elliott* hearing consisted of over 100 witnesses and 150 exhibits. Similarly, the Restatement states that at a minimum the parties must have access to procedural elements allowing "the parties to obtain evidence." While this should be balanced against the need for the speedy resolution, such a balance should not be used to spirit away a defendant's ability to defend itself in subsequent suits.

Of course, in offensive nonmutual estoppel even if the judgment is of sufficient quality it is in the court's discretion to allow or deny preclusion (*Parklane*). If fairness considerations counsel against the application of preclusion, the court will likely demand the issues be relitigated.

Here the application of NMCE seems unfair. The subsequent proceeding affords significantly more advantageous procedural opportunities for Townsend. The very problems suggested by the quality determination suggested above would be remedied by a proceeding in district court. It seems particularly unfair to Townsend to allow offensive nonmutual preclusion in an original district court action when the first action was started in an agency with reduced protections at the option of the original plaintiff. Also, to the extent that preclusion is about maximizing judicial efficiency by avoiding repetition, it is relevant that such a limited administrative proceeding may have little predictive value for the outcome of a full trial on the merits in a federal court.

## Question 2

Charles Townsend is likely to challenge the Boston Five decision on two grounds. First, that the agencies denial of their demand for a jury trial violated the Seventh Amendment. Parties are entitled to a jury trial where, on balance, two factors support their claim (*Tull*): (1) where the 18th-century action would have been brought in a court of law, rather than equity, and (2) where the relief sought is legal, rather than equitable, in nature. In this case, the Boston Five's action, though deceptively contractual in appearance, likely has no historical analogue. The relief the Boston Five sought was mixed; the relief they received was legal only. *Tull* seems to suggest that the relevant inquiry is the relief sought, not received, which makes sense. Considering that historically only equity courts could supply both remedies requested, and that the equitable relief seems the more significant in this case, the denial probably does not violate the Seventh Amendment.

In any event, this case may be covered by the "public rights" exception (*Atlas Roofing*). The right asserted in this case is likely public – more like an OSHA fine than a fraudulent conveyance claim. The initial suit included the government in its sovereign capacity and *Nordberg* suggests that a qualifying public right can be asserted by or exist against the government in its sovereign capacity so long as it is closely entwined with a federal regulatory system. The problem is that this is a federal cause of action existing against a state government and so a lot will depend on how expansive the court reads this exception.

Second, they will likely claim that the procedures employed by the agency are a violation of Fourteenth Amendment due process because they categorically do not provide for live testimony and in the present case denied the request to depose the Secretary of State.

Due process requires a balancing between the private interests at stake against the government's interest and any public interest. (*Goldberg*) This case falls squarely between *Goldberg*, requiring at a minimum a fair opportunity to defend oneself by confronting witnesses and presenting arguments and evidence orally, and *Eldridge*, where the court did not require live testimony as it was unlikely the testimony would materially supplement the written record.

Since the statute prohibits live testimony, a fair adjudication is unlikely if the record cannot be completed by deposition of the relevant parties to the litigation. Moreover, the additional cost to the system is low since both parties would bear their own deposition costs – the only cost is that inherent in adjudicating from a well established, as opposed to a poorly established, record. Consequently, it seems likely that Townsend would succeed on a due process claim.

---

*Note: This situation is not covered by § 1783.*

*It is worth noting that this analysis is somewhat problematic since it implies that if the second defendants were to bring their suit in the USEA, that the first agency decision would have preclusive effect since the procedures are the same. This type of forum shopping is never desirable.*

*One could argue that the action resembles a tortuous interference with contract claim that probably would have sounded in law at the time of the enactment of the Seventh Amendment (then as a specialized application of trespass on the case). However, this would be true only if the claim was intentional interference, negligent interference with contract remains almost universally unrecognized in American jurisdictions even today.*

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of the full quotation.

## Question

Assume that Alabama has by statute prohibited its courts from dismissing any lawsuit on the ground of forum non conveniens, and that the state follows the First Restatement on Conflicts of Laws.

Peter Pettigrew was born and raised in Mexico. Dedalus Diggle and his friend, Ginny Weasley, were born and raised in Alabama. All three are currently students at San Antonio College in San Antonio, Texas. On May 4, 2007, the three students took Diggle's car across the boarder to Mexico to blow off steam. On May 6, as the students were returning with Diggle driving, the car struck a telephone poll about 10 miles south of the US/Mexico border. The car, damaged but still operational, was driven to a doctor in Mexico, who diagnosed Pettigrew as having a case of whiplash. The doctor put Pettigrew in a neck brace, whereupon both he and the car were transported back to San Antonio. Subsequently, Pettigrew's stay in a San Antonio hospital resulted in major complications, and he incurred about $110,000.00 in medical expenses.

On April 28, 2008, Pettigrew sued Diggle in Alabama state court, claiming that Diggle's negligent driving of the automobile caused Pettigrew's injuries. On May 12, 2008, Diggle removed the case to Alabama federal court. Diggle then answered, asserting as a defense that the road where the accident occurred was in a poor state of repair and had confusing signs, and thus that the accident was not his fault. On the same day as he filed his answer, Diggle moved to dismiss the case on the grounds of forum non conveniens, arguing that the case should be litigated in Mexico.

You are an associate in the law office that represents Pettigrew, and the date is June 9, 2008. The partner handling the case asks you to prepare a memorandum, 625 words or less, analyzing what steps might be taken, and arguments might be made (as well as possible responses), to keep the litigation in a court located in the State of Alabama (where the partner thinks that the jury pool will be more favorable). The partner also asks whether there is any need for haste on the subject.

## 2008-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-01-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 – Model Answer 01

First, we should immediately move for remand. Diggle's removal was improper, since Alabama is his home state. § 1441(b). If we make the motion within the 30-day deadline from the removal, the case will be remanded to Alabama state court, which will apply Alabama state law prohibiting forum non conveniens dismissal. Note that under Alabama's First Restatement choice of law rules, the Alabama state court will still apply Mexican tort law.

If a motion to remand is not made before June 11, the case will remain in federal court, which has original jurisdiction over the case. § 1447(c). The plaintiff and defendant are diverse, as their status as students has not made them domiciliaries of Texas, and the amount-in-controversy requirement is met. § 1332. In federal court, the choice of state or federal forum non conveniens rules is an *Erie* question.

In moving for a forum non conveniens dismissal, Diggle is not directly invoking any federal statute (such a dismissal is governed by neither § 1404 nor § 1406), so he cannot apply the rule from *Hanna*, applying federal statutes wherever they are valid and applicable. This leaves a conflict between state law and federal judicial practice, which should be resolved by the line of reasoning from *Erie*, *York*, *Byrd*, and *Hanna* (that *York* should be limited by the "twin aims" of *Erie*). Here, the question of forum is potentially outcome-determinative, as Alabama and Mexico could have different choice of law rules that affect the outcome (Diggle will counter that both are likely to apply Mexican tort law). Moreover, we can argue that use of state law here may well encourage forum-shopping, a factor under *Hanna*. Plaintiffs can choose forums that are unlikely to allow forum non conveniens dismissal (ironically, exactly what we are trying to do in this case). Justice Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna* would disagree, as the law regarding forum non conveniens is unlikely to influence the primary behavior of negligent drivers. Diggle could also argue that, though the decision under review is not a transfer but a dismissal, *Stewart* is still better respected by applying federal law. Though about a statute, it answered fundamentally the same question, and specifically mentioned that forum selection can be considered a procedural matter.

If both remand and application of state law under *Erie* fail, we have to argue the merits of the forum non conveniens dismissal, which should be made only if the balance of equities strongly favors the defendant. *Gulf Oil*. Here, litigation in Alabama is probably not too inconvenient for Diggle, as he's domiciled there. Diggle will argue that litigation in Mexico is not a large burden on Pettigrew, for the same reason, but the symmetry of the situation makes it unlikely that the deference granted to plaintiff's choice of forum would be overturned. Also, Pettigrew currently lives in the US. The evidence is split between the two locales (the car, witnesses, and defendant made it back to the US after the accident, but the allegedly dangerous road is in Mexico). Since Pettigrew's choice of forum appears motivated by legally valid reason and not by forum-shopping (since Alabama will likely apply Mexican tort law anyway), the deference granted to his choice is very high. *Iragorri v. United Technologies*. Diggle may be able to more easily obtain a § 1404 transfer to Texas (venue is good as defendant resides there, § 1391(a)(1), and that's where parties and evidence are currently located), but that would effect no change of law. *Van Dusen*.

## 2008-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-01-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 – Model Answer 02

The first thing to do is move to remand back to state court, where Alabama law ensures that the forum non conveniens motion will fail. Although the parties are diverse, Diggle is still a resident of Alabama, since there is no evidence that he intends to make Texas his permanent residence. Since the state suit was in Diggle's home state, he had no right to remove to federal court and Pettigrew can move to remand, as long as he does so before June 12, staying within § 1447(c)'s 30-day time limit for non-subject-matter-related defects. Since the parties are diverse and the amount in controversy requirement is met, the federal court has diversity jurisdiction.

Failing that, Pettigrew can argue that the federal court should use the Alabama law on the motion to dismiss. He can use *Walker* to argue that there is no "unavoidable" clash between any federal procedural law and state law, since forum non conveniens is a matter of common law. Since there is no such conflict, *Hanna*'s arguably procedural test does not apply, and the "twin aims of *Erie*" are considered instead: discouraging forum shopping and avoiding inequitable administration of laws. Allowing plaintiffs to dismiss on forum non conveniens grounds in federal courts but not state courts will encourage plaintiffs to ensure their cases are brought into state court, creating inequitable administration of laws and giving defendants who get their cases into federal court an inequitable advantage over defendants who must remain in Alabama state court. Additionally, Pettigrew can use the test of *York* and *Ragan*, which mandates that state law be used if changing to federal law would "substantially affect" any right granted by the state. Dismissing the case from the Alabama courts would affect the right given by Alabama to sue for negligence, since moving the case to Mexico, with its different system of justice, will certainly substantially affect the trial.

Diggle could counter that moving the case to Mexico is not really outcome determinative: it would not make the case impossible, in the way that using a shorter statute of limitations would do, since the lawsuit could still continue in Mexico. He could also analogize from *Stewart* and argue that forum non conveniens is a clearly procedural matter that is legitimate federal law under *Erie* because it does not impinge on substantive rights.

Pettigrew can argue in the alternative that a forum non conveniens dismissal is not justified. The one witness and the car are located in the United States. Evidence of medical costs as well as the accuracy of the whiplash diagnosis is in the United States, where Pettigrew was treated. The burden on Pettigrew of going to Mexico is high; although he is a citizen of Mexico, he is currently attending school in Texas. A judgment against Diggle, an American citizen, would be far easier to enforce if it was issued by an American court.

Diggle might counter that it would be easier for Mexico to apply Mexican law than Alabama: since the accident occurred in Mexico, and since the First Restatement of Conflicts of Laws requires that the law of the locus of the injury be used, any trial in Alabama would have to use Mexican law. Any evidence regarding the conditions of the roads where the accident occurred is in Mexico; if the road has since been changed, any witnesses of the prior condition of the roads are likely to be in Mexico. Since a forum non conveniens motion should be dismissed "unless the balance is strongly in favor of the defendant," *Gilbert*, Pettigrew is likely to prevail.
