## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of the full quotation.

## Question

On July 1, 2008, due to an inability to raise operating capital on faltering credit markets, the multilayered entities that previously ran the "Walmart" national chain of superstores in the United States sold all of their assets (including the stores and the "Walmart" brand name) to a single corporation, MegaShop, Inc. MegaShop was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in New York. MegaShop executives decided to keep the Walmart brand name and all of its stores, the operations of which remained essentially unchanged. Indeed, industry observers commented that consumers "never knew the difference."

On July 15, Ms. Padma Patil, a resident and citizen of South Carolina, was traveling in her car on the way to see her sister, Parvati, who lived in Great Falls, Virginia, right next to the Maryland/Virginia border. On her way through North Carolina, Padma stopped at a Walmart store and bought a tacky lamp as a present for her sister. The shade of the lamp pictured beach scenes of Georgia, both Carolinas, Virginia, and Maryland. When Padma arrived at her sister's house and plugged in the gift, the lamp exploded, causing serious injuries to both Patils. Both were hospitalized in a Maryland hospital, and each ended up incurring over $90,000.00 in medical expenses.

You are an associate in the law firm who represents the Patils. The partner in charge of the case did some research on jury pools in various states. The partner decided that the most sympathetic jury pool was likely to be in South Carolina, and so on September 2, 2008, suit was filed (naming both Patils as plaintiffs and MegaShop, Inc. as the sole defendant) in a United States District Court for the District of South Carolina (South Carolina has only one federal district). The complaint alleged that MegaShop, Inc. negligently designed and manufactured the lamp, and that it also was strictly liable in tort for the Patils' injuries.

On September 17, MegaShop moved to dismiss the action for lack of venue. The partner has asked you for a memo analyzing two related issues:

- The legal standards applicable to MegaShop's motion, as well as how the facts currently known relate to those standards; and
- The additional facts needed to defend against the motion. On this latter point, the partner asks you to discuss BRIEFLY (two paragraphs at absolute most) what a discovery plan to find out these additional facts might look like, identifying what discovery tools might be most useful in this regard.

Note: Assume that South Carolina's long-arm statute provides that its trial courts may exercise personal jurisdiction in any case when such exercise is consistent with the United States Constitution.

## 2008-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-02-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 – Model Answer 01

Since the cause of action is in tort, and does not involve intellectual property or a specific plot of land, the only applicable venue statute is 28 U.S.C. § 1391, sections (a), which addresses venue in diversity suits, and (c), which addresses venue for corporations. Venue lies against MegaShop, the single defendant, in the District of South Carolina (SC) if it "resides" there, § 1391(a), that is, if it was subject to personal jurisdiction there on September 2, when the Patils' suit was filed, § 1391(c). Under Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 4(k)(1)(A), in turn, MegaShop was subject to personal jurisdiction in the federal District of SC if it was subject to the personal jurisdiction of its state courts. Since the state long-arm statute permits personal jurisdiction over a defendant to the fullest extent consistent with the U.S. Constitution, the venue inquiry collapses to the single question of whether personal jurisdiction over MegaShop is proper in SC.

Given the facts of the case, we can almost certainly demonstrate general, and possibly also specific, jurisdiction over MegaShop in SC. Through its (predecessors') continuous, systematic association with SC in operating Wal-Mart stores throughout the state, MegaShop has made itself amenable to suit there even where unrelated to its specific SC-related actions. See *Perkins v. Benguet*. While plaintiffs often find that demonstrating specific jurisdiction is less burdensome, there are some potential weaknesses to a specific jurisdiction argument in this case. Under *International Shoe*'s multi-factor test, it is clear that MegaShop will not be inordinately inconvenienced by litigation in SC, and that the level and nature of MegaShop's business activity in SC constitutes "purposeful availment" of the state's business. See *Hanson v. Denckla*. However, the connection between MegaShop's SC business and the substance of the Patils' suit is somewhat tenuous.

To be sure, Padma Patil started her trip in the state, SC has an interest in adjudicating its residents' claims, and the tacky lamp's beach scenes included SC beaches, which could indicate MegaShop's efforts to connect the tacky lamp to SC. However, the lamp was purchased in North Carolina (weakening our argument that its scenes connect it to SC), and the Patils' injuries occurred in Virginia. Thus, depending on SC's choice of law rules, either North Carolinian or Virginian tort law, rather than South Carolinian, will apply. Moreover, none of the relevant events (except Padma's departure) took place — and none of the relevant evidence can be found — in SC. Thus, in this case, general jurisdiction may be easier to demonstrate.

In order to develop the case for specific jurisdiction, we need to tie the tacky lamp to SC. Thus, we need to know whether the tacky lamp was actually available for purchase in SC, and if so how many were sold there, which we can find out through interrogatories but which MegaShop might plausibly claim is more readily available to us through the MegaShop sales and inventory documents we will also request. Additional information about the lamp itself, like where and by whom it was manufactured, should also be available through interrogatories and document requests.

In order to develop the case for general jurisdiction, we need to know whether MegaShop's presence in SC is sufficiently systematic and continuous. See *Perkins*, *Helicopteros*. We can use interrogatories and document requests to gather information about how many stores it has in SC, when they opened, and whether Wal-Mart's presence in the state has been continuous. A deposition of a MegaShop executive would be helpful on this score, either a central planner or someone responsible for the Southeast region of the country.

## 2008-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-02-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 – Model Answer 02

## Part 1: Legal Standards and Applicable Facts

§ 1391 governs MegaShop's motion to dismiss. In conjunction, § 1391(a) and (c) mean that venue lies in the District of South Carolina if that court has personal jurisdiction over MegaShop. The district court has in personam jurisdiction if the state court does. F.R.C.P. Rule 4(k)(1)(A).

Since the South Carolina long-arm statute extends to the limits of the U.S. Constitution, the Patils need only show that South Carolina state jurisdiction over the defendant would be consistent with Due Process: that the defendant has made "minimum contacts" with the forum state. *International Shoe*. They can argue under a theory of general or specific jurisdiction.

The Patils' strongest argument is that there is general jurisdiction. There are numerous Walmart stores, Sam's Clubs, distribution centers, and thousands of Walmart employees in South Carolina. This is far more than what was found insufficient in *Helicopteros*.

The Patils may also be able to argue that there is specific jurisdiction by showing limited contacts between the defendant and the forum. Such contacts might include (a) its sale of a product to a South Carolina resident, (b) its sale of a product to a resident who started her trip in South Carolina, or (c) its sale of a product that depicted beach scenes from a particular area, including South Carolina. The first two contacts above likely fail since neither shows that MegaShop was seeking the benefits or protections of South Carolina such that it "purposefully availed" itself of the forum. See *Hanson v. Denckla*. In addition, the fact that the lamp exploded does not seem to arise out of any of these contacts, which is required under a theory of specific jurisdiction. As a result, a theory of specific jurisdiction is unlikely to succeed.

In addition to showing that the defendant had minimum contacts with the forum state, the plaintiffs will also need to show that the exercise of in personam jurisdiction over the defendant would not offend notions of "substantial justice and fair play." *Burger King*. Since South Carolina law is unlikely to apply in this action (the accident did not occur in South Carolina and the evidence is not in South Carolina), one might argue that it is unfair for the trial to be held there. Nonetheless, MegaShop is a large company and can afford to defend far from home — especially since it has such a large presence in South Carolina. In addition, the interest of the forum state in providing redress to its citizens and the interest of the plaintiff in obtaining relief in a convenient forum suggest that the exercise of in personam jurisdiction would be fair.

## Part 2: Additional Facts and Discovery Plan

To defend against the motion to dismiss for lack of venue, the Plaintiffs should focus on obtaining information relating to: (1) the nature and extent of MegaShop's business in South Carolina; (2) the market in which it sells the lamp; and (3) the manufacture and design of the lamp. The first category of information will go to establishing the pervasiveness of MegaShop's contacts in South Carolina (to help support a theory of general jurisdiction). The second category of information will go towards proving that the defendant purposefully availed itself of the South Carolina forum. The third category will establish whether the manufacture or design of the lamp took place in South Carolina, thus enabling a showing that the cause of action arose out of those contacts.

To obtain these facts, the Plaintiffs should request document productions relating to the above categories of information, submit interrogatories to MegaShop's management, and depose several of the defendant's employees. The employees deposed should include those involved in the manufacture and design of the lamp, the sales heads responsible for determining where the lamp will be sold, and regional management that could provide information on MegaShop's activities in South Carolina.

## 2008-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-02-model-answer-03.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 – Model Answer 03

MegaShop's motion to dismiss for lack of venue is very likely to fail. Under § 1391(a), venue is proper in any judicial district where the defendant resides if the case is in federal court under diversity jurisdiction, and under § 1391(c), a corporation is deemed to reside in any district in which it is subject to personal jurisdiction. Therefore, venue is proper if MegaShop is subject to personal jurisdiction in South Carolina.

Rule 4(k)(1) provides that the federal court can exercise personal jurisdiction if MegaShop is subject to personal jurisdiction in South Carolina, and the court will likely find that MegaShop is subject to personal jurisdiction in that state under a theory of general jurisdiction and possibly under specific jurisdiction as well. Since South Carolina's long-arm statute provides that the court may exercise personal jurisdiction in any case when such exercise is consistent with the Constitution, only a due process analysis is necessary to determine if an exercise of jurisdiction is proper.

General jurisdiction is appropriate if the defendant's activities in the state are so substantial that it should reasonably expect to be subject to a suit there on any claim and would not suffer an inconvenience from defending there. MegaShop is a massive corporation with stores in every state, including a likely large presence in South Carolina. It is very possible that the court will find that MegaShop has sufficient contacts — such as a significant number of stores, employees, sales revenue, and directed marketing — with the state that it should reasonably anticipate having to litigate in South Carolina. The court will also likely find that, being such a large corporation, MegaShop will suffer little to no inconvenience litigating in the state.

The court may also find that specific jurisdiction exists under the minimum contacts test laid out in *International Shoe*. The picture on the product indicates that it was in part intentionally aimed at South Carolina, suggesting purposeful availment. If the defective product is sold in South Carolina, the state would have an interest in regulating the product to protect its citizens. The plaintiff is also a South Carolina resident, and the court is likely to take into consideration the interest of the plaintiff in having the trial near her home. MegaShop, on the other hand, is a large corporation that could likely defend itself in South Carolina with little to no inconvenience. However, unless there is proof that the defective product was also sold in South Carolina, the court is less likely to find specific jurisdiction since the injury occurred outside the state and the contacts with South Carolina are largely unrelated to the cause of action.

During discovery, it will be important to determine the nature and extent of MegaShop's business in South Carolina and information on the manufacture and sales of the lamp. This could be accomplished through the use of interrogatories, depositions, and requests for production. Depositions of the regional manager and national sales manager would likely be appropriate. Interrogatories can be used to identify the appropriate employees for deposition. Requests for production should include documents relating to the manufacture of the lamp and sales figures on the lamp, particularly sales in South Carolina. It would be important to discover if the flaw was limited to a single batch or even just that one lamp, or if it was a widespread problem that affected all of the lamps and perhaps other similar lamps, and to what extent, if any, the defective products were sold in South Carolina. We should also request documents regarding the extent of MegaShop's business in South Carolina.

# Assignment 3 (Graded)

## Logistics and Rules

If you are worried about your ability to type quickly enough, you may use your 150-minute time block to write the assignment out long-hand, then use any length of time to type precisely (including all spelling, punctuation, and other errors) what you wrote. If you choose this option, hand in both the long hand and the typed version.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

9. Your response is due the due date/time below. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of the full quotation.

## Question

Assume the following background: Invoking its powers under U.S. Const. amends. XIV–XV, on January 2, 2006, Congress passed the Election Improvement Act of 2006 (the "EIA"), Title 1 of which created the United States Election Agency (the "USEA"). Congress designed the USEA to administer certain issues relating to voting rights. Relevant portions of Title 1 of the EIA read as follows:

---

**Section 2: Remedies**

If the USEA finds a violation of state or federal election law, or that some person or entity has breached a contract or committed a tort relating to elections, the agency may direct the responsible person or entity to provide such monetary relief to anyone damaged thereby as the USEA shall deem appropriate. The USEA may also provide appropriate equitable relief.

**Section 3: Procedures**

The USEA shall adjudicate all disputes with the utmost expedition. In keeping with this directive re speed, the USEA shall make findings of fact and conclusions of law based on a written record submitted by the parties. The record may include affidavits, documents and other exhibits, interrogatory responses, and deposition transcripts. In no event, however, shall the USEA hear live testimony, nor shall the USEA have the power to compel persons who would rather not do so to testify before it. In all other matters, the USEA may use such procedures as it deems appropriate, including the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. There shall, however, be no appeal from USEA rulings to any court (state or federal).

**Section 4: Option To Sue in Federal Court Without First Proceeding to USEA**

Any person or party injured by a violation of state or federal election law, or by a breach of a contract or the commission of a tort, with respect to the conduct or administration of elections may (without first proceeding in the USEA) sue the person or entity who caused the injury in a federal district court of competent jurisdiction. The court may award any and all relief, equitable or legal, as it deems appropriate.

---

In the November 7, 2006 election, voters in various municipalities around the State of Massachusetts complained of long waits at polling booths. In a November 8, 2006 interview with The Boston Globe, Secretary of State William Galvin claimed that his office was not responsible for any delays that might have occurred. According to Secretary Galvin, any problems that might have existed were the fault of the Charles Townsend Printing Services, Inc., the printing company the Secretary's office had hired to print the Massachusetts paper ballots. Charles Townsend is incorporated in Massachusetts, where it also has its principal place of business. Secretary Galvin stated that he had originally ordered 3,100,000 paper ballots to be printed, but that one month before the election, he had orally increased the order to 4,000,000. In fact, according to Secretary Galvin, Charles Townsend had provided only 3,100,000 ballots.

On November 14, five citizens of the City of Boston, each an associate of the prestigious Boston law firm of Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan, LLC, initiated a proceeding in the USEA against Charles Townsend. In subsequent affidavits and deposition testimony, the "Boston Five," as they became known, stated that they had arrived at Boston Polling Place 157 at 9 am, but had had to wait until 5 pm to cast their votes. The Boston Five further averred and testified that because they had been absent from work the whole of November 7, Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan had charged each of them a day against allotted vacation leave time. As calculated from their salaries, the value of a full day of vacation for each was $750.00. The Boston Five's legal theory was that Charles Townsend had breached its contract with the State of Massachusetts to print enough ballots, causing them the monetary damage as detailed above. The Boston Five also submitted a copy of The Boston Globe article containing Secretary Galvin's interview, but provided no other evidence.

On November 21, after receiving appropriate notice of the action, Charles Townsend filed an answer in the USEA denying the Boston Five's allegations and moved for an order compelling the Secretary of State to give a deposition regarding the alleged oral contract modification. Charles Townsend further provided affidavits from its officials to the effect that it had received a contract to print 3,100,000 ballots for the 2006 election, that it had complied with that contract, and that it had never received any modification request (oral or written) from Secretary Galvin. On November 23, the USEA denied the motion to compel Secretary Galvin's deposition (citing Section 3, above), then announced that it would rule in about a month.

On November 28, Charles Townsend sued the Boston Five in the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts, seeking a declaratory judgment that it was not liable in any way whatsoever to the Boston Five. Charles Townsend requested that the District Court expedite the case. On December 6, the Boston Five moved to stay the District Court litigation, citing the fact that the USEA was already adjudicating the dispute. On December 8, the Massachusetts District Court granted the Boston Five's motion for a stay, observing, "I expect the USEA to resolve this dispute, whereupon I can simply enter judgment on preclusion grounds."

On December 11, Charles Townsend filed a notice of appeal from the District Court's stay order. Later that same day, it filed an emergency motion in the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit requesting that its appeal be expedited. The First Circuit granted the motion to expedite.

You are a law clerk for one of the First Circuit judges assigned to the case. Your judge asks you for a memorandum addressing the following questions:

1. Is there federal court subject matter jurisdiction over Charles Townsend's lawsuit?

2. Is there appellate jurisdiction over Charles Townsend's appeal?

3. Assuming a "yes" to questions (1) and (2), is the District Court's stay appropriate? Focus your answer on whether the District Court could appropriately give preclusive effect to an eventual USEA decision.

## 2008-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-03-model-answer-01.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 – Model Answer 01

## Question 1: Federal Subject Matter Jurisdiction

There is likely federal subject matter jurisdiction here. Because this is a declaratory judgment action, the district court would have to decide if there was subject matter jurisdiction over an imaginary suit; if so, then the court does have federal subject matter jurisdiction. This is a major exception to the well-pleaded complaint rule. As applied, given the explicit grant of a cause of action (triggering arising-under jurisdiction) for suits in Section 4 of the EIA, Townsend can likely proceed in a declaratory judgment action because the Boston Five could have sued Townsend in an imaginary suit under EIA § 4.

## Question 2: Appellate Jurisdiction

There is likely appellate jurisdiction over this action, but on fairly narrow grounds. Under the collateral order doctrine, as articulated in *Cohen v. Beneficial*, there are three factors relevant to this inquiry. First, the order must be collateral to the merits — that is, it must be separable from the merits. Second, the prior decision itself has to be final as to that issue: the order must finally resolve the issue to which it relates. Finally, a later appeal itself would be too late and the rights would be lost.

As applied to this case, the *Cohen* exception would seem to apply. The stay in this case would be collateral to the merits in allowing the USEA to hear the case. Second, the stay appears to be final as to the issue of hearing the case to full conclusion (i.e., reaching the merits). There may, however, be one way out of this with the judge's phrasing of his stay order. The judge expects the claim to be resolved by the USEA; however, should the claim not be resolved by the USEA in a way that is satisfactory to the judge, he would in effect be able to reopen the case, and the stay would not be final. As to the third factor, save for the judge's looseness in language, it seems as though if the stay is not lifted, claim preclusion might kick in, preventing an appellate court from effectively reviewing the stay.

The other bases for appellate jurisdiction are unlikely to work. A stay is not otherwise a final decision. Rule 54(b)'s element of more than one claim among many is not met. § 1292(a)(1) only applies to refusal to issue an injunction. Finally, § 1292(b) appealability requires (1) a controlling question of law; (2) substantial ground for difference of opinion; and (3) that it materially advance the ultimate resolution of litigation. Importantly, a § 1292(b) appeal requires both permission of the District Court and the Court of Appeals, and neither court made any of the necessary findings. A writ of mandamus is an extraordinarily unlikely action here and unnecessary.

## Question 3: Appropriateness of the Stay

The district court's stay seems inappropriate in (prospectively) honoring the USEA's administrative procedures. An agency's adjudications, according to the Restatement, would be honored if the following standards are met: (a) adequate notice; (b) right to present evidence and opportunity to be heard; (c) formulation of issues of law and fact; (d) a rule of finality specifying when a final decision is entered; and (e) such other procedural elements as might be necessary.

Section 3 of the EIA is woefully inadequate; due process issues are raised. Element (a) seems to be satisfied in the fact pattern because Townsend receives adequate notice. Critically, however, element (b) is missing because the EIA does not empower the USEA to compel witnesses — including, but not limited to, the Secretary of State in Townsend's request. Without the ability to compel even written affidavits from witnesses, the adjudication is stripped of its essential function — investigation. Those who can voluntarily obtain the information will almost always win, a clear process violation. Taken together, *Elliott* and *Marrese* in the civil rights context indicate that any preclusive effect given to an agency — whether issue or claim preclusion — must be contingent on adequate processes at the agency level. Those processes do not exist with the USEA; that is, EIA § 3 is insufficiently robust.

## 2008-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-03-model-answer-02.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 – Model Answer 02

## Question 1: Federal Subject Matter Jurisdiction

Yes, there is arising-under subject matter jurisdiction over Townsend's lawsuit. For a court to find subject matter jurisdiction in a declaratory judgment, the party who would file the "coercive action" under the facts of the case must have subject matter jurisdiction; a defense under federal law is insufficient. Because the Boston Five could have sued in a federal district court under § 4 of the EIA, this creates arising-under jurisdiction. Therefore, Townsend can sue for declaratory judgment in federal court.

## Question 2: Appellate Jurisdiction

Yes, there is probably appellate jurisdiction pursuant to the collateral order doctrine. The district court's order to stay the litigation because of a parallel proceeding is similar to that in *Colorado River*. These stays are appealable under the *Cohen* exception to interlocutory appeals. The collateral order doctrine requires that for an order to be appealable it must: (1) conclusively determine the disputed issue (i.e., be final); (2) resolve an important issue collateral to the merits; and (3) be effectively unreviewable if immediate appeal were not allowed.

Here, (1) the stay would conclusively determine the issue of whether the case will go forward; (2) the stay is collateral to the merits of the case (which is a contract dispute); and (3) the stay is effectively unreviewable on appeal from a final judgment because, assuming the USEA's decision merits preclusive effect, Townsend's federal suit would be dismissed for claim preclusion and so it would not matter to the First Circuit whether the stay was erroneous or not.

Any other basis for appellate jurisdiction would probably fail: (a) a stay is not otherwise a final order (the case is not dismissed or otherwise formally disposed of); (b) the stay could not be appealed under Rule 54 because there is only one claim being made by the parties (whether the order for ballots was increased); (c) there were no findings from the judge to trigger § 1292(b) (the judge did not say that the order involves a controlling question of law, or that there is substantial ground for difference of opinion, and that immediate appeal of the order may materially advance the ultimate termination of litigation); (d) there was no injunction to trigger § 1292(a)(1); and (e) issuing a writ of mandamus is only appropriate in extraordinary instances and this is not such an instance.

## Question 3: Appropriateness of the Stay

The district court's stay is not appropriate because an eventual dismissal on preclusion grounds in reliance on the USEA proceedings would probably be in error. This case is vastly different from *Colorado River*, where the federal court stayed its proceedings pending the resolution of concurrent state court proceedings involving the same matter; here, the concurrent litigation is taking place in the USEA — a federal agency.

An agency's procedures must be of sufficient quality to merit issue or claim preclusion, and the USEA's procedures appear insufficient. Two of the purposes of the doctrine of res judicata are to prevent the waste of scarce judicial resources and courts reaching inconsistent results, but these benefits do not take precedence over giving litigants a fair trial with procedural protections. To balance these competing interests, the Second Restatement of Judgments states that agency determinations are given preclusive effect only if the proceedings entail essential adjudication elements including: (1) adequate notice; (2) the right to present evidence and legal arguments, and to rebut evidence and arguments; (3) formulation of issues of law and fact; (4) a rule of finality; and (5) other procedural safeguards. These factors are necessary to ensure that preclusive effect is given only to agency determinations that mirror the due process safeguards available in federal litigation.

In this case, the USEA is resolving a factual dispute over whether the order for ballots was increased, based solely on a newspaper article and a written record without any live testimony to assess the parties' relative credibility. Moreover, the USEA may not compel testimony of any witness, no matter how critical to the litigation, nor does the USEA allow appeals of its decisions. Because of the USEA's stark procedural inadequacies, the district court should not give preclusive effect to the agency's findings; therefore, a stay pending the litigation of the parallel suit would be inappropriate.

## 2008-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2008-assignment-03-model-answer-03.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 – Model Answer 03

## Question 1: Federal Subject Matter Jurisdiction

Yes. In a declaratory judgment action, the court must evaluate who would have otherwise brought the lawsuit (a "coercive action") and determine if there would have been subject matter jurisdiction over that suit. The Boston Five could have sued Charles Townsend under the EIA, a federal law. Thus, there is federal subject matter jurisdiction over Charles Townsend's declaratory judgment action.

## Question 2: Appellate Jurisdiction

There is likely appellate jurisdiction over Townsend's appeal under the collateral order doctrine. Under *Cohen*, an order on a "collateral matter" may constitute an appealable final judgment if it (1) conclusively determined the question, (2) resolved an important question independent of the merits, and (3) is effectively unreviewable on appeal from the final judgment.

The decision on this issue was concluded and closed. By granting the stay and stating that it would apply claim and issue preclusion to the USEA, the district court essentially made a final determination that it would not look into the facts of this case (assuming the District Court can give preclusive effect to the USEA hearing's decisions). The decision to stay the case was collateral to the merits of the case, and involved an important question, as it means that Townsend's declaratory judgment action would not be tried in the district court. Finally, a decision to stay this declaratory judgment action is essentially unreviewable on appeal, because the district judge's stay amounts to a refusal to adjudicate the claim. The idea behind the stay is that, once the USEA decides the issues relevant to the claim, the federal court would then honor that determination as res judicata.

If the collateral order doctrine is insufficient, then there is probably not appellate jurisdiction under any other theory. Townsend is unable to appeal the order under § 1291 as a final order because the stay is not otherwise a "final order" — it did not resolve any claim. He cannot appeal under Rule 54(b) because the action does not present more than one claim for relief. It also may not be reviewed as an interlocutory appeal: Rule 23(f) is limited to review of class certifications in class action suits; § 1292(a)(1) applies only to the grant, denial, or modification of an injunction; and the requirements of § 1292(b) are not satisfied because there were no findings raising an issue involving a controlling question of law. Finally, a writ of mandamus is unlikely because it should issue only in extraordinary circumstances.

## Question 3: Appropriateness of the Stay

The stay is inappropriate, because the procedures of the USEA do not meet the standards required to make its decisions worthy of preclusive effect. Thus, the district court's rationale for granting the stay does not justify its decision. In *Colorado River*, a similar stay was justified on the grounds that there was an interest in promoting efficient judicial administration. Here, however, because the decisions of the USEA do not deserve preclusive effect, it is likely that waiting for the decision of the USEA will not save such resources.

According to the Restatement, a valid and final adjudicative determination by an administrative tribunal has the same preclusive effect as a judgment of a court. However, in order for an administrative proceeding to have this preclusive effect, it should provide: (1) adequate notice to persons bound by the adjudication; (2) the legal right to present evidence and legal argument; (3) a formulation of issues of law and fact in terms of the application of rules; (4) a rule of finality; and (5) a conclusive determination of the matter in question.

The USEA proceeding seems particularly suspect under requirement (2) in that it will resolve factual disputes about whether the ballot order increase took place based only on a written record. The USEA proceedings do not allow for live testimony. The USEA, therefore, will be making decisions without the evidentiary benefit of live witness testimony. Furthermore, the USEA's reliance on the newspaper article means that its decision will be based, in part, on an article that may be entirely biased or unreliable. This problem could be exacerbated by the fact that the USEA cannot compel the testimony of critical witnesses. Finally, because there is no right to appeal, these problems cannot be resolved in a later proceeding. In effect, giving preclusive effect to the decisions of the USEA would raise serious Due Process concerns because the USEA proceeding likely does not offer all of the protections required under this clause.

On another note, giving preclusive effect to the decision of the USEA may not be appropriate because it involves voting, an important federal right. Cf. *Gjellum* (involving state agencies).
