## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of a full quotation of the standard.

---

## Question

Assume Maryland, Virginia, and DC all follow the First Restatement approach on choice of law, that all three "states" follow federal forum non conveniens law, and that all three states have long-arm statutes that allow their state courts to exercise jurisdiction when allowed by the due process clause.

Peter Pettigrew was born and raised in South Carolina, where he lived until he attended first college (entering in 2005), then law school (entering in August of 2009) at the University of Virginia ("UVa"), which is located in Charlottesville, Virginia, in the jurisdiction of the United States District Court for the Western District of Virginia. In April of 2009, upon finding out that he had been accepted to UVa Law, Pettigrew decided to save on tuition by becoming a Virginia resident. Accordingly, with his parents' help, he placed a down payment on a small condo in Charlottesville, registered to vote there, and obtained a Virginia drivers' license. After law school, Pettigrew planned to move to D.C. to work for the Justice Department.

Dedalus Diggle, Pettigrew's friend, also was born and raised in South Carolina. Like Pettigrew, Diggle attended UVa undergrad, but upon nearing graduation in 2009, he took the Foreign Service exam, passed with flying colors, and was posted in August of 2009 to Barbados. The order sending Diggle to Barbados stated that his assignment was to last until further notice.

Before leaving for Barbados, Diggle decided to loan his car to his girlfriend, Ginny Weasley, a domiciliary and citizen of Washington, D.C. Diggle frequently visited Weasley in D.C. during Diggle's college years. In addition, the Ernie McMillan Car Repair Shop, Diggle's favorite car servicing location, was located in D.C. When in D.C. for a visit, Diggle would take the car to Ernie's for servicing.

Just before he left for Barbados, Diggle visited Weasley in D.C. and took the car around to Ernie's for a checkup. Ernie told Diggle that the car had a slow power steering fluid leak, and that he should have it fixed within the next few weeks. In his excitement for his departure to Barbados, however, Diggle forgot to tell Weasley about the leak.

In November of 2009, unbeknownst to Diggle, Pettigrew and Weasley began having an affair. To demonstrate her love, Weasley decided to treat Pettigrew to a beach vacation in Ocean City, Maryland. Weasley drove Diggle's car down to Charlottesville, Virginia, picked Pettigrew up, and the two drove to Ocean City, Maryland. After a blissful week, the two began the return trip in the car back to Charlottesville.

While still in Maryland, near the Maryland/DC border, with Weasley driving, the car spun out of control and hit a tree. Weasley called a towing service, which towed the car to Ernie's Repair Shop. (Ernie subsequently looked at the car and discovered that it had no power steering fluid in it.) Pettigrew was taken to a hospital in DC and treated for his injuries, which required an extended hospital stay and resulted in bills of over $100,000. After his hospital stay, he returned to Charlottesville to continue school.

Pettigrew broke up with Weasley. He then sued both Weasley and Diggle in Maryland state court for negligence. His theory against Diggle was that Diggle's negligent failure to tell Weasley to get the steering fluid leak fixed had caused the accident. His theory against Weasley was that she had been negligent in not getting the car checked out upon receiving it from Diggle.

You are Weasley's attorney. Your excellent friend from Harvard Law School, Luna Lovegood, is Diggle's lawyer. You meet with Lovegood, and both of you decide that Maryland state court juries are extremely pro-plaintiff. You would prefer to litigate in Virginia federal court if you could, but you believe that anywhere is better than Maryland state court. Lovegood just wants to do whatever is best for Diggle, and she agrees that anywhere would be better than Maryland state court.

### Question (a)

What is your best strategy for getting the case to Maryland federal court? Would it help to try to get one defendant out of the case?

### Question (b)

Suppose you're able to get the case to Maryland federal court. Can you get the case to Virginia federal court? Why or why not?

### Question (c)

Now suppose you're unable to get the case to Maryland federal court. What strategies might you pursue to force Pettigrew to litigate somewhere else? (Where else?) You should treat Washington, D.C. as just another state for purposes of this question.

## 2009-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-01-alysson-ford-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## Removal to Maryland Federal Court

The best strategy to get the case to Maryland federal court would be to get Diggle dismissed from the case for lack of in personam jurisdiction (IPJ) and then to remove the case to federal court on the basis of diversity jurisdiction. There is no evidence that Diggle had any contact with Maryland — neither the continuous and systematic contacts necessary for general jurisdiction, *Helicopteros*, nor the minimum contacts required for specific jurisdiction under *International Shoe*. The fact that Diggle's car was involved in an accident in Maryland is insufficient to confer jurisdiction over him since the decision to take the trip to Maryland was the unilateral action of Weasley and Pettigrew, *see Hanson*, and Diggle had no reason to foresee that the car would be taken to Maryland under the stream of commerce test. *See World-Wide Volkswagen*. Once Diggle is dismissed, Weasley can remove the case to Maryland federal court under §§ 1441 & 1446 based on diversity jurisdiction since she is a domiciliary of D.C. and Pettigrew is a domiciliary of either Virginia or South Carolina, and the hospital bills indicate that the amount in controversy is over $75,000.

Alternatively, both Diggle and Weasley may be able to jointly remove the case to federal court based on diversity jurisdiction. Diggle could argue that he is a domiciliary of Barbados since he intends to remain there indefinitely, which would ensure complete diversity regardless of Pettigrew's domicile. He could also claim that he is a domiciliary of South Carolina while Pettigrew is a domiciliary of Virginia, as there is no evidence that he intended to remain in Virginia after school, whereas Pettigrew has purchased a condo, registered to vote, and obtained a driver's license there. Alternatively, Diggle could argue that he intended to remain indefinitely in Virginia and thus his domicile changed to Virginia while he was in school, but that Pettigrew is still a South Carolina resident since he intends to move to D.C. after law school and thus has no intention to remain indefinitely in Virginia. However, it is possible that the court may find that both Diggle and Pettigrew are domiciliaries of the same state (either SC or VA), thus destroying diversity jurisdiction under § 1332. *See Strawbridge*.

## Transfer to Virginia Federal Court

It is unlikely that the case can be transferred to Virginia federal court. In order to transfer a case under either § 1404 or § 1406, the transferee district must be one in which the case could have been brought by the plaintiff. Although it is likely that the Virginia court would have had IPJ over Diggle and Weasley based on their former residence and road trip, respectively, it probably could not have laid venue under § 1391. Neither of the defendants live in Virginia, and the fact that the trip passed through Virginia and part of the affair may have been conducted in the state is probably not a substantial enough portion of the events. It is unlikely that venue could have been laid under § 1391(a)(3) since it appears that the action could have been brought in D.C., where the negligence occurred and where there would have been IPJ over both Weasley and Diggle.[^1]

## Other Strategies

Diggle and Weasley might be able to get the case dismissed under forum non conveniens by arguing that D.C. would be a more convenient forum. Under the balancing test from *Gulf Oil* and *Piper Aircraft*, Weasley and Diggle could argue that D.C. is a more appropriate forum since one of the parties (Weasley) lives there, the witnesses (Ernie and hospital staff) are based there, the evidence (the vehicle and hospital records) is there, and the negligence (failure to repair the car) took place in D.C. However, the court would probably refuse to dismiss the case under forum non conveniens because of the marked preference for allowing a plaintiff to choose his forum and because it is likely that Maryland law will apply under the First Restatement on choice of law since the injury took place there.

[^1]: Which statute is proper may depend on how the case is removed to Maryland federal court. If Diggle is dismissed from the case, then venue was probably properly laid (because the crash took place in Maryland, probably meeting § 1391(a)(2)) and the case should be transferred under § 1404. By contrast, if Diggle is not dismissed from the case, then venue would not have been proper since the court did not have IPJ over him, and the case should probably be transferred under § 1406.

## 2009-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-01-amir-ali-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question (a)

The best strategy for getting the case to MD federal court would be to remove the case based on diversity jurisdiction. Since the amount in controversy requirement is satisfied and no defendant is domiciled in MD, we can remove to federal court if we can establish complete diversity. To get complete diversity, we need to establish that Pettigrew and Diggle are domiciled in different states (Weasely is not an issue, since she is domiciled in D.C. and Pettigrew is definitely not domiciled in D.C.). Our best case is to argue that Pettigrew is domiciled in VA and that Diggle is domiciled in SC or Barbados. We would point to the fact that Pettigrew has lived in VA for four years, purchased a condo, registered to vote, and received a VA driver's license – all of which makes it look like he intends to stay in VA. On the other hand, Diggle only went to VA for college and nothing indicates that he ever intended to stay there; therefore, we would contend that he is still domiciled in SC, where he was born and raised, or that he is now domiciled in Barbados. There are also other possible arguments; for example, it would also be possible to argue that Pettigrew is domiciled in SC and Diggle is domiciled in VA or Barbados.

Alternatively, to make the case for complete diversity an easy one, we could get Diggle to move for dismissal based on lack of IPJ. None of the facts suggest that Diggle has the pervasive contacts required to establish general jurisdiction, and Diggle probably does not have sufficient minimum contacts in MD for specific jurisdiction. *Int'l Shoe*. None of the facts suggest that Diggle has ever been to MD and Diggle had no idea that Weasely would take his car into MD, so there was no "purposeful availment" here. *Cf. Kulko*. If we can get Diggle out of the suit, it would be easy to remove to MD federal court because there is no question that Pettigrew and Weasely are domiciled in different states.

## Question (b)

We probably could not get the court to transfer this case to Virginia Federal Court under 1404(a) or 1406. These provisions only allow a district court to transfer a case to another district where that case might/could have been brought. Presumably SMJ would be fine in VA federal court, given that we are already in MD federal court. However, it is not absolutely clear that VA could have exercised IPJ over Weasely. Presumably Weasely has some contacts in VA, given her relationships with [at least] two people in the state. Weasely also availed herself to VA law by picking Diggle up, but this may not be sufficient to say that Pettigrew's negligence suit arises out of Weasely's contacts with VA. Finally, Pettigrew probably could not have brought this case in Virginia Federal Court because he could not have laid venue under § 1391(a). *See Hoffman v. Blasky*. We could try to argue that Pettigrew could have laid venue under § 1391(a)(2) because a substantial part of the events occurred in VA. Perhaps if almost all of the driving took place in VA we would have an argument; otherwise, it appears as though we're sunk under § 1391.

## Question (c)

We could definitely argue FNC. Our strongest argument would be that D.C. is a more convenient forum. Specifically, we would point to the fact that key evidence (the car) and key witnesses (Ernie & hospital staff) are located in D.C. We could also argue that, although the *crash* took place in MD, the negligence at issue actually took place in D.C.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Additionally, at least one of the defendants is a domicile of D.C.; whereas, none of the litigants are domiciled in MD. The MD court may still choose to hold onto the case, given that MD law will likely be applied because the last event necessary, the crash, took place in MD. *See First Restatement*.

## 2009-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-01-kai-fan-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

The best strategy is to have Diggle dismiss the suit for lack of in personam jurisdiction (IPJ). Defendants cannot remove a case to federal court unless the federal court has original jurisdiction (§ 1441). Since Pettigrew filed state law tort claims, the basis of federal jurisdiction has to be diversity. Both Diggle and Pettigrew grew up in SC and attended college in VA, so the court might treat them as citizens of the same state. To ensure complete diversity, Diggle should try to dismiss the suit and leave Weasley as the only defendant. The exercise of IPJ requires minimum contacts (*International Shoe*). One party's unilateral act cannot create minimum contacts between another party and the forum state (*Hanson*; *World-Wide Volkswagen*). While Diggle's car was involved in an accident in Maryland, it was Pettigrew and Weasley who unilaterally drove the car there. Diggle grew up in SC, attended school in VA, and worked in Barbados. He lacks minimum contacts with Maryland, and he will probably succeed in dismissing the suit. Once Diggle is out of the case, Weasley can remove it to Maryland federal court. Federal court has diversity jurisdiction because Weasley is a DC citizen, and Pettigrew is clearly not a DC citizen. In addition, Pettigrew's medical bills were over $100,000, which satisfies the amount in controversy requirement under § 1332.

If Diggle is unable to get out of the lawsuit, it is still possible to remove the case to federal court. To prove complete diversity, Diggle may argue that Pettigrew is a citizen of VA. A person is a citizen of the state that she intends to reside indefinitely (*Mas v. Perry*). Pettigrew attended college and law school in VA, registered to vote there, and obtained a VA driver's license. It is true that Pettigrew planned to move to DC and work for the DOJ. However, this plan is contingent on DOJ's acceptance of Pettigrew's job application. As long as Pettigrew lacks a definite intent to leave VA, he remains a citizen there. Diggle, on the other hand, is not a citizen of VA. He stayed in VA only for college, and moved to Barbados immediately after graduating college. Since his assignment to Barbados will last until further notice, Diggle is probably a citizen of Barbados. Weasley, the other defendant, is a citizen of DC. Thus, the requirement of complete diversity can be satisfied, even if Diggle remains in the case.

Once the case is removed to MD federal court, Weasley and Diggle can try to transfer it to VA federal court. The transferee court has to be a proper venue, and it must have IPJ over defendants (*Hoffman v. Blaski*). The IPJ of VA federal court is determined by VA's long-arm statute (Rule 4(k)(1)(A)). Assuming that VA's long-arm statute reaches constitutional limits, VA federal court probably has IPJ over Diggle. Diggle attended college in VA, so he has substantial contacts with VA. On the other hand, it is unclear whether VA has IPJ over Weasley. Weasley had a relationship with Diggle, a former VA resident. In addition, she drove to VA to pick up Pettigrew. However, there is no analogous case suggesting that such contacts are sufficient for IPJ. Therefore, the court may go either way on this issue. In addition, VA court is probably not the proper venue. Weasley and Diggle do not reside in VA, so § 1391(a)(1) does not apply. They can try to argue that a substantial part of the events occurred in VA, because the trip started in VA (§ 1391(a)(2)). However, the vacation was in MD, and the accident also occurred in MD. Given the lack of proper venue, the attempt to transfer will probably not succeed.

If the case remains in MD state court, Weasley can try to move it to DC by arguing forum non conveniens. The relevant factors for forum non conveniens include, among others, access to evidence, the cost of obtaining attendance of witnesses, local interest in the controversy, and choice of law (*Gilbert*). Based on the First Restatement, the location of injury determines choice of law. Since the accident occurred in MD, MD law applies. However, other factors suggest that DC is the better forum. Most of the evidence is available in DC: the car is in DC, and so are the hospital records. It is also less costly to call witnesses in DC. Ernie, who first discovered the leak, works in DC. The doctors who attended Pettigrew's injuries are also in DC. In addition, MD has no substantial interest in adjudicating the case. Weasley's negligence occurred in VA, and Diggle's occurred in DC. Pettigrew, the victim, is not a MD citizen, and he visited MD as a tourist. Therefore, the lawsuit would constitute an unreasonable burden on MD jurors. The factors weigh in favor of a conclusion of forum non conveniens.

# ASSIGNMENT 2009-assignment-02 — Assignment 2 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 2009

Source: content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-02.md

## 2009-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

# Assignment 2 (Graded)

## Logistics and Rules

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials to date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. Compose a response to the assignment of 750 words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than 750 words, the first 750 will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. You must complete your response within 150 minutes of the time you have finished downloading the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. Your response must be typed. If you are worried about your ability to type quickly enough, you may use your 150-minute time block to write the assignment out long-hand, then use any length of time to type precisely (including all spelling, punctuation, and other errors) what you wrote. If you choose this option, hand in both the long hand and the typed version.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than the professor regarding this assignment until its due date/time below. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although questions regarding the assignment submitted via email will be read, it is unlikely that they will be answered. If answered, the question and answer will be forwarded to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time below. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. Only one method is acceptable for handing in your answer: the Drop Box system under the "Course Materials" section in the course web page. Note that the Drop Box will close on the due date/time below.
11. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for the grading system used.
12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.
13. The due date/time for this assignment is Friday, Oct 22, at 9:40 am.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time.

---

## Question

The South Carolina Bank Health Act (the "SCBHA") was adopted by the South Carolina legislature and signed by the governor in June of 2008. Relevant portions of the SCBHA read as follows:

**Section 1: Statement of Purpose**

The Legislature finds that banks in this state and across the nation face an unprecedented liquidity crisis due to bad mortgage loans, and that this liquidity crisis poses a grave threat to the public interest due to the citizenry's great reliance on banks. Among the causes of this crisis are:

- an inability of banks holding mortgages to foreclose and to evict delinquent homeowners promptly, which prevents banks from quickly seizing and selling the property and removing bad loans from their books . . . .

**Section 5: Summary Eviction: Notice**

Any bank holding a mortgage on a property located in South Carolina may institute summary foreclosure and eviction proceedings against a mortgagor in default of a loan by filing a complaint in a Housing Court of the relevant county. Upon filing of such a complaint, the county sheriff of the Housing Court shall immediately give notice of the action to the mortgagor/homeowner. Notice of the action may be given to the mortgagor in any of the following ways:

(a) via hand delivery/personal service on the mortgagor;

(b) via posting of notice on the relevant property; OR

(c) via electronic notice, if the mortgagor has provided a cell phone number and email address to the bank on the mortgage application. Electronic notice under this subsection shall consist of both of the following:

> (i) a telephone call to a cell phone number previously provided by the mortgagor to the bank; AND
>
> (ii) an email attaching the complaint and summons in .pdf form to an email address previously provided by the mortgagor to the bank.

The sheriff of the county Housing Court is deemed to have complied with subsection (c)(i) of this section if he/she leaves a message on a voicemail system associated with the cell number notifying the mortgagor of the Summary Eviction action, the Housing Court Case number, and the address of the Housing Court.

**Section 8: Summary Eviction: Proceedings**

(a) If, in ten days after the Housing Court sheriff has given notice to the mortgagor of summary eviction proceedings under Section 5, the mortgagor fails to file an answer to the Summary Eviction action, or fails to otherwise appear before the court or notify the court of the mortgagor's intent to dispute the summary eviction, the mortgagor is deemed to have consented to foreclosure and eviction, and the bank may proceed forthwith to claim title to the property and sell it to satisfy the amount of the outstanding loan. A Housing Court judgment to this effect shall issue immediately and is final. No motions to reconsider or for relief from judgment shall be entertained, and no appeal will lie.

(b) If instead the mortgagor files an answer disputing the Summary Eviction action, the Housing Court judge shall without exception hold a trial on the issue within seven weeks of the answer's filing. The Housing Court shall issue its ruling orally from the bench at the close of trial, which ruling shall be reduced to a written judgment that same day.

(c) If Housing Court renders judgment in favor of the bank, the mortgagor shall vacate the premises within ten days, with the bank to take possession at the end of such time. If, however, within that ten-day time period, the mortgagor files notice of an intent to appeal the judgment, possession shall remain with the mortgagor, provided, that the mortgagor posts a bond with the court in an amount sufficient to cover the monthly rental value of the property multiplied by 6.[^1]

---

Helga Hufflepuff, an uneducated farmhand, was and is a domiciliary and citizen of South Carolina. Gringotts Bank, Inc. was incorporated in and has its principal place of business in North Carolina (specifically, in an office building located in the Eastern District of North Carolina). In January of 2008, Hufflepuff signed a mortgage contract with Gringotts Bank so as to buy a log cabin just on the South Carolina side of the North Carolina-South Carolina border in York County. In her mortgage application, Hufflepuff provided her personal cell phone number and her work email to the bank. The amount of the corresponding loan was $40,000; the assessed value of the cabin was $45,000. The contract obligated Hufflepuff to pay $2,000 per month in principal and interest until the loan was repaid.

Hufflepuff made the February–April payments but missed the May and June payments. She had realized she would have trouble continuing to pay $2,000 per month.

On July 1, 2008, in a telephone conversation (Hufflepuff used her cell phone), Gringotts demanded that Hufflepuff resume payment of $2,000/month. When Hufflepuff responded in the call that she definitely would never do so, Gringotts informed her that it considered the loan in default, and that it intended to foreclose on the loan, seize the property, and sell it. Gringotts told Hufflepuff that someone would notify her by calling her cell phone (the bank verified that it had the right number) when Gringotts initiated a summary eviction proceeding, and that she could have as few as ten days to respond.

On July 2, 2008, the farm Hufflepuff worked for announced that it had gone bankrupt and would close its doors and shut down all systems (including email) on July 9. In despair, Hufflepuff decided to go on a two-week camping trip by herself. She left on July 10, 2008. She took her cell phone with her, but discovered that it did not receive a signal out in the woods where she was camping. On July 11, 2008, Gringotts filed its Summary Eviction Complaint in South Carolina Housing Court in York County. That day, the York County Housing Court sheriff called Hufflepuff's cell phone number and left a voicemail message notifying her that Gringotts had filed a Summary Eviction action and giving the Housing Court Case number as well as the address of the Housing Court. Also on that same day, the Sheriff sent an email attaching the complaint and summons in .pdf form to Hufflepuff's work email address, but the email was returned as undeliverable.

On July 25, when Hufflepuff returned from her trip, she heard the cell phone voicemail message. She immediately went to York County Housing Court and obtained a copy of the relevant papers; a Housing Court clerk told her that she had missed the deadline to file an answer, that she had thereby consented to the eviction and to the seizure of her log cabin, and that a judgment had already issued. Hufflepuff asked if a lawyer could be appointed at state expense to represent her, and was told no. Hufflepuff was forced to leave her log cabin on August 1, and the property was sold that same day.

On August 15, Hufflepuff won the South Carolina state lottery, and is now a millionaire.

Hufflepuff is angry about how she was treated. She wants to sue York County to make sure that no one else gets treated the same way; she also wouldn't mind getting a money judgment against York County to teach it a lesson, although she doesn't need the money. She says that she really could have used a lawyer on July 25, when she went to Housing Court. She also says that she could have asked her sister for a loan to "pay something to somebody to let me keep the log cabin for a while until we got things worked out" if that would have made a difference. Hufflepuff heard somewhere that she might do better in federal court, but above all she wants to avoid a jury trial because she's afraid that the jury might end up finding out that many years ago she was a drug dealer and was convicted on a cocaine charge.

### Question (a)

Can Hufflepuff sue York County in federal court? On what grounds and legal theories? Is she likely to succeed? Discuss fully.

### Question (b)

How can Hufflepuff best avoid a jury trial in her federal court lawsuit?

[^1]: Research has shown that appeals of summary eviction judgments from the South Carolina housing courts (which are expedited) typically take about five months to complete.

## 2009-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-02-alspector-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Part A

Hufflepuff should be able to sue York County in federal court. She can assert that York County has deprived her of her Fourteenth Amendment right to procedural due process under 42 U.S.C. § 1983, which provides a cause of action against a state actor who limits any federally protected rights. Because her claim arises under a federal statute, suing in federal court should not be an issue. Hufflepuff can use a few theories.

First, Hufflepuff can attack the notice provisions of the SCBHA. Under *Mullane*, the government is required to make a reasonably calculated effort to serve notice, and this should appear as if the government actually wants to give notice. Leaving messages on cell phones, generally, might be insufficient because people change cell numbers often because of loss or theft. Low-income mortgagors might have infrequent computer access, might not know how to read PDFs because they are unfamiliar with the format, or the email might not be received because of spam filters. In Hufflepuff's case specifically, the government knew that the email was never received, so they did not even fulfill their own level of sufficiency of notice, and they did not seem like they were making a reasonable effort. Under *Jones*, the government is required to follow up if it knows that the notice wasn't received. However, Hufflepuff was warned that she would be contacted via cell about the foreclosure, the bank confirmed her number, and she did indicate that she understood email by including it in her mortgage materials. The court might see Hufflepuff's disappearance as purposeful avoidance of the foreclosure. Nonetheless, the court will probably find that the county should stick with traditional notice, like certified mail and notice in hand, as required in § 5(a).

Second, SCBHA § 8 also poses a due process issue under *Mathews*. The *Mathews* factors are: (1) risk of erroneous deprivation, (2) claimant's interest in the property, and (3) the State's interest. The risk of error is large because the mortgagor might have some way to defend the suit, and the default judgment would stick despite the small 10-day window to file an answer (which might not even be enough time to find a lawyer). Like in *Doehr*, the private interest is high because it affects a person's property interest and, even more extreme than *Doehr*, § 8 would deprive them completely of shelter. This affects both the mortgagor and their family, which makes the private interest even larger. The bank's interest is low, because the benefit they receive from quickly obtaining title to the building is low. The bank also does not have to post a bond as a security for an unlawful eviction. However, the government presumably has a high interest, since the legislature called the crisis a "grave threat to the public interest" (§ 1), which weighs against Hufflepuff. The appeals process outlined in § 8(c) also provides due process difficulties — a person being foreclosed upon is unlikely to have enough money to pay five months of mortgage payments. Balancing the above, the court would most likely find § 8 to be a violation of due process.

Additionally, Hufflepuff could complain that she should be entitled to a court-appointed attorney (*Lassiter*). However, the court is unlikely to find this persuasive, as her liberty is not at stake. And as noted above, because of the small 10-day window between service and answer, an attorney might not have helped at all.

## Part B

In order to guarantee a bench trial, Hufflepuff should drop any claims for damages and only seek equitable remedies. Under the first element of the *Terry* test, it is unlikely that York could ask for a jury trial because there is no real historical analogy for attacking procedural due process. Therefore, turning to the second, and more heavily weighted, part of the *Terry* test, Hufflepuff's requested relief should be purely equitable.

## 2009-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-02-ferriss-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Part (a): Can Hufflepuff sue York County in federal court?

Hufflepuff can sue York County in federal court. She can allege that York County's actions were in violation of her rights to procedural due process guaranteed under the Fourteenth Amendment. 42 U.S.C. § 1983 provides the statutory basis for her cause of action.

Hufflepuff can first claim inadequate notice before deprivation of her property. The bank may claim they put her on notice that they planned to foreclose, also advising of the 10-day response period. She may have been negligent in being incommunicado for so long. However, the test is not whether she actually received notice but whether the government made reasonable efforts. *Greene* dictates that reasonability of notice is decided with reference to the other available methods. Here, the other available methods would also have been unsuccessful.

However, the government is required under *Mullane* to make an effort reasonably calculated to give notice to the defendant. It seems the government was not acting as if they actually wanted to give notice under § 5(c) when they ignored the fact that the email was undelivered. Also, *Flowers* requires that the government make follow-up attempts via other methods when it knows that its first attempt failed. Notice can be insufficient even if sufficient notice would also have failed.

Hufflepuff may also claim that she had an insufficient opportunity to be heard. *Mathews v. Eldridge* outlines a three-factor balancing test for identifying insufficient procedure: (1) the claimant's interest in the property; (2) the increased risk of erroneous deprivation; (3) the State's interest. Hufflepuff's interest is exceptionally strong because she is at risk of losing her home. However, the state's interest also seems strong, if § 1 is taken at face value. *See also Di-Chem*. It seems that there is significant risk of erroneous deprivation because there is only 10 days to respond before default. People frequently are out of touch and 10 days might not be long enough to mount a proper defense. Additionally, the bank isn't really required to make any kind of evidentiary showing before the mortgagor potentially defaults by accident — all they have to do is file a complaint.

On the other hand, evidence presented is likely to be straightforward in a breach of contract case and could easily be presented on appeal. *See Shaumyan v. O'Neill*. The government may argue that Hufflepuff can appeal and even retain possession of the property via a bond, making the risk of deprivation small. However, considering that most people similarly situated cannot pay their mortgage payments, much less a bond equivalent to five months of payments, it may be a hopelessly ineffective procedural safeguard. The fact that Hufflepuff won the lottery and could, as a matter of fact, actually afford such a bond is merely a happy coincidence.

## Part (b): How can Hufflepuff best avoid a jury trial?

Hufflepuff should avoid triggering York County's Seventh Amendment right to a jury trial. For causes of action that did not exist in 1791, *Chauffeurs v. Terry* provides a two-part standard: (1) determine if the claim is most analogous to a historical equitable or legal action; (2) observe whether equitable or legal relief is requested. It is difficult to think of an analogous action at common law in this case (challenging the judicial procedures of an administrative court). Therefore, Hufflepuff should seek a form of equitable relief like an injunction and avoid monetary damages, since the kind of relief (as usual) will be dispositive. She should not seek both forms of relief because, under *Beacon Theatres v. Westover*, the court will hear issues of law first in order to preserve the right to a jury trial.[^1]

[^1]: Note from Prof. Greiner: *Beacon Theatres* will not be covered until the end of the semester.

## 2009-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-02-scharf-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Part (a)

Hufflepuff can sue York County, a state actor, in federal court under 42 U.S.C. § 1983, alleging deprivation of her Fourteenth Amendment procedural due process rights of notice and opportunity to be heard.

Since a liberty interest was not implicated, the presumption rests against Hufflepuff having had a right to court-appointed counsel. *Lassiter*. The *Mathews* test is unlikely to yield an overturning of the *Lassiter* presumption in this case, so this would not be a good claim for Hufflepuff.

### Notice

Notice must be reasonably calculated to inform the notified party of the pending action. *Mullane*. It would be difficult for Hufflepuff to argue that the voicemail and email sent were not reasonably calculated to inform her — she had provided both to her bank, and she had been warned that she would be notified by phone of her eviction.

However, where notice is known to be ineffective, there is a responsibility to make further reasonable attempts at notice. *Flowers*. Here, both attempts at notice were arguably ineffective — the notice sent to Hufflepuff's email address bounced and the call to her cell phone did not result in a connection.

This argument turns on the constitutional "effectiveness" of a voicemail message as notice. While there is nothing to indicate that a voicemail would be an unconstitutional method of notice where it meets the *Mullane* reasonably calculated test, that the call to Hufflepuff presumably went straight to voicemail without ringing (she was in an area without service) might tilt the facts towards a holding that this voicemail constituted ineffective notice, requiring further reasonable attempts.

Hufflepuff's claim is not without merit, but it is unlikely to be successful — her provision of her phone number and email address as preferred forms of communication are sticky facts in the context of *Mullane*, and the voicemail will likely be tough to *Flowers* away, as the Sheriff had no reason to know that Hufflepuff would not retrieve the message in a timely fashion.

### Opportunity to Be Heard

Hufflepuff could argue that she was not given a sufficient opportunity to be heard pre-deprivation. The controlling test is that in *Mathews*, which weighs: (1) the affected private interest(s); (2) the risk of erroneous deprivation; (3) the government's interest as counterbalanced against the private interests implicated.

(1) The affected private interest is a significant one — loss of a home. The procedural safeguard of allowing a foreclosed mortgagor to stay in her home pending appeal is offset by the hefty bond requirement.

(2) The risk of erroneous deprivation is great, due to the short windows for filing an answer or appeal. The likely economic position of those being foreclosed against and their resultant relatively limited access to counsel is relevant in this context.

The additional burden of longer windows would be limited. The statute already allows for up to seven weeks before trial. Increasing the length of the ten-day window for filing an answer to the summary eviction notice thus seems unlikely to so heavily weigh on the government's interest as to negate the risk of erroneous deprivation present in the statute as it stands.

(3) The government's interest in ensuring the rapid processing of foreclosures is great, if § 1 of the SCBHA is to be taken at face value.

In sum, this is a tricky argument for Hufflepuff, but it is probably her best claim. The short windows and the high bond requirement are good facts for Hufflepuff. *Sniadach* stands for the proposition that deprivation of the indigent can be a trip-wire — many of those being foreclosed on will assumedly rely on their foreclosed property as their only form of shelter. Depriving them with so few safeguards and in such rapid fashion weighs *Mathews* factor 1 heavily against the constitutionality of the SCBHA.

## Part (b)

Hufflepuff should drop her claims for monetary damages, and seek only equitable relief. The Seventh Amendment preserves the right to jury trial, which at the time of its enactment extended to legal but not equitable actions. The remedy sought is the most important factor governing the right to a jury trial. *Chauffeurs*.

## 2009-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

# Assignment 3 (Graded)

## Logistics and Rules

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials to date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. Compose a response to the assignment of 750 words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than 750 words, the first 750 will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. You must complete your response within 180 minutes of the time you have finished downloading the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. Your response must be typed. If you are worried about your ability to type quickly enough, you may use your 180-minute time block to write the assignment out long-hand, then use any length of time to type precisely (including all spelling, punctuation, and other errors) what you wrote. If you choose this option, hand the typed version to the professor's mailbox (with your student ID number on it), and upload the typed version to the dropbox.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than the professor regarding this assignment until its due date/time below. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although questions regarding the assignment submitted via email will be read, it is unlikely that they will be answered. If answered, the question and answer will be forwarded to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time.

---

## Question

*Semtek* (CB page 1348) held that federal common law governed the preclusive effect of a judgment of a federal court sitting in diversity, but that the federal common law rule would be to borrow the state preclusion law of the state in which the rendering federal court sat. Thus, *Semtek* involved the preclusive effect of a judgment by a federal court adjudicating a state cause of action. What should the rule be in the reverse situation? That is, when a state court adjudicates a federal cause of action, what source of law should govern the preclusive effect of the resulting judgment, and what should the content of that law be?

To sharpen this question, imagine the following situation:

Suppose New York state law provides that non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel should be applied whenever possible, without regard to whether the plaintiff seeking to invoke the estoppel could have joined in the prior lawsuit. New York gives judges no discretion on this point. Thus, as a matter of state law, New York does NOT follow the limits on non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel that the Supreme Court articulated in *Parklane Hosiery* (CB page 1313), which stated the federal (common) law on this issue. Assume that New York state procedural law has a summary judgment procedure identical to that of Fed. R. Civ. P. 56.

Two friends, Primus and Segundus, were walking down a street in Manhattan when they were stopped by two cops, Officer Devil and Officer Angel. The officers were looking for thieves who had burgled a residence in the area. What happened next is a matter of dispute. It is undisputed that Officer Devil eventually used a police taser on both Primus and Segundus in arresting them. Primus and Segundus contend that the use of the taser was unjustified. Officer Devil contends that the two friends were drunk, responded belligerently to simple questions, and subsequently resisted arrest, forcing him to use the taser. Officer Angel contends that the two friends and Officer Devil all lost their tempers, and that the whole thing was an unfortunate incident that spiraled out of control. No charges were ever filed against either Primus or Segundus.

Primus filed a § 1983 action (alleging police brutality) against Officer Devil and Officer Angel in New York State court. Officer Devil removed the case to federal court, but the federal court granted Primus' motion to remand because Officer Angel had not agreed to the removal (in fact, Officer Angel wanted to litigate in state court). The case went to trial. A jury found that Officer Devil was liable for police brutality under § 1983, finding specifically that the two friends did not pose a physical threat sufficient to justify the use of a taser. The jury found, however, that Officer Angel was not liable.

After the Primus case was over, Segundus filed his own § 1983 action against both Officer Devil and Officer Angel in New York state court. Segundus moved for summary judgment on the issue of liability against Officer Devil, arguing that the issue of whether Officer Devil justifiably used a taser had been fully litigated in Primus' lawsuit.

---

What do you think the law should be, with respect to Segundus' summary judgment motion, in this sort of situation? Should New York state, federal common law, or something else govern the preclusive effect of the Primus judgment? If federal common law, should the federal common law rule be to allow the New York state court to use the New York law requiring non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel? Or should the federal common law be to require the New York state court to use the rule articulated in *Parklane Hosiery*, which would prevent the New York state court from applying non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel?

**NOTE:** This is a "should" question. It asks what the law SHOULD be in light of the policies and concerns discussed throughout the course, not what the law is. The quality of your thoughts is what matters. You will get no credit for doing research attempting to figure out what the law "is" in this situation.

In case it helps, here are some things you might want to consider in your answer (you need not address all of these questions, or indeed any one of them — these are just to help your thinking):

- Which of the policies *Erie* attempted to further are implicated by the fact situation above, and the general question being asked?
- *Hart v. American Airlines* (CB, page 1334) posed somewhat similar issues (although the posture there was different — how?), and purported to decide the case by reference to state policies. Are the ideas upon which the *Hart* court relied legitimate? How do they relate to *Colorado River*?
- It is clear that state courts use state procedural law when litigating state court lawsuits that involve a federal claim. Is that helpful here?

## 2009-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-03-davis-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

When a state court adjudicates a federal cause of action, federal general common law — specifically the *Parklane* rule — should govern the preclusive effect of the resulting judgment. The alternatives would frustrate well established and prudent principles of judicial economy and fairness by encouraging forum shopping and the inequitable administration of justice. *See Erie*; *Semtek*. The alternatives would also promote excessive judicial abstention.

## Forum Shopping

Take for instance the Segundus lawsuit. If the reviewing state court does not adopt the *Parklane* rule, and instead follows New York preclusion law by applying offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel, then the reviewing court will likely find Devil liable. This creates a strong incentive for future defendants, especially those who are involved in suits with multiple potential plaintiffs, to secure the *Parklane* rule, which would improve the odds of preventing the use of offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel in future lawsuits. In order to take advantage of *Parklane*'s balancing test in a subsequent action, such defendants would need to remove to federal court in the initial suit (note: *Parklane* suggests that its holding is only binding on reviewing courts when the rendering court is a federal court). The defendant's incentive to remove would likely cause an increase in federal litigation. If, however, a federal common law rule (shaped by *Parklane*) governs the preclusive effect of state court judgments on federal causes of action, then the two regimes would have uniform preclusive structures, and the defendant's incentives to litigate in federal court (or vertically forum shop) would be weakened.

## Inequitable Administration of Law

The lack of a uniform *Parklane* rule would also lead to the inequitable administration of the law. For instance, if Angel had consented to removal in the first case, then the subsequent Segundus litigation (assuming both defendants opted to remove again) would have been governed by the *Parklane* rule. This would have resulted in an outcome in defendants' favor because of *Parklane*'s balancing requirements. Because they did not remove, however, the rendering court's judgment (in the absence of requiring a federal common law rule) is governed by New York preclusion law, which virtually guarantees a finding of liability. Hence a lack of a uniform preclusion framework allows the substantive outcome (a finding of liability under § 1983) to vary based on whether defendants can meet removal requirements. The ability of removal to dictate the substantive outcome of cases, and the resulting bias against defendants, is something *Erie* was particularly keen to prevent. Although we don't have identical barriers to removal here (*Erie* was a diversity case) we still get similar inequitable administration of law unless we adopt a uniform preclusion law that governs all judgments of federal causes of action in the same manner.

## Counter-Arguments and Abstention

Critics of an approach using the *Parklane* rule could argue that a federal common law rule that allows a state court to adopt the New York rule may do more to promote fairness in the application of laws. For one, the preclusion approach favored by New York state law might do a better job of reducing the incidence of inconsistent results (there would be less risk of the defendant losing in suit #1 and prevailing in suit #2 on the same issue). *Hart* echoes this principle of fairness in its decision to adopt offensive collateral estoppel. By arguing that it would prevent defendants from being able to re-litigate issues already decided, *Hart* also suggests that judicial economy, or the desire to avoid duplicative litigation, is also a justification for a strong preclusion policy.

Nevertheless, even if there is some merit to the fairness arguments in *Hart*, giving state courts the choice to follow New York preclusion law would ultimately be improper because it would allow a New York court to routinely follow *Hart* by invoking notions of judicial economy and disposing of litigation that it otherwise had the authority to consider. Such an approach would violate the principles in *Colorado River*, which articulated a federal court's obligation to exercise jurisdiction over cases that Congress authorized it to hear barring proof of exceptional circumstances. Under *Colorado River*, the fact that litigation is duplicative is not enough of a reason to refuse to hear a case. Since we are dealing with federal causes of action, created by Congress, similar principles should bar a rule that would allow a state court adjudicating such a case to summarily apply offensive collateral estoppel and dispose of arguments in a federal cause of action without at least engaging in *Parklane*-like balancing.[^1]

[^1]: Note from Prof. Greiner: This last paragraph implicates principles not taught in Civ Pro — namely, a state court's duty to litigate a federal cause of action over which it has jurisdiction. In *Alden v. Maine*, the Supreme Court held 5-4 that state courts do not always have to litigate federal causes of action if they do not want to. Assignment 3 is designed to get students to think about what the law should be based on the assigned reading, not what the law is.

## 2009-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-03-greiner-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3: Alternative Response

*[The TAs all contended that federal law should govern the preclusive effect of a state rendering court's judgment in an arising-under case. The following is a draft response that goes the other way.]*

Although the question does not concern a federal court sitting in diversity, *Erie* principles have never been limited to the diversity jurisdiction context. Rather, per Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna*, *Erie* and its progeny have been understood to articulate basic principles of our federalist system. For this reason, *Erie* is cited as the foundation of the understanding that a state's highest court is the ultimate arbiter of what state law is, even if a state-law issue arises outside the context of a diversity case (which happens in, for example, criminal law cases involving the interaction between state criminal law and federal constitutional protections). Thus, *Erie* principles, with federalism in the background, are relevant here. Concerns of forum-shopping and inequitable administration of laws might lead one to believe that the same rule should govern the preclusive effect of a judgment in a case arising under federal law over which state courts have concurrent jurisdiction, regardless of whether the rendering court is a state court or a federal court.

That said, Congress exercises great power over the distribution of federal/state power in our system, and that power should be at its height when Congress creates the cause of action. What choices has Congress made here? First, it has passed § 1738, which courts have interpreted to require that (at a minimum) subsequent courts give at least the preclusive effect to a rendering court's judgment as would be afforded in the rendering court's state. If that interpretation holds when the subsequent court is a federal court, as *Migra* suggests, then Congress has told us that federal courts will sometimes have to give more preclusive effect to a judgment from a rendering state court than they would have had the same judgment come from a rendering federal court. According to Congress, that's life.

Second, in the Primus-Segundus situation, Congress has not chosen to exercise a power it could have exercised had it wanted to assure that the preclusive effect of § 1983 suits would be governed by federal law: it could have lodged exclusive jurisdiction in the federal courts over § 1983 lawsuits, the way it has with federal antitrust laws (for example). If state courts were unable to adjudicate § 1983 lawsuits, their preclusion principles would almost never be an issue.

The arguments in the first paragraph of this response prove too much; they apply to any difference that might matter to the outcome, per the discredited *York* test. Further, all they prove is that there should be uniformity of law between state and federal courts. But as *Semtek* showed, vertical uniformity can be achieved by requiring federal courts to apply state preclusion law, and that would work if we just allowed state preclusion law to apply even in arising-under cases. That feels odd, but is it really all that odd? Prior to *Erie*, federal courts used state procedural rules to adjudicate federal arising-under cases. True, we now have the FRCP, but that is because Congress (again, Congressional power is critical here) chose to pass the Rules Enabling Act. No one would seriously contend that *Erie* requires federal courts to have their own procedural rules. So our constitutional system can certainly survive some geographic variation, even among federal courts.

All this suggests that what matters here is whether the application of state preclusion law would threaten any federal interest, which we then might balance against state interests, per *Byrd* and *Gasperini*. As a general rule, Congress in § 1738 has said that it can live with the application of state preclusion law in the federal courts when the rendering court is a state court, and under § 1738, it does not matter whether the state (rendering) court is litigating a state or federal law cause of action. Thus, while a federal interest might be threatened in a specific case, Congress did not see a threat to federal interests sufficient to enact a general prohibition against using state preclusion law in arising-under cases (to the contrary, § 1738 affirmatively requires this). And it is hard to see what serious federal interest is threatened by refusing to allow Officer Devil two bites at the apple. New York should be allowed to apply issue preclusion in Segundus' lawsuit if it so desires.

## 2009-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-03-sharon-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

When a state court adjudicates a federal cause of action, federal common law as set forth in *Parklane Hosiery* should govern the preclusive effect of the resulting judgment.

While *Semtek* borrowed from state preclusion law to define the content of federal common law, *Semtek*'s concerns about federalism and *Erie*'s twin aims require a different result when the rendering court is adjudicating a federal cause of action rather than sitting in diversity. *Erie* suggested that federal courts sitting in diversity should apply state law on substantive issues so as to protect the autonomy of states in making important judicial determinations. *Semtek*'s holding was likely influenced by these concerns, deferring to states' decisions about the proper preclusive effect to give to prior judgments. However, when a state court adjudicates a federal claim, this calculus is altered. Because federal courts sitting in diversity have no "ownership" over the state claims being litigated (merely serving as a neutral forum to protect out-of-state defendants) it makes sense to defer to state preclusion law. Similarly, because state courts adjudicating federal causes of action are not experts in the underlying federal claims, they should defer to federal common law regarding preclusion. Thus, *Semtek*'s deference to state law to define the content of federal common law is less persuasive in the context of federal claims than in diversity.

*Semtek*'s concerns about *Erie*'s twin aims also weigh in favor of a different result when adjudicating federal claims. *Erie* sought to avoid "substantial variations [in outcomes] between state and federal litigation" which would "likely . . . influence the choice of a forum." *Hanna*, 402. To advance this aim, state court judgments on federal causes of action should be afforded the same preclusive effect as federal court judgments on similar claims. Otherwise, parties would face two different preclusion regimes based on where the plaintiff brought the lawsuit initially and whether the defendant could remove to federal court — a result *Erie* aimed to prevent. In Segundus' suit, for example, if the state reviewing court were to apply New York's unlimited offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel (ONMCE) regime to Primus' judgment, Devil would almost surely be found liable again. Yet, if Primus had initially brought the case in federal court or if Angel had consented to removal, the reviewing court would have applied the *Parklane* rule, likely preventing ONMCE and allowing Devil to re-litigate liability. Thus, if a state reviewing court did not apply *Parklane*, *Erie*'s goal of preventing substantial variations in outcomes between federal and state litigation would be obstructed.

These divergent outcomes encourage vertical forum shopping, as defendants have an incentive to remove the initial lawsuit to federal court to benefit from the *Parklane* rule. *Semtek* sought to avoid these incentives, concerned that defendants would systematically remove to take advantage of more favorable preclusion rules. *Semtek* also suggests that the need for a uniform preclusion rule governing all federal question judgments is greater than for diversity judgments, where it was acceptable for states to apply different preclusion rules.

*Hart* offers a different methodology than *Semtek* for determining the content of preclusion law: rather than borrowing state preclusion law, the court substitutes its own policy determinations about the desirability of ONMCE. Yet, even following *Hart*'s policy-driven methodology, *Parklane*'s rule is superior for securing objectives of efficiency and fairness. First, unlimited ONMCE undermines efficiency by encouraging plaintiffs to "wait and see" rather than joining litigation early, resulting in multiple lawsuits which impede judicial economy and create the kind of piecemeal litigation that *Colorado River* sought to avoid. *Hart* is correct that when there are already multiple lawsuits, ONMCE can promote efficiency by preventing the expenditure of resources inherent in re-litigating liability and diminishing the risk of inconsistent verdicts. Yet, *Parklane* seeks to prevent multiple lawsuits, structuring incentives so that plaintiffs cannot reap benefits from sitting on the sidelines when they could have joined early on. Second, *Parklane* promotes fairness by ensuring that defendants are only bound by unfavorable verdicts when they had the incentive and opportunity to litigate the first suit vigorously, thus guarding against unfair results which could arise if the first suit was frivolous or did not afford sufficient procedural guarantees. Thus, while *Hart* assumes that a defendant "has had his day in court," *Parklane* seeks to ensure that this opportunity was meaningful before determining that the defendant "should not be permitted to litigate the question anew." *Hart*, 1334. Finally, *Parklane* promotes repose, preventing defendants from being surprised by offensive use of a prior judgment when future lawsuits were not foreseeable.

## 2009-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2009-assignment-03-tava-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

When a state court adjudicates a federal cause of action, federal common law (as articulated in *Parklane Hosiery*) should govern the preclusive effect of the resulting judgment.

As the Court noted in *Semtek*, the preclusive effect of a case cannot easily be classified as procedural or substantive law. On the one hand, the preclusion philosophy of a reviewing court would not affect the "primary conduct" of the parties in litigation, because it would not shape the underlying decisions of the parties that led to the dispute (*see* Harlan in *Hanna*). Yet it may be outcome determinative (*see Guaranty Trust*). A defendant who lost in a prior case would be held liable again in a court that broadly applies offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel but be able to successfully defend herself and prevail in a court that does not.

Indeed, as the post-*Erie* line of cases suggests, the purpose of the procedure/substance distinction is largely predicated on policy grounds, geared towards ensuring that federal and state courts do not overly encroach onto each other's domain. Such an observation likely guided the *Semtek* Court. Given that *Semtek* involved a diversity case implicating state causes of action, the Court erred on the side of caution to protect federalism. By borrowing state preclusion law in such a context, federal courts ensure that they do not overly shape the future consequences of state law, thus respecting state courts' superior knowledge over complex issues of state law and the weight they deem appropriate to accord to a prior state-law judgment.

Conversely, a state court adjudicating a federal question case should apply federal preclusion law. Yet because the nature of the issues being litigated is federal rather than state (unlike in *Semtek*), the source of the federal common law regarding preclusion should also be federal in nature. Thus, the state court should "borrow" the *Parklane* approach to offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel. This approach would mirror *Semtek*'s rationale and ensure that the future impact of federal law would largely be shaped by federal courts, the "experts" in such law. Moreover, this approach would further the "twin aims" of *Erie* and enhance judicial efficiency.

First, applying *Parklane* would ameliorate vertical and horizontal forum shopping by ensuring that one uniform offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel framework would apply to all federal causes of action everywhere. In contrast, allowing state preclusion law to attach would promote such behavior. Given that state courts are courts of concurrent jurisdiction, a plaintiff could select a state forum that has an offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel framework in her favor.

Admittedly, the vertical forum shopping concern is not as severe in this context as in a diversity case; unlike in *Semtek*, a defendant in a federal question case could not be "boxed" into her home state court. Yet removal is still subject to limitations — for instance, a defendant must gain the approval of all her co-defendants.

Second, if state and federal courts were to both apply the *Parklane* approach to offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel to federal causes of action, federal law would be more consistently applied. Based on a plaintiff's choice of forum, a defendant may face two different outcomes were state courts to apply their varying approaches to offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel. Here, if Angel had initially agreed to removal in Primus' lawsuit, a federal court would have adjudicated the decision. In such a situation, Segundus' choice of forum is crucial. If Segundus chooses to litigate in federal court, which would apply *Parklane*, he would likely not be granted offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel (given that he could have easily joined Primus' case). Thus, the defendants could re-litigate and win. In contrast, if Segundus chooses to litigate in NY state court, under its preclusion law, he would likely be granted offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel and the defendants would lose.

Third, applying the *Parklane* approach would enhance judicial economy. While *Hart* sought to improve judicial economy by giving greater preclusive effect to a rendering court's decision (which here would favor NY preclusion law), the *Parklane* approach promotes judicial economy in an alternative manner by putting parties "on notice" and structuring incentives. If parties knew that *Parklane* would always attach to federal causes of action, regardless of where litigated, parties would have an incentive to join in litigation early on, rather than "waiting and seeing." Further, since the *Parklane* approach is a case-by-case balancing test, it would be more just — providing for an individualized fairness assessment of whether offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel should attach based on the specific parties in play.

## Suggestions for Your Answer
