## Assignment 1: Question

Assume the following background facts and law (note all of this is fictional). In late January of 2010, Congress passed (and President Obama signed) the Emergency Home Mortgage Act (the "EHMA"), Section 1 of which reads as follows:

> **Section 1: Importance of this Act And the Programs It Creates**
> The Congress hereby finds that homeowners in the United States face a crisis in paying their mortgages that is unprecedented in the history of our nation or even the world. The financial consequences of this crisis are catastrophic to the United States economy and, thus, to our national security. Accordingly, the provisions of this statute are perhaps the most important to have passed Congress in its history.

Under the EHMA, each state was granted the power, as a matter of federal law, to change the terms of any contract or mortgage relating to a home so as to make those terms more favorable to a homeowner. The EHMA requested that each state appoint a state "mortgage czar" to exercise this modification power. Most states duly appointed mortgage czars.

Helga Hufflepuff was born in Charlotte, North Carolina, and lived there with her parents through high school. Her parents died in her senior year of high school, though, and as she had no other relatives nearby, she decided to leave the Charlotte area for college to pursue her dream of a career in international business in New York. She got into the Honors College at the University of South Carolina, and in an effort to save money on tuition, she decided to establish residency in South Carolina. Accordingly, using the money her parents had left her, she bought a small house in Columbia, South Carolina, paying $80,000 down and signing a mortgage requiring her to pay $2,000/month. The transaction closed in February of 2010.

The mortgage contract was with Gringotts Bank, Inc., which was incorporated in Delaware. Gringotts did most of its business in South Carolina, where its loan portfolio included everything from business and car loans to mortgages. Its primary operational headquarters was in Durham, North Carolina, and it held its board and shareholders' meetings there. Its CEO was Lord Voldemort, who lived in the Durham area. Lord Voldemort, a multi-millionaire, had vehemently opposed the EHMA, arguing that it was beyond Congress' powers under the Constitution. He had donated substantial amounts of money through various front entities to members of Congress who opposed the EHMA, and had purchased campaign ads on TV stations around the nation arguing that the EHMA was another big government effort designed to kill American economic freedom.

Hufflepuff made the February–May payments but missed the June and July payments. She had realized in late April that she would have trouble continuing to pay $2,000 per month. Accordingly, she applied for a modification to the terms of her mortgage from the South Carolina EHMA mortgage czar. In late May, the South Carolina mortgage czar sent Gringotts Bank (copy to Hufflepuff) an "Order" purporting to modify the terms of Hufflepuff's contract so that she only had to pay $1,500 per month. The Hufflepuff Order was one of several that the South Carolina mortgage czar issued to Gringotts.

Initially, Gringotts announced that it would comply with these orders and informed Hufflepuff that she need only pay $1,500/month on her mortgage. But Lord Voldemort, back from a 2-month period of viewing his yacht in a series of races, heard of the modifications of various mortgage contracts from Gringotts' website. Incensed that the EHMA was being used/enforced, Voldemort returned to Gringotts' central offices in North Carolina. There, he instructed the bank staff to ignore all mortgage czar modification orders.

On July 1, 2010, Gringotts notified Hufflepuff in a telephone conversation that it considered the South Carolina EHMA mortgage czar's "Order" to be unconstitutional and demanded that Hufflepuff resume payment of $2,000/month. When Hufflepuff responded in the call that she definitely would never do so, Gringotts informed her that it considered the loan in default, and that it intended to foreclose on the loan, seize the property, and sell it. Within the space of four weeks, the bank used available South Carolina legal procedures to evict Hufflepuff from the house and to sell the property. Hufflepuff lost her down payment and is currently homeless as she attends her classes.

In early September, Hufflepuff wrote a letter to Gringotts threatening to file a breach of contract lawsuit (on the theory that her mortgage contract was properly and constitutionally modified by the South Carolina mortgage czar's order) in one month against Gringotts in North Carolina state court.

You represent Gringotts. Lord Voldemort has been an excellent client of yours for some time, bringing tens of millions of dollars in business to your 2,400-attorney law firm over the past three years. You are anxious to please him at all costs. Lord Voldemort is currently facing lawsuits for civil fraud filed by various business partners in North Carolina state court, so he is anxious to avoid having the Hufflepuff litigation occur there. He wants to litigate in South Carolina federal court. Never a patient man, Voldemort doesn't want to wait for Hufflepuff to sue Gringotts, and wants to file suit in two weeks. What advice can you give this valued customer?

## 2010-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-01-fonseca-sabune-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1: Model Answer (TA Draft)

Civil Procedure Fall 2010
Professor Greiner
669 words

---

Gringotts can get to court before Hufflepuff, and choose the forum, by bringing a declaratory judgment in South Carolina federal court. Since Gringotts is suing for a declaratory judgment, subject matter jurisdiction is based on whether there would be SMJ over the "coercive action" that would be filed by the party who "should" be the plaintiff (Greiner imaginary lawsuit rule). A federal defense is not sufficient. *Mottley*. In this case, the actual plaintiff is Hufflepuff, who would bring a state law breach of contract claim. This presents a problem because of the well-pleaded complaint rule, since in this case the "real" complaint would be based on state law. Accordingly, some could argue that we could not bring this declaratory judgment in federal court.

However, federal arising under jurisdiction may also lie over state-law claims that implicate significant federal issues. See *Smith*. It could be argued that *Smith* only applies if the federal issue arises as part of the plaintiff's cause of action, which would not be the case here since the plaintiff's claim is based on state law. However, *Grable* established a two-factor test for determining whether there should be federal jurisdiction. First, the courts will consider whether there is a substantial federal interest. We will argue that this is established because Hufflepuff must first prove that EHMA, a federal statute, is unconstitutional before reaching her state law claim. The text of Section 1 of EHMA states that the financial crisis is "catastrophic to the United States economy, and, thus, to our national security," indicating that this statute is responding to a significant federal issue. Next, the *Grable* test considers whether adjudicating this lawsuit in federal court will upset the division of labor between federal and state courts. Federal courts will only exercise their jurisdiction if that balance will not be disturbed. The question presented here is purely legal, like the one in *Grable*, so it is not likely to upset this balance. Once a federal court has resolved this issue, similar litigation can move forward in state courts.

Even if arising under jurisdiction is not present, we may be able to get into federal court under diversity jurisdiction. Since Hufflepuff lost her down payment of $80,000, she meets the $75,000 amount-in-controversy requirement. To establish diversity, Hufflepuff and Gringotts must be domiciled in different states. Hufflepuff's domicile is likely not North Carolina. Although she was born there and lived there with her parents through high school, after her parents died, she subsequently bought a house and established residency in South Carolina. Since she has not expressed any intention of returning to North Carolina, it is not likely that she will be considered domiciled there. Hufflepuff may be considered domiciled in South Carolina based on her residency and home ownership there. However, if she intends to leave the state immediately after college and move to New York, she may not be considered domiciled in South Carolina because she is lacking intent to stay there. Gringotts is incorporated in Delaware, so will necessarily be considered domiciled in that state. A company is also domiciled in the state in which its principal place of business is. Based on the Supreme Court's recent ruling in *Hertz*, the principal place of business should be determined based on the nerve center test. Gringotts' operational headquarters are in North Carolina, probably establishing its nerve center in that state. Therefore, it will likely be considered domiciled there for the purposes of diversity. While it's not totally clear where Hufflepuff will be considered domiciled, since it probably will not be in North Carolina, and certainly will not be in Delaware, it is fairly likely that diversity will be established.

Finally, we must ensure that a federal court in South Carolina would have personal jurisdiction over Hufflepuff. Since Hufflepuff is living in South Carolina, she has established minimum contacts for the courts in that state to have personal jurisdiction over her. Since we're filing in South Carolina, we will have subjected ourselves to the personal jurisdiction of the courts there.

## 2010-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-01-tsoumpas-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1: Model Answer

---

Gringotts should file a declaratory judgment lawsuit in South Carolina federal court. To hear the case, the court must have subject matter jurisdiction through either "arising under" or diversity jurisdiction. Because the declaratory judgment act does not create a federal cause of action, we must show that Helga Hufflepuff (HH) could have filed her lawsuit in federal court. Imaginary Lawsuit Rule.

Establishing "arising under" jurisdiction under both Article III and § 1331 may prove difficult because breach of contract is a state law claim. The federal law at issue, the EHMA, would not be the basis of HH's cause of action, but only an anticipated defense. Thus, according to the Well Pleaded Complaint Rule, the EHMA would not establish "arising under" jurisdiction. *Mottley*.

Still, Gringotts could argue that a federal court can exercise "arising under" jurisdiction because the case involves an important federal issue: the constitutionality of the EHMA. Using the *Grable* balancing factors, Gringotts can argue that this federal issue (1) is very important, as Section 1 of the Act characterizes EHMA provisions as "the most important to have passed Congress in history"; (2) is legal, not factual; (3) is live; and (4) will not upset the state/federal balance. Given that Gringotts Bank has modified several contracts to comply with the EHMA, it will likely show that the issue is live. However, if too many contracts were modified, a federal court may decide that the federal issue will arise too frequently and upset the state/federal balance.

Alternatively, Gringotts can try to establish diversity jurisdiction. § 1332 allows a federal court to exercise jurisdiction over a state law claim if (1) the parties are completely diverse and (2) the claim meets the amount in controversy requirement. Being that HH lost her $80,000 down payment, she will likely satisfy the $75,000 amount in controversy requirement.

To be diverse, HH and Gringotts may not be domiciled in the same state. Gringotts is a domiciliary of both Delaware and North Carolina. Delaware is its state of incorporation and North Carolina, as Gringotts' operational headquarters, is its principal place of business. Nerve Center Test. Gringotts must show that HH is a domiciliary of South Carolina because diversity jurisdiction fails if HH is deemed a domiciliary of North Carolina.

Gringotts can argue that HH is a domiciliary of South Carolina because she moved and officially established her residency there. It can also argue that she lacks a definite plan to leave because she bought a house there. However, HH can argue that her domicile never changed from North Carolina (her place of birth) because she does not plan to reside indefinitely in South Carolina. She plans to move to New York to pursue a career in international business.

Even if Gringotts establishes federal subject matter jurisdiction, it still must show that a South Carolina federal court can exercise personal jurisdiction over HH. Gringotts must show that (1) HH has minimum contacts with South Carolina and that (2) jurisdiction comports with fair play and substantial justice. *International Shoe*. Being that HH lives in South Carolina, bought a house there, and applied to the South Carolina mortgage czar for her mortgage modification, Gringotts will have little difficulty satisfying either prong of the test.

## 2010-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-01-weiss-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1: Model Answer

---

To seize the forum, Gringotts should file a declaratory judgment action in SC federal court. However, because the DJ statute (28 U.S.C. § 2201(a)) does not itself create a grant of SMJ (*First Federal*), Gringotts must show that the federal court either has jurisdiction in diversity or as a federal question. In determining this question, the federal court will look to the "imaginary lawsuit" or "coercive action"—the lawsuit HH would have filed against Gringotts if she had been the one originating the action.

Under federal question (arising-under) jurisdiction, a federal court must have both a constitutional and statutory grant of power. Article III gives federal courts a very broad mandate (*Osborn*), so that isn't a problem. However, § 1331 has been interpreted much more narrowly, requiring that the plaintiff assert a claim that arises under federal law in his well-pleaded complaint (*Mottley*). For purposes of this rule, anticipated defenses do not matter. Thus, since the court will be looking at HH's imaginary complaint, her breach of contract (state law cause of action) claim will fail the well-pleaded complaint rule and preclude federal jurisdiction on this theory.

Nevertheless, federal question jurisdiction can exist in a few very rare situations when state law creates the cause of action but the plaintiff's right to relief depends on federal law (*Smith*). Under the multi-factor analysis set out in *Grable*, a federal court may hear this type of claim when the state action implicates a contested and substantial federal issue and when federal adjudication of the matter will not upset the federal-state balance of judicial responsibilities. On the first prong, Gringotts can argue that this case certainly implicates an important question of federal law, since in order for HH to obtain relief, she needs to prove the constitutionality of the EHMA. Furthermore, Congress has acknowledged that this statute is especially important in Section 1 of EHMA. A trickier argument is on the second prong. Since HH's mortgage modification was one of several issued by the SC czar to Gringotts (to put this in perspective, several modifications in the first four months of the Act's existence to one of probably one hundred banks in one of fifty states), there may be a significant problem in the disruption of federal-state balance.

Alternatively, Gringotts can argue that SMJ exists by way of diversity (§ 1332). Diversity jurisdiction is proper when there is complete diversity of citizenship (*Strawbridge*) and when there is $75,000 in controversy. The amount-in-controversy requirement here is easily met—HH lost her $80,000 down payment. Diversity of citizenship is more difficult. Citizenship for persons is determined by domicile (*Mas*), which itself is defined by residence in a state and indefinite leaving time. Gringotts can argue that HH is a SC citizen because she has residency in SC and lacks a definite plan to leave (after all, she bought a house there and isn't just staying in dorms or renting). However, HH might counter that she is still a citizen of NC (where she was born) because she is only in SC for college and has (albeit indefinite) plans to leave the state some day for a career in NY.

If the court decides that HH is a NC citizen, this would defeat complete diversity because Gringotts is also a citizen of NC. Corporations are citizens of two states—the state in which they are incorporated and the state in which they have their principal place of business (defined in *Hertz* to be their "nerve center" where the headquarters are). In this case, Gringotts is both a citizen of Delaware (incorporation) and of NC (nerve center). In the end, diversity jurisdiction probably will be established because of HH's indefinite plans to leave SC.

If a lawsuit is filed in SC federal court and SMJ is found, HH still might argue that the court lacks IPJ over her. According to *International Shoe*, a court may only exercise IPJ if the defendant has sufficient minimum contacts and the exercise of jurisdiction comports with notions of fair play. In this case, considering that HH bought a house in SC, has lived there for a good amount of time, has paid taxes there, etc., the court will most likely find that it has IPJ.

# ASSIGNMENT 2010-assignment-02 — Assignment 2 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 2, 2010

Source: content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-02.md

## 2010-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

# Assignment 2 (Graded)

Civil Procedure 2, 2010
Professor Jim Greiner

---

## Logistics and Rules

**Due date/time:** 9 am Thursday, Oct 14
**Word limit:** 950 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours, 15 minutes

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials to date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me three days before the assignment is posted if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time below. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

---

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of the full articulation of the standard.

---

## Question

Assume the following: California and Arkansas follow the First Restatement on choice of law (and you know the choice of law approach in New York). Both the New York and the Arkansas Rules of Civil Procedure include rules worded identically to those of Fed. Rs. Civ. P. 8 and 12. In the 2008 case of *Granger v. Skeeter*, a defamation action, the Arkansas Supreme Court held that the Arkansas version of these rules established a system of notice pleading akin to the Seventh Circuit's decision in *American Nurses* and the United States Supreme Court's decision in *Swierkiewicz*. The New York Court of Appeals held similarly regarding its version of the rules in the 2009 case of *Weasley v. Malfoy*. Both courts considered and explicitly declined to follow the Supreme Court's interpretation of Fed. Rs. Civ. P. 8 and 12 in *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal*.

Arkansas has an antidiscrimination statute similar to Title VII (or, if you prefer, to § 1983, as applied to these facts). As applied to the Paula Jones fact situation, the essential elements of this Arkansas statute are as follows:

1. that the plaintiff refused to engage in a sexual relationship with the defendant;
2. that the plaintiff suffered adverse employment consequences;
3. that these adverse consequences were orchestrated by the defendant because of the plaintiff's refusal to engage in a sexual relationship;
4. and that the defendant's actions were taken "under color of state law."

Assume that there is no res judicata (preclusion) or limitations problem with Paula Jones' ability to file a second lawsuit against Bill Clinton. (In other words, I don't want you discussing or considering preclusion or limitations in this assignment!) Assume that Bill Clinton is currently domiciled and residing in Manhattan, which as far as federal courts go is in the jurisdiction of the Southern District of New York.

Jones has decided to sue Bill Clinton (again) using exactly the same factual allegations as were in paragraphs 6–57 of her first, Eastern District of Arkansas complaint in the case (those paragraphs are attached to this assignment), except that instead of alleging that Clinton is president of the United States and living in D.C., she will allege that he is a New York resident and domiciliary (assume that he is in fact a Manhattan resident and domiciliary). She refuses completely to consider changing any of the factual allegations in the complaint. The relevant language of the complaint is attached to this assignment. Assuming she can get a court to apply it, her sole and only cause of action would be the Arkansas anti-discrimination statute. She plans on requesting $200,000 in damages.

You are Jones' lawyer. In what courts might Jones sue Clinton? What would be the legal advantage or disadvantage of the various choices? What legal issues would the various choices raise? (Do not consider the political aspects of any lawsuit, or jury pools, or judge selection, or convenience of the parties, including forum non conveniens.) What would you recommend?

## 2010-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-02-fisher-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

---

Jones probably can only sue Clinton in Arkansas state court, Arkansas federal court, NY state court, and NY federal court because these are the courts where she can get IPJ. There is IPJ over Clinton in NY because there is general jurisdiction in the state where the defendant resides. It should be fairly easy to establish IPJ in Arkansas because Clinton had minimum contacts there (all of his acts giving rise to the complaint occurred in Arkansas, *International Shoe*, and as the former governor of Arkansas, it is reasonably foreseeable that he would be haled into Arkansas court for his conduct as governor, *World-Wide Volkswagen*). We probably could not get IPJ in CA since Clinton has not had minimum contacts there. Since there is IPJ in Arkansas and NY state courts, there is also IPJ in Arkansas and NY federal courts. 4(k)(1)(A).

There is SMJ in Arkansas and NY federal courts because the parties are diverse and the amount in controversy exceeds $75,000. Article III § 2; 28 U.S.C. § 1332.

We can lay venue in NY federal court since Clinton resides there. § 1391(a)(1). We can lay venue in Arkansas federal court because Arkansas is the locus of the events at issue. § 1391(a)(2).

We want to litigate where our complaint will be subject to notice pleading standards because our complaint would probably survive a 12(b)(6) motion under notice pleading. Under *Iqbal* pleading standards, however, our complaint would probably be dismissed. The complaint doesn't include well-pleaded factual allegations that adverse employment consequences were orchestrated by Clinton because of Jones's refusal to engage in a sexual relationship. Rather, the complaint at most includes legal conclusions in regard to element three of the Arkansas antidiscrimination statute. This is not enough to nudge our claim across the line from conceivable to probable.

If we sue in NY or Arkansas state court and the case stays there then notice pleading standards would be used. While both courts will probably apply Arkansas substantive law (Arkansas uses the First Restatement so the law of the state where the injury took place governs; NY uses Second Restatement and under an interests analysis Arkansas law would probably govern), each court will use their own procedural law. The standard for the motion to dismiss is likely to be considered procedural for horizontal choice of law purposes and state courts always use their own procedural law. Since both courts use notice pleading standards, we would be in good shape if the case stayed in either state court.

I recommend that we sue in NY state court because Clinton is likely to remove to federal court if we sue in Arkansas and then the *Iqbal* standards might apply. If we sue in NY, however, Clinton will be stuck with state notice pleading standards because he cannot remove from his home state court to federal court on the basis of diversity. § 1441(b).

We want to avoid suing in federal court to begin with because then the *Iqbal* pleading standards might apply. Figuring that out requires an *Erie* analysis. We could try to argue that the state notice pleading standards should apply since they don't directly conflict with FRCP 8 and 12 (a *Walker*-like move). Even though the state RCP are identical to FRCP 8 and 12, it will still be hard to establish there is not a direct conflict since the state court and SCOTUS have interpreted the rules to call for very different standards. We could argue that *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* should be read narrowly to only apply to potentially high cost discovery suits, but this would be a hard sell since our case arguably could be a high cost discovery suit. In addition, *Iqbal* seems inconsistent with this narrow interpretation since the court could have tried to contain discovery if that was the main concern. Therefore, the Court will probably find a direct conflict.

Under *Hanna*, if an on-point FRCP directly conflicts with state law, the FRCP applies as long as it is valid. The Court will probably consider FRCP 8 and 12 valid because pleading standards are rationally capable of classification as procedural regulations. Also, the Court will probably find that FRCP 8 and 12 do not abridge, enlarge, or modify a substantive right under the REA because pleading standards do not have a substantial impact on state policy unrelated to litigation.

We could try to argue that the *Iqbal* judicial interpretation of FRCP 8 and 12 is not the FRCP itself. Rather, we'd argue that the judicial interpretation qualifies as a federal judicial practice and thus is subject to the outcome determinative test as modified by *Hanna*, which calls for consideration of the twin aims of *Erie*. We'd argue that the vertical choice of law would affect forum shopping and the equal administration of law at the time of filing (since vertical choice of law would impact whether a case is dismissed) and at the outcome stage (if the complaint is dismissed in federal court but allowed in state court, the state suit might end successfully). Clinton might push back that the vertical choice of law won't impact primary conduct (sexual harassers probably aren't considering pleading standards during their misconduct) but the Court will probably still find that state law applies since the primary conduct test (based on *Hanna* concurrence) is secondary to the time of filing test (based on *Hanna* majority).

Since we can't be sure whether the federal court will apply the state or federal pleading standards, we are best off avoiding the whole mess by suing in NY state court.

## 2010-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-02-istel-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

---

Jones can likely only get IPJ in Arkansas state and federal court, and NY state and federal court. Jones has general jurisdiction over Clinton in NY (a court in the state in which a defendant resides has general jurisdiction over the defendant), and IPJ in Arkansas under the minimum contacts analysis: Clinton's acts giving rise to the complaint occurred in Arkansas (*International Shoe*); it was thus reasonably foreseeable that he should anticipate being hauled into court there (*World-Wide Volkswagen*). Federal court adopts the jurisdictional authority of the court of the state in which it sits. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A).

Jones has SMJ in both federal courts given the diversity of the parties and that the amount in controversy is over $75,000. 28 U.S.C. § 1332. Venue is proper in both states: § 1391(a)(1) establishes venue in the judicial district where any defendant resides (NY); and § 1391(a)(2) establishes venue where a substantial part of the events occurred (Arkansas). Because there are no minimum contacts in California, IPJ is unlikely there.

Given her options, Jones should choose her forum based on which court applies the most advantageous pleading standards. While federal courts are likely to apply *Iqbal*/*Twombly*, where Jones would have to prove that the facts alleged are plausible and not just possible, state courts are likely to use notice pleading standards (*Granger*), which just require that the defendant be on notice of the claim (*Conley*) and apply even to nearly incomprehensible complaints (*Dioguardi*). While Jones's complaint will almost certainly survive a 12(b)(6) motion under the latter standard, it may not satisfy the third prong of Arkansas's Anti-Discrimination statute under the heightened *Iqbal* standard: while the complaint specifies the details surrounding Jones and Clinton's interaction, it does not concretely establish a plausible link between their interactions and her adverse employment consequences, or how Clinton was directly involved. For instance, the complaint asserts that the reason they gave for her employment transfer was untrue, but does not provide direct evidence supporting its conclusory statement that the "real reason" for her transfer was because of her denial of Clinton's advances (see ¶ 39).

As such, Jones should try to keep her complaint in state court to be evaluated under notice pleading's lower standard. Both NY (Second Restatement, interest analysis) and Arkansas (First Restatement, place of injury) will apply Arkansas substantive law. Regardless, the 12(b)(6) standard is likely to be considered procedural for horizontal choice of law purposes, meaning both NY and Arkansas will use their own procedural law, and notice pleading still applies. To avoid removal, Jones's first choice should be litigating in NY state court (a defendant cannot remove in his backyard on a diversity claim—§ 1441(b)), over Arkansas state court where Clinton could remove.

If Clinton did remove, Jones would try to argue that since it is a diversity case, and the twin aims of *Erie* are implicated, state law should be used. Jones's first hurdle would be arguing either that (1) the state and federal laws don't conflict; or (2) that there is not a federal rule on point.

Jones would have a hard time arguing that state law and federal law do not conflict, given that federal courts have interpreted FRCP 8 and 12 under *Iqbal* to imply a much higher standard than state courts have used. Using *Walker*, Jones could try to argue that *Iqbal* should be interpreted narrowly to avoid a direct conflict where possible. However, given the political concerns of the suit (how it might affect their jobs if politicians could get sued on conclusory allegations), and its consequent similarities to *Iqbal*, it is likely that the courts will find a direct conflict.

If the court found a direct conflict between state and federal law, Jones would have a hard time arguing *Erie* choice of law analysis, given that when there is a controlling federal rule, it takes precedence over state policy (REA; *Hanna*). Here, this seems applicable given that (1) the rule applies to the issue at hand; and (2) is valid under the REA, in that it does not "abridge, enlarge, or modify the substantive rights of any litigant" (i.e., that it is procedural).

To avoid this, Jones could argue *Iqbal* is not a federal rule, but just the court's interpretation. Without a federal rule on point, Jones could argue that the *Erie* analysis should be used.

If Jones made it past this hurdle, she would have to avoid Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna*, where she would have a hard time arguing that the availability of different rules drove the conduct giving rise to the complaint (it is unlikely that Clinton considered pleading standards when interacting with Jones).

Instead, Jones could argue under *York* that since the outcome would be different in applying federal law as opposed to state law, state law should be applied. (NB: *York* is no longer good law.)

Jones's best argument would be *Hanna*'s majority. Jones would show that this is exactly the kind of case implicated by forum shopping and the inequitable administration of laws, in that Clinton would decide to remove to federal court solely to achieve a different result. And, *Hanna*'s majority overrides Harlan's concurrence.

Overall, because federal court could go either way on whether *Erie* should be employed, and even if employed whether state law should govern, it would be best to keep the case in state court, preferably in NY where Clinton can't remove.

## 2010-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-02-ratner-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

---

Structurally, before considering which court would be the most advantageous for Jones, it is essential to determine in which courts she will be able to sue at all. Because the alleged facts giving rise to the complaint took place in Arkansas, it is likely that Jones will be able to establish minimum contacts and get IPJ over Clinton there. *International Shoe*. Given Clinton's obvious connection to both Arkansas and Jones, he will not be able to escape IPJ under *World-Wide Volkswagen*. Similarly, because Clinton's residence and domicile are in NY it is a certainty that Jones will be able to get IPJ over Clinton in his new "home" state. Since IPJ will likely be satisfied in either state, it will also be the case that Jones has IPJ over Clinton in federal court in both Arkansas and NY. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A). California is a bit trickier. Although it would seem that Jones can bring suit there because she is a resident, it is unlikely that she will be able to establish that Clinton satisfies the minimum contacts requirement. *International Shoe*.

Back to Arkansas and NY: because Jones's claim ($200k) satisfies the amount in controversy requirement (28 U.S.C. § 1332) and because complete diversity exists between the parties (*Strawbridge*), Jones likely will be able to establish SMJ in either federal court. Venue is also not a problem in either NY or Arkansas federal court. Under § 1391(a) jurisdiction is permissible in the defendant's home state (i.e., NY), and SMJ in Arkansas is possible under § 1391(b), which allows jurisdiction in the locus of a substantial part of the events.

Having established that the two states available are likely NY and Arkansas, and that federal court in either state is also likely available, the question becomes which forum provides a greater advantage. This is a question of pleading standards. Given the recent decisions in *Granger* and *Skeeter*, it is likely that both NY and Arkansas state courts will apply notice-pleading standards, whereas federal courts are likely to apply the heightened *Iqbal*/*Bell Atlantic* "plausibility" standard. Jones's other lawyers may have failed to satisfy the third prong of Arkansas's Title VII ("adverse consequences were orchestrated by the defendant because of the plaintiff's refusal to engage in a sexual relationship"): while the complaint spells out the details of the alleged meeting between Clinton and Jones, at no point does it concretely link her workplace treatment to Clinton, and so is likely insufficient under the *Iqbal* standard (which requires more than just that the facts suggest the possibility of the defendant's liability) to recite conclusory statements like "It was a pretext for the real reason which was that she was being punished for her rejection of the various advances made by Clinton." ¶ 39. As such, we're going to want to be in the court with the lowest bar for pleading requirements (and *DioGuardi* suggests that the notice standard is lower than it seems).

While both Arkansas (First Restatement) and NY (Second Restatement) will likely apply Arkansas substantive law, for horizontal choice of law purposes the motion to dismiss standard will likely be considered procedural, so each state court would apply its own procedural law. Both states have a notice standard for motions to dismiss, making the question one of removal. Because Clinton would almost certainly remove to federal court in Arkansas within the required 30 days (§ 1441(a)) but would be unable to under § 1441(b) (precluding defendants from removing to federal court in their home state), the optimal court for Jones is NY state court.

But could she get the state pleading standard in federal court? This is going to be tough, but there are several vertical *Erie* arguments. The issue is that it sure looks like state and federal law (Rules 8 and 12) directly conflict. Jones's best argument would be under *York*: since the question would be dispositive of the issue (outcome determinative) state law would apply. Similarly, Jones could try a *Hanna* twin-aims argument, noting that forum shopping and inequitable administration of the laws are both implicated here (since the different pleading standards would be guiding Clinton's choice to remove). Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna* is a problem: Jones is fighting an uphill battle if she tries to argue that the parties' primary conduct was affected by the different rules applied in federal and state court (put differently, pleading standards were likely not on Clinton's mind when the "occurrence" occurred). Finally, while it is also possible that, under *Walker*, Jones could argue for a narrow reading of *Iqbal*, limiting it to its high-discovery cost and public persona facts, since those same facts likely apply with regard to President Clinton the chances of success here are bleak. Of course, the biggest problem here is that a controlling federal rule appears to be directly in conflict with the state rule, and that the federal rule is valid under the Rules Enabling Act because it does not substantially affect a substantive right. 28 U.S.C. § 2072.

This paints something of a rosy picture, though; it seems highly unlikely that a federal court would apply the state pleading standard. Clinton would remove if Jones filed in Arkansas, elevating the standard to *Iqbal*, creating a problem for Jones in terms of the third prong of Title VII. Thus, Jones should bring this case in NY state court, sticking Clinton in his adopted backyard.

# ASSIGNMENT 2010-assignment-03 — Assignment 3 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 2, 2010

Source: content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-03.md

## 2010-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

# Assignment 3 (Graded)

Civil Procedure 2, 2010

## Question

Assume that Massachusetts law is identical to federal law in all respects regarding preclusion.

Assume the following background: On January 2, 2006, Congress passed the Election Improvement Act of 2006 (the "EIA"). One of the reasons Congress passed the EIA was in reaction to a growing practice among states, including Massachusetts, to outsource the administration of municipal elections. Essentially, states (including Massachusetts) were hiring contractors to print ballots, provide voting machines, hire poll workers, count votes, and report final results to secretaries of state.

Section 405 of the EIA provided as follows:

> The Congress finds that the practice of outsourcing the conduct of municipal elections, while valid under federal law, poses grave risks to citizens' right to vote, as protected by the United States Constitution. Accordingly, should any state contract with a private entity (the "Contractor") to conduct a municipal election in that state, that contract shall be deemed to include a term imposing upon the Contractor the duty to assure that a sufficient number of ballots are available in each polling location so as to prevent undue delay in voting. This term shall not be waivable by the state.

The EIA did not provide a federal cause of action for any aggrieved party.

In early 2009, Massachusetts Secretary of State William Galvin contracted with Weasley & Weasley, Inc., a corporation organized under the laws of Massachusetts with its headquarters in Boston, to administer all aspects of the 2009 City Council and mayoral elections in Boston. When these elections were conducted in early November of 2009, however, there were widespread reports of long lines at polling locations around Boston. According to a report a week later in the Boston Globe, the delays were due to Weasley & Weasley's mistake in not printing enough ballots for the election. The article stated that Weasley & Weasley had estimated the number of ballots that would be needed by looking at turnout for the 2005 Boston municipal elections; in the 2009 election, however, the departure of several incumbents in the City Council had enlivened the overall contest, and turnout had been much higher than in 2005. The Boston Globe article detailed how every person knowledgeable about Boston municipal elections had predicted that turnout in 2009 would far exceed that of 2005.

Two people voting that day were Luna Lovegood and Pansy Parkinson. Both had had to wait in line from 7:30 am until 3:45 pm on Election Day to vote, and thus neither had been able to attend work that day. Both had been born in and had grown up in Boston and had recently turned 18, but had decided to take a year off between high school and college to work for Luna's father, Xenophilius, researching Crumple-Horned Snorkacks. They were paid $100 per day for this service, but because they had been unable to come to work on Election Day, they each lost the $100 they would otherwise have earned.

In August of 2010, Luna and Pansy went off to college, Luna at the University of New Hampshire and Pansy at UMass Boston. Xenophilius bought Luna a condo in Durham, NH to live in while she was at school. Luna fell in love with New Hampshire. She decided she wanted to live there forever. She intended to major in journalism at UNH and to start her own magazine, to be based in Manchester, NH, focusing on stories no one else would print.

Neither Luna nor Pansy was able to get over the anger they felt at the day's wages they had lost on Election Day of 2009. In August of 2010, on her second day of school, Luna sued Weasley & Weasley in Massachusetts federal district court, alleging in paragraph two of her complaint that the court had "subject matter jurisdiction over this action under 28 U.S.C. §§ 1331 and 1332." She claimed Weasley & Weasley's failure to provide a sufficient number of ballots for the election constituted a tortious interference with her employment contract with Xenophilius, and that this violation was intentional, willful, and malicious. She sued for $100 in actual damages and $100,000 in punitive damages.

Weasley & Weasley answered, denying liability. Its answer asserted that the EIA provision purporting to insert a sufficient-number-of-ballots term in the Galvin/Weasley & Weasley contract was unconstitutional as beyond Congress' powers, and because the term had never been validly inserted, there had been no wrongdoing on its part. After conducting discovery, Luna moved for summary judgment, and Weasley & Weasley cross-moved for summary judgment. On November 7, 2010, after hearing oral arguments on the motions, the judge announced (orally) that she thereby granted Luna's motion for summary judgment and denied Weasley & Weasley's motion. The judge further stated that she would decide an amount of damages at a later date and enter final judgment in accordance with the summary judgment grant and the damages decision then. On November 12, the parties reported to the court that they had reached a settlement agreement (terms undisclosed), and pursuant to that settlement agreement, Luna dismissed her case with prejudice. Luna later reported to Pansy that she received a sizeable check from Weasley & Weasley but was not allowed to disclose the amount.

Pansy has contacted you about filing a lawsuit in Massachusetts state court based on a tortious interference claim, just as in Luna's lawsuit. She is interested only in suing in Massachusetts state court, nowhere else. She wants to know what use she can make of the result in Luna's case to help her lawsuit. Can she make any use of it? To the extent it is unclear whether Pansy CAN make use of the result in Luna's lawsuit, SHOULD she be able to do so? Explain.

As applied to the facts of this case, the Restatement (Second) of Torts, § 766, provides as follows: "If [Weasley & Weasley] intentionally and improperly interferes with the performance of a contract . . . between [Luna] and [Xenophilius] by inducing or otherwise causing [Luna] not to perform the contract, [Weasley & Weasley] is subject to liability to [Luna] for the pecuniary loss resulting to [Luna] from the failure of [Luna] to perform the contract." Assume punitive damages are available if Luna proves her case on this tort.

You should NOT consider the merits of this lawsuit.

## 2010-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-03-powell-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3: Model Answer

---

Pansy's attempt to use Luna's settled suit against W&W will likely be unsuccessful. Pansy's most compelling invocation of Luna's suit is via nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel (NMOCE).

Federal common law will govern Pansy's state court NMOCE claim. *Semtek*. Issue preclusion may be invoked when the new action presents (1) the same issue, (2) that was actually litigated, and (3) was necessary to the decision. *Cromwell*; *Russell*. NMOCE additionally requires that the plaintiff could not have entered the original suit. *Parklane Hosiery*.

It is clear that Pansy's suit would address the same tortious interference and constitutional issues as Luna's. Luna and W&W's completion of discovery and filings are almost certainly sufficient to satisfy the "actually litigated" requirement.

However, Pansy will face a great—I think insurmountable—hurdle in convincing the court that the issue was decided. While the judge's ruling must have resolved the constitutional and tortious interference issues, an oral bench ruling with a written decision to follow is hardly a final decision. Opinion-writing is highly valued in the judicial system because it forces judges to thoroughly review the issues. While the writing may confirm a judge's instincts, it may alternatively lead a judge to change an untenable position and reverse her decision. Thus, W&W would not have been allowed to appeal the judge's announced decision. § 1291. Since Luna settled the case before the judge issued a written decision, W&W can likely convince the state court that the judgment was not final and thus the issue was never decided.

In the unlikely event that the state court finds the above three elements apply, it will need to consider the appropriateness of NMOCE. In evaluating NMOCE, the courts are concerned that permitting it misincentivizes plaintiffs to "wait on the sidelines" until another plaintiff has a favorable ruling, and causes unfairness to a defendant who did not vigorously defend against initial claims or who may have additional procedural opportunities in subsequent cases. See *Parklane Hosiery*. Therefore, *Parklane Hosiery* prohibits a plaintiff from invoking NMOCE if she could have joined in the initial suit.

Pansy most likely could not have joined in Luna's federal suit. While Luna's complaint alleges that the court had both arising under and diversity jurisdiction over the claims, I think it was only permitted to sit in diversity.

W&W will be eager to show that Luna's suit was based on § 1331 arising under jurisdiction because then Pansy could have joined the suit under § 1367 supplemental jurisdiction. Under § 1367, claims that "form part of the same case or controversy" can be brought together. If Pansy could have joined Luna's suit she will fail the *Parklane Hosiery* test in trying to issue preclude W&W. This limits plaintiffs' incentives to wait on the sidelines. If, though, as I argue below, Pansy's claim were based only on § 1332 diversity, Luna would not have been able to join the suit. She would have needed to seek inclusion under FRCP 20, which is denied under § 1367(b) when supplemental jurisdiction will destroy complete diversity. (Here it would—Luna and W&W are clearly Massachusetts citizens.)

Federal jurisdiction of Luna's suit was under § 1332 diversity. Tortious interference with a contract is a state claim, so Luna's § 1331 claim must be via the exception annunciated in *Smith* and *Grable* for claims that implicate significant federal issues. Certainly Luna's claim relied on the EIA, a federal statute; however, that is insufficient to get into federal court. See e.g. *Shoshone*; *Empire Healthchoice*.

Rather, there must be a contested and important federal issue that doesn't upset Congressional division of power intentions. *Grable*. W&W will argue that the constitutionality of the EIA and the protection of voting rights are important federal issues that would benefit from the expertise and consistency of the federal judiciary.

However, opening the federal courts' doors simply because a party has stated a Constitutional argument would swing the doors wide open and burden the federal judiciary with claims better handled in state courts. Unlike the significant bond issue in *Smith* and tax issue in *Grable*, a statute discussing the outsourcing of elections is insignificant. See *Merrell Dow* n.12 (reconciling *Smith* and *Moore* based on the importance of the issues at stake). In addition, permitting an arising under claim would upset Congress's intended division of power. First, states run elections and should thus deal with any ensuing litigation. As a corollary, since each state conducts its elections differently, there would be no benefit to the consistency of the federal judiciary. Further, Congress did not provide a federal cause of action. Though not dispositive, this is revealing. Unlike the quiet title actions at issue in *Grable*, the EIA could obviously invite federal claims. Thus Congress's silence reflects its intention to leave federal courts out of the issue. See *Grable* (discussing *Merrell Dow*); *Empire Healthcare*.

Therefore, the District Court was sitting in diversity when it entertained Luna's claim. Pansy would have been unable to join that suit, thus satisfying *Parklane Hosiery*'s final prong.

Nonetheless, I do not think NMOCE will be available to Pansy because of the quality of the initial "decision."

## 2010-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-03-saadi-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3: Model Answer

---

According to *Semtek*, the source of law is federal common law. Pansy (PP) might try to employ nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel (NOCE) against Weasley & Weasley (WW); however, it is uncertain whether she could have joined in Luna's (LL) suit, which implicates policy concerns courts have expressed in their hesitation to apply NOCE.

Issue preclusion probably does not apply. The issues in PP's case are the same: tortious interference with contractual obligations and WW's claim regarding the constitutionality of the EIA. Given that LL's case concerned only these issues, they were probably necessary for the decision orally to grant LL's but deny WW's motion for summary judgment. (*Rios*.) However, LL's case probably was not actually and fully litigated. (*Cromwell*.) Although the judge issued an oral statement of her decision, the case was settled before final judgment was entered. Not only does the lack of an actual decision render this case inappropriate for issue preclusion, but policy considerations also weigh against it. Even though applying issue preclusion might serve judicial efficiency, precluding WW from reasserting its claims on the unconstitutionality of the EIA would greatly disadvantage WW, especially if WW has no other possible defenses, as it would automatically trigger liability. Furthermore, other potential plaintiffs probably exist from the election incident. If WW is issue precluded, they can keep suing and asserting NOCE against WW until an inconsistent judgment arises—10 or 300 suits from PP's—which is unfair to WW. Thus, issue preclusion probably should not apply.

Given that the "same party" requirement has been relaxed, *Blonder-Tongue*, if issue preclusion were applicable, PP might attempt to invoke NOCE, thereby barring WW from raising the defenses of unconstitutionality it employed in LL's suit in PP's subsequent suit. (*Parklane*.) Most of the *Parklane* factors are satisfied. There are no prior inconsistent judgments. WW also had sufficient incentive to litigate in LL's suit given the high punitive damages at stake. (However, if WW believed it unlikely that punitive damages would have been awarded, whether WW had sufficient incentive might be more uncertain.) While it is possible that some procedural elements may differ from federal to state court, there are no major procedural advantages available to WW in state court that were not available to WW in federal court. Finally, PP, as a Massachusetts resident, probably could not have joined in the first suit. Given that LL likely is a NH resident and WW is a Massachusetts corporation, if PP had joined in the first suit, she would have destroyed complete diversity under § 1332. (*Strawbridge*.) However, if a § 1331 arising-under claim is available PP could have joined the first suit. PP's claims probably arise out of a common nucleus of operative facts and are part of the same case or controversy as LL's claims; therefore, it might seem as if she could have joined via supplemental jurisdiction. (*Gibbs*.) Yet the EIA did not provide a federal cause of action. Thus, § 1367(b) would have barred PP from joining, because PP would have been a party seeking to intervene as a plaintiff under Rule 24, thereby destroying complete diversity. The only other possibility is if LL's case fulfills the *Smith* factors, in which a case may arise under federal law even though the state law creates the cause of action. Here, LL's claim of tortious interference with contractual relations was indeed a state law cause of action that depends upon interpretation of the federal EIA and its constitutionality. Additionally, the *Smith* test requires that the issue is a legal issue (as interpretation of a statute is), and both important and seriously disputable (which this case might not be). Thus, PP might have tried to join LL's former suit.

Regardless of whether PP can invoke NOCE, courts have discretion in determining whether plaintiffs generally may. (*Parklane*.) If PP could have joined in the first suit, it seems unfair to allow her to invoke NOCE now. First of all, if WW did not have sufficient incentive to litigate (see above) or if it did not foresee the possibility of future lawsuits, allowing the use of NOCE would be unfair to WW. Furthermore, PP intentionally might have waited for LL to litigate the issue, so that if LL lost, PP would know what strategies to avoid, and if LL won, PP could simply assert NOCE against WW.

If PP could not have joined in LL's suit, however, she might be allowed to use NOCE now, as the concerns of PP gaming the system and unfairness to WW are greatly minimized. WW would not suffer from procedural disadvantages that would render the use of nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel unfair in this case (there is no obvious change in the rules that put WW at a disadvantage by being in state court) and could still raise a different defense, as WW would only be barred from raising unconstitutionality of the EIA. (*Cromwell*.)

## 2010-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2010-assignment-03-yap-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 3: Model Answer

---

Although Pansy and Luna effectively have the same underlying case, it is unlikely that Pansy will be able to make use of Luna's prior litigation to help her case. Pansy will want to invoke issue preclusion on the issue of tortious interference on the grounds that the issue was decided at summary judgment, but in order to do so Pansy will need to overcome two specific hurdles: first, she must show that the judge's announcement in Luna's case was a decision of enough finality to support preclusive effect; second, she must demonstrate that she could not have joined the first suit, and that offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel is proper. Because she is unlikely to be able to demonstrate either, it is doubtful that she will be able to make use of Luna's suit.

Whenever dealing with issues of preclusive effect, it is important to examine the source of the possible preclusion. Here, because we are dealing with the preclusive effect of a federal court decision in state court, federal common law governs. *Semtek*.

The baseline requirements for collateral estoppel are: (1) the issue was actually litigated, (2) the issue was necessary to the first decision, and (3) the second issue is substantially similar to the first. Requirements (1) and (3) have been met, as the motions were argued, and Pansy and Luna suffered the same injury from the same event.

However, because the parties settled before a final decision was issued, whether a finding of tortious interference was "necessary to the decision" is unclear. In order to be "necessary to the decision," an actual decision needs to be reached. Here, the parties settled and dismissed after an oral announcement by the judge in Luna's favor. Bearing in mind that the judge retains the right to change her mind prior to the issuance of a final order, the court is unlikely to find that, under common law principles, the decision was of a sufficient quality to sustain an application of collateral estoppel.

Second, because Luna and Pansy are not the same party, Pansy will need to convince the court to apply offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel. The touchstone for this inquiry is whether Pansy could have joined the initial lawsuit. *Parklane*. Joinder of the initial lawsuit requires an examination of the rules for supplemental jurisdiction, which in turn demands a resolution of the subject matter jurisdiction for the initial lawsuit.

If jurisdiction for Luna's suit was purely diversity (she qualifies, as she is domiciled out of state and the amount in controversy is $100,100), then Pansy could not have joined, as she is domiciled in Massachusetts. The court would not have had original jurisdiction, and the rules for supplemental jurisdiction prevent its exercise in diversity suits over additional plaintiffs who would destroy complete diversity. 28 U.S.C. § 1367(b). However, if subject matter jurisdiction for Luna's suit was federal question jurisdiction, then Pansy could have joined—either with original jurisdiction, or under supplemental jurisdiction, which allows pendent parties (28 U.S.C. § 1367(a))—and the court would likely deny application of collateral estoppel.

Luna's complaint rests on a state cause of action, so the pertinent test is whether the claim "necessarily raise[s] a stated federal issue, actually disputed and substantial…[the resolution of which will not disturb the] balance of federal and state judicial responsibilities." *Grable*. Federal question jurisdiction will not be granted merely when a state cause of action includes violation of a federal statute as one of its elements. *Merrell Dow*. However, Weasley & Weasley dispute the validity of the underlying statute, decision of which would effectively decide this claim and any future claims under the statute, and the issue is one of limited scope that would not result in significant litigation moving to the federal courts. *Grable*. Moreover, the issue is not fact-bound; it is a purely legal question of the extent of Congress's powers, and thus does not fall under the *Empire* exception. Federal question jurisdiction is thus likely. As a result, Luna will probably fail to qualify for offensive non-mutual collateral estoppel under *Parklane*.

Thus, Pansy is unlikely to be able to make use of Luna's lawsuit in her own case.

Of course, the *Parklane* analysis rests final discretion in the trial court, but it's not entirely clear that Pansy can present a strong case for application of issue preclusion. First, the facts disclose exactly the type of behavior the Court was worried about in *Parklane*: waiting on the sidelines and jumping in after finding out about a favorable verdict. Second, given the tentative nature of the decision and the lack of clear and reviewable reasoning, it seems inappropriate to grant preclusion. Conversely, however, there are strong consistency and efficiency arguments weighing for preclusion, including the fact that the defendant effectively gets a second bite at the apple by settling after an adverse announcement. There is no clear answer, and any decision must balance the crowdedness of its docket, the likelihood of additional litigation if preclusion is applied—which is probably fairly high, given that it was a citywide mistake—and the gravity of the issue at stake.
