## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Assumptions

Assume the following:

- that South Carolina has a two-year statute of limitations on tort claims;
- that Georgia has a three-year limitations period on tort claims;
- that South Carolina follows the First Restatement approach to choice of law;
- that Georgia follows the Second Restatement approach to choice of law;
- that South Carolina has a declaratory judgment act statute identical in all ways to 28 U.S.C. § 2201, so that South Carolina state courts can issue declaratory judgments just as federal courts can do so, but that neither the state statute nor the federal statute affects any court's subject matter jurisdiction; and
- that the United States Food and Drug Administration (the "FDA") regulates the language that must appear on the labels of prescription drugs, with an eye toward assuring that these labels include information sufficient to allow a consumer to make an informed choice about the dangers of any drug and/or its side effects.

> [One final note: This hypothetical concerns a lawsuit. In fact, the lawsuit in this hypothetical might be preempted by federal law. If you do not know what "preempted" means here, terrific! Either way: pay absolutely no attention to preemption issues. Assume that there is no issue regarding preemption with respect to the lawsuit in this case.]

## Facts

Libatius Borage, Inc., was incorporated in Delaware in 1972. Libatius makes drugs to treat various skin maladies. Its latest product is called Essence of Dittany; Libatius' marketing materials call the drug a "nearly magical cure-all that can help relieve symptoms for a variety of ailments," but thus far, the FDA has only approved Essence of Dittany for use to treat a skin disease called spattergroit, a most grievous affliction of the skin that causes gruesome pockmarks and blemishes. Libatius directs all operations from its corporate offices in Atlanta, Georgia, where all of its directors, officers, and scientists live. But it manufactures Essence of Dittany, and all of its other drugs, in its plant in Allendale, South Carolina, just north of the Savannah River. The Allendale plant is Libatius' only manufacturing plant, and the company ships its products from this plant to pharmacies around the world. Indeed, over 85% of the company's several hundred employees work in the Allendale facility.

When the FDA approved Essence of Dittany as a treatment for spattergroit,[^1] it issued an order requiring Libatius to put the following warning on the label of each bottle for Essence of Dittany: "Warning: persons suffering from toenail fungus should not take this drug, as life-threatening side effects may result." Double-blind randomized experiments had shown that a person who took Essence of Dittany when she already had toenail fungus had a dramatically increased chance of having the fungus spread to and attack vital organs.

[^1]: Prior to Libatius' discovery of the Essence of Dittany drug, the only other treatment for spattergroit known to be effective was too unpleasant and embarrassing to be recommended for widespread use.

<!-- page: 4 -->

Due to an oversight by one of the supervisors in the Allendale plant, in late 2008, a set of 500 bottles of Essence of Dittany did not carry the exact language the FDA required. Instead, the labels on these bottles said only, "Warning: persons suffering from toenail fungus should not take this drug."

One of those bottles was shipped to a pharmacy in Georgia, where it was purchased by Fred Weasley in February of 2009. Fred and George had grown up together in Athens, Georgia. During high school, the two had started a mail-order novelties and gag gifts business called "Weasley's Wheezes" specializing in sulfur-laced whoopee cushions. The business grew quickly. In 2007, both Fred and George entered college at the University of South Carolina's campus in Columbia, South Carolina, but after completing their first years in May of 2008, they took a leave of absence from USC to open an actual physical shop on Devine Street in Columbia. In a letter dated May 30, 2008, they assured their mother, Molly, that they intended to return to USC at some point, but said that business was simply too good at the moment to waste time in the classroom. As of today, neither has re-enrolled at USC, and they continue to operate Weasley's Wheezes out of their Devine Street shop. They live in an apartment upstairs from their shop.

As noted above, in February of 2009, Fred bought one of the Essence of Dittany bottles with the shortened warning phrase from a pharmacy in Georgia when he and George were in Georgia visiting Molly. Both Fred and George had recently been diagnosed with spattergroit, and had received a prescription for Essence of Dittany from a South Carolina doctor. Both Fred and George also had toenail fungus. While staying in Molly's Georgia home, both Fred and George took the Dittany, and the next day, both were in an Athens hospital receiving intensive medical treatment. Both survived, and had fully recovered by the end of April, 2009, but each spent three weeks in the hospital and medical bills for each totaled over $200,000.

In May of 2011, Fred and George wrote Libatius a letter demanding $300,000 each in medical bills, pain, and suffering. They stated in their letter that the negligence of the Libatius employee in the South Carolina plant had caused a defective warning label on the bottle of Essence of Dittany that Fred had purchased. Fred and George contended that had the warning label included the language "as life-threatening side effects may result," they would not have taken the drug. As evidence of negligence, the letter pointed out that the label had not used the FDA's required language, and thus that Libatius had violated an FDA order. Fred and George demanded that payment of $300,000 to each of them, for a total of $600,000, be made by November of 2011, or else they would sue Libatius in Georgia federal court.

You are Libatius' lawyer. After consulting with your client, you thought your best chance of winning a lawsuit was by litigating it in South Carolina state court, since Libatius employed a lot of people in South Carolina, and you were sure that you can somehow put the idea in the heads of the local judge and jury that a successful lawsuit might cause Libatius to fire employees at the plant. You decided to try to seize the forum. On August 16, 2011, you sued both George and Fred in South Carolina state court and served both with process the same day. Your complaint requested that the court issue a declaratory judgment, under the South Carolina declaratory judgment statute, that your client owed nothing to Fred or George as a result of their taking the Essence of Dittany. Among the arguments you raised was that South Carolina's 2-year statute of limitations expired no later than April of 2011, so any lawsuit George and Fred might file would be time-barred.

<!-- page: 5 -->

On August 18, 2011, Fred left for a long-planned safari in Africa. George hired a lawyer on August 23, 2011, but due to an unexpected breakdown in cell phone communications, no one could get in touch with Fred, so the lawyer could only represent George. On September 6, 2011, George's lawyer filed a petition for removal in federal court, signed only on George's behalf, and thus removed the lawsuit from South Carolina to federal court.

You want to get the lawsuit back to South Carolina state court. The date is now September 27, 2011. You also want to win the lawsuit on your statute of limitations argument.

## Questions

**Question 1:** Discuss whether the judge is likely to grant a motion you might file to remand the case back to South Carolina state court. Be sure to discuss all possible arguments supporting a remand. Also, is there any reason to file this remand motion quickly?

**Question 2:** Now assume that the federal court does in fact remand the case back to state court. You are now back in South Carolina state court. Will you win on your statute of limitations argument? Should you?

## 2011-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-01-atwood-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

The judge will remand the case back to state court.

The judge will grant a motion to remand the case back to South Carolina state court because there is definitely a procedural violation and probably a lack of federal jurisdiction. There is a procedural violation because not all defendants agreed to removal. Thus if a timely remand motion is filed, it will be granted. Because a remand motion must be filed within 30 days, and 3 weeks have already passed, the motion should be filed ASAP.

The case could probably also be remanded on the ground that there is no federal jurisdiction. Diversity jurisdiction likely doesn't exist. Libatius, as a corporation, is domiciled in Delaware because this is its place of incorporation. Its second domiciliary is its principle place of business. This is likely Georgia because its decision makers and directors sit there, making Georgia its decision making "nerve center". The boys could claim they are domiciled in South Carolina, and thus trigger diversity jurisdiction. Although attending school out of state does not usually trigger a change in domiciliary, in this case the boys dropped out of school and are operating a business. This question is a close call, but the judge would probably still find the boys to be citizens of Georgia because they are technically still intending to re-enter school in South Carolina, meaning diversity between the parties does not exist.

Even if the judge found the boys to be domiciliaries of South Carolina, the case should still be remanded back to state court. This is because a case cannot be removed on the basis of diversity if the forum is the home state of the party who removed the case. In this case, if the boys are domiciliaries of South Carolina they cannot remove the case to federal court in their home state. Thus, no matter where the court determines the boys are domiciled they are out of luck for getting the case into federal court on diversity grounds so long as a remand motion is filed within 30 days. Because three weeks have already passed, however, Libatius must file the remand motion within the next week.

Arising under jurisdiction is also likely absent. The *Mottley* well pleaded complaint rule dictates that a federal cause of action be present in the original complaint. Because this is a suit that asks for a declaratory judgment, however, the imaginary lawsuit rule applies and the court will look to the cause of actions present in the coercive action. In this case the coercive action claims are state claims, not federal claims, so arising under jurisdiction is not triggered. The defendants might try to claim that the *Smith*/*Grable* exception is triggered because the case pertains to FDA regulations, an important federal administration. This is likely not enough to trigger this exception, however, because the question at issue involves Libatius' actions, not whether the FDA labeling requirements are sufficient. Thus, the FDA issue does not necessarily need to be raised for the boys to win. Furthermore, FDA regulations, while important, are not a substantial and disputed national issue. Thus, a *Smith*/*Grable* exception claim would fail and the case would probably be remanded to state court.

The suit will be dismissed on statute of limitations grounds.

Libatius will be able to win the statute of limitations argument. Although a choice of law issue may present itself in deciding the merits of the case, in horizontal choice of law issues (like the present dispute) statute of limitations is considered procedural so the South Carolina court will apply its own statute of limitations. The statute of limitations for the suit in South Carolina is two years, and since more than two years has elapsed the court will dismiss the suit.

If the boys had filed the suit themselves in Georgia there would be no statute of limitations problems, so it seems patently unfair that Libatius should be able to seize the forum in a way that deprives the boys of their day in court. This demonstrates the problem with classifying statute of limitations laws as procedural. Statute of limitations is not a small procedural matter like paper size, but rather an important issue that often changes the outcome of cases so it is more aptly characterized as a substantive issue. This is especially essential in the context of declaratory judgment statutes where the defendant can choose the forum. Forum selection has always been a privilege relegated to the plaintiff, so the combination of allowing the defendant to choose the forum and classifying statute of limitations as procedural is particularly dangerous.

## 2011-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-01-frye-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Question One

The judge is likely to remand the case, either on procedural grounds or for lack of jurisdiction.

### Procedural Grounds

All defendants must sign the motion to remove, and here Fred did not sign it. The requirement that all defendants have to sign the motion to remove is meant to protect the interests of any defendant who wishes to remain in state court. Here Fred's interests were not taken into account. In order for the case to be remanded on this procedural ground, the remand petition must be filed within thirty days of the removal, or the case will stay in federal court, unless the court lacks SMJ. Because thirty days will be up very soon, the motion should be filed as soon as possible.

### Jurisdictional Grounds

The case could also be remanded for lack of jurisdiction.

#### *Arising Under*

There is no arising under basis for jurisdiction. The Weasleys' theoretical suit is the basis for jurisdiction, since the declaratory judgment act is not meant to broaden the federal courts' jurisdiction. The Weasleys would have brought a tort claim for negligence, which is a state law cause of action. The Weasleys could argue that there is a federal issue involved because the suit involves the FDA's warning, which would probably have been mentioned in their well-pleaded complaint. Under *Osborn* and the Constitution, it is likely the court could hear the case because it includes the tangential federal issue about the FDA. However, under § 1331 as interpreted in *Mottley*, arising under jurisdiction requires a federal law cause of action, which is not present here. The only possibility of arising-under jurisdiction is the *Smith*-*Grable* exception, but it probably would not apply here. First of all, the Weasleys do not necessarily have to prove that the FDA has authority to issue the warning or any other fact about the FDA in order to prevail on their claim. The fact that the employee was negligent is bolstered by the FDA's order, but the court could find negligence even without it. The FDA's shallow involvement in the facts of the case also indicates that the case will likely not involve a substantial federal issue, and it is unlikely that the court would find anything about the FDA's powers of regulation, etc. Furthermore, this is not an issue that all of the federal courts have to decide in the same way. Accepting this case could also "sweep-in" too many other cases, as any plaintiff whose case involves drugs regulated by the FDA could try to get their case into federal court. On balance the issues to be decided seem to be primarily state issues, so it is unlikely the case would fit under the exception.

#### *Diversity*

The complaint would allege at least $600,000 in damages, so the amount in controversy requirement of § 1332 is met. Libatius is domiciled in the state where its "nerve center" is located, which is in GA, since it directs its operations from there and its directors, officers, and scientists work there. It is also domiciled in the state where it is incorporated, which is DE. In order for diversity to exist, the Weasleys cannot be domiciled in either of these states.

The Weasleys are living in SC. However, it is unclear that they intend to stay in SC long-term. Generally starting a business in the state would probably be enough for an assumption that they are staying in the state long-term, but their letter to their mother that they intend to return to school indicates they are not settled and may move to a different state when they are done with school. There is at least a chance that a court would find them domiciled in GA.

However, even if the court did find they were domiciled in SC, the case is still improperly in federal court, and the court should remand on the basis that the Weasleys were defendants sued in the state where they are domiciled. Under § 1367, if the sole basis of federal SMJ is diversity, the defendant cannot remove if they are sued in the state where they live. Since this is a procedural ground and it is unclear where the court would find the Weasleys to be domiciled, the remand petition should be filed within 30 days.

## Question Two

Generally statutes of limitation (SOL) are considered procedural under both of the restatements. The SC court will apply its own law to procedural questions. The SOL has run in SC, and the judge will likely dismiss on SOL grounds.

This result is completely unfair. Since the defendant in the true case is bringing the declaratory judgment, it seems unreasonable that the defendant should get to pick the SC court, which will dismiss the case and possibly prevent the Plaintiff from bringing it later. The real Plaintiff would have brought the case in GA, and the GA statute of limitations will not run until April 2012. The Plaintiff is the wronged party, which is why he or she is generally allowed to pick the forum and to act as "master of their complaint" in bringing the claims he or she feels best capture the wrong suffered. This result curtails both of these rights, with the outcome that the Weasleys may be left with no way to redress their grievances. The result is a triumph of technical rules over substantive justice.

## 2011-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-01-jlee-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

The goals of Libatius (LB) are twofold: (1) remand our declaratory judgment (DJ) suit to South Carolina state court, and (2) succeed on our statute of limitations (SOL) argument. Both goals are eminently achievable.

## Remand of the Declaratory Judgment Suit

Our strongest argument in favor of remand comes in the form of a procedural challenge to the Defendants' removal action. Because Fred Weasley was unreachable during his sojourn in Africa, George Weasley was the only signatory on the removal petition. Removal of the suit was therefore improper because removal requires agreement by all defendants. 28 U.S.C. § 1446(a). Pursuing this argument requires haste, however, because motions to remand on the basis of a defect other than subject-matter jurisdiction (SMJ) must be made within 30 days. 28 U.S.C. § 1447(c).

Moreover, removal of a suit is only proper if the suit could have been brought in federal district court in the first instance. We can therefore challenge removal on the basis that the DJ suit would have lacked SMJ in South Carolina federal district court.

When considering issues of SMJ in a DJ suit, one's analysis should focus on the "imaginary" suit, also known as the coercive action, that the DJ defendant would have brought as a plaintiff (Imaginary Lawsuit Rule). Because the Weasleys threatened a tort suit premised on negligence, the "imaginary" claim would have relied on state law and could not have established "arising under" jurisdiction under Article III and 28 U.S.C. § 1331. This problem cannot be remedied simply by using LB's failure to adhere to an order by a federal regulatory agency (the FDA) as evidence of negligence because the argument does not change the nature of the Weasley's state claims.

There are rare occasions, however, when "arising under" jurisdiction lies over state-claims that implicate significant federal issues. *See* *Smith*; *Grable*. Indeed, the *Grable* court stipulated that a federal court may hear this type of action if (1) the state action implicates a contested and substantial federal issue, and (2) federal adjudication of the matter will not upset the federal-state balance of judicial responsibilities.

The Weasley's "imaginary" suit does not satisfy these requirements. First, their claim concerns a single company's failure to adhere to a specific FDA order. While the harm resulting from LB's actions may be significant (potential for death) and numerous (500 bottles mislabeled), the actual federal interest is quite narrow. Unlike *Grable*, there is no federal issue that must be resolved prior to deciding the state claim, and the suit does not involve a significant, wide-ranging federal question (compare *Grable*, which considered the type of notice required when the IRS seizes private property). The government would have little to no interest in securing a federal forum for this question.

Regarding the second prong, federal adjudication of the Weasley's claim poses a clear threat to the federal-state division of labor. A decision that "arising under" jurisdiction exists in this case could permit any state tort action premised on a private actor's failure to adhere to a federal agency order to be brought in federal court. Given the expansive scope of federal regulation, this could lead to a flood of state claims into the federal judiciary and greatly upset the existing balance.

Alternatively, the Weasleys could argue that their suit possesses diversity jurisdiction, which requires that every plaintiff is diverse from every defendant. *Strawbridge*. Corporations are considered domiciliaries of the states in which they incorporated and of their principal place of business (*Hertz*'s "nerve center" test) — for LB, this means Delaware and Georgia respectively. Determination of a citizen's domicile depends on the state in which the citizen resides plus the intention to remain. The Weasleys will argue that they are domiciliaries of South Carolina given that they reside in the state and have demonstrated an intention to remain via their ownership and operation of Weasley's Wheezes.

However, removal is not permitted when a state court action is brought in the state in which the defendant is domiciled. 28 U.S.C. § 1441(b). Thus, even if the Weasleys successfully argue that they are domiciliaries of South Carolina for the purposes of diversity jurisdiction, removal is still nonetheless precluded by § 1441(b), as the DJ action was brought in South Carolina state court. The Weasleys might respond by arguing that removal is proper because they are, in fact, the plaintiffs of the Imaginary Lawsuit/coercive action; however this argument should not succeed because the § 1441(b) rule is intended to protect the actual defendants of a state court action and not Imaginary/coercive action defendants.

Our argument that the Weasleys' removal action conflicts with § 1441(b), like the procedural argument discussed above, must comply with the 30-day limit of § 1447(c). Thus, we should move to remand as soon as possible.

## Horizontal Choice of Law on SOL

SOLs are considered procedural in nature for horizontal choice of law, and thus the SOL of the forum state should govern. South Carolina's two-year SOL for tort claims will therefore apply in this case, and any tort suit brought by the Weasleys concerning injuries resulting from consumption of Essence of Dittany will be time-barred. This might strike some as patently unfair because the Weasleys had originally threatened to bring suit against LB in Georgia federal court, which has a three-year SOL rule for tort claims, and thus would not have found their claims time-barred. In this way, LB has not only seized the forum, but seized victory as well.

# ASSIGNMENT 2011-assignment-02 — Assignment 2 (2011)

Source: content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-02.md

## 2011-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

# Assignment 2 (Graded)
Civil Procedure 2, 2011
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 26, 2011
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Monday, Oct 31
**Word limit:** 900
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me three days before the assignment is posted if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will choose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.
12. **PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE.** Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

## Question

Imagine that the State of Durmstrang has a set of rules of civil procedure that are worded exactly the same as the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. In particular, the wording of Durm. Rs. Civ. P. 8(a)(2), 12(b)(6) are identical to those of Fed. R. Civ. P. 8(a)(2), 12(b)(6). However, in January, 2011, the Durmstrang Supreme Court issued an opinion in the case of *Finch-Fletchley v. Macmillan*. The following language was included within it:

> Although the verbiage of Durm. Rs. Civ. P. 8(a), 12(b) is identical to that of Fed. Rs. Civ. P. 8(a), 12(b), we specifically decline to follow the recent Supreme Court cases of *Bell Atlantic v. Twombly* and *Ashcroft v. Iqbal*. In our view, these two cases were unfaithful to the concept of notice pleading and impose far too high of a burden on plaintiffs with meritorious claims who need access to discovery tools to generate evidence to prove their cases. The problems with *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* go beyond their abandonment of the plain language of the rules. Nor is the problem here limited to the idea that plaintiffs with legitimate claims might not file suit, thinking that doing so would be futile under the "plausibility" standard. Rather, the problem is that the Supreme Court of the United States seems to have forgotten that lawsuits, particularly those sounding in tort, have a purpose beyond compensating victims: to deter wrongful conduct, particularly by powerful companies in a position to hurt many citizens of Durmstrang at once. In our view, were we to follow the *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* decisions in our interpretation of our own Durmstrang rules, the result would be an insufficient level of deterrence of harmful conduct among companies doing business in our state. We instead adhere to the notice pleading standard, as exemplified by the case of *Dioguardi v. Durning* and Form 11 of the Durmstrang Rules of Civil Procedure (which is identical to Form 11 of the Federal Rules).

### Part 1 (Suggested words: 500–550)

Should the United States District Court for the District of Durmstrang, litigating a case in which the only basis of jurisdiction is diversity and in which the relevant horizontal choice of law rules result in the application of Durmstrang substantive law, be required to apply Durmstrang law (as articulated in *Finch-Fletchley v. Macmillan*) when adjudicating a motion to dismiss under Rule 12(b)(6)?

Your answer to this question can be a single essay, or one broken down into subparts, whatever you prefer. Your answer should, however, address all of the following sub-issues:

(a) What would the answer to this question be under the three "Erie tests" we have learned in the class thus far?

(b) What role, if any, does the Rules of Decision Act, 28 U.S.C. § 1652, play in the analysis?

(c) What role, if any, does, should limits on when defendant/defendants are allowed to remove play in the analysis?

**Note:** Except for subpart (a), this is a "should" question. I'm asking what you think the answer should be. To write a good response, you need to consider the principles we've learned in class, as well as the competing policies behind the various Erie and motion-to-dismiss cases we studied. But in this question (except for subpart (a)), I'm not asking you to predict what a court would do when faced with this question.

### Part 2 (Suggested words: 350–400)

Should Durmstrang state judges, in cases in which the plaintiff files in Durmstrang state courts and asserts causes of action created by federal statutes (i.e., federal claims), be required to follow *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal*?

**Note:** This one is completely a "should" question. Again, I'm asking what you think the answer should be. To write a good response, you need to consider the principles we've learned in class, as well as the competing policies behind the various Erie and motion-to-dismiss cases we studied. But in this question, I'm not asking you to predict what any particular court would do when faced with this question.

## 2011-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-02-feliciano-hansen-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## Part 1

**(a)** Whether the Durmstrang Federal Court (the "DFC") will apply state or federal law depends on which test is applied.

Under Harlan's inquiry, we must determine whether the application of a federal rule would affect the primary conduct of the parties involved in litigation. As is made clear in the opinion, the DFC believes that it will. Under Harlan's concurrence, the state rule should control.

Under *Hanna* we must ask whether the federal rule is valid and on point. Then, we must decide whether the federal and state rules clash, which depends on how broadly or narrowly one reads the rule. A FRCP is valid if it comports with the REA (does not "abridge enlarge or modify substantive rights") and Congress had the constitutional power to pass the law and did so validly — though it's questionable after *Iqbal* and *Twombly*, 8(a)(2) and 12(b)(6) still meet this criteria. Moreover, given that the language of the rules are identical, it is clear that the federal rule addresses the same exact issue and clashes. The federal pleading standard should control.

*York* counsels that the line between substance and procedure is determined by whether it affects the outcome of the litigation — if it affects the outcome then it is, by definition, substantive. Pleading standards are the first available tools for killing a lawsuit. If the DFC were to religiously enforce the federal standard, cases that might have otherwise survived a 12(b)(6) motion in state court, would be thrown out. Under *York* the state rule should apply.

**(b)** The RDA states that the *laws* of the several states are the rules of decision. *Erie* clarifies that "law" is not some 'transcendental' form independent of a particular judge or jurisdiction — in fact, the law is what judges say it means. Because *Erie* defines law in this way, it is clear that the state interpretation of 8(a)(2) and 12(b)(6) is state law.

**(c)** Removal limitations should be a discretionary factor in a judge's analysis on whether the federal or state pleading standard should apply depending on how a case got to federal court. Removal limitations prompt a more case-by-case consideration of fairness to litigants and litigation strategy, particularly in regards to Durmstrang defendants ("DDs").

Removal limits DDs access to a federal forum. DDs are unable to remove if sued in state court as they are in their home state — *i.e.*, "home cooking" isn't a relevant issue. Plaintiffs who did not want a DD to benefit from the higher federal pleading standards could just sue that defendant in state court. Out-of-state defendants in a diversity action would always be able to remove.

If the parties have arrived at the DFC, as opposed to a state court, we should ask how and why they got there. If a DD is in state court, it is likely because the plaintiff saw a benefit to trapping him there; in contrast, if a DD is in the DFC, it is only because the plaintiff has allowed it, either by filing in the DFC initially or by not contesting the defendant's removal action. But, why would a plaintiff allow the case to be heard in a federal forum? There could be lots of reasons, but, clearly, guarantee of a more lenient pleading standard wasn't one of them. Since the defendant never, in some sense, had a right to the federal standard, and the federal standard did not matter that much to the plaintiff, you might say that it doesn't matter whether the state or federal rule is applied — that is, unless there's a separate compelling reason to favor one over the other.

What about the out-of-state defendant? He always had a right to the federal forum. In that case, does it make sense to favor the plaintiff as the plaintiff never had the ability to trap the out-of-state defendant in a state court which applied the more relaxed state standard? Could we even say that the plaintiff has, in some sense, the *right* to trap? Should the out-of-state defendant have a right to a different pleading standard just by virtue of diversity when the relevant horizontal choice of law rules result in the application of Durmstrang substantive law to the case? Really, how concerned are we about prejudice at this point?

## Part 2

The state courts should probably have an obligation to the federal courts the way the federal courts do to the state courts. Admittedly, underlying the *Erie* doctrine is a strong current of federalism that just isn't present here, not to mention there is no statutory basis for a reverse *Erie* doctrine (*i.e.*, there is no corresponding § 1652). On the other hand, ensuring that the federal laws are being applied in a consistent manner throughout the states seems to be very much in line with the original spirit of the *Erie* opinion (discouragement of forum shopping and prevention of inequitable administration of laws). One of *Erie*'s chief policy concerns was that parties should not be able to achieve different results depending on whether or not they file in state or federal court. When federal courts are "adjudicating a state-created right solely because of the diversity of citizenship of the parties" they should, in essence, act like "another court of the state" (*York*). Why shouldn't the state courts behave in the same fashion?

## 2011-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-02-whitney-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

## 1.

### What would the answer to this question be under the three "Erie tests"?

Federal courts sitting in diversity apply state substantive law and federal procedural law. *Erie* helps us figure out what counts as "substantive" and what counts as "procedure."

*York* = "Outcome Determinative" Test. "Would applying the federal rule change the outcome of the case?" If it would → substantive → apply state law.

**Application:** Are pleading standards outcome determinative? We know (especially given the Durmstrang Supreme Court's articulation of *why* it was rejecting code pleading) that pleading standards can be outcome determinative; some cases that would make it through a 12(b)(6) under notice pleading get kicked out under code. Thus, outcome determinative → state law.

**Hanna Rule:** Two tests:

(1) if there's a valid and applicable federal law on point → apply it.

Difficulty: how widely do you interpret the federal rule (*Walker*);

(2) If the federal law isn't directly on point → use twin aims of *Erie* (avoid forum shopping, avoid inequitable administration).

**Application:** We are likely in (1) land because the two laws conflict. Though, argue that the federal move to code pleading should only apply to super complex (*Bell Atlantic*) and national security-ish (*Iqbal*) cases.

Also ask whether the federal rule is valid under the REA. SCOTUS can lay down general rules of procedure for district courts but those rules can't abridge, enlarge (etc.) substantive rights. Under *Hanna*, ask if the rule "really" regulates procedure (i.e., regulates the judicial process for enforcing rights and duties recognized by substantive law). Pleading standards sure seem to. Next, ask if the rule would modify (etc.) substantive rights. Court will likely say it doesn't (as far as I know, the Court has never knocked a rule down at this step). Thus, federal rule ok.

**Harlan Concur Rule:** Use state law if using federal would substantially alter primary conduct.

**Application:** As the State Supreme Court articulated, code pleading standards don't adequately deter harmful conduct. That "harmful conduct" is primary conduct. Thus, state notice pleadings apply.

### What role *should* the RDA play?

The RDA says that state law provides the "rules of decision" in diversity suits. But what counts as state law? Is it just what the states have written down or does it include state high court interpretations of the law? Answer: "state law" includes state high court interpretations of state rules. So, looking at the RDA in relation to *Erie*, the federal judge must treat the state high court's interpretation of the pleading standards as state law.

### What role *should* limits on D's ability to remove play?

§ 1441(b) prohibits defendants from removing to federal court diversity cases if ANY defendant is sued in his home state.

We're in federal court. That means the defendant was either (1) sued in federal court directly or (2) was sued in state court but, being not domiciled in Durmstrang, subsequently removed to federal court. Plaintiffs are, for the most part, the masters of their complaint — so what should we make of this?

It may depend on how this case got to federal court. Under (1), the plaintiff was willing to run the risk of heightened pleading standards by suing directly in federal court. What incentivized that? Better jury pool? More favorable judges? If that's the case, the judge may feel less likely to see the pleading standards, at least in this case, as that important (since the P likely crosses the plausibility threshold). (2) might be more interesting. Imagine the case of the disadvantaged P who was harmed by out-of-state-D's product. Say minimum contacts have been met and P can sue D in P's own state. D can now remove to federal court and disadvantage P. In that case we, especially for torts, feel particularly sympathetic to P and, accordingly, think out-of-state-D's strategizing shouldn't work. So, in the end, the story behind how they ended up in federal court sways our sympathies.

## 2.

First, given this is arising under, D could remove to federal court. Here they aren't. So, they likely think the pleadings cross the plausibility standard and are interested in other state-court-benefits. In that case, it just might not matter which pleading standard is used. Thus, forum shopping concerns are limited. But, we're talking about a federally created cause of action. Above we spent time debating that pleadings are really substantive. If that's the case and federal courts have required code pleading, perhaps we should consider the substantive law itself (the "merits") to have a built-in higher getting-in-the-door nature. If that's the case, by lowering the pleading standards state courts are, in essence, not only inequitably administering the law but, we might say, changing substantive federal law itself. Federalism concerns. But, even if you clearly think the "merits" of a case have nothing to do with whether a P can prove them before discovery, you're still faced with inequitable administering of the law. And, if you have a normative problem with the federal court applying code pleadings in diversity for that reason, and are thus twisting *Hanna* to find a way out of *Iqbal*, it's hard to see how one can also hold, with much intellectual consistency, that states shouldn't apply federal procedure to federal causes of action.

---

*Or their attorney forgot about the code pleading move — in which case, that attorney should be sent to Azkaban.*[^1]

[^1]: Footnote in original.

# Assignment 3 (graded)

**Civil Procedure 2, 2011**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 21
**Due date/time:** 4 pm on Wednesday, Nov 30
**Word limit:** 750
**Time limit:** 2.5 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me three days before the assignment is posted if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.

12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

## Question

### Statutory Background

Assume the following background: Invoking its powers under U.S. Const. amends. xiv–xv, on January 2, 2006, Congress passed the Election Improvement Act of 2006 (the "EIA"), Title 1 of which created the United States Election Agency (the "USEA"). Congress designed the USEA to administer certain issues relating to voting rights. Relevant portions of Title 1 of the EIA read as follows:

---

**Section 2: Remedies**

If the USEA finds a violation of state or federal election law, or that some person or entity has breached a contract or committed a tort relating to elections, the agency may direct the responsible person or entity to provide such monetary relief to anyone damaged thereby. The USEA may also order appropriate equitable relief.

---

**Section 3: Procedures**

The USEA shall adjudicate all disputes with the utmost expedition. All decisions will be made by USEA administrative law judges. In keeping with this directive regarding speed, the USEA shall schedule and hold any evidentiary hearing (trial) within 20 days of a complaint being filed. In addition, the USEA may consider any other submissions of the parties it deems appropriate, including affidavits, documents, and other exhibits. No form of discovery shall, however, be permitted in USEA adjudications, nor shall the USEA have the power to compel persons who would rather not do so to testify before it. A plaintiff initiating a proceeding within the USEA shall serve a summons and complaint upon the defendant by mailing via regular mail to the defendant's last known address, which mailing must be posted within three days of the initiation of the complaint.

<!-- page: 3 -->

**Section 4: Review of a USEA Decision**

Any person aggrieved by a USEA decision or order may appeal that order to the United States Court of Appeals of the circuit with geographic jurisdiction over the area in which the events leading to the proceeding took place. The Court of Appeals' review shall be limited to the procedures used to adjudicate the USEA decision. The Court of Appeals may not review whether the evidence the USEA considered was sufficient to support the USEA's decision.

---

### Facts

In the November 2, 2010 election, voters in various municipalities around the State of Massachusetts complained of long waits at polling booths. In a November 8, 2010 interview, published on The Boston Globe's website, Secretary of State William Galvin claimed that his office was not responsible for any delays that might have occurred. According to Secretary Galvin, any problems that might have existed were the fault of the Charles Townsend Printing Services, Inc., the printing company the Secretary's office had hired to print the Massachusetts paper ballots. Charles Townsend is incorporated in Massachusetts, where it also has its principal place of business. In the Boston Globe interview, Secretary Galvin stated that under the contract, the decision of how many ballots to print was left to Charles Townsend's discretion. Originally, Charles Townsend announced that it would print 3,100,000 ballots. According to Secretary Galvin, one month before the election, he had sent Charles Townsend a letter stating that 3,100,000 ballots would not be enough, and urging the company to print 4,000,000. In fact, according to Secretary Galvin, Charles Townsend had printed only 3,100,000 ballots, despite early indications that the turnout on Election Day would be high. Galvin told the Boston Globe reporter that this decision was "rank negligence."

On November 15, five citizens of the City of Boston, each an associate of the prestigious Boston law firm of Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan, LLC, filed a complaint in the USEA against Charles Townsend. In their complaint, the "Boston Five," as these five plaintiffs became known in the media, alleged that they had arrived at Boston Polling Place 157 at 9 am, but had had to wait until 5 pm to cast their votes. Each alleged that as a result, they had been unable to fulfill their primary function for their law firm, reviewing documents, on that day. As a result, the law firm had charged them a vacation day. Each associate sought $900.00 in damages, as well as an injunction requiring Charles Townsend to take out a newspaper advertisement apologizing for the fact that its negligence had caused inconvenience to voters. The Boston Five's complaint made clear that they were relying on a negligence theory (a common law tort tried to the King's Bench in the olden days). The USEA immediately issued a scheduling order setting the trial for 9 am on December 3, 2010, and stating that it would issue its final decision as soon as possible thereafter.

On November 16, the Boston Five placed a copy of their complaint, a USEA summons, and the USEA's scheduling order in the mail addressed to Charles Townsend, Inc., Number Four Privet Drive, Boston, MA. The address came from the (book) version of the 2010 Yellow Pages found in the offices of Garrett, Monroe, & Duncan. On November 20, one of the Boston Five called the number listed underneath the address in the 2010 Yellow Pages for Charles Townsend to discuss possible settlement; the call did not go through because the number had been disconnected.

<!-- page: 4 -->

On December 3, the USEA convened its trial on the Boston Five complaint, at 9 am. At 9:30 am, a harried-looking lawyer named Josephina Bosley, from Charles Townsend's office of general counsel, arrived. Bosley stated that the first she or anyone else at Charles Townsend had heard about the USEA proceeding was when she read an article about the trial that morning in the online edition of The Boston Globe. Bosley explained that in March of 2010, Charles Townsend had moved its office location and, correspondingly, changed its telephone number. The company's new address and telephone number had been available on the company's website since mid-April, 2010. Bosley requested a 30-day delay in the trial to allow her to prepare her company's defense. She also demanded a trial by jury. Finally, she objected to all aspects of the proceedings on due process grounds. The USEA administrative law judge denied the requests, stating that he had no ability under Section 3 of Title 1 of the USEA (quoted above) to grant any of Bosley's motions.

The trial commenced. Each of the Boston Five testified that he/she had lost a day's work waiting to vote. Over Bosley's objection, a copy of the Boston Globe article in which Secretary Galvin had stated that the election day delays were due to Charles Townsend's failure to print enough ballots was admitted into evidence. Although Bosley cross-examined the Boston Five, she was unable to shake their testimony. Bosley moved the judge to issue an order requiring Secretary Galvin to testify before the judge in person; the judge denied the motion, citing Title 1, Section 3 of the USEA (quoted above). Bosley sought an order requiring the Boston Five to produce a copy of the letter Secretary Galvin had allegedly sent to Charles Townsend requesting that more ballots be printed; that order was denied. Bosley renewed her previous objections and motions; the objections were overruled, and the motions denied. After that, Bosley introduced no evidence and called no witnesses.

At the close of the evidence, the USEA administrative law judge ordered a 90-minute recess. At the end of the 90 minutes, the administrative law judge stated that he saw no reason to deliberate any further, and handed out a ruling that he had written during the recess. The opinion found for the Boston Five, ordered Charles Townsend to pay each of them $900.00, and issued an injunction requiring Charles Townsend to take out an advertisement in The Boston Globe apologizing for its negligent conduct and the inconvenience it had caused to all voters.

### Assignment

You work under Bosley in the Charles Townsend office of general counsel. She orders you to write a memorandum analyzing what possible grounds the company might assert in an appeal under Title 1, Section 4 of the USEA decision to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit. Compose your memo. (Assume that the First Circuit will have appellate jurisdiction.)

## 2011-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-03-joch-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

There are two major issues for appeal — those with due process and those with the right to a jury trial.

The first potential due process issue on appeal is the inadequate notice. It is clear that the Boston Five's actions did not send mail that was "reasonably" expected to inform the parties of the suit in a timely manner. *Mullane*. Specifically, when the Boston Five member found the phone number associated with Townsend was disconnected, he likely had a duty to follow up and make additional attempts to find Townsend, especially since the number and address came from the same Yellow Pages source. *Jones v. Flowers*. Since the information was reasonably available on the Townsend web page and would require no "heroic efforts" but rather a simple web search, it was not unreasonable to expect the Five to find the actual current address.

The second due process issue is the adequacy of the procedural weapons available to Bosley and Townsend at the hearing itself. Because Bosley was not able to challenge the Globe article in any way — through objecting to its admission, confronting Secretary Galvin, or compelling production of the letters stating that the under-printing was Townsend's fault — it was unlikely that the hearing actually ensured the protection of any of Townsend's rights. Due process is flexible but in this situation the *Mathews* factors were likely not met. The private interest affected was a sum of money — and potentially a large sum of money from future suits if the Five proved successful (a difficult nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel issue). There was a high risk of error because unsubstantiated statements from a political figure facing public outcry were admitted with no realistic way to challenge them, whereas compulsory appearance of the Secretary and production of letters would have offered Townsend adequate opportunity to rebut his statements. But compulsory appearance is apparently not within the procedural ambit of this agency; that makes things worse in due process terms, not better. The costs would have been relatively low since the hearing was quick and the government has a high stake in a correct outcome since it deals with election law.

The final due process issue concerns time to prepare. It is possible that the amount of time given to prepare was inadequate. *Roller*. Whether 20 days is "reasonable" is arguable.

The second major issue is the denial of a jury trial. The first step in the two-part *Tull*/*Terry* test is resolved squarely on Townsend's side; the "negligence" claim is a common-law tort that would have been brought in law. However, the second part of the test is generally considered the more important of the two, and is less clear. The relief sought has elements both of 18th century law and equity claims. There is a clear legal remedy in the form of compensatory damages. There is, however, equitable relief in the form of the injunction requested. The old "clean-up" rule would likely not apply here since the legal damages seem to dominate and at best the equitable relief is of only equal importance to the legal relief. [NOTE TO STUDENTS: YOU WOULD NOT HAVE ENCOUNTERED THE CLEAN-UP RULE WHEN YOU DID THIS ASSIGNMENT. THE REFERENCE TO THE CLEANUP RULE IS DESIGNED TO HELP YOU FOR EXAM PREPARATION.] Under the *Tull*/*Terry* test a jury trial seems warranted.

It might be countered that this is a new "public right" created by Congress through the USEA and therefore not subject to a jury trial, since no such right existed in 1791. *Atlas Roofing*. Here, the fact that the government is not a party is not dispositive but is certainly to be considered. Ultimately, the power to bring a claim rests in private hands — here, those of the Five — and the claim was ultimately one based on a theory of negligence which was neither statutorily created nor a public right. *Granfinanciera*. Since this is solely a new forum to adjudicate private rights, the *Tull*/*Terry* test must be applied as above (and likely comes out in Townsend's favor).

Because she objected at all stages Bosley preserved her right to appeal all these issues. Additionally, though under the USEA the court cannot review the sufficiency of the evidence to support the ALJ decision, that does not come into play here because the court is analyzing fundamental constitutional and procedural rights rather than the particular sufficiency of evidence.

## 2011-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-03-pruden-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

The primary issues on appeal are denial of jury trial, insufficiency of notice provided to the defendants, and overall denial of due process in this hearing.

## JURY TRIAL

The Seventh Amendment preserves the right to a jury trial for all causes of action in which the common law provided for a jury originally. To see if a jury right attaches, we find the closest analogue to the novel claim in eighteenth century common law (although this case doesn't really involve a novel claim), paying particular attention to the remedy sought, and look to see whether that claim would have carried the right to a jury. *Chauffeurs*. The plaintiffs in this case brought one cause of action, a common-law tort negligence claim allowed for under the Act, but requested two forms of relief, damages and an injunction. The tort action, heard by the King's Bench, is a claim at law. Regarding remedy, however, we have both damages requested and an injunction; damages typically means law/jury, injunction means equity/no jury. On one hand, the letter of apology seems like a pretty trivial claim, so we should yield to the damages and provide a jury trial. However, the plaintiffs only sought a total of $4,500 in damages, so perhaps the (potentially embarrassing and business-damaging) published letter places a greater burden on the defendant, meaning no jury right and trial to an ALJ would be OK. However, under *Beacon Theatres*, the court may be forced to try the damages claim in front of a jury before entertaining the claim for injunction, although the court's emphasis on collateral estoppel of the defendant's counterclaim in a subsequent action means that that case is distinguishable. [NOTE TO STUDENTS FROM PROF. GREINER: YOU WILL NOT HAVE READ BEACON THEATRES BY THE TIME YOU DID THIS QUESTION, AND THE TAs WILL NOT EXPECT YOU TO DISCUSS IT; THIS LAST SENTENCE IS DESIGNED TO HELP YOU IN EXAM STUDYING.]

If the nature of the plaintiffs' claims otherwise entitles the defendant to a jury trial, the public rights exception does not apply. Unlike OSHA in *Atlas Roofing*, the government in this case is not a party acting as a regulator; the USEA provides only a forum for adjudication. Hence, this case is actually much closer to the court's reasoning in *Granfinanciera* distinguishing *Atlas Roofing*, and Charles Townsend would retain its right to a jury.

## NOTICE AND OTHER DUE PROCESS

As far as notice is concerned, the statute imposes upon the plaintiff only the duty to serve by regular mail to the defendant's last known address a copy of the summons and complaint within 72 hours of the date of initiation. The plaintiff arguably complied with this statute, using the last edition of the yellow pages to locate an address. However, the additional step taken by the plaintiff, namely calling the phone number attached to the last known address and finding it disconnected, is reminiscent of *Jones v. Flowers*. In *Jones*, the Court held that the returned certified mail should have signaled to the plaintiff that the address was incorrect and that additional steps were required. Here, the disconnected phone number perhaps should have signaled that the address in the yellow pages was not current. In an age of the internet, in which companies regularly keep current addresses on their websites, this rule makes sense, and the plaintiffs should have known to search for a website when their call could not be completed.

We may first look at the *Mathews* factors to see if they provide guidance about other additional process due to the defendant. On one hand, requiring the plaintiff to bring in Secretary Galvin, or at the very least the letter directed to the Boston Globe, would have dramatically reduced the risk of error in these proceedings. The agency relied on not only a conclusory, out of court statement, but a secondary source's description of this statement. However, bringing in Secretary Galvin or forcing the plaintiffs to take steps to subpoena the letter would carry significant additional cost, so the *Mathews* factors do not provide a clear outcome. We may also look to *Vitek*, and reason by analogy that if we are to find a party liable for negligence, we should at least allow that party to challenge that there was some negligence. As it stands, this holding, written up by the administrative law judge during a 90 minute recess, could potentially have preclusive effect on future lawsuits and/or USEA proceedings (think about the number of voters who might sue!) through nonmutual offensive collateral estoppel and prevent the defendant from challenging this negligence in other fora. The additional process mentioned above seems justified given what little process was granted to the defendants in this proceeding.

## 2011-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2011-assignment-03-zager-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

First, Townsend may argue that it was improperly denied its right to a jury trial. This claim will likely satisfy the two-part test described in *Granfinanciera*. Since the Boston Five's complaint is based on a negligence theory (heard by the King's Bench (law) before the merger of law and equity), the first part of the two-part test comes down in Townsend's favor. The second part of the two-part test is more equivocal, since the remedy sought by the Boston Five includes both damages (law) and injunctive relief (equity). The appeal court's decision on whether the remedy is more legal or equitable in nature may hinge on which of the two remedies is seen by the court to "dominate" the action. However, when both legal and equitable relief is sought, the court must preserve the right to a jury trial whenever possible (per *Beacon Theatres*). [NOTE TO STUDENTS FROM PROF. GREINER: YOU WILL NOT HAVE READ BEACON THEATRES BY THE TIME YOU DID THIS QUESTION, AND THE TAs WILL NOT EXPECT YOU TO DISCUSS IT; THIS LAST SENTENCE IS DESIGNED TO HELP YOU IN EXAM STUDYING.] Given the appellant-friendly valence of both parts of the two-part test, it's likely that the appeals court will recognize Townsend's right to a jury trial. The appeals court may consider whether the Boston Five's claim is appropriately heard by an ALJ, since the procedures in this case arise out of a federal statute, and thus may present an issue of "public rights" that can be adjudicated in an administrative hearing. However, this case is not one in which the government is involved in its sovereign capacity under a statute that creates enforceable public rights (like *Atlas Roofing*), but private rights between individuals (in particular, a common law tort claim). This is a one-two punch for the ALJ trial: the case does not represent a public rights adjudication, and the ALJ probably cannot seat a jury. That may mean the end of this statutory scheme, but . . . who cares?

Second, Townsend may argue that it was denied procedural due process, in at least three ways. First, the Boston Five's attempt to notify Townsend does not meet the notice standard put forth in *Mullane*. When the Boston Five were notified that Townsend's previous phone number had been disconnected, they were put on notice that the contact information in the phone book was inaccurate and were therefore required to take additional reasonable steps to contact Townsend (per *Jones v. Flowers*). The appeals court is likely to find that a simple web search would have been just such a reasonable step; in this case, a step that would have led the Boston Five straight to Townsend. This is not a situation in which Townsend was a missing person, and a single letter to a last known address was the best the plaintiffs could do (in contrast with *Dobkin v. Chapman*). Indeed, it's possible that the appeals court may consider the "regular mail" provisions of the USEA to be inadequate and strike down the statute as not requiring sufficient notice (per *Wuchter v. Pizzutti*). Second, the seventeen days that elapsed between the Boston Five's unsuccessful attempt to contact Townsend and the start of the trial was insufficient for Townsend to properly respond (per *Roller v. Holly*). Third, Townsend should have had the opportunity to challenge the Globe article as part of its fundamental right to cross-examine witnesses (per the seven-element mini-code of civil procedure set out in *Vitek*). This procedural failure goes to the heart of due process — by allowing to go unchallenged a hearsay statement by a politician to the news media, the USEA procedures undermined the validity of the trial. Even the *Mathews* factors can be said to support Townsend's position: the judgment damaged Townsend's bottom line and reputation, the risk of error is vast when evidence cannot be challenged, and the state governments already have a well-established procedure in place for adjudicating common law tort suits (use of which in this case would not present any special burden on the government or any of the parties).
