For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

Assume the following (all parts of which are totally fictional).

Neither South Carolina nor North Carolina have the doctrine of respondeat superior. In other words, in neither South Carolina nor North Carolina may a plaintiff sue an employer for a tort committed by the employer's employee on the theory that the employer is responsible for the acts of an employee committed within the scope of her employment. Georgia, Virginia, and Delaware all have the doctrine of respondeat superior. Georgia, Virginia, and Delaware all follow the Second Restatement approach to choice of law. South Carolina and North Carolina follow the First Restatement approach to choice of law.

Arthur Weasley lives and works in Durham, NC, as a mid-level employee for the North Carolina Department of Inventions. Many years ago, Mr. Weasley inherited what is now (apart from his seven children) his pride and joy: a vintage, fully restored (with only original parts), mint condition Nimbus 1955 automobile, worth approximately $200,000. As just noted, Mr. Weasley lives and works in Durham, NC, but he has a side job as a salesman for his sons' joke shop business. This side job requires him to travel periodically to other parts of North Carolina, to South Carolina, and to Georgia for business.

There is only one shop in North Carolina with mechanics skillful enough, and with enough specialized knowledge, to work on Mr. Weasley's Nimbus. This shop, Crabbe's Repairs in Charlotte, is operated by the Crabbe Corporation. Crabbe Corp. is incorporated in Delaware; holds its board meetings, houses its chief executive officer and chief financial officer, and makes procurement and supply decisions in Danville, Virginia; and operates all of its 15 different car repair shops in various locations in North Carolina. As the name suggests, Crabbe's Repairs in Charlotte is located in Charlotte, NC.

One fine day, Mr. Weasley drove his Nimbus to Crabbe's Repairs in Charlotte for some repairs. Because he was an excellent customer, he was met by Crabbe's Repairs in Charlotte's chief mechanic and facility manager, Ludo Bagman. Bagman took his excellent customer for a cup of coffee, and the two discussed what needed to be done with the Nimbus. The following conversation occurred:

Bagman: "Now, Mr. Weasley, I know you have to travel regularly to Georgia on business. I can have the car ready for you tomorrow, but will you be taking it into Georgia tomorrow? I need to know because of that brake issue we've been discussing for some time now. If we don't get those brakes repaired, I'm worried that the car might not do well on a drive to Georgia; it's too long to go without taking care of the brakes."

Weasley: "Yep, you're right, Ludo. Let's go ahead and do the brake repairs today. I'll pick the car up tomorrow morning and do my trip to Atlanta, Georgia later in the morning."

Bagman: "Sounds great, Sir. And now, how are Molly and the kids?"

Weasley: "They're wonderful, Ludo, thanks for asking. You really must come see them. In fact, it will be a terrific chance for you to get away from your home and your work. You haven't left Charlotte for the

<!-- page: 3 -->

past three or four years, have you? It seems as though you hardly ever get out of your home in the downtown Charlotte area, do you? I mean, I know you were born here and have lived here all of your life, but it's time you got away from your home and your work for a bit. Come to Durham one day."

Bagman: "That's a wonderful invitation, Sir. It really would be lovely to see how all of your boys, as well as Ginevra and Molly, are doing. It would also be a nice thing for my wife, Nymphadora. I'm afraid we're about to lose our house to foreclosure because of a variable interest rate mortgage we signed, and we're not sure how we'll live next. Quite frankly, we're completely broke, and we could use a distraction. Thank you very much, Sir."

Weasley: "Call me 'Arthur.'"

The next day, Mr. Weasley came to pick up his Nimbus from Bagman at Crabbe's Repairs in Charlotte. Bagman greeted Mr. Weasley as usual. Before paying for the repairs, Mr. Weasley signed a "Contract and Statement of Work" detailing exactly what repairs had been made as well as the cost of each repair, broken down by parts and labor. The Contract and Statement of Work listed the brake repairs as having been complete.

Later that morning, Mr. Weasley drove the Nimbus to Atlanta, Georgia on business. On the way back home, while still inside of Atlanta city limits, Mr. Weasley's car came off the road and smashed into a nearby clothing establishment, Madame Maulkins' Robes for All Occasions. The Nimbus was totaled.

Mr. Weasley believes that the reason the Nimbus came off of the road was that Ludo negligently repaired the brakes. Mr. Weasley likes Ludo, but he LOVED his vintage Nimbus. He REALLY wants to get enough money to try to buy another one, and he doesn't care who he has to sue to get that money.

Mr. Weasley hires you as his lawyer. He asks you what his options are in terms of where he might sue, and the advantage and disadvantages of a lawsuit in each place. There is one catch: Mr. Weasley has heard that federal courts are pro-defendant. He VERY MUCH wants to keep his lawsuit out of federal court if at all possible.

**What advice do you give him?**

## 2012-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-01-geiger-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Arthur Weasley ("AW") has two objectives: receive a judgment for the value of the car and avoid federal court. For the first goal, AW must sue at least Crabbe Corporation ("CC") because Ludo Bagman ("LB") has no assets (his house is in foreclosure) and is therefore judgment-proof. He must also sue in a state that will apply a set of state laws that includes respondeat superior so CC is liable for LB's negligence. For his second goal of avoiding federal court, AW must sue in a state court from which CC cannot remove; one way to do so would be to assure that there is no diversity jurisdiction over the parties. Arising under jurisdiction is inapplicable here because the cause of action derives from state common-law.

Of the relevant states, GA, VA, and DE apply respondeat superior. Assuming the matter stays in state court, AW must choose a jurisdiction that will apply the substantive law of one of these states. For horizontal choice of law purposes, a state following the First Restatement doctrine of *lex loci delictus* applies the substantive law of the state in which the accident occurred, in this case Georgia. *See Alabama RR*. A state following the Second Restatement will look to the forum state with the most significant contacts in the case. *Babcock*. In this matter NC is the center of gravity, as both the plaintiff and the person accused of negligence are NC citizens, the alleged negligence took place in NC, and the site of the accident was completely fortuitous. GA law is much more favorable to AW than NC law, so he will want to bring suit in a state applying the First Restatement (NC or SC).

To avoid permanent removal to federal court in NC or SC, AW must avoid completely diversity of the parties (the amount-in-controversy requirement is easily fulfilled). *Strawbridge*; 28 U.S.C. § 1332. AW and LB are both domiciles of NC; while LB may move in the future and thus currently may not have the intention to remain in NC indefinitely, *Mas*, until he becomes domiciled in a new state he remains a NC resident for diversity purposes. AW's true target, CC, is domiciled in DE and VA. *Hertz*. A defendant cannot remove for diversity purposes if she is sued in her own domicile. AW can sue CC and stay in state court if he (1) sues LB and CC, thereby destroying complete diversity, or (2) sues CC in DE or VA. AW cannot sue in DE or VA for choice of law purposes (see above), so he must sue both LB and CC in either NC or SC, where lack of complete diversity will keep the matter in state court.

The remaining question is, in which of these states (NC and SC) can the court exercise personal jurisdiction over the defendants? Neither defendant has contact with SC and an SC court's judgment would be unenforceable. *Int'l Shoe*. NC has general IPJ over LB as a NC citizen. NC can also exercise IPJ over CC because it operates all of its repair shops in NC and (allegedly) committed the negligence there, creating the minimum contacts necessary under *International Shoe*. AW's best plan is to sue both LB and CC in NC state court: (1) the court will apply GA's substantive law of respondeat superior, thereby holding CC liable for the alleged negligence; (2) the defendants cannot permanently remove to federal court because there is not complete diversity; and (3) NC can exercise IPJ over both defendants.

[N.B.: One might argue that GA has IPJ even under *Volkswagen* because LB specifically knew AW was taking the car there and could reasonably anticipate that an accident caused by his negligence could lead to action against him. It is unlikely, even if one accepts that argument, that LB meets *Calder*'s three-step test, and *Nicastro*'s plurality opinion also runs against GA exercising IPJ.]

## 2012-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-01-lee-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Staying out of federal court

Arthur Weasley (AW) can stay in state court because there is no federal subject matter jurisdiction (SMJ) in this case. AW's negligence claim is a garden-variety tort so there is no federal SMJ through arising under or the *Smith*/*Grable* exception.

Moreover, suing Ludo Bagman (LB) along with Crabbe Corporation (CC) will destroy complete diversity and keep the case in state courts (*Strawbridge*). Diversity jurisdiction demands complete diversity and meeting the $75,000 amount in controversy (AIC) requirement. AIC is met because the Nimbus was worth $200,000, but there is no complete diversity as AW and LB are both domiciled in North Carolina (NC). AW is a NC domicile since he lives and works in Durham without any intention to move to another state. Similarly, LB is a NC domicile as he lives in Charlotte and does not plan to leave. CC is domiciled in Delaware (DE), its place of incorporation, and Virginia (VA), its nerve center (*Hertz*), but again, that does not matter because § 1332 requires complete diversity. AW will want to bring a suit against both LB and CC since suing LB, who is "completely broke," will not get him damages.

If AW only sued CC, he should do so in VA or DE to prevent CC's removal to federal court for diversity. AW and CC have complete diversity and AIC is met, so in order to box CC in state courts, AW will have to file in CC's home courts of VA or DE (§ 1441(b)(2)). As explained below, VA and DE are not ideal courts for AW anyway so he should not file there.

## State courts

There are various state courts AW can choose from: NC, SC, GA, VA, DE.

GA, VA, and DE use the Second Restatement, which means they will apply NC law since NC has the most significant relationship to the case (*Babcock*). NC has strong interest since both AW and LB are from NC, CC does lots of business in NC, and the repair services and contract signing occurred in NC. NC does not follow respondeat superior, so AW cannot sue CC for LB's negligence. This is unfavorable because AW needs to sue CC to get any sort of damages (again, LB is penniless). Thus, AW should not file in GA, VA, and DE.

NC and SC apply the First Restatement, which in this case would apply GA law as that is where the blood was shed (*lex loci delictus*). GA applies the doctrine of respondeat superior, which would allow AW to sue CC for LB's negligence.

Between NC and SC, AW should file in NC because SC has no personal jurisdiction (IPJ) over either defendant. Neither LB nor CC has minimum contacts with SC (*International Shoe*). LB does not appear to have ever traveled to SC. CC does not sell its services in SC nor does it target or avail itself to the state in any way (*Volkswagen*, *Asahi*, *Nicastro*).

<!-- page: 2 -->

In contrast, NC state court would have IPJ over both LB and CC. LB is a NC domicile, so NC has general IPJ over him. Through its fifteen stores in NC, CC's contact with NC is arguably continuous and systematic (*Helicopteros*), but it is rare that a state exert general IPJ over an out-of-state defendant. However, NC certainly has specific IPJ over CC. CC has minimum contacts (*International Shoe*) and purposefully availed itself to NC (*McGee*). CC should reasonably foresee being haled into NC court (*Volkswagen*).

## Conclusion

NC state court is the best forum for AW to sue both LB and CC for negligence.

Word count: 595

## 2012-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-01-rush-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Word Count: 571 Words

**Weasley should sue Bagman and Crabbe in North Carolina state court.**

## Choice of Law Issues

If Weasley sues only Bagman he is unlikely to win enough money to pay for a new Nimbus because Bagman is "quite broke." Weasley needs to sue in a state that will allow Weasley to hold Crabbe Corp. responsible for the tort (presumably Crabbe Corp. is rich and can pay). GA, VA, and DE utilize the respondeat superior doctrine, which will allow Weasley to hold Crabbe Corp. responsible. Therefore, Weasley should sue in a state that will use GA, VA, or DE substantive law.

As Second Restatement states, GA, VA, and DE will use the law of the state that has the most significant relationship to the issue. (*See Babcock*). In this case, that state is probably NC because the brake repair happened in NC, Bagman and Weasley are both domiciled in NC, and the courts will need to grapple with the Bagman-Weasley contract which was signed in NC. However, as First Restatement states, NC and SC courts will use GA state law because the last event necessary to create the cause of action (the car crash and brake failure) happened in Georgia. (*Lex loci delictus*). Therefore, choice of law considerations suggests Weasley should bring the suit in either NC or SC courts.

## State IPJ Issues

Although choice of law suggests that Weasley should bring suit in either NC or SC, jurisdictional requirements only allow him to do so in NC. Weasley cannot bring suit in SC because the state does not have IPJ over Bagman. Bagman is not domiciled there and does not have minimum contacts in the state. (*International Shoe*).

NC courts have general personal jurisdiction over Bagman because he is domiciled there. Bagman was born and raised in NC and has lived there all his life. (*Mas*). NC has no general jurisdiction over Crabbe which domiciled in its place of incorporation (DE) and nerve center (VA) (*Hertz*). However, NC has specific personal jurisdiction through minimum contacts (*International Shoe*; *Kulko*). Given the 15 businesses in NC Crabbe should have reasonably foreseen being hauled into NC state court (*See Volkswagen*). Moreover, Crabbe has "purposefully availed itself" of the NC state laws to run its businesses in NC. Finally, unlike in *Ashai*, the interests of the plaintiff, defendant, forum state, and justice system do not raise concerns about whether it is just to bring suit against Crabbe in NC.

## Federal vs. State Courts

Weasley will be able to avoid federal court. Federal courts will not have arising under jurisdiction because the issues are state tort and contracts law, typical state law issues. There is no *Smith*-*Grable* exception because there is no substantial or disputed federal issue (and this seems like a case that might disrupt the traditional balance of power between state and federal courts if it were moved to the federal arena).

Weasley can also ensure that federal courts will lack diversity jurisdiction, and thereby assure that the defendant(s) will not be able to remove, by suing both Crabbe and Bagman. The claim in this case meets the amount in controversy requirement of more than $75,000, but although Weasley and Crabbe are diverse, Weasley and Bagman are not. The parties will not be *completely* diverse as required by *Strawbridge*.

## 2012-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

Assignment 2 (graded)
Civil Procedure 2, 2012
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

Posting date: October 17, 2012
Due date/time: 9 am on Thursday, Oct 25
Word limit: 900
Time limit: 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me three days before the assignment is posted if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.

12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

Assume the following, all of which might be (and probably is) completely fictional: South Carolina has only one federal district court. South Carolina's limitation period for all tort cases is four years. Virginia's limitations period for all tort case is three years. In 2007, Congress passed a statute entitled "A Uniform Tort Limitations Period for Federal District Courts," which provided as follows: "Notwithstanding any state law to the contrary, in any action sounding in tort filed in or removed to a federal district court, the action must be commenced within two years of the event triggering the plaintiff's entitlement to relief, or else the action is subject to dismissal upon motion." South Carolina follows the First Restatement of choice of law, while Virginia follows the Second Restatement. Both South Carolina and Virginia have long-arm statutes that allow their courts to exercise jurisdiction to the limits of the federal constitution.

On July 1, 2008, due to a inability to raise operating capital on faltering credit markets, the multilayered entities that previously ran the "Walmart" national chain of superstores in the United States sold all of their assets (including the stores and the "Walmart" brand name) to a single corporation, MegaShop, Inc. MegaShop was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in New York. Megashop executives decided to keep the Walmart brand name and all of its stores, the operations of which remained essentially unchanged. Indeed, industry observers commented that consumers "never knew the difference." (Assume the Walmart business entities that were bought in this transaction were the Walmart business entities that you know and love in real life.)

On July 15, 2009, Ms. Padma Patil, a resident and citizen of South Carolina, was traveling in her car on the way to see her sister, Parvati, who lived in Alexandria, Virginia, which is located in the Eastern District of Virginia. On her way through North Carolina, Padma stopped at a Walmart store and bought a tacky lamp as a present for her sister. The shade of the lamp pictured scenes from famous South and North Carolina beaches, including a tacky picture of famous surfer Nicholas Flamel (a South Carolina native) posing in Myrtle Beach (in South Carolina), along with a series of shots of sports hero Victor Krum at South of the Border, a novelty shop and restaurant location on the South Carolina side of the North Carolina/South Carolina border. When Padma arrived at her sister's house and plugged in the gift, the lamp exploded, causing serious injuries to both Patils. Both were hospitalized in a Maryland hospital, and each ended up incurring over $90,000.00 in medical expenses.

You are a partner in the law firm who represents the Patils. On behalf of both Patils, your law firm filed an action in South Carolina federal district court on September 10, 2012. The complaint alleged that MegaShop, Inc. negligently designed and manufactured the lamp, and that it also was strictly liable in tort for the Patils' injuries. On September 25, 2012, the attorney for Megashop contacted you and suggested that the parties consent to a change of venue to the Eastern District of Virginia. After researching recent jury awards in the Eastern District of Virginia and comparing them to those of the District of South Carolina, you agree. On October 1, 2012, the case was transferred to the Eastern District of Virginia. On October 2, Megashop's attorney filed a motion to dismiss, alleging that

<!-- page: 3 -->

the suit is time-barred (in other words, a motion to dismiss for failure to filing within the applicable limitations period). Will the motion succeed?

Word limit: 900 words
Time limit: 3 hours

## 2012-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-02-gould-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## CivPro Assignment 2 — Sample Answer

The federal court in Virginia will likely dismiss the suit as time-barred. The court must consider which law applies — an inquiry with both a vertical component (between the federal statute and state law) and a horizontal component (between South Carolina and Virginia state law). The only limitations period that would allow the suit to proceed is South Carolina's. Because its four-year limitations period will likely not apply — as per the analysis below — the suit will likely be dismissed.

### Jurisdiction and Venue

The suit was originally filed in South Carolina federal court. We begin by asking whether jurisdiction is proper. Subject matter jurisdiction is, given that the parties are diverse and the amount in controversy exceeds the statutory minimum (1332). There is personal jurisdiction on two grounds: general personal jurisdiction, because of the ubiquity of Megashop stores in the state, and specific personal jurisdiction, because by selling South Carolina-related tourist memorabilia the company evinced an intent to avail itself of the South Carolina market (*Int'l Shoe*, *McGee*).[^1] Under 4(k)(1)(a), the federal court piggybacks on the long-arm statute of the state in which it sits, and South Carolina's personal jurisdiction statute goes to the full extent of the constitution. Venue is proper for two reasons. First, the venue inquiry collapses to the jurisdictional inquiry (1391(c)), and we established above that the defendant is subject to personal jurisdiction in South Carolina. Second, a transfer is always permitted when consented to by both parties (1404(a)), which is the case here.

### Horizontal Choice of Law

Given that jurisdiction in South Carolina is proper, the federal court in Virginia will use South Carolina's horizontal choice of law principles. Because statutes of limitations are procedural for horizontal choice of law purposes, if Virginia applies any state limitations period (as opposed to the federal one) it will use South Carolina's limitations period (*Ferens*).

### Vertical Choice of Law

There is now a collision between federal law (a two year limitations period under which the suit would be time-barred) and the applicable state law (South Carolina's, in which the limitations period is four years and the suit could continue). To determine whether to apply the state or federal limitations period, we turn to our three *Erie* tests.[^2]

**York.** Under *York*, the difference between state and federal law is clearly outcome determinative, since the suit lives under state law but not under federal law. As a result, we should use the **state limitations period**.

It bears mention that the facts in this hypothetical are extremely similar to those in *York*, but with one key difference: in *York* there was no federal limitations period, so the choice was between no limitations period and the state limitations period, whereas here the choice is between two different limitations periods. It remains unclear whether the *York* court would have reached a different conclusion if given the facts in our hypothetical — the answer depends on which of the different rationales for *Erie* was driving the *York* court's decision. If the *Erie* doctrine rests on federalism, then Congress has no power to declare substantive rules in certain areas, and this area (statutes of limitations on tort actions) is a traditional state law domain with which Congress should not interfere.[^3] If *Erie* was a separation of powers decision, concerned predominately with federal courts overstepping their bounds, this case does not raise concerns, given that there is a statute (passed by Congress and signed by the president) setting out the federal statute of limitations. Closely related is the prudential rationale for *Erie*, which maintains courts should not make federal general common law without congressional consent. It similarly does not apply here because we are dealing with a statute rather than common law. Finally, if the *Erie* doctrine rests on Equal Protection (fear of discrimination against in-state plaintiffs, see *Swift*), it is irrelevant here because the parties are diverse, thereby obviating the potential for the discrimination that existed in *Swift*.

**Hanna majority.** Under Step 1 of the *Hanna* majority's test, the federal limitations period is valid and applicable. It is valid because Congress has the power to create lower federal courts (Art. III, Sect. 2), which includes the power to regulate the goings-on in those courts (Rules Enabling Act, Art. I, Sect. 8, cl. 18). It is applicable because it speaks directly to the case at hand — we can't really read the statute so as to not apply here. As a result, the Supremacy Clause dictates that the **federal limitations period** applies, and the case is dismissed.[^4]

**Harlan's concurrence.** The same result occurs under the test that Justice Harlan sets out in his *Hanna* concurrence. Differing limitations periods almost certainly won't affect parties' primary conduct, so there's no need to try to reconcile state and federal limitations periods. Instead, the **federal limitations period** applies, and the case is dismissed.

[^1]: Even though this particular lamp was sold in NC, not SC, the presence of SC images, and our assumption that Megashop stores in SC carried the same product, indicates that the company purposefully availed itself of the SC market.
[^2]: NB: Later in the semester you will learn a fourth *Erie* test, but for now there are only three.
[^3]: Given the expansion of federal power in the decades since *Erie*, this rationale is likely dead.
[^4]: NB: Given that the issue is resolved in Step 1, there is no need to do a Step 2 analysis.

## 2012-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-02-hamidi-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Civil Procedure
Prof. Greiner
Fall 2012

## Assignment 2 Sample Answer

The motion will fail only if the four-year (South Carolina) statute of limitations applies. In order to determine if the four-year statute of limitations applies, we must first determine whether the South Carolina court's choice of law decision transfers with the case, and then whether the federal statute trumps the state one.

### SC HCOL follows the case to Virginia.

The SC court's choice of law rules will follow the case to Virginia because the SC court had proper subject matter jurisdiction (SMJ), proper personal jurisdiction (PJ), and properly laid venue — in short, because the SC court was a "happy" one.

The SC court had proper 1332 diversity jurisdiction because the parties (SC & VA vs. NY/DE) were completely diverse (*Strawbridge*) and because the amount in controversy exceeded $75,000.

The SC federal court had both specific and general personal jurisdiction over Walmart (here, Walmart = Megashop). The SC court had general personal jurisdiction because the size and market share of Walmart's activity in the state likely rose to the level of "systematic and continuous" activity (*Perkins*). Even if the SC court couldn't establish general jurisdiction over Walmart, they could likely establish specific personal jurisdiction under a minimum contacts analysis (*Shoe*). Walmart purposefully availed themselves of the SC market and its legal protections (*Hanson*) by specifically creating and marketing SC-related products. They should have possessed a reasonable anticipation of being hailed into court, exceeding mere foreseeability (*Volkswagen*). Rule 4(k)(1)(a) then allows the federal court to piggyback on state grants of jurisdiction, and SC's California-style long-arm statute (which goes to the limit of the constitution) grants such jurisdiction.

The Patils properly laid venue in the SC court under Rules 1391(c) and 1391(a). Rule 1391(c) deems that a corporation has residency in any state where it is subject to the court's personal jurisdiction. Therefore, for the purposes of venue, Walmart also has residency in SC. Rule 1391(b) then allows a court to lay venue in any jurisdiction where a defendant resides.

### VA Court will follow SC HCOL and classify statute of limitations as procedural.

The VA court will follow SC's HCOL rules, including SC's decision as to whether statutes of limitation are substantive or procedural (*Ferens*). SC uses the 1st Restatement (*lex loci delicti*), under which statutes of limitations are always procedural (*Alabama RR*). This means the SC court would have used its own (4-year) statute of limitations. As a result, the VA court will use the SC court's 4-year statute of limitations.

<!-- page: 2 -->

### The case requires Erie analyses because the SC statute appears to conflict with the federal one.

### The motion fails under the York test (VA court would use SC's 4-year limitations period and not dismiss).

Under a *York* analysis, the motion fails because the decision to use the 4- or 2-year limitations period determines the outcome of the case. Because the decision is outcome-determinative, *York* would classify the state law as a substantive one warranting federal deference. Most obviously, this satisfies *York*'s rationale of eliminating forum shopping (a practical rationale behind *Erie* as well). But when it comes to some of *Erie*'s competing rationales, the reasoning begins to break down. This case is very similar to *York* but distinct in one critical way — there was no federal limitations statute in *York*. Therefore, the separation of powers undercurrent in *Erie* (concern about federal courts "creating" laws through Federal Common Law and stepping on Congress's toes) doesn't hold up — here the federal court isn't "creating" anything, it's applying a federal statute. This points toward using the federal statute under *York*. However, the federalist rationale behind *Erie* (powers not given to Congress by the Constitution are held by the states) suggests Congress may have exceeded its powers by venturing into tort law and its remedies. Under that *Erie* rationale, the *York* court would likely defer to the state statute.

### The motion succeeds under the Hanna majority test (VA court would use federal 2-year limitations period and dismiss).

During step one of a *Hanna* majority analysis, we ask whether the relevant federal law is valid and applicable — if so, we apply the federal law (Supremacy Clause, Art 6 § 2). Here, the law will likely be considered valid because Article 3 § 2 grants congress the power to create the federal courts and because Article 1 § 8 ("Necessary & Proper" clause) grants congress the power to create laws concerning those courts. The law is applicable and is therefore in direct conflict with the state statute of limitations. You simply can't construe "notwithstanding state law to the contrary" in a way that lets you *Walker* your way out of this one. The *Hanna* majority analysis thus concludes after step one, and the court will have to apply the federal statute.

### The motion succeeds under the Harlan concurrence test (VA court would use federal 2-year limitations period and dismiss).

Harlan cares most about primary conduct, and using one limitation or another certainly wouldn't have changed either party's pre- or post-lawsuit behavior. As such, the federal law trumps the state one, the motion succeeds, and the case would be dismissed.

> **Instructor note:** Since we're not dealing with an FRCP, the Rules Enabling Act is irrelevant. This is a case like *Stuart v. Ricoh*.

## 2012-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-02-may-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

855 words

In assessing Megashop's motion to dismiss, the Virginia federal judge must decide whether to apply federal or state law (vertical COL = Erie analysis) and if the latter, which state's law to apply (horizontal COL). The motion to dismiss will be granted if either the federal 2-year SOL, or Virginia's 3-year SOL, applies.

Let's start with the horizontal COL. The Virginia federal court decides whether to use the COL analysis of the South Carolina federal court based on whether the latter is happy or sad. (*Van Deusen*). Is South Carolina a happy court? This is a state-law tort claim that gets subject-matter jurisdiction via diversity: Ps from SC and Virginia and D from Delaware/NY; at $90,000 each the AIC is well over the amount in baseline. The court probably has general personal jurisdiction over Megashop because of its vast presence in all 50 states. (*Perkins*). If we can't get GIPJ, we'll have to go for specific IPJ, which requires both a long-arm statute and minimum contacts to satisfy constitutional due process concerns. (*International Shoe*). We have a state long-arm statute that goes to the limits of the Constitution, so per 4(k)(1)(a), the federal court piggybacks on the state statute. For minimum contacts, we can make a somewhat strained argument that putting into the stream of commerce a lamp targeting South Carolina consumers with images of their state constitutes purposeful availment under the WWV line of cases. Courts sometimes consider P's interest in a convenient forum, although the extent to which this interest weighs heavily varies tremendously (e.g., the plaintiff's convenience didn't seem to matter much in WWV). In any case, we probably have GIPJ. Venue is entirely statutory: per 1391(b)(1), venue is proper anywhere a defendant resides, and per 1391(c)(2), a corporation resides anywhere that personal jurisdiction is proper. Since we have PJ, we have venue.

Because South Carolina is happy, Virginia federal court will apply the horizontal COL rules that the SC federal court would have applied: procedural rules of the forum (SC), and substantive rules of lex loci (Virginia). Because SOLs for horizontal COL are procedural, the Virginia federal court would apply SC's SOL — IF it applies state law at all.

Here we move on to vertical COL. *Erie* tells us that a federal court in a diversity case will apply substantive state law and federal procedural law. But if there is a conflict between the state and federal procedural law, then the federal court must use one of the *Erie* tests to decide which law to apply.

Under *York*, the federal judge should apply state law if the decision to do so would be "outcome determinative." The Patils' case lives or dies based on whether SC or federal SOL applies, so *York* points to applying state law. This outcome seems to speak to the federalism interest some see in *Erie* — that federal laws should not trump state laws in diversity cases; however, it's worth remembering that *York* dealt whether a federal court should apply a state SOL in a situation where there *was* no federal SOL. In other words, for the court in *York* to apply anything other than state SOL would be common law, which is what *Erie* had aimed to stop. Thus, the facts of *York* spoke more clearly to the separation of powers rationale — judges shouldn't make up laws. But if that's the rationale, then in the Patils' case, the presence of a statute means federal law should apply. Similarly, if *Erie* was a prudential case, then the court should apply federal law because Congress' position is clear. In the end, the outcome determinative test in *York* is

<!-- page: 2 -->

dead — any difference between laws will at some level be outcome determinative, so it's useless as a test. We move on to *Hanna* majority.

This is a two-part test: is there a valid, applicable federal statute, rule, or Constitutional provision on point? If so, use it. If not, analyze in light of the twin aims of *Erie* (discouraging forum-shopping and promoting equitable administration of state law). Validity refers to whether Congress had the power to pass the law at issue, and here, Congress would seem to have the power to pass a limitations period pursuant to its power to create the lower federal courts. Re applicability, here it's hard to see any non-risible argument that a federal SOL for tort claims is not somehow applicable vis-à-vis a state SOL for torts. Even in *Walker*, which created the presumption of narrowly construing laws to avoid conflicts, the court had a difference in tolling dates to work with. No such opportunity exists here. So, *Hanna* majority points to applying federal law.

*Hanna* concurrence: the test is whether applying federal instead of state law would affect "primary behavior" of a sort generally regulated by states. It's hard to see how an SOL would affect primary behavior in the same way as *Multicolored Taxi*, where the decision of which law to apply actually promoted incorporating in one state versus another. Since it won't affect primary behavior, apply federal law.

Thus, given that *York* is dead, the motion will likely be granted.

For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

**Part 1)** You are a legal staffer for Congresswoman Hermione Granger. Congresswoman Granger has been doing what she often does, which is read social science studies that might suggest reforms of the legal system. As an initial matter, Congresswoman Granger wants to understand more completely the law governing the US jury system. She asks for a memorandum that names two federal civil procedure doctrines that allocate power between judge and jury, and briefly (in two or three sentences for each doctrine) discusses the rationales, advantages, and disadvantages for each.

**Part 2)** While you are in the midst of drafting your memorandum responding to her first request, Congresswoman Granger comes to you again. She has in her hand a study by a group of social scientists purporting to show that persons adjudicating disputed issues of fact do a better job ferreting out truthful versus untruthful testimony if they do not see live testimony. Partly on the basis of this study, Congresswoman Granger has become convinced that the jury trial in civil cases as presently constituted is not a good idea. She asks you for a memorandum that discusses the implication of the following bill that Representative Granger proposes to put before the U.S. House of Representatives:

"Resolved:

- That a constitutional amendment repealing the Seventh Amendment our Constitution shall be drafted and passed by this House of Representatives.
- That federal judges wherever possible shall avoid deciding cases on the basis of live testimony, and shall resolve disputed issues of fact on the basis of transcripts of witness testimony, preferably testimony taken by deposition during the discovery phase of litigation."

Specifically, Representative Granger would like to know whether you think this proposal is a good idea, *i.e.*, should the proposed bill be enacted? Among other things, Representative Granger asks that you discuss how her proposed changes would affect debates over the need for/appropriateness of so-called "gatekeeping" stages of pre-trial civil litigation, such as motions to dismiss for failure to state a claim and motions for summary judgment. She asks you to include in this discussion an analysis of how, if at all, the proposed changes might lead to arguments for altering standards used to adjudicate (a) motions to dismiss for failure to state a claim, and (b) motions for summary judgment.

Word limit: 900 words
Time limit: 2.5 hours

## 2012-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-03-belyea-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Civil Procedure — Prof. James Greiner
Assignment 3 Model Answer — TA
Word counts: Part 1: 249 / Part 2: 643

**1.** "Law vs. equity" is a historically-based allocation of power, linked to the Chancellor's role in finding facts for "equity" matters and the King's Bench law courts, which utilized a jury. Today, despite a "step one" historical analysis, the distinction turns largely on form of remedy sought by the plaintiff (e.g. compensatory or punitive damages are "law" and so produce a right to jury trial; specific performance and injunction are "equity" matters for judicial determination). Disadvantages include that a constitutional protection turns on the interpretation of similar remedies — e.g. ejectment at law and disgorgement at equity. An advantage is consistency with the phrasing of the 7th Amendment's "preserv[ation]" of a right to trial by jury; absent a historically-based doctrine, interpretation of that word would require reinterpretation.

"Fact vs. law" allocates findings of fact to the jury and findings of law to the judge, as both a function of historical practice and for practical considerations regarding comparative decisionmaking capacity. An advantage is that juries can provide a community standard on disputed matters of fact, or important non-fact-based standards (e.g. obscenity or negligence), insulating the judge from making both "filtering" and substantive decisions about standards, while reserving the more technical function of statutory interpretation to the judge. Disadvantages include (i) that judicial fact-finding at pre-trial stages introduces inconsistencies in pursuit of the purported value — incorporating community standards, (ii) the increasing complexity of civil litigation, and (iii) that complicated disputes in a modern economy place outcomes in the hands of underprepared jurors.

**2.** These changes, one affecting the right to jury trial and the other providing guidance to judges regarding live testimony, must be more fully defined in order to assess their impact on "gatekeeping" debates. The proposed repeal will need to identify whether it prohibits jury trial in all civil cases or whether jury trial is simply no longer a protected right. More relevant to "gatekeeping" are the lack of clarity on the proposed standard of "wherever possible" and whether oral argument will still be standard regarding the application of law to facts. If judges have significant discretion to allow witness testimony and if oral argument is permitted, the impact will likely be limited.

Assuming limited discretion regarding "possible" and restrictions on oral argument, these changes implicate the "gatekeeping" functions of pre-trial stages, a delicate balance between individuals' ability to seek justice and the system's need to preserve efficiency and protect parties from baseless suits with price tags that necessitate settlement. The proposals' impact will depend on whether you see cost reduction as primarily needed at discovery stage or at trial stage. If cost control is needed at the discovery stage (the view motivating *Iqbal* and *Twombly*), this proposed change counsels for strict(er) pleading standards since it elevates the record's determinative status — *increasing* incentives to spend heavily on discovery. If, by contrast, the cost of litigation is thought to lie in conducting trials, this change could support a shift in the federal system away from *Twombly* and *Iqbal*, back to notice pleading, since the dreaded litigation costs had been curtailed.

These changes could mean significant savings on overall litigation costs by restricting costs subsequent to record-creation. However, the likelihood of seeing an increase in already-striking discovery costs could reinforce the federal system's shift to code pleading. If legislators agree the proposal could generate cost savings, they should clarify a notice pleading standard to ensure the courts do not close the gates too aggressively as a reaction to these reforms.

<!-- page: 2 -->

A motion for summary judgment should only be granted where there are no disputed issues of material fact, i.e. where it is irrelevant whether determinations of fact are made by the judge or jury, based on transcribed or oral testimony. Consequently, this change *should* make limited difference regarding debates about or standards for motions for summary judgment. In practice, if oral arguments are eliminated, final adjudication would occur largely on the basis of the written record. This collapses the decision on summary judgment and final judgment. It is unclear whether a judge, facing no jury to decide matters of fact and no oral testimony that may sway, would rule on motion for summary judgment or would simply decline to rule and immediately issue a final judgment.

The two proposals act in concert to increase judges' power over cases and to isolate consideration of evidence within the judge's head. This increase and isolation increases the chance for error or abuse, counseling broader appellate review of district court decisions (note that at least one traditional basis for limiting appellate review, that the trial judge heard the witnesses testify live, would no longer be extant). It is not clear, however, that error would be more likely to occur at summary judgment stage or that, indeed, the motion would continue to be a useful instrument. To the extent that oral argument may be eliminated and discretion to hear testimony constrained, efficiency and consistency may be served by combining summary judgment and final decision into one ruling, allowing "grant of summary judgment" in those cases where there are no issues of material fact, thus focusing the judge's decision on the matters of law. Potential for mistake and error could be avoided (and any increased demand on appellate courts' mitigated) by requiring judges who are deciding issues of fact to articulate fully the basis on which they decided (see FRCP 52).

## 2012-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-03-gressel-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 Draft Answer

**1A) Law vs. Equity.** This doctrine allocates cases to *juries* that are historically parallel to suits at law (or suits asking for compensatory damages), while allocating cases to *judges* that are historically parallel to suits at equity (or asking for specific performance). (*Chauffeurs*). The advantage of this doctrine is its fidelity to the text of the 7th Amendment, which does not create but rather "preserves" the right to a jury trial. Yet, the jury trial has been called "the sword in the bed of the merger of law and equity" because now that courts of law and courts of equity have merged, this test is not easily applied in practice. Indeed, the two prongs of the *Chauffeurs* test often point in opposite directions; because the second prong (the relief sought) is more important than the first, this begs the question of whether prong one (concerning historical parallels) is relevant to current lawsuits.

**1B) Fact vs. Law.** This doctrine allocates questions to *juries* that require fact-based determinations, such as questions of "reasonableness" or "negligence," while allocating questions of law to *judges*. This doctrine is justified on the basis that juries are a "cross-section of society," and are therefore better at applying community standards to fact-bound questions. Judges, on the other hand, have expertise in construing written instruments, and they can provide more uniformity in their rulings. One downside is that the law-fact distinction may be murky in certain types of litigation — i.e. patents (*Markman*) — and therefore this distinction may not provide an appropriate guide for whether a judge or jury should rule on a given issue.

**2)** Granger's proposed changes (G'sPC) would likely affect "gatekeeping" functions of pre-trial litigation in a number of ways. Concerning motion for failure to state a claim, a major question here is the extent to which G'sPC could shape the current movement from notice pleading towards a "plausibility" standard (as embodied by *Bell Atlantic*/*Iqbal*). If the key concern in the Court's embrace of the *Iqbal* standard is to control for the costs of discovery, then G'sPC's extra emphasis on the production of transcripts may bolster the argument for tighter "gatekeeping" functions: a tighter plausibility standard would mean that fewer lawsuits would make it to discovery (which could become ever more expensive under G'sPC). However, if the costs of litigation are mainly associated with impaneling a jury and lengthening the length of the trial, then perhaps notice pleading would be desirable under G'sPC: more lawsuits could go to discovery, where information would be revealed and parties would have the opportunity to settle, thereby saving the cost of continued litigation (which could easily overwhelm the system if too many cases made it to trial). Therefore, notice pleading, paired with opportunities to settle and to have the case be dismissed at the summary judgment phase, could serve the legal system well by creating multiple avenues for resolving (or dismissing) the case before the trial begins.

At the summary judgment phase, the impact of G'sPC becomes more complicated. Here, the judge functions not only as a "gatekeeper," by keeping certain cases out of litigation, but he/she also plays a key role in deciding which questions present "genuine issues of material fact" — and therefore, which questions merit further examination at the trial phase. Under G'sPC, it is unclear what the fate would be for cases that were deemed to present genuine issues of material fact. Without a jury, and with only testimony based on the record produced at discovery, what additional tools would a judge have at his/her disposal to resolve these "live" questions during the trial stage? Not only would the decision-maker be the same, but also there is little reason to believe that the record would change at trial.

While G'sPC understandably seek to address the shortcomings of the jury trial, taken together they pose a threat to the various checks and balances that have developed to accept and then filter relevant information before it reaches a decision-maker. Given the extent to which the production of depositions and other evidence occurs largely without the oversight of the judge, there is potential for manipulation of depositions by the parties. At this stage, real cross-examination is not possible

<!-- page: 2 -->

because the parties are attempting to explore the nature of their cases and thus cannot focus on a clean presentation of evidence in their favor, and so there is less opportunity for one party to challenge the evidence presented by the other. (*Celotex* also says that the evidence doesn't have to be admissible at this stage — you just have to show that you could prove these things in trial). Normally, during the summary judgment stage, the judge does not make credibility determinations about the evidence (*Scott v. Harris*). Rather, this would happen at trial: evidence would be presented to the judge and jury, and each party's counsel would challenge this evidence. If G'sPC were adopted, it is unclear how the evidence would be presented to the judge at trial (would it remain unchanged from the pre-trial record?), and whether the judge could make important determinations about validity and credibility without the participation of parties and/or witnesses. It would therefore be difficult to support G'sPC as an appropriate remedy to some of the failures of the modern jury trial.

## 2012-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2012-assignment-03-roberts-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

**1.**

**A. Law/equity.** Under current civil procedure rules/Seventh Amendment interpretation, whether a particular party can demand a jury trial turns on whether it is an action in law or equity. This is a vestigial historical structure dating back to 1600s England, but it does carry some advantages. Under *Tull* (especially as interpreted by *Chauffeurs*) the remedy sought is often determinative; this allows juries, which sit temporarily, to judge cases where the remedy is a one-time transfer of money, while judges implement and oversee ongoing injunctions.

**B. JAMOL.** FRCP 50 allows a litigant to ask the judge presiding over a jury trial for judgment as a matter of law at the close of the other party's case and again after an adverse jury verdict. Because the standard for granting such a motion is that a reasonable jury could not find against movant, granting such a motion either suggests a fear that the seated jury is unreasonable (50(a) motion) or accuses the completed jury of having acted unreasonably (50(b)). However, this process can be efficient: if a judge grants a 50(b) motion but is reversed above, there is no need to retry the entire case, as there is a contrary jury verdict that can be entered immediately.

**2.**

As drafted, your proposal leaves too much uncertainty. Repealing the Seventh Amendment's guarantee of a jury trial in certain civil cases is not equivalent to abolishing their use. Under your proposal, Congress could still provide for jury trials by statute, or could give courts the option of when to sit juries (see below). If you intend to rid the courts of juries in civil trials, this policy should be part and parcel of the repeal. If you instead intend to throw the question of jury availability to Congress, you should propose the framework that you think appropriate to answer this question going forward.

Perhaps Congress should attach the right or option of having a jury trial to specific causes of action that it deems particularly well-suited to determination by a jury. As a starting example, this would likely include actions that reflect the conscience of the community, such as suits alleging defamation or obscenity. Alternatively, rather than choose to avoid having to determine jury availability for each cause of action independently, Congress might either amend the FRCP (itself an interesting procedure, as amendments to the Rules do not typically start with Congress) or enact a statute specifying factors that judges should consider in determining whether to permit parties to try their civil cases to a jury.

However you and Congress address the above concerns, the second element of your proposal will also likely require a major re-think of the current standards by which judges "gatekeep." Because there would be no live testimony at trial, absent a change in the summary judgment standard we would ask a judge to assess evidence without making a credibility judgment to determine if there are disputes as to material facts (summary judgment standard) and then, if there are disputed issues of material facts, to assess the exact same evidence while making credibility judgments during a paper-based "trial" to resolve the disputes. Requiring judges to sequentially evaluate the same evidence at different standards would prove untenably artificial.

It is unclear how Congress and the courts would respond to this newly bizarre role of motions for summary judgment. One option would be to abolish summary judgment and instead

<!-- page: 2 -->

either allow the parties to move for final judgment after a certain period of discovery (much as they can currently move for summary judgment) or have the courts set a scheduled time for a final decision much as they currently schedule a trial date. If FRCP 56 is indeed removed, there will be greater pressure on courts to control their docket via 12(b)(6) motions to dismiss, as every case that survives that hurdle will be entitled to a considered decision on the merits by the judge. This might accelerate the apparent return to code pleading of the past decade. As commentaries about *Twombly* and *Iqbal* have pointed out, a heavy hand at the motion to dismiss stage requires significant pre-complaint investment by plaintiffs. Such investments are often much more expensive (for the plaintiff) than is finding the same information in the discovery process, so we may worry about inefficiently increased costs of litigation.

I also have several other concerns about the feasibility of placing such emphasis on deposition evidence. It will greatly raise the stakes and combativeness of the discovery period, as it will be not merely foundational but instead the whole ball game. This could greatly tax judges' ability to manage cases — for example, they may need to make evidentiary rulings during depositions to prevent the transcripts from being saturated with hearsay or otherwise invalid evidence. The prospect of a more intense discovery process might further incentivize judges to aggressively grant 12(b)(6) motions.
