# Assignment 1 (Ungraded)
**Civil Procedure 2, 2013**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 1, 2013
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Monday, Oct 7
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Facts

Assume the following (all parts of which are totally fictional).

Both New Hampshire and Massachusetts have long-arm statutes allowing their court systems to exercise jurisdiction "in any case in which such exercise would be consisting with the United States Constitution."

In January of 2007, Congress passed the National Medical Malpractice Reform Act (the "NMMRA"). The NMMRA took a wide variety of steps affecting the medical profession and regulating the provision of malpractice insurance. As relevant here, however, the NMMRA also included certain provisions regarding the establishment of a national registry of radiological images.

---

**Section 601: Findings:** The Congress finds that the process of detecting cancerous masses suffers from the lack of a national depository of radiological images that would allow radiologists to compare images for a particular patient to images in the depository that are known to be cancerous or non-cancerous. The lack of a depository causes thousands of mistaken or missed diagnoses of cancer each year, costing the Medicare and Medicaid programs millions of dollars and having a deleterious effect upon interstate commerce.

**Section 602: Establishment of a National Registry:** The Secretary of Health and Human Services is hereby directed to establish a National Registry of radiological images. The Secretary shall establish a classification system of such images, indexing them in a manner that he shall determine.

**Section 603: Deposit of Electronic Images:** Any provider of medical services who receives any funds under the Medicare or Medicaid programs, and who produces, receives, examines, or obtains any radiological image of any patient, shall forward an electronic copy of that image to the National Registry, along with all information about the patient required to enable the Secretary to index the image pursuant to Section 602 of this statute.

---

As soon as the NMMRA was passed, several medical providers announced that they would refuse to comply with portions of it. One of these providers was St. Mungo's Medical Center. "St. Mungo's Medical Center" is actually just the doing-business-as name of St. Mungo's, Inc., a corporation incorporated in Delaware and whose sole facility was an enormous, multi-purpose medical center in Boston, MA. All of St. Mungo's directors and officers maintain their offices in Boston.

<!-- page: 3 -->

St. Mungo's regularly runs advertisements in the largest-circulation print newspapers in each of the 50 states as well as in websites targeting persons seeking medical information around the country. The ads are adapted to the character of each state. For example, one running in New Hampshire newspaper said, "Has living in the granite state been hard on you? Come get well in Boston! St. Mungo's can get you better. St. Mungo's: Complete Medical Services (Like Magic!)." St. Mungo's also runs ads on websites advertising vacation opportunities in the Boston area. St. Mungo's refuses to allow its physicians or medical staff to perform any service outside of the Boston area. Anyone who wishes to receive care from a St. Mungo's physician or office must travel to Boston to do so. A number of St. Mungo's patients are beneficiaries of the Medicare and Medicaid programs, and St. Mungo's accepts reimbursements and payments pursuant to both programs.

Shortly after the passage of the NMMRA, St. Mungo's chief executive officer Madame Poppy Pomfrey issued a series of press releases. The following is an excerpt from one of these press releases: "Sections 601-603 of the NMRRA are unconstitutional invasions of patient privacy and of the physician/patient relationship . . . We will not allow St. Mungo's radiologists to consult the images in the national registry, nor will we provide images to that registry."

Irma Pince was born in New Hampshire in 1969. She suffered from grinding poverty through much of her life. She succeeded in graduating from high school at age of 18, but from then until after age 40, she was homeless, and she lived either on the streets or in homeless shelters in either Boston, MA or Concord, NH,[^1] depending on when she wanted a change of scenery and when she could get a ride from one place to the other. In early 2009, she stopped going regularly to Concord, and lived continuously in a particular homeless shelter located in Boston. From 2009 to early 2011, she traveled to Concord twice. Both times were around Christmas, when she got a ride to Concord, where she stayed for about two weeks. There, she saw friends and her only surviving family, an older brother. Her older brother was also homeless, and he lived his entire life on the streets of Concord.

In early 2011, a librarian at the Boston Public Library took an interest in Pince. This librarian found Pince a rich patron, who lived in Concord. The rich patron provided Pince a small income that would continue on two conditions: (i) she must enroll in college and live in college dorms during the school year, and (ii) she must come to Concord during the summers to live in a room in the patron's house. Accordingly, Pince enrolled in the UMass Boston program of library sciences in the Fall of 2011. She spent the summers of 2011 and 2012 in her patron's house in Concord.

In 2008, while still homeless, Pince began suffering from a pain in her left hip. She saw a St. Mungo's advertisement in a New Hampshire newspaper while she was in Concord, NH. Accordingly, the next time she was in Boston, she went to St. Mungo's. Suspecting cancer, a general practitioner there ordered an MRI of Pince's hip. The radiologist who reviewed the resulting MRI images followed St. Mungo's policy, meaning that she neither consulted any image in the National Registry nor provided an electronic copy of Pince's image to the National

[^1]: Concord, NH and Boston, MA are located approximately 67 miles apart. Depending in traffic, the drive between the two can be made in about 75 minutes.

<!-- page: 4 -->

Registry. The radiologist pronounced that Pince had no cancer or other major disease in her hip, and Pince's general practitioner told Pince to take ibuprofen for her pain.

In June of 2012, while she was living in her patron's house, Pince's hip pain became unbearable. She saw a doctor in a New Hampshire medical facility, who took the unusual (but cost-saving) step of requesting that the MRI images from the 2008 MRI at St. Mungo's be forwarded to a Concord-based radiologist. The Concord-based radiologist compared Pince's MRI image (the one taken at St. Mungo's) with several in the National Registry and preliminarily diagnosed her as having bone cancer. A biopsy confirmed the diagnosis.

In August of 2012, Pince sued St. Mungo's in New Hampshire State Court. In her complaint, Pince sought $2 million in compensatory damages and $10 million in punitive damages.[^2] The complaint alleged that the St. Mungo's radiologist's failure to consult the National Registry created by Sections 601-603 of the NMMRA constituted negligence per se.[^3] For the purposes of this assignment, a plaintiff can argue that a defendant commits "negligence per se" if she shows that the defendant violated a statute that was designed at least in part to protect the safety of persons such as the plaintiff. In this case, Pince is alleging that the St. Mungo's radiologist's failure to consult the National Registry constituted negligence under Massachusetts or New Hampshire Law.

[^2]: You may assume that under applicable law Pince may sue St. Mungo's for the acts or omissions of its employee physicians and radiologists.
[^3]: For the purposes of Civil Procedure class only, here is an explanation of "negligence per se": suppose a plaintiff alleges that a defendant has failed to exercise due care under the circumstances and that as a result the plaintiff was injured (the classic "negligence" cause of action). Ordinarily, a plaintiff would have to identify what due care under the circumstances would require for a particular fact situation (before demonstrating that the defendant did not do what due care required). If, however, a plaintiff can demonstrate that the defendant's conduct violated some other law designed at least in part to protect persons like the plaintiff, then the plaintiff can say that the defendant's conduct is a "per se" violation of due care. The idea is that it MUST be the case that the defendant failed to exercise due care because the defendant violated some external provision of law. Here, Pince alleges that St. Mungo's failure to follow the requirement of the NMMRA (when the St. Mungo's radiologist did not consult the national registry) was a violation of the NMMRA, and thus constitute negligence per se.

## Questions

**Question 1:** On what grounds might St. Mungo's move to dismiss Pince's New Hampshire state court lawsuit? Will the motion to dismiss succeed?

On September 10, 2012, St. Mungo's, using a Boston law firm, sued Pince in the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts under 28 U.S.C. § 2201. The complaint requested a declaratory judgment that St. Mungo's owed nothing to Pince. Pince was personally served, at her patron's house in New Hampshire, with a summons and a copy of the complaint on September 12.

<!-- page: 5 -->

**Question 2:** On what grounds might Pince move to dismiss, move to stay, or otherwise object to St. Mungo's federal court lawsuit? Will such motions or other objections succeed?

## 2013-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-01-birbrair-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

Word Count: 894

**Question 1:**

St. Mungo's ("SM") may move to dismiss by arguing that the New Hampshire state court lacks personal jurisdiction over it. Because state courts are courts of general jurisdiction, the court has subject matter jurisdiction over the complaint. It is unclear, however, whether it has PJ over SM.

NH lacks general jurisdiction over SM, which is incorporated in Delaware and conducts all of its business in MA. Because NH's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution, the state statutory and federal PJ inquiries merge. To find specific jurisdiction under the Due Process Clause, there must be sufficient minimal contacts between the defendant, the forum and the litigation (*International Shoe*).

Pince can argue that NH has PJ over SM because SM purposefully availed itself of the right to conduct business in NH by running advertisements in its newspapers. Pince will note that some of the ads are adapted to NH, suggesting that SM could have anticipated being haled into court there (*World-Wide Volkswagen*).

SM could respond, however, that the ads are not strongly targeted (for example, they run in general newspapers and are not specifically sent to sick people). Furthermore, SM bars its employees from performing services in NH and does not send products to the state. If the SM could anticipate being sued in NH based solely on the newspaper ads, then SM would be subject to PJ in all 50 states, a curious result the court will likely avoid by granting the motion to dismiss. The court may also find that NH lacks an interest in providing a forum for this dispute, which does not explicitly involve businesses or services located in NH (though it does involve one of its citizens), and that Pince has minimal interest in litigating in NH since MA is easily accessible. In addition, most of the evidence, witnesses and documentation involved in the case would likely be located in MA. This would also be a basis for moving to dismiss on forum non conveniens grounds, but for the reasons already expressed, the motion would probably fail.

**Question 2:**

Pince cannot contest PJ or venue; she has spent years in Boston and went to SM to receive treatment, creating sufficient minimum contacts. Although she was tagged in NH, one need not be served in a state to create PJ there. Venue can also be laid in MA because most of the events giving rise to the claim occurred in Boston.

Pince could argue, however, that the MA federal court lacks SMJ over the state law negligence claim. 28 U.S.C. §2201 does not create a federal cause of action, so one must look at the suit the coercive action plaintiff would have brought to determine whether the suit arose under federal or state law. In order to get into federal court, there must be either a federal question or complete diversity between Pince and SM and an amount-in-controversy of $75,000 or more.

Diversity depends on whether Pince is domiciled in NH or MA. SM is domiciled in DE (incorporation) and MA (nerve center). Pince's domicile is trickier. She obviously began in NH, and typically "you are where you are until you get where you're going." Although she has not lived there regularly since 2009, her brother and patron remain there, she spends her summers in NH, and she has sought medical and legal services there recently. It is also unclear whether she will return to MA while she receives cancer treatment, which may weigh in favor of finding that she is domiciled in NH.

<!-- page: 2 -->

On the other hand, though being a student in MA wouldn't normally change her domicile (*Mas*), the two years she spent there between 2009 and 2011 might have. She lived in a homeless shelter at the time, though, which might not suggest an intention to stay. If she is domiciled in NH, complete diversity and AIC ($12M) are met. If she is domiciled in MA, a motion to dismiss for lack of SMJ will be granted unless the *Smith/Grable* exception applies to Pince's state cause of action.

The court might find that the suit necessarily raises an important federal issue embedded in the state law cause of action (whether the NMMRA is constitutional). The court would then need to consider additional *Smith/Grable* factors, such as how many cases would be swept from state courts into federal court, whether the case needs a federal forum, and whether the issue is a pure question of law or a mixed question of law and fact. The court would likely decline to make a *Smith/Grable* exception, however, because it would sweep a great deal of negligence per se cases into the federal system.

Lastly, Pince may ask the MA federal court to grant a *Colorado River* stay while the NH state suit is pending. The precondition of a parallel state court proceeding among the same parties adjudicating mostly the same issues, is met. Ordinarily, the court should still go forward, though it will consider a number of factors, including which court is more convenient for parties and witnesses (in this case, both are convenient), which case has moved further along (both are new), any evidence of special Congressional intent (there is none), and judicial efficiency. Thus, because of the strong presumption against granting such stays, except in the 7th Circuit, a motion to stay will likely fail.

## 2013-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-01-stern-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

**1.**

__Motion to Dismiss for Lack of Personal Jurisdiction (IPJ) over St. Mungo's (SM)__

SM is unlikely to succeed in a motion to dismiss Pince's claim based on lack of IPJ in NH.

NH's long-arm statute grants its court system the power to exercise jurisdiction over defendants outside NH, depending on the extent to which the defendant has formed a connection with NH.

SM is a citizen of DE (where it's incorporated), and MA (nerve center), so general jurisdiction in NH is out.

Regarding specific jurisdiction, SM requires that all employees perform their medical work only in MA. However, SM purposefully availed itself of NH through advertising (*International Shoe*). The use of unique NH characteristics in the phrase, "Has living in the granite state been hard on you?", shows SM's intent to benefit from NH. SM shouldn't be surprised by a lawsuit drawing it into NH court when its advertisements are designed to attract patients from that state.

Moreover, NH's interest in litigating the case weighs in favor of keeping it there. NH has a stake in protecting its residents against faulty medical diagnoses and debilitating healthcare costs. Pince also has an interest in litigating in NH, where she currently resides, because she is in unbearable pain and would have difficulty traveling to MA. While the tort occurred in MA, and MA state law would likely apply, it wouldn't be difficult for a NH court to apply MA law. Similarly, while some of the litigation materials will be in MA, it should be easy enough to transport documents and X-Rays across the border to NH. Therefore, I think a motion to dismiss for lack of IPJ is unlikely to succeed.

**2.**

__Motion to Dismiss for Lack of Subject Matter Jurisdiction (SMJ)__

Pince can succeed on a motion to dismiss SM's declaratory judgment (DJ) suit for lack of SMJ only if she is domiciled in MA and the court does not find an important federal question embedded in her state law claim.

A DJ suit, such as the one SM is bringing, can only be heard in federal court if the "coercive action" plaintiff could have brought the case in federal court. We need to check diversity jurisdiction and arising under. For the former, there must be complete diversity (1332) to remain in federal court. We certainly meet the required amount in controversy ($12 million), but we don't necessarily have diversity of citizenship. Per above, SM is a citizen of MA and DE, so we only have diversity if Pince is domiciled in NH. Pince was born in NH, so she starts out domiciled there. The two years she spent in a MA homeless shelter were likely out of necessity rather than a desire to stay, and she always returned to NH during the summer while going to school in MA. These factors suggest that her domicile never changed from NH. In that case, Pince's motion to dismiss for lack of SMJ likely would not succeed because the DJ suit can be heard in federal court on the basis of diversity.

<!-- page: 2 -->

If she is domiciled in MA, however, the court should dismiss for lack of SMJ unless SM can show that there is an important federal question embedded in Pince's claim (*Smith-Grable*). Here, Pince's malpractice case is a state law claim, but her claim of negligence per se hinges on compliance with the federal statute. SM might succeed if the court believes the claim raises an important federal question because it involves evaluating the constitutionality of the statute. SM also accepts reimbursements pursuant to Medicaid and Medicare, both federal health care programs, so the hospital's compliance with the statute would be an issue of strong interest to the federal government. However, Pince's case is a typical state law tort claim. The outcome of the lawsuit would likely not affect other states or have a major impact of concern to the federal government greater than would other negligence per se claims hinging on federal statutes. Exercising federal SMJ any time a state law claim depends on a negligence per se claim (based on a federal law) would probably sweep too many cases into the federal court system. On SM's side, the facts do not appear to be in dispute, so the issue is likely to be a clean issue of law. Therefore, I think state law likely would apply, and her motion to dismiss would probably succeed.

__Motion to Stay__

Pince also has a chance of succeeding on a motion to stay. In general, federal courts have an unflagging obligation to exercise the jurisdiction given them. Now that a suit between the same parties over the same issues is pending in state court, the federal court might be inclined to stay SM's claim for purposes of judicial economy (*Colorado River*). Furthermore, the state court claim was filed first, the tort claim is a traditional matter of state law, and there is little difference between the two courts with respect to convenience of the parties since Boston and Concord are geographically close to each other, and at least SM could travel easily between them. These factors all weigh in Pince's favor for succeeding in her motion to stay SM's lawsuit. On the other hand, there is nothing here like the McCarren Amendment that suggests a congressional policy in favor of adjudicating these cases in federal court, so the issue is unclear.

## 2013-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-01-zahoory-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

**Question 1:**

NH's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Due Process Clause, so the only IPJ issue is the constitutional one. St. Mungo's can make a limited appearance in NH state-court to argue that any attempt by NH to exercise IPJ over Mungo's would violate its Due Process rights.

Mungo's can argue that being hauled into a NH state court would violate "traditional notions of fair play and justice" because Mungo's lacked minimum contacts with NH. (*International Shoe*). Other than advertising in NH, Mungo's has done nothing to demonstrate that it has purposefully availed itself of NH law. All of Mungo's medical business occurs at its medical center in Massachusetts (MA). While Pince may argue that Mungo's specifically directed its advertisements to the "Granite State," this sole reference to NH in an advertisement, one that serves no functional purpose other than to catch eyes, seems insufficient.

Ultimately, even though it is a close call, it does not seem that NH's interests are significant enough to support exercising IPJ over Mungo's. Pince can argue that it wouldn't be difficult for Mungo's to litigate in NH, while it would be difficult for her to come to MA because of her bone cancer. It may also be true that NH has an interest in protecting its residents from negligent medical practices. However, Mungo's can argue that MA state law applies to this case and Mungo's never purposefully availed itself of NH law. Also, considering that the alleged tortious conduct occurred in MA and all the witnesses and evidence are in MA, Mungo's can make a strong argument that it would be more efficient to hear this case in MA.

**Question 2:**

Considering that Pince repeatedly made trips to MA to obtain medical treatment at Mungo's, a federal court in MA applying 4(k)(1)(a) to piggy back on MA's long-arm statute would have specific personal jurisdiction over Pince.

Additionally, since the district where Mungo's filed its suit is also where the events underlying this cause-of-action took place, venue is also proper.

However, Pince could argue that the MA federal court in which this case was filed lacks SMJ. According to the coercive action rule, a MA federal court can only have SMJ over Mungo's 28 U.S.C. 2201 action if the "imaginary" plaintiff (Pince) could have filed the case in that federal court herself. Accordingly, Pince must argue that this court would have lacked both federal question jurisdiction (FQJ) and diversity jurisdiction over this case had Pince filed it in MA federal court.

While Pince's claim has a cursory relation to federal law because of the NMRA, her cause-of-action arises out of state tort law. Thus, Mungo's would have to argue that Pince's state-law claim implicates important federal issue and should fall under the *Smith-Grable* exception. It is unlikely that Mungo's could rely on the *Smith-Grable* exception to argue for FQJ. First, unlike in *Smith*, Pince's right to relief does not depend on the constitutionality of a federal law. Even if the NMRA being held unconstitutional may affect Pince's negligence-per-se argument, Pince could still make a tort case in state-court for negligence. Second, the federal interest in this case is weak considering the federal government has minimal interests in resolving common medical-malpractice cases that are traditionally handled at the state level. Third, by hearing this case, the federal court risks upsetting the federal-state balance by sweeping in too many state cases into federal courts. Even though there don't seem to be any significant disputed issues of fact, it is unlikely that a court balancing the federal and state interests in this case would recognize FQJ under the *Smith-Grable* exception to FQJ.

<!-- page: 2 -->

However, Mungo's could probably bring its case to federal court relying on diversity (§1332). Pince was born in NH and thus began her life as a NH domiciliary. Relying on *Mas v. Perry*, Mungo's could argue that the few years Pince spent living in a Boston homeless shelter after four decades in NH were not enough to demonstrate her intention to stay in MA. According to *Mas*, attending university in a different state is not necessarily enough to change a person's domicile. Thus, the time she spent living in MA from 2011–2012 was not enough to make her a domiciliary of MA. Since Pince is probably domiciled in NH, and this case meets the amount-in-controversy requirement, a MA federal court could probably exercise diversity jurisdiction over this case.

Considering that the MA court would have diversity jurisdiction over this case, Pince could ask the federal court to stay its hearings and ultimately abstain from hearing this case. However, looking at *Colorado River* this argument is likely to fail. Federal courts have an "unflagging" duty to hear the cases properly before them and the differences between this case and *Colorado River* are significant. First, there is no hint that Congress preferred that these cases be heard exclusively in state court and there is no complicated state regulatory regime in play. Second, the state-court case started only a month before the federal case. Third, since Pince travels to Boston regularly, it wouldn't be difficult for her to make it to the federal courthouse in MA. Finally, this case does not involve issues of special concern to states, considering malpractice suits are heard in federal courts regularly. Ultimately, looking at the *Colorado River* factors, it is unlikely that a federal court with proper SMJ could abstain from this case.

For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

## Assignment 2, Question

The Virginia Bank Health Act (the "VBHA") was adopted by the Virginia legislature and signed by the governor in June of 2008. Relevant portions of the VBHA read as follows:

### Section 1: Statement of Purpose

The Legislature finds that banks in this state and across the nation face an unprecedented liquidity crisis due to bad mortgage loans, and that this liquidity crisis poses a grave threat to the public interest due to the citizenry's great reliance on banks. Among the causes of this crisis are:

. . .

-- an inability of banks holding mortgages to foreclose and to evict delinquent homeowners promptly, which prevents banks from quickly seizing and selling the property and removing bad loans from their books . . . .

### Section 5: Summary Eviction: Notice

Any bank holding a mortgage on a property located in Virginia may institute summary foreclosure and eviction proceedings against a mortgagor in default of a loan by filing a complaint in a Housing Court of the relevant county. Upon filing of such a complaint, the bank/plaintiff shall immediately give notice of the action to the mortgagor/homeowner. Notice of the action may be given to the mortgagor by delivering to him or her a copy of the summons and complaint in any of the following ways:

- (a) via hand delivery/personal service on the mortgagor;
- (b) via posting on the relevant property, accompanied by mailing via first class mail to the address of the relevant property; OR
- (c) via electronic notice, if the mortgagor has provided an address on a well-known social media platform (such as Facebook) as well as an email address to the bank on the mortgage application. Electronic notice under this subsection shall consist of EITHER of the following:
  - (i) notice given using the social media platform (such as a posting on the mortgagor's Facebook page) notifying the mortgagor of the Summary Eviction action, the Housing Court Case number, and the address of the Housing Court; OR
  - (ii) an email attaching a copy of the complaint and summons to an email address previously provided by the mortgagor to the bank.

<!-- page: 3 -->

### Section 8: Summary Eviction: Proceedings

- (a) If, in fourteen days after notice has been given to the mortgagor of summary eviction proceedings under Section 5, the mortgagor fails to file an answer to the Summary Eviction and mortgage foreclosure action, or fails to otherwise appear before the court or notify the court of the mortgagor's intent to dispute the action, the mortgagor is deemed to have consented to foreclosure and eviction, and the bank may proceed forthwith to claim title to the property and sell it to satisfy the amount of the outstanding loan. A Housing Court judgment to this effect shall issue immediately and is final. No motions to reconsider or for relief from judgment shall be entertained, and no appeal will lie.
- (b) If instead the mortgagor files an answer disputing the Summary Eviction and mortgage foreclosure action, trial to the Housing Court judge shall without exception ensue within two weeks of the answer's filing. This is a hard deadline that shall not be appealable. Neither party shall be entitled to take discovery, and the case will be tried to the judge without a jury. Either side shall be entitled to present evidence according to the Virginia Rules of Evidence and to be represented by counsel at its own expense. The Housing Court shall issue its ruling orally from the bench at the close of trial, which ruling shall be reduced to a written judgment that same day.
- (c) To facilitate the Housing Court judge's provision of written judgments, each party may submit proposed findings of fact and conclusions of law to the judge on the first day of trial, which the judge may choose to adopt as part of the written judgment.
- (d) If Housing Court renders judgment in favor of the bank according to subsections (a) or (b) of this section, the mortgagor shall vacate the premises within seven days of the judgment's issuance, with the bank to take possession at the end of such time.
- (e) There shall be no appeal from a Housing Court judgment.

---

Padma Patil, an uneducated farmhand, was and is a domiciliary and citizen of Virginia. Gringotts Bank, Inc. was incorporated in and has its principal place of business in North Carolina (specifically, in an office building located in the Eastern District of North Carolina). In January of 2013, Patil signed a mortgage contract with Gringotts Bank so as to buy a log cabin just on the Virginia side of the North Carolina-Virginia border in York County, located in the Eastern District of Virginia. As part of her mortgage application, Patil "friended" Gringotts on her Facebook page and provided her work email. The amount of the corresponding loan was $60,000; the assessed value of the cabin was $70,000. The contract had an unusual clause in which the interest rate on the mortgage would go up if interest rates on U.S. Treasury Bonds went down. Initially, the mortgage obligated Patil to pay $1200 per month.

Each month, Gringotts sent Patil a bill via regular mail, to her log cabin address, for her mortgage payments, and Patil made the February-April payments. But in May, interest rates on U.S. Treasury Bonds fell, and Gringotts notified Patil by regular mail letter to the log cabin

<!-- page: 4 -->

address that she would be obligated to pay $2000 per month in principal and interest. Patil received the letter and wrote back to Gringotts. She explained that she had in fact received the letter regarding the higher mortgage payment, but that she had not understood the unusual interest rate clause in her mortgage, and that she would never be able to pay $2000 per month. The Gringotts loan officer responded by letter to her log cabin address that if she failed to pay the $2000 per month, it would consider the mortgage in default and would foreclose. In May and in June, Gringotts mailed Patil mortgage bills for $2000. Patil mailed checks for $1200 in May and June.

On late June, 2013, Gringotts mailed Patil a demand letter requiring her make up the $1600 shortfall and begin payment of $2000/month. When Patil immediately responded via letter that she definitely would never be able to do so, Gringotts mailed her a letter informing her that it considered the loan in default, and that it intended to foreclose on the loan, seize the property, and sell it.

On July 2, 2013, the farm Patil worked for announced that it had gone bankrupt and would close its doors and shut down all systems (including email) on July 9. On July 7, Padma found a job working at another farm, this time as a farm supervisor. The new job had a substantially higher salary, and Patil realized (after a night doing complicated math) that she would be able to make the $2000/month payments after all. To celebrate, Patil decided to go on a two-week camping trip by herself. She left early in the morning of July 10, 2013. She asked her mother, aged 73 (and a retired state forest ranger who had never seen a computer in her life), to stop by her house every day to feed her dogs and horse, and to check her mail. Her mother was to open her mail and, if there was anything important, to light a signal fire on a nearby hilltop (where her Mother lived). Before leaving, Patil changed her Facebook page so that anyone using it would see the following message there: "Just got new job! Will be away for two weeks in the country without internet access--see you when I get back! xoxo OMG LOL".

Patil checked the hilltop every day of her camping trip, but saw no signal fire from her mother. On the afternoon of July 10, 2013, Gringotts filed its Summary Eviction Complaint in Virginia Housing Court in York County. That afternoon, a bank employee posted a note on Patil's Facebook page providing her with Housing Court Case number as well as the address of the Housing Court.

On July 25, when Patil returned from her trip (she stayed an extra day), she checked her Facebook page and saw the notice of the lawsuit. The next day, she went to York County Housing Court and obtained a copy of the relevant papers; a Housing Court clerk told her that she had missed the deadline to file an answer, that she had thereby consented to the eviction and to the seizure of her log cabin, and that a judgment had already issued. Patil asked if a lawyer could be appointed at state expense to represent her, and was told no. Patil was forced to leave her log cabin on August 1, and the property was sold that same day. Patil had no place to live until first paycheck at her new job, so she had to camp out in the woods for a few weeks in a nearby national forest.

Patil is angry about how she was treated. She wants to sue York County to make sure that no one else gets treated the same way; she also wouldn't mind getting a money judgment against York County to teach it a lesson and to supplement her new paycheck. She says that she

<!-- page: 5 -->

really could have used a lawyer on July 25, when she went to Housing Court. She also says that she could have asked her sister for a loan to "pay something to somebody to let me keep the log cabin for a while until I started getting paychecks at my new job." Patil heard somewhere that she might do better in federal court, but above all she wants to avoid a jury trial because she's afraid that the jury might end up finding out that 15 years ago she was a drug dealer and was convicted on a cocaine charge (she has since undergone rehab and has been clean for over a decade).

## Questions

**(a)** Suppose Patil sues York County in federal court under section 1983 in the Eastern District of Virginia. What grounds and legal theories would be available to her? Discuss the merits of each theory.

**(b)** What choice can Patil make to avoid a jury trial demand by York in her federal court lawsuit?[^1]

[^1]: If you have ever heard of the 11th Amendment or the doctrine of sovereign immunity, please pay no attention to either one for the purposes of this assignment.

## 2013-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-02-bendheim-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

**(a)** There are two main avenues available: (1) challenge the VBHA on due process grounds or (2) challenge the sufficiency of Gringotts' notice on due process grounds.

**(1)** Pati can allege that the VBHA is invalid because it provides insufficient process. Various aspects of the VBHS can be challenged.

**First**, the statute's timing may be unreasonably short, at least when looked at in the aggregate. The two-week period to answer the action, the expedited two-week period before trial, and the seven days that Patil had to vacate her home might each alone be legitimate, but together may not afford enough time to provide process. In ascertaining whether more process should be required, we look to *Matthews*. Here, where (1) the private interest — one's home, usually their largest asset — is threatened, (2) there is a high likelihood that a few more days would significantly reduce the risk of error, and (3) neither the government nor Bank have meaningful interests in *not* waiting (the extra days add no more administrative costs to the government and only marginally more cost, if any, to the bank), the *Matthews* test may point strongly in favor of providing more time, and to the conclusion that the timeframe afforded insufficient process.

**Second**, the statute denied the opportunity to appeal. While *Vitek*'s mini-code doesn't mention right to appeal as a core element of due process, its absence here further exacerbated the unduly short timeframe. Had the timeframe been short, but Patil been allowed to appeal, perhaps the scheme as a whole would have provided sufficient process. But where, as here, there is a very small window for activity and no opportunity to appeal a (perhaps hasty) decision, there is a stronger argument that due process has been denied.

**Third**, VBHA provides no right to counsel. Patil is unlikely to succeed in arguing this, alone, to overturn the ruling, because *Lassiter* suggests that a government need pay for counsel only where physical liberty is at stake (and if counsel isn't available to keep one's children, it almost surely isn't available to keep one's home). However, the absence of counsel again exacerbates the short timeframe because a lay person might need more time to investigate and to comply with court procedures.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Fourth**, the VBHA denied the opportunity to take discovery. Again, while not required under *Vitek*, given the lack of access to counsel and the short timeframe, the inability to see the bank's evidence might have made it near-impossible for Patil to defend herself. That said, there is little factual dispute in this case, and the basis for the foreclosure is pretty obvious, so lack of discovery is, as applied, less problematic.

**Fifth**, there was no right to a jury (even though Patil doesn't want one, if it gets the ruling thrown out, it's a worthwhile challenge). The 7th Amendment doesn't apply to the states, so she had no federal constitutional jury trial right, but federal appellate judges like juries, and the absence of a jury might weigh on how a court sees the whole scheme.

**(2)** Patil can likewise allege that Gringotts failed to adequately provide notice of the suit by only posting on facebook. Though it is sufficient under the VHBA, Patil might argue that her status indicating that she would be away from the computer — and thus unable to see posts — was enough to warn Gringotts that notice via facebook wouldn't reach her, and that under *Flowers* they would need to notify her another way. On the other hand, in *Flowers* there was affirmative proof of lack of receipt, whereas here, Gringotts would have had to have taken extra steps to ascertain that Patil would be away from her computer. Still, if it is incumbent on the notice-provider in *Flowers* to read the notification that the mail was not delivered, it's not a great stretch to require similar diligence here, requiring a good-faith effort to ensure that Patil actually would see the post. In addition, *Greene* indicates that situations in which it's more likely that notice will fail may require heightened diligence. Regardless of VBHA's allowing notice-by-facebook, the nature of the medium likely requires Gringotts to be at least somewhat more conscientious.

It's particularly salient that until the facebook post, Patil and Gringotts had communicated — effectively — solely through the mail, and that it is odd and perhaps unforeseeable that Gringotts would suddenly choose to provide electronic notice. That said, while it might be fair for Patil to assume that she would receive notice by mail, and thus to assume that the scheme she set up with her mother would suffice, it's highly irresponsible for her to take a 15-day trip to the countryside when days earlier, Gringotts alerted her that they planned to foreclose. Nonetheless, Patil's foolishness doesn't relieve Gringotts of their notice requirement (see *Mennonite Board of Missions*), and so she has a strong argument here.

**(b)** In order to avoid a jury trial, Patil should only ask for an injunction — and not damages — because injunctions sound in equity, and thus have no right to a jury.

## 2013-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-02-rosen-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

**Question 1:**

First, Patil can claim she did not receive constitutional notice. In *Greene*, the Court held flyers on an apartment door to constitute insufficient notice. A facebook post may similarly be insufficient — it is easily missed on a wall covered in a myriad of other posts. The fact, then, that the Act permits notice through facebook post may per se render the Act unconstitutional, even when (unlike in this case), the specific party *did* receive actual notice (see *Wuchter*).

Even if facebook notice might *sometimes* be permissible, in Patil's case it was not. First, the bank knew there was a superior method of notice: they had been successfully corresponding with Patil by mail for weeks. Second, Patil's facebook away message may have alerted the bank that its notification had failed, requiring, under *Jones*, additional effort from the bank. This argument is not a slam dunk: in *Jones*, the notifying party received evidence of non-receipt by registered mail. Here, the away message was not specifically targeted at the bank; furthermore, there may be privacy-based policy reasons to not require a bank employee to read a mortgagee's facebook posts. Patil can also argue that the bank's sudden and unanticipated switch from one form of notice to another was per se unreasonable: a pattern of use may provoke reliance behavior in a party (here, Patil), and the sudden switch may suggest bad faith on the part of the bank. The bank may respond that Patil negligently failed to receive notice, as she should have been checking her facebook page. The reliance argument may counter this attack.

Beyond notice, Patil can point out four potential infractions of due process in the Housing Court: the lack of appealability, discovery, or court-funded representation, and the brief and rigid time frames. The first three elements won't render the system unconstitutional by themselves: *Vitek* suggests that appealability and discovery are not constitutionally required, and *Lassiter* closes off a right to counsel (Patil's potential injury — losing a home — is less severe than the injury the Court addressed in either case — being committed or losing custody of a child, respectively). Nevertheless, though the lack of these elements does not per se render the system unconstitutional, their absence removes significant safeguards which might otherwise mitigate the burdensome timeframe.

The Court has three extremely brief time frames, none of which may be extended for any reason. First, a party has only two weeks during which to receive notice and file an answer. There is no suggestion anywhere in the Act that any circumstance would merit an extension — not illness, the death of a loved one, nor an unexpected absence. The 14 days is thus a blunt instrument, inflexible and insensitive to the exigencies of human existence.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Second, the trial begins within two weeks of a party's answer, and that party must be prepared, on the first day of trial, to submit proposed findings of fact and conclusions of law and to navigate the complex VA rules of evidence. The lack of representation and discovery make this brief time frame especially burdensome. With no right to discovery, a party must gather all relevant evidence herself — within only two weeks. With no state-funded lawyer, she must understand the VA rules of evidence with no assistance. Indeed, Patil didn't understand the legal fine print of her mortgage, and it is likely that evicted mortgagees, as a class, often suffer from this legal confusion: such confusion suggests they especially need more time to prepare for a trial with complex legal rules. Evicted mortgagees are also likely to be poor; taking time off work to prepare for trial would thus be especially burdensome.

Third, a party has only seven days to vacate the premises of her home upon losing at trial. Again, the defining characteristics of the class in question render the rigidity of this timeframe especially onerous. This provision virtually guarantees that numerous poor Virginians will end up homeless. Taken together, these three brief, rigid time frames may make it impossible for someone like Patil to truly be heard.

A court will weigh Virginia's interests against Patil's. The state is facing a liquidity crisis that is in part caused by banks' inability to foreclose "promptly." Virginia thus has a clear interest in promptness which parallels the government's interest in *Matthews*. Nevertheless, a *Matthews* test helps here: comparing extendable time frames with those herein would reveal that a slightly more flexible timing scheme might meet the state's interests without burdening the mortgagee and sporting a high risk of error.

As an additional final factor, no jury is available. It isn't required as a matter of federal constitutional law (the 7th Amendment doesn't apply to the states), but if a case happened to turn on a credibility determination, a federal court might think the absence of a jury potentially significant.

**Question 2:**

If Patil were willing to seek equitable relief — shutting down the Housing Court to protect others — rather than financial damages, she could more easily prevent York from receiving a jury trial (see *Atlas*'s right to jury test).

---

## Assignment 3, Question

The Committee in charge of recommending alterations to the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure to the US Supreme Court is considering a proposal to replace portions of the Fed. Rs. Civ. P. with the following rule.

> **Rule X: Compulsory Joinder:**
>
> (a) Notwithstanding any existing rule or doctrine of common law to the contrary: Any person pleading a claim for relief (whether in the nature of a claim, counterclaim, cross claim, or third-party complaint) shall join any additional person against whom she knows she has a claim for relief and shall assert such claim, provided that the claim against the to-be-joined party arises out of the same transaction or occurrence (or series of related transaction or occurrences) as the first claim for relief.
>
> *Interpretive note: For the purposes of this rule, "person" means any entity or individual who can sue or be sued in court.*
>
> (b) Nothing in this rule should be interpreted to create subject matter jurisdiction in the federal courts where it does not otherwise exist.

You are a staff attorney for the Committee. The Committee asks you for a memorandum addressing the following questions.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Questions

**1)** What would be the effect of Rule X in the following situation under the best interpretation of the Rule?

Antioch Peverell, a famous opera singer domiciled in South Carolina who earns $200,000 per performance, received an outpatient tonsillectomy. The throat surgeon was Dr. Gabrielle Delacour, a Georgia domiciliary. Dr. Delacour performed the surgery in the Atlanta offices of Dursley Medical Care, Inc. ("DMC"). DMC is incorporated in Georgia with its principal place of business in South Carolina. The anesthesiologist in the surgical procedure was one Dilys Derwent, a Georgia domiciliary in town for the week. DMC employs Dr. Delacour and Dr. Derwent. And all jurisdictions relevant to this question have the doctrine of respondeat superior.

Three months after the surgery, Peverell could not hit his high notes and had severe dizzy spells, causing him to miss three of his performances. He consulted a different doctor, who told Peverell he had received an inappropriate mix of anesthesia and that the surgery on his tonsils had been botched.

Peverell sued Dr. Delacour in Georgia federal court for medical malpractice. Peverell and Delacour settled, and pursuant to the settlement, Peverell dismissed his lawsuit with prejudice.

Peverell then brought suit in Georgia state court against DMC and Derwent for medical malpractice. DMC and Derwent moved to dismiss, or in the alternative, for summary judgment, citing Rule X. What result?

**2)** In your opinion, should this proposed rule be adopted? What would be the advantages of the rule? The disadvantages?

## 2013-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-03-cassio-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Civil Procedure 2 — Assignment 3 — Draft Model Answer
**2013**

**(1)** Traditional claim preclusion attaches where the second lawsuit concerns the same claim and the same party and the first judgment was "on the merits," unless the rendering court lacked subject matter jurisdiction over the second lawsuit's theory of relief (*Marrese*). Given section (b) of the proposal, Rule X should not alter this SMJ caveat. It will, however, extend the initial description of claim preclusion to: same claim, any known justiciable party, "on the merits."

As Peverell's case involves medical malpractice, it is presumably governed by state tort law. The first lawsuit was nevertheless allowable in Georgia federal court because there existed complete diversity and the AIC was greater than $75K. The Georgia federal court would also have had diversity jurisdiction over the claim against Derwent, and so Derwent's motion to dismiss on the basis of Rule X should be granted.

Complete diversity did not exist, however, between Peverell and DMC, because DMC maintains its principal place of business in South Carolina. And according to § 1367(b), the Georgia federal court could not have exercised supplemental jurisdiction over DMC either. Thus in spite of Rule X, claim preclusion should not attach, and Peverell's case against DMC should go forward.

**(2)** One reason we might like Rule X is that it prevents a plaintiff from playing jury roulette. This goal can to some extent be accomplished with defensive nonmutual collateral estoppel. But not all states allow DNMCE, and under *Semtek*, we know that state law matters in diversity cases. But even with DNMCE, some issues will undoubtedly remain after a first (failed) lawsuit that can be used to secure liability in a second. And, of course, DNMCE would not apply if the plaintiff *prevails* in the first lawsuit (and yes, there might be a subsequent lawsuit if the plaintiff prevailed in lawsuit #1 when, for example, the plaintiff is unable to recover in full from the defendant in the first lawsuit). By forcing the plaintiff to join all known parties in the first instance, we give the plaintiff precisely one fair trial on the basis of one transaction or occurrence.

Another reason to favor Rule X is that it is likely to reduce the transaction costs of litigation. While the first lawsuit will probably be more complicated and last longer than

<!-- page: 2 -->

otherwise, we can eliminate subsequent lawsuits both where the plaintiff sues other defendants and where the defendants sue each other over the allocation of losses. If the first lawsuit is *too* complicated, presumably a court can exercise its Rule 42 authority to cut it up without jeopardizing the plaintiff, who will have already satisfied her Rule X obligations.

Finally, Rule X may increase judicial administrability. A full and complete picture of the facts may be more likely with all parties involved.

One possible downside to Rule X is unfairness to defendants. It may be that some defendants would prefer to proceed quickly to a jury trial, while others thrive by extending the litigation process for years without ever reaching a jury. The less sophisticated individual defendant may thus be stuck with the lawsuit hanging over her head for ages longer than otherwise, while absent Rule X the plaintiff may have been happy to sue her separately.

Another possible downside is unfairness to plaintiffs. A plaintiff may be in need of damages now, but will be forced to choose between delaying a lawsuit while she puts together a case against every possible defendant, and potentially losing the ability to sue a defendant who was actually most at fault or had the deepest pockets.

Finally, there is some inherent uncertainty in the proposed rule that could be problematic. What does it mean to "know" you have a claim for relief against another person? Theoretically it should be enough if you have knowledge of facts which would survive a motion to dismiss. But it would require a very odd lawsuit indeed to hold you to this standard. Essentially, the defendant you were going after in lawsuit 2 would have to argue that you knew she was likely at fault when you initiated lawsuit 1 — but then if she loses at the motion to dismiss stage she will have to argue that in spite of that stuff you ought to have known, she is nonetheless not liable. Alternately, Rule X may incentivize plaintiffs to do inadequate homework, so that they do not "discover" claims for relief against other parties until they finish their first lawsuit.

## 2013-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2013-assignment-03-miller-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 Response

## 1)

The claims Peverell has against Delacour, Derwent, and DMC all arise from the same transaction or occurrence (or related occurrences), the tonsil surgery. Therefore, under the proposed rule Peverell had to join all three defendants in his initial lawsuit if possible. However, DMC's principal place of business is in South Carolina, making it a domiciliary of South Carolina like Peverell, so joining DMC in the initial lawsuit would have destroyed complete diversity and would therefore have been invalid.

Since Peverell could have joined Derwent in the initial lawsuit, proposed Rule X would preclude Peverell from suing Derwent now. The suit against Derwent should be dismissed.

Since Peverell could not have joined DMC in the initial lawsuit, proposed Rule X has no effect, and Peverell can proceed with his lawsuit against DMC.

## 2)

The proposed rule should not be adopted. The primary argument in favor of adopting the rule is the improved judicial economy of disposing of all claims related to the same transaction or occurrence at once, the same justification for the compulsory counterclaim rule. The supplemental jurisdiction that allows the compulsory counterclaim system to work (§ 1367(b) would never oust jurisdiction over a counterclaim because a counterclaim is not a claim "by a plaintiff"), however, does not apply to this rule and therefore judicial economy is not automatically improved as it is under the counterclaim rule. As the Peverell case illustrates, since there is no supplemental diversity jurisdiction DMC could not have been joined in the federal court lawsuit, and the rule would likely not have a large effect on the number of lawsuits filed.

The rules of non-mutual offensive and defensive collateral estoppel, where they apply, already disincentivize filing multiple lawsuits. If a state (state law matters, see *Semtek*) allows nonmutual defensive collateral estoppel, any issues the plaintiff loses in the first lawsuit are automatically lost in the second, and any issues won in the first lawsuit cannot be used to preclude the second defendant even if he could have been joined in the first lawsuit. Thus, the plaintiff has nothing to gain and everything to lose by litigating the same issues multiple times. The plaintiff would also obviously have to bear the exorbitant costs of multiple lawsuits even in the best case scenario. Thus, the rule accomplishes little that a rule allowing NMDCE could not.

Any minor judicial economy gains could be offset by the costs of litigating the application of the new rule itself. The rule requires joinder of all parties against whom the plaintiff *knows* she has a claim for relief. Determining who the plaintiff "knew" she had a claim for relief against at the time she filed the lawsuit could be a difficult and fact-intensive inquiry that defendants could use to drag out litigation unnecessarily. Additionally, the proposed rule requires joinder when the claims arise out of the same or related transactions or occurrences. As *Nesbit* and *Jones v. Morris Plan Bank* demonstrate, the scope of this phrase seems to be dependent on the facts and desired policy outcome of a specific case and context, making it hard to predict and inviting litigation.

<!-- page: 2 -->

In addition to these increased litigation costs, the proposed rule could also lead to unjust outcomes. The same phrases that invite increased litigation, i.e. "knows" and "same transaction or occurrence," could also frustrate an otherwise valid claim if decided incorrectly by a court, or construed in a way that would have been difficult or impossible to predict *ex ante*, *see Jones*. The debatable judicial economy gains that the proposed rule would bring are outweighed by the costs of potential errors that the rule creates, and the rule should not be adopted.

## Assignment 1, Question

Assume the following (all parts of which are totally fictional).

California Revised Statutes, § 3333, provides:

> In any action sounding in tort, involving a plea for damages for emotional distress, and not involving the death of the plaintiff or any member of the plaintiff's family, a plaintiff who proves that she has suffered emotional harm shall be entitled to an award of exactly $20,000, no more and no less.

The damage limit described above applies in full to the common law tort of "Outrage" or "Intentional Infliction of Emotional Distress" in California. Oregon law does not specify the amount of damages that can be awarded in Outrage lawsuits, and appellate courts in Oregon have upheld damages awards as high as $5,000,000.00.

Both California and Oregon recognize the tort of Outrage and define it as follows: "One who by extreme or outrageous conduct intentionally or recklessly causes severe emotional distress to another is subject to liability, and shall pay damages as appropriate under the law, for such emotional distress." Both states define "recklessness" as the "failure to exercise due care to eliminate a known risk of harm." Under the law of both Oregon and California, the tort of "Outrage" is not complete until the plaintiff suffers the emotional distress that gives rise to the claim.

California follows the Second Restatement on Choice of Law. Oregon follows the First Restatement on choice of law. Both states have the doctrine of "respondeat superior," meaning that a company is liable in tort for the actions of employees that are within the scope of the employees' duties.

Weasley's Wizard Wheezes, Inc. ("WWW") is incorporated in Delaware. It manufactures all of its nearly magical internet products in its enormous manufacturing facility just outside of Portland, Oregon, where it employs about 2000 people. WWW's plant is

<!-- page: 3 -->

considered one of the best places to work in the state, featuring a fully complete gym with a swimming pool, a first class dining facility, and live musical performances every Friday at 5:30 pm. The Mayor of the City of Portland regularly visits the facility to make sure that all is well at one of the City's biggest employers.

WWW's owners are Fred and George Weasley; between them, they own 65% of the company's voting stock. Fred is the Chief Executive Officer, the Chief Operating Officer, and the Chief Financial Officer. George serves as both General Counsel (he obtained his law degree from Hogwarts in 2003) and the Chair of the Board. Both are domiciled in Sacramento, California, where they enjoy life in their large two-house compound just outside the city limits. They have a small building on the compound with five rooms in it that they use as their offices, where they make all major decisions on behalf of the company. Their two executive assistants, Angelina Johnson and Oliver Wood, came to work in this small building, which also had a fully wired room for board meetings. The compound also has a single-building work area, where Fred and George create their latest prototypes.

The Weasleys were excited. They had just created a new product, the Weasley's Amazing Machine (the "WAM"). The WAM represented the next generation of WWW's line of Internet-based gaming products. It consisted of a full body suit that the user could wear while standing or sitting on a specially designed platform that could simulate the movements of anything from an airplane to an earthquake. The result was a total immersion in the simulation. The WAM came with a menu of over 1500 simulation-based games that a user could experience, all of them interactive and based on simulated worlds that existed on the Internet.

The Weasleys wanted to make a big splash with the WAM, and they thought the best way to do so would be to provide a group of WWW's 30 best customers with a free copy of the device for their exclusive use for a period of two months. They hoped that the users would create Youtube videos about the WAM that would go viral and attract more viewers than Gangnam Style. Accordingly, the Weasleys contacted 30 customers to invite them to a party at their Sacramento compound. The invitations were sent via gmail (which does not reveal the email recipients' physical locations).

The party, which occurred on February 23, 2013, was a roaring affair; Psy (a Korean K-pop star) came to perform for the 30 customers, the Weasleys, and their two assistants, Johnson and Wood. No alcohol was served, however, as each customer had been instructed to bring a vehicle with adequate trunk space for a special "end of party gift." During the party, the guests were treated to a tour of the Weasley's Sacramento compound, including a 30-minute look inside of Fred & George's workroom. Fred and George gleefully described how they had created the 30 WAM prototypes from scratch entirely within this workroom. At the end of the party, the Weasleys presented each of

<!-- page: 4 -->

the 30 customers with his or her own WAM. The customers were overjoyed. Each loaded up his or her WAM and headed home.

One of these customers was Nymphadora Tonks, who preferred to go by her surname, "Tonks." Tonks drove her WAM back to her house in Hogsmeade, Oregon, just across the California/Oregon border. She installed the unit in her bedroom, fired it up, and called the office where she worked to say that she would be taking a three-week vacation. Too embarrassed to say how she really planned to spend the time, she told her office that she would be on a romantic getaway with Remus drinking wine in the Napa valley. She and Remus spent the next 20 days more or less continuously in the WAM bodysuit, taking turns playing the games. They were both delighted. At the end of the three-week WAM binge, both returned to work, but they continued to enjoy the WAM for a few hours in the evening and a little more on the weekend.

In late June of 2013, while surfing the Internet from her home in Hogsmeade, Tonks stumbled across a new website and blog called "AllAboutWWWFans.com." When she went to the site, she was horrified to see, on the first page, pictures of Remus and herself in various states of undress in their home bedroom. After she clicked on a panel that said, "Click here if you want to learn more about these losers!", she found text that described accurately her height and weight, that stated that she exercised regularly and had good reflexes, and that she had told her officemates that she would be away on a 3-week vacation in the Napa valley but that she had really spent 8 hours a day in "a new WWW product not on the market yet." She explored the website further and found private information, including photos, of each of the attendees of the WAM party.

That night, Tonks and Remus threw their WAM in the trash. The next day Tonks sought psychiatric counseling for grief and shock from a counselor in Sacramento. The counselor diagnosed her with post-traumatic stress disorder.

The day after, Tonks consulted a partner in the law firm for which you work.

You are an associate in that law firm. The partner for whom you work asks for your help. She says that she has some indication that WWW was collecting private data on WAM users without the users' knowledge or permission, and that Tonks wants to sue WWW for the tort of Outrage. But, says the partner, "I'd like to try to stay out of federal court if at all possible. Federal courts have been issuing bad rulings lately on Outrage suits, and I'm worried that we won't win the case. That said, I'd still like to hear whether the federal courts might be an option for us." The partner asks you to write a memo discussing the following question: in what court systems might Tonks be able to file a lawsuit against WWW? What would be the advantages and disadvantages of each in terms of the availability of damages? How do you respond? *(Note from Professor Greiner: You may assume that there is no federal law applicable to damages in this case, so that the question of damages is governed by state law.)*

## 2014-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-01-broer-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1: Model Answer

**Word count: 897**

Tonks can file suit in six systems: CA state court, OR state court, federal court in CA, federal court in OR, DE state court, or federal court in DE. She should file in CA state court because it's likely CA state court will apply OR law on outrage, so Tonks can avoid CA's cap for damages, and because WWW would be unable to remove to federal court.

## CA State Court

CA has general IPJ over WWW because WWW is a citizen of CA; its principal place of business/nerve center (*Hertz*) is in Sacramento.

Problem for Tonks is that CA's outrage law caps damages, so she wants OR outrage to apply. HCOL analysis suggests that CA state court will probably use OR substantive law: Is outrage law procedural or substantive? If procedural, CA law would apply. But outrage is substantive, so CA's COL rules apply. CA is Second Restatement state, so case is split up into issues. Focusing on damages alone, both center of gravity theory and Section 6 factors suggest that OR law should be applied since the harm was done in OR and because OR has legitimate interest in protecting its citizens. There is some uncertainty here though since Second Restatement is subjective, and CA could argue that it has a strong interest in protecting its corporations from high damages.

Another advantage of CA state court filing is that WWW cannot remove because WWW is a citizen of CA (§ 1441(b)(2)).

## OR State Court

Tonks needs to establish that OR state court has IPJ over WWW, which may be difficult. OR likely doesn't have general jurisdiction over WWW because its only connection to the state is its manufacturing plant there, and *Daimler* makes general IPJ hard to establish. OR might be able to establish specific jurisdiction. Assuming OR has long arm statute, constitutional basis for IPJ is through minimum contacts test (*International Shoe*). First, the connection between WWW, OR, and the subject matter is tenuous: WWW's only contact with OR are its production facility, but the prototype WAMs in this case weren't made at that plant. WAM was brought to OR by the unilateral action of third party, Tonks (*Hanson*). Moreover, WWW didn't know Tonks lived in OR; invitations were emailed. On other hand, Tonks can argue that WWW intended WAM users to take WAMs back to their homes, and WWW eventually knew WAM was operating in OR. Second part of minimum contacts analysis is to consider other factors, which cut both ways in this case. E.g., WWW didn't purposely avail itself of OR with in WAMs. But, OR has an interest in litigating and protecting its citizens. So disadvantage to bringing suit in OR state court is it's unclear if court has IPJ over WWW.

Advantage to bringing suit in OR is that OR law on outrage and damages would apply, and there'd be no damages cap. Outrage tort is substantive, and OR is First Restatement. Where was blood spilled? All of the tortious activity happened in OR: Tonks' emotional distress all happened in OR. So, apply OR substantive law. Tonks thus can get high damages in OR state court.

## Federal Court in CA

Case cannot get into federal court through FQJ because there's no federal tort of outrage and no federal issue at stake.

Case can get into CA federal court through DJ: Tonks domiciled in OR; WWW is domiciled in CA (and DE) (*Hertz*). Complete diversity satisfied. However, AIC depends on whether OR or CA law governs on damages. Problem is that if CA's damages cap applies, AIC is not met.

<!-- page: 2 -->

So what law is applied? Federal court will do HCOL outlined above for a CA state court. Likely that OR substantive law would be applied, so AIC would likely be met. DJ thus satisfied.

Federal court in CA has IPJ over WWW via 4(k)(1)(a): since CA state court has IPJ, so too does a federal court in CA.

Venue is okay since WWW is citizen of CA (§ 1391(b)(1)).

Disadvantage to bring suit in federal court is that federal courts have not been receptive to outrage claims.

## Federal Court in OR

SMJ analysis is similar to federal court in CA: no FQJ; must have DJ. Complete diversity met, and because federal court in OR will apply OR law (same HCOL analysis as discussed for OR state court above), AIC is met.

IPJ carries up from OR state court's IPJ via 4(k)(1)(a).

Venue is fine under § 1391(b)(2), since a substantial portion of events occurred in OR: this is where the data was taken from Tonks and transmitted back to WWW.

Same disadvantage here: federal courts have not been receptive to outrage claims.

## DE State Court or Federal Court in DE

A DE court has IPJ (general jurisdiction) over WWW (and thus DE federal court has IPJ under 4(k)(1)(a)) because WWW is incorporated in DE and is thus a citizen of that state. Federal court SMJ is the same as above, venue lies because WWW is citizen of DE.

An advantage of filing in DE is that WWW cannot remove because WWW is a citizen of DE (§ 1441(b)(2)).

If DE is a 1st Restatement HCOL state, analysis would be like OR, above. If DE is a 2d Restatement HCOL state, analysis would be like CA, above. DE law would not apply.

## 2014-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2
