<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1: Model Answer

Tonks (NT) is domiciled in Oregon. She lives in Oregon and we have no indication that she is only living there temporarily and has a permanent home elsewhere. WWW is a citizen both in Delaware (where it is incorporated) and in California, which is where its nerve center is (where they make all of their major decisions, have board meetings, and design their prototypes, *Hertz*).

NT could file in CA state court, and WWW will not be able to remove; WWW is a CA citizen. Because WWW is a citizen of CA, the CA court will have IPJ through general jurisdiction. CA is risky in terms of the damage cap because they are a 2nd Restatement state. If the CA damage cap is procedural then it will apply in CA state court and the damages will be capped at $20K, which is not something NT will want. If it is substantive, 2RST calls for a more in depth analysis which will weigh various factors from the Restatement and allow different state law to cover different issues. The damages limit will likely be seen as substantive, and the CA court would probably apply OR damages law since NT's injury was felt there, NT is a citizen of Oregon, and WWW has a large production facility in OR, so OR has an interest in both protecting its citizens and whether companies with a large presence in OR are creating products which spy on its consumers (many of whom are likely from OR). Also, the data was stolen and transferred from OR. However, CA could also argue it has an interest in protecting companies domiciled in their state from massive liabilities through the cap, and the party was in CA and the device was designed in CA. So while CA is nice because WWW cannot remove, NT cannot be sure that there won't be a $20K damage limit on her award.

If she files in OR state court, NT could face a problem with establishing IPJ over WWW. However, WWW produces all of its products in OR and employs over 2000 people there, so the proportion of its business conducted there compared to anywhere else could establish general IPJ. *Daimler*. If not, the court could have specific jurisdiction based on minimum contacts between WWW and OR. *International Shoe*. WWW has purposefully availed itself to OR by conducting business there, and the tort was completed in OR (because this was where NT felt the emotional distress). WWW could counter that the prototypes were not made in OR, WWW did not know where NT lived (her email address did not give a location) and that NT took the prototype to OR through unilateral third party action. *Hanson*. However, the difficulties in establishing IPJ might be worth it to guarantee that OR law (meaning no damage cap) will apply. OR is 1st Restatement, so HCOL analysis is "where is the blood?" The tort began (stealing and transmission of data) and ended (NT feels distress) in OR, so OR law will apply. A major disadvantage to filing in OR state court is that WWW could remove the lawsuit; there is diversity SMJ, see immediately below, and no bar to removal.

WWW cannot remove from CA state court and there is no federal cause of action or embedded federal question (*Smith*; *Grable*), but NT could file in CA federal court. If she does, there will be complete diversity (see above, *Strawbridge*) but the AiC requirement will only be met if the CA damage law doesn't apply. IPJ will apply through 4(k)(1)(a) (piggy back on state). Venue is proper because WWW is a citizen of CA. For COL, the federal court will use the HCOL rules of the state in which it sits to determine substantive law. From the analysis above, this means that NT could still possibly be

<!-- page: 2 -->

limited to a $20K damage award in CA federal court, plus there is still problem of federal courts not being receptive to outrage claims.

With OR federal court, similar to above, could only come in through diversity. Complete diversity is still met and this time there would be no problem with AIC because, as mentioned above, federal court will do HCOL analysis based on rules of state it sits. That would mean the first restatement, so likely OR law, which is good. Venue might be harder to lay given that WWW is not a citizen of OR but considering most of the tortious events occurred in OR, it shouldn't be a problem. IPJ will carry up through 4(k)(1)(a), but OR federal court will have same disadvantage as CA federal court.

Finally, NT could file in DE state court. Since WWW is incorporated in DE, it is a citizen there and the court would have IPJ over WWW. Like CA state court, WWW could not remove. To know which state's law would apply, we would need to know if DE is a first or second Restatement state (law would either be CA or OR law, DE law would not apply). If R1, analysis is like OR state court, above; if R2, analysis is like CA state court, above. Given the distance between DE and OR, NT might find filing in DE to be inconvenient. DE federal court would also be like OR or CA federal court, depending on HCOL, as per DE state court.

Thus NT should file in CA state court because WWW will not be able to remove and there is a good chance that the CA damage limit will not apply.

# Assignment 1: Model Answer

**Word Count: 872**

**SMJ citizenship:** Tonks (T) is domiciled in Oregon. WWW is a citizen of Delaware (state of incorporation) and California (principal nerve center, *Hertz*).

**T could sue in California State/Federal court, Oregon State/Federal Court, or DE State/Federal Court, but her best option is to bring her action in California State Court because WWW will not be able to remove to Federal Court.**

**Federal Court SMJ Analysis:** There is no FQJ because there is no Federal CoA or embedded federal issue. Complete diversity is satisfied because the parties do not share domiciles (*Strawbridge*), but the Amount in Controversy needs to be over $75,000 for diversity jurisdiction. AiC will depend on which law governs the limit on damages: if CA's $20k damages cap is in effect, T might be awarded less money *but* she won't have to litigate in federal court.

**California State Court:** If T files in CA state court, WWW is boxed in because removal is not allowed when an action is brought in a state where the defendant is domiciled. 28 U.S.C. § 1441(b)(2). IPJ over WWW exists through general jurisdiction: WWW is a CA citizen. The damages limit is substantive for HCOL purposes, so a court applies its choice of law rules to determine which state's laws govern. CA uses the 2nd restatement HCoL: the court dissects the event to determine which state has a controlling interest on a particular issue. CA will probably apply OR's rules for damages because that is where the harm occurred (i.e. where the need for damages exists), WWW is a major employer there so OR cares about protecting its residents from their activities, and substantial events (i.e. the use of the WAM) occurred there. However, an argument exists for CA law: by enacting a damages cap, CA may be attempting to protect its companies from crushing liability, and substantial events occurred in CA (the party, WAM's creation).

**California Federal Court:** Re SMJ, see above. Under 4(k)(1)(a), the federal court piggybacks on CA state court's long arm, so IPJ over WWW is satisfied, see above. Venue is proper because WWW is a CA citizen. 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b)(1). A federal court will use the HCoL rules of the state it sits in (CA) to determine the substantive law, see above. T would face the same problems as in CA state court along with the problems of litigating "Outrage" in federal court.

**Oregon State Court:** If T files in OR state court, WWW could remove the suit to federal court because of Diversity Jurisdiction, which we apparently don't want. T also needs to establish OR's IPJ over WWW. WWW's activity in OR is continuous and systematic (large manufacturing plant with thousands of employees), and the astronomical proportion of business conducted there should be enough for general jurisdiction. *Daimler*. If not, specific jurisdiction is established via minimum contacts. *Int'l Shoe*. WWW might argue that a significant portion of WWW's tortious activities occurred in CA (prototype built, WAM party). However, the photos were taken in OR and the data was received from OR. WWW also purposefully avails itself of OR law when conducting business, and the tort occurred in the course of business so there is connection and reasonable foreseeability. *McGee*; *WWV*. T unilaterally acted by driving to OR, but WWW knowingly invited people from around the country (even if email didn't specify geography), and WWW asked them to soberly transport the WAM in their cars so it knew this

<!-- page: 2 -->

prototype would be traveling somewhere by car (stream of commerce). *Kulko*; *Asahi*. Considering the close proximity and WWW's manufacturing presence there, it's not inconvenient for WWW to litigate in OR (so not a violation of DPC). Thus, IPJ is likely through either general or specific jurisdiction.

Under the 1st RST, the court will use "where is the blood?" to determine which state's laws govern. Here, the last event necessary for the tort to arise was when the plaintiff suffered emotional distress. T suffered emotional distress at her house in Oregon when she saw the photos and she immediately threw out the machine and sought counseling. Therefore, OR law governs and T is more likely to win greater damages.

**Oregon Federal Court:** Diversity jurisdiction exists because the lack of a damages cap means AiC is satisfied. IPJ is established through piggybacking on 4(k)(1)(a). Venue is proper because T can argue that a substantial portion of the tortious events occurred there (WWW will argue that the prototype was built in CA, but the photos were taken in OR and the use of the physical machine occurred there). 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b)(2). Federal Court would apply OR HCoL, so there is no cap on damages, but federal court is still hostile to Outrage claims.

**Delaware State/Federal Court:** IPJ exists over WWW through general jurisdiction (4(k)(1)(a) analysis same as above), SMJ in federal court same as above, and venue is proper because WWW is a DE citizen. If T files in state court, WWW can't remove for the same reason as CA state court. Choice of law analysis depends on if DE is a 1st (analysis like OR, above) or 2nd (analysis like CA, above) restatement state, but either way DE law wouldn't apply because no significant events leading up to or right after the tort occurred there.

# Assignment 2 (Graded)
**Civil Procedure 2, 2014**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 24, 2014
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Thursday, October 30, 2014
**Word limit:** 900
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.
12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

## Assignment 2, Question

The North Carolina Rules of Civil Procedure ("NCRCP"), which govern proceedings in North Carolina state court, provide as follows:

**Rule 65: Installment Contracts, Pretrial Seizure and Return of Goods Not Realty**

(a) This section provides the right of a seller regarding pretrial seizure and possession of goods or tangible items of property, not realty (hereinafter called the "Goods"), who sells to a buyer on a written, signed installment contract.

(b) A seller of Goods in a contract providing for periodic installment payments by a buyer may move the court for a pretrial seizure, and for possession pending completion of the litigation, of those Goods, as follows.

> (1) The seller shall file with the court a complaint for an unpaid balance due on the installment payments, which complaint shall describe the facts and circumstances of the sales contract and the nature of the Goods, and shall describe the payments made and missing on the installment contract. The seller/plaintiff shall NOT serve the buyer/defendant at this time.
>
> (2) The seller shall file with the complaint a certified copy of the contract as well as of proof of all payments received on the contract.
>
> (3) The seller shall file one or more affidavits, written by persons with personal knowledge of the events described, and sworn under penalty of perjury, attesting to the non-payment of one or more installments due, to the expiration of any applicable cure period, to the notification by the seller to the buyer of the past due

<!-- page: 3 -->

> amounts, and to the continued refusal by the Buyer to make the relevant payments or to return the Goods to the Seller.
>
> (4) The seller shall file with the court a motion and accompanying arguments thoroughly discussing any applicable law. Such motion shall request an ex parte[^1] hearing from the Court at its earliest convenience.

(c) The Court, acting through the first available judge, upon receiving a motion as described in section (b)(4), above, shall at its earliest convenience schedule an ex parte hearing on the motion, at which time it will review the papers filed by the Seller. If the Court finds that the affidavits, certified copies, and other evidence presented by the Seller, establish that the Seller is likely to succeed on the merits of its complaint for non-payment of installment payments duly owed, it shall issue a writ directing the Sheriff of the Court to

> (1) seize, using force if necessary, the Goods from the Buyer and to return them to the possession of the Seller, and
>
> (2) to serve upon the Buyer, in person (or in the case of an incorporeal entity, upon a high-level official of that entity) all of the papers described in section (b), above, along with a summons to appear before the Court.

(d) Litigation shall then ensue on the Complaint in the ordinary course of business.

[^1]: An ex parte hearing is one in which only one party (here, the seller), shows up to speak to the court.

---

North Carolina has a long-arm statute that allows its state courts to exercise personal jurisdiction in any circumstance "permitted by the United States Constitution."

Slughorn, Inc. was a small corporation incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Charlotte, NC. Slughorn, Inc.'s sole product is Felix Felicis, a nearly-magical catalyst essential to an improved chemical process to manufacture a highly popular and profitable chocolate frog candy. Slughorn's small (three-person) manufacturing plant is located in Charlotte, NC. Slughorn's sole method of advertising is the Internet. Its webpage shows a beautiful aerial, nighttime view of the Charlotte skyline, and each of the subsequent pages shows a similar view of a North Carolina City. The customer order page shows a photograph of happy beachgoers around a sign that says, "Outer Banks, NC." Customers place orders by sending an email to the company's email account, slughorn@gmail.com. Customers with questions about any aspect of dealing with the company are similarly instructed to send the company an email at its business email address. The website states that payments to Slughorn are accepted via electronic transfer to an account in the Local Savings and Loan Bank of Charlotte, NC,

<!-- page: 4 -->

which is a small bank with two locations, both in Charlotte. No other means of contact (including a business address) is on the company's website, although its Charlotte, NC address can be found in the company's articles of incorporation, which are available online at the website of the Secretary of State of Delaware. Note that because Felix Felicis is a catalyst, it is not consumed or destroyed in the manufacturing process.

Honeydukes, Inc., is a small candy company incorporated in South Carolina with its principal place of business in Columbia, SC. Honeydukes makes chocolate frogs. On February 4, 2014, Honeydukes sent an email from its business email account placing an order for 10 ounces of Felix Felicis, with a purchase price of $300,000.00. Slughorn responded by emailing Honeydukes its standard business contract, by which delivery would be made immediately by special courier, with Honeydukes to pay $30,000 each month, payments due at the beginning of each month, first payment due on March 1, 2014. Honeydukes signed the contract. Delivery of the Felix Felicis was made to Honeydukes' office by a courier on February 19, 2014.

Honeydukes made the March 1, April 1, and May 1 payments via wire transfer to Slughorn's bank account at the Local Savings and Loan Bank of Charlotte, NC. On May 29, however, it emailed Slughorn that it would not be making further payments, stating that the Felix Felicis it had received was impure and of low quality, and that as a result, its manufacturing operation of chocolate frogs was operating at half speed. Honeydukes offered to pay $60,000 more (for a total of $150,000) in final settlement of all debts owed Slughorn. Slughorn responded with an email stating that it did not believe Honeydukes' assertion of a bad product, and demanding that payments be made on schedule. Honeydukes responded that it would make no further payments until its complaints about the quality of the Felix Felicis were resolved. There was no further communication between the parties, and no further payments were made.

On September 17, Slughorn filed the following documents in the United States District Court for the Western District of North Carolina:

- A complaint for breach of contract reciting that Honeydukes had agreed via email contract to pay $300,000 in $30,000 monthly payments for 10 ounces of Felix Felicis; that delivery of the product had been made on February 19, 2014, that the product delivered was of high quality, and was still in Honeydukes' manufacturing plant in South Carolina; that Honeydukes had made the first three payments but had missed the payments due in June, July, August, and September; that Slughorn had sent an email demand for the payments due; that Slughorn had refused payment. The complaint sought payment of $120,000.00 in damages for the missed payments.
- Certified copies of the email contract and all emails exchanged between the parties.
- Sworn affidavits by Slughorn employees with knowledge of the events above describing them in full.

<!-- page: 5 -->

- Certified copies of bank statements from the Local Savings and Loan Bank of Charlotte, NC, showing that Honeydukes had made the first three payments due on the contract but none since.
- A motion requesting the court to issue a writ of possession under North Carolina Rule of Civil Procedure 65, such that the sheriff would seize the Felix Felicis from Honeydukes and return it to Slughorn, pending litigation. The motion was accompanied by a brief discussing the law and a request for a rapid ex parte hearing.

On September 30, 2014, the North Carolina federal court held a hearing. The judge reviewed the papers Slughorn had filed and issued an order directing the federal sheriff to repossess the Felix Felicis. On October 1, 2014, the sheriff drove to Honeydukes' headquarters in South Carolina, served all of Slughorn's court papers on the Honeydukes' Chief Executive Officer, and demanded possession of the Felix Felicis. Later that afternoon, Honeydukes placed a vial containing the substance into the hands of the sheriff, who drove it back to Slughorn's offices in Charlotte.

You are an associate in the law office that represents Honeydukes. The partner you are working for says that Honeydukes has told her that it REALLY needs the Felix Felicis to continue its manufacturing operation. Even at half efficiency, it is making a lot of money on the chocolate frogs, and it cannot manufacture chocolate frogs without the substance. The partner asks you to describe any and all arguments your client might make against the process Slughorn used to obtain repossession of the substance, or otherwise against the lawsuit. The partner tells you NOT to discuss the issue of whether the Felix Felicis that Slughorn provided was in fact faulty; she's still investigating the facts on that issue.

What arguments could your client use?

## 2014-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-02-brodziak-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

NC has SMJ under § 1332 because complete diversity exists between Slughorn, citizen of NC (central nerve) and DE (incorporation) and Honeydukes, citizen of SC (central nerve and incorporation). The amount in controversy is above $90,000. Venue is also proper under § 1391(b)(2) or otherwise § 1391(b)(3), if there actually is IPJ.

## Argument 1: No IPJ

First, Honeydukes can argue that there is no IPJ, which would require the lawsuit to be dismissed, and the Felix Felicis (FF) returned. NC District Court can use 4(k)(1)(A) to use NC's long arm statute, which goes to the limits of the constitution (general or specific jurisdiction). There is no general jurisdiction because Honeydukes is not domiciled in NC and no tag (*McArthur*).

Honeydukes can argue no specific jurisdiction because it does not have minimum contacts with NC (*International Shoe*). Honeydukes can argue that it did not know Slughorn was in NC, since the website had no business address and wiring money to a NC bank account is not enough to know that the money is actually going to a company in NC. Therefore, it did not purposefully avail itself of NC laws (*McGee*). Honeydukes can also argue that NC's long arm statute does not specifically say that there is specific jurisdiction for out-of-state buyers who miss installment payments (*Hanson*). Slughorn can argue that it only advertised via its website, and its website was full of pictures and statements attesting to its NC location. Slughorn also required that customers pay via electronic transfer to a bank in NC.

## Argument 2: Erie Challenge

Second, Honeydukes can raise an Erie challenge. The most relevant federal rule is FRCP 65 (preliminary injunction). FRCP 65 requires a movant to show likelihood of success on the merits, irreparable harm, a balance of the equities, and that an injunction is in the public interest. Under FRCP 65, Slughorn would be unlikely to get a preliminary injunction because it will not suffer irreparable harm (can sue for damages later).

Under a *York* analysis, Honeydukes would want to argue that the outcome of either test would be the same. However, given the different standards between the two tests, that is unlikely to be successful. Therefore, using federal law would change the outcome of the lawsuit, so need to use the state law.

Under the *Hanna* majority analysis, Honeydukes should argue that there is a valid (rule doesn't abridge a substantive right, REA) and applicable FRCP (rule determines whether property can be seized before a full trial). Slughorn could argue that FRCP 65 is inapplicable because preliminary injunctions are used to prevent people from doing certain things, not a tool of property repossession. *Walker* supports a narrow interpretation of FRCPs. If Rule 65 is NOT applicable, we'd probably use state law. There may be some federal common law right-of-replevin procedure that Slughorn might use, but (i) we didn't learn about it, and (ii) using the "twin aims of Erie" test (which we should use when we're talking about federal common law) almost always results in state law.

Under the Harlan concurrence analysis, Honeydukes would want to argue that using the federal rule would not change the pre-litigation conduct of the parties (parties would not take method of litigation into account when forming contract; federal rules wouldn't require different insurance policies), so the federal rule should apply. Slughorn could argue that he would have drafted a different contract, such as requiring more payment upfront, had he known he would have had to meet the high standard of preliminary injunction. If this is true, then the FRCP affects the preliminary conduct of the parties, and state law should apply.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Argument 3: Procedural Due Process Violation

The third issue that Honeydukes can raise is that the procedure under NCRCP 65 violates procedural due process (PDP) because there is insufficient opportunity for Honeydukes to be heard before its property was seized (*Vitek*). PDP applies even in temporary deprivations (*Sniadach*). Honeydukes should argue that there is no reason for urgency that overrides their right to a hearing pre-deprivation (*Fuentes*). Slughorn could argue that it retains a property interest in the FF, and therefore has an interest in preventing waste to the property, especially if it can show that there is a limited quantity of FF (*Mitchell*). Honeydukes should also argue that Slughorn was not required to post a bond (*Fuentes*; *Doehr*), it had no opportunity to get its property back, such as posting a counterbond (*Fuentes*), and that there was no immediate post-deprivation hearing. Slughorn can argue that it was required to make a substantial showing of facts in his complaint before the property was seized (not just a conclusory allegation that he was entitled to the goods, *Fuentes*), he was required to file additional written evidence including affidavits by witnesses with knowledge of the events, that the decision to seize the property was made by a judge (*Mitchell*), and that there is no obligation on Honeydukes to file anything in court to get their property back since post-seizure, there will be a trial to determine final possession.

## Argument 4: Jurisdiction Over Property

The final argument that Honeydukes could make is that since NCRCP 65 authorizes repossession of property, it is a writ of replevin. A writ of replevin is analogous to a quasi in rem procedure, where courts can generally only exercise jurisdiction over property that is within their jurisdiction (*Shaffer*). Honeydukes can argue that NC district court does not have jurisdiction, since the property was in SC when the NC court authorized the sheriff to seize it.

## 2014-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-02-cohen-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

## SMJ

There is diversity jurisdiction. 28 U.S.C. § 1332. Slughorn is citizen of Delaware (incorporation) and NC (central nerve). Honeydukes is a citizen of SC (central nerve and incorporation). AIC is at least $90,000 > $75,000.

## IPJ

Honeydukes could argue no IPJ (and if no IPJ, then lawsuit would have to be dismissed and the FF returned). FRCP 12(b)(2). Establishing IPJ requires long-arm statute and a constitutional basis. Here, the NC long-arm statute goes to the limits of the constitution, so IPJ OK if there is general or specific jurisdiction: federal court "piggy-backs" on long-arm statute of state in which it sits. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A).

Honeydukes can argue no general jurisdiction: NC is not Honeydukes's home state, and no in-state tag.

Honeydukes can also argue no specific jurisdiction. The minimum contacts/*International Shoe* requirement applies in all exercises of specific jurisdiction. *Shaffer v. Heitner*. Honeydukes can argue that it did not purposefully avail itself of NC law, since it may not have been aware that Slughorn was an NC corporation, and therefore did not target that forum. *See Hanson v. Denckla*; *J. McIntyre*. Slughorn's website provides no business address. The fact that Honeydukes made payments to an NC bank is insufficient to establish that Honeydukes was aware Slughorn was based in NC; wiring money through a bank that happened to be in NC doesn't establish minimum contacts with NC; the money could end up anywhere. Neither the NCRCP nor NC's long-arm statute provides notice to out-of-state buyers that specific IPJ will be exercised against them if they miss installments. *Compare McGee* (specific long-arm) *with Hanson* (no specific long-arm).

## Venue

Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) because a substantial amount of the events giving rise to the claim occurred in the district and, if there is IPJ, then venue is also proper under § 1391(b)(1).

## PDP

Honeydukes can argue insufficient opportunity to be heard. Temporary deprivation is a property deprivation requiring PDP. *Sniadach*. Honeydukes was denied the right to attend Slughorn's ex parte hearing and had no opportunity to be heard before the property was seized. *See Vitek*. Furthermore, Slughorn presented no argument for urgency. Because Felix Felicis (FF) is not destroyed during manufacturing, this is not an emergency situation requiring immediate seizure to prevent destruction of the FF. Another indication of insufficient PDP is lack of a bond requirement. *See Doehr*. Under NCRCP 65, Slughorn was not required to post a bond before property was attached, and Honeydukes had no opportunity to post a counterbond to regain possession.

There are some elements of NCRCP that support its constitutionality: the fact that Slughorn had to file a specific complaint detailing the allegations against Honeydukes, including sworn affidavits and certified copies of email exchanges, the fact that the presiding decision maker was a judge not clerk, *see Vitek*, and the fact that Slughorn retained some property right in FF. Although these elements establish a minimal level of PDP, they are insufficient to overcome the fact that there was simply no urgency here to justify the pretrial seizure, and that Honeydukes had no opportunity to be heard prior to attachment of its property. *See Fuentes*.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Erie

Honeydukes can argue pretrial seizure of the FF is a procedural issue, so federal law should govern, and federal law would not allow this seizure.

*York*: a rule is substantive if outcome determinative. The most analogous FRCP is Rule 65. For preliminary injunctions, movant must show likelihood of success on the merits, irreparable harm, balance of equities, and that public interest would be served. Slughorn can show none: no likelihood of success on the merits because no IPJ, no irreparable harm because could sue for damages for later, balance of equities favors Honeydukes because Honeydukes cannot manufacture chocolate frogs without FF (whereas Slughorn shows no need to possess the FF except as a litigation tactic), and public interest would be disserved because buyers would be discouraged from doing business if property could be seized without hearing. Under *York*, use state law.

Harlan concurrence, *Hanna*: a rule is substantive if it would affect primary conduct. There is no reason that the parties' primary conduct (conduct before lawsuit filed) would be different if they could access federal law in addition to state law. The difference between federal and state law would be that state law allows access to pretrial seizure. Because state law has no requirement for Slughorn to post a bond and no opportunity for Honeydukes to post a counterbond, these companies would have no reason to take out different insurance policies or restructure their finances to plan for this contingency. Therefore, use federal law.

*Hanna* majority/*Stewart*: Honeydukes argues Rule 65 is valid (would not "abridge, enlarge, or modify any substantive right" — *see* REA) and applicable (requires similar showings as NCRCP 65, including likelihood of success on the merits). However, Slughorn argues that FRCP 65 is inapplicable because does not govern pretrial seizure. Slughorn argues therefore, to discourage forum-shopping and ensure equitable administration of the law, NCRCP governs.

## Jurisdiction Over Property

NCRCP 65 essentially grants a right of replevin, which operates analogously to a Quasi in Rem lawsuit. Therefore, one should expect that in a writ of Replevin case, as in a Quasi in Rem, courts have jurisdiction over corporeal property only when the property is physically present in the jurisdiction. Honeydukes can argue that because the FF was in SC when the writ was issued, the NC court did not have jurisdiction over the property.

## 2014-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-02-jiang-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2: Model Answer

There is diversity SMJ (Slughorn is a citizen in NC and DE, Honeydukes in SC, and amount in controversy exceeds $75K). There is venue because a substantial portion of events are centered in NC. There are four remaining arguments Honeydukes can make against Slughorn's lawsuit and seizure of the Felix Felicis (FF).

## 1) NC Court Has No IPJ

First, IPJ requires a long-arm statute and a constitutional basis. NC's long arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution, and under 4(k)(1)(a), the NC federal court can piggyback on that state statute.

Second, IPJ requires either general or specific jurisdiction. Honeydukes can argue there is no general jurisdiction because it does not do business in NC, and there was no tag (*Pennoyer* and *Burns*). Moreover, Honeydukes does not have minimal contact with NC to establish specific jurisdiction (*Int'l Shoe* and *Shaffer*). Looking to the contacts among the defendant, forum and nature of the litigation, Honeydukes can argue that it did not purposefully avail itself to NC law because it was not even aware that Slughorn was in NC (*McGee*). While Honeydukes wired money to a bank in NC, that is likely insufficient to establish minimum contacts. This is analogous to *Asahi*, where simply putting a product, or in this case money, into a "stream of commerce," or in this case a bank, does not satisfy purposeful availment to that state. Moreover, much like the company in *McIntyre*, Honeydukes did not target any action towards NC. Even under a loosely interpreted effects test, Honeydukes can argue that unlike *Calder*, they did not know effects would have been in NC.

## 2) NC Violated Procedural Due Process

Honeydukes can argue that it did not have an opportunity to be heard before its property was taken away. Because there is no alternative process to compare, *Matthews* is less helpful here. Instead, we look to *Vitek* and the Restatement of Judgments (2d) § 83[^1] to compare whether the NC scheme is constitutionally viable.

While the NC scheme had some safeguards such as sworn affidavits, an independent arbitrator, a specific complaint, a detailed brief, and evidentiary requirements, these are unlikely to overcome the inability for Honeydukes to call witnesses and answer questions at the hearing (*Vitek*). First, there is no justification for why the FF had to be seized so urgently. There was no need, presented by Slughorn or the state, to immediately seize the potion. Second, while the nature of proof is document-based (a contract), the dispute about product quality likely requires more witness testimony and information from the other side. Lastly, because the potion is old property, the lack of a bond requirement further supports a due process violation. Slughorn was not required to post a bond, and Honeydukes was never given an opportunity to post a counterbond to regain possession of the potion.

[^1]: Note: You'll see this later in the semester.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## 3) The Federal Court Incorrectly Applied State Law (*Erie*)

Honeydukes can argue that the NC court should have used federal law instead of NCRCP 65. Instead, they should have applied FRCP 65 governing preliminary injunctions (or some other analogous pretrial seizure device) because the seizure of the potion was a procedural issue.

Under the *York* test, a court should use state law if the issue would affect the outcome of the case. Slughorn would have been unlikely to seize FF with a preliminary injunction. Not only would he have a low likelihood of success given the lack of IPJ, there is no irreparable harm (the potion wouldn't be destroyed in manufacturing and damages later is an adequate remedy). Furthermore, in balancing the equities, Honeydukes is likely harmed more because they need the potion to survive. Lastly, the court could decide that the possibility of a company taking back a critical component of manufacturing is adverse to the public interest. Because Slughorn would have had a different outcome under the federal rule, *York* would say to use state law.

However, the Harlan concurrence in *Hanna* test dictates the use of federal law. If a rule affects primary conduct, then it is a substantive issue. However, in this case, the usage of state or federal law would be unlikely to factor into Slughorn and Honeyduke's actions as they were negotiating the contract. Therefore, it does not affect people's pre-litigation actions and is a procedural issue. The Harlan concurrence in *Hanna* test would apply federal law.

The Hanna-Stewart test agrees. First, there is a valid and applicable federal rule of civil procedure, FRCP 65. Because FRCP 65 is governed by the Rules Enabling Act, it is valid, and it does not abridge on any substantive state right. Moreover, it is applicable because it is a pretrial method of ordering a certain action. Because a valid and applicable federal rule exists, the Hanna-Stewart test deems the use of federal law.

## 4) NC Federal Court Did Not Have Jurisdiction Over the FF to Seize It

Honeydukes can argue that the seizure of the FF is analogous to a Quasi in rem case where the potion was seized to be potentially used as later relief after the plaintiff proves his case. However, in such seizures, the issuing court can only seize property that is physically present within its jurisdiction. The FF was in SC at the time of the writ's issuance, and therefore, the NC federal court did not have jurisdiction to seize it.

## 2014-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 (Graded)
**Civil Procedure 2, 2014**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** November 7, 2014
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Thursday, November 13, 2014
**Word limit:** 850 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.
12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

## Assignment 3, Question

In 1998, the Massachusetts legislature passed (and the governor signed) the "Limitations System Act of 1998." The law provided that in Massachusetts, a two-year limitations period would apply in products liability (tort) actions, but that limitations-based dismissals are not on the merits for claim preclusion purposes. The next year, in 1999, the New Hampshire legislature passed (and the governor signed) the "Litigation Fairness Act of 1999." Two of the provisions of this Act provided that in New Hampshire, a six-year limitations period applies to products liability (tort) actions, but that limitations-based dismissals are on the merits for claim preclusion purposes. The cause of action of "products liability" is a state law claim, not a federal law claim.

New Hampshire's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution.

Olivanders is a Massachusetts corporation with its principal place of business in Massachusetts. Olivanders makes wands. Ordinarily, the wands are of the finest quality, and magicians everywhere prize them. Olivanders sells wands only in two ways: (i) from its wand shop in Boston, where it does all of its manufacturing; and (ii) through Independent Sellers. Olivanders' relationship with its Independent Sellers is unusual. A person who wants to be an Olivanders Independent Seller must demonstrate, to the satisfaction of Mr. Olivander (the corporation's founder, chief operating officer, and largest shareholder), that she knows how to use and care for wands properly. Then, the would-be Independent Seller signs a contract with Olivanders specifying that she is an independent contractor making her own decisions as to where to sell, how to advertise, and to whom to sell. Olivanders then hires a private investigator to conduct a background check. If the Independent Seller clears the background check, then on any particular day, at 7 am, she may pick up no more than five wands from Olivanders' Boston location. Each wand has with it a removable tag specifying that it is an Olivanders product along with the minimum price that Olivanders will accept for the wand. An Independent Seller must return any wands still in her possession by 7 pm the same day to the Olivanders'

<!-- page: 3 -->

Boston shop. For any wands not returned, the Seller must pay Olivanders the price on the removable tag.

Luna Lovegood was an Olivanders Independent Seller. On March 3, 2012, she picked up five wands at 7 am. As she left, she told Mr. Olivander, "I've been having great luck selling wands in New Hampshire. I'm going there today, and I hope to make lots of sales!" Mr. Olivanders replied, "Yes, I encourage you to head to New Hampshire. I've always thought that I'd sell more wands if my Independent Sellers were just willing to cross the border north." Luna drove from Boston to Concord, New Hampshire, where she sold one of the wands to Pansy Parkinson, a domiciliary of New Hampshire. That very afternoon, Pansy attempted to use her new wand at her job for the New Hampshire Ministry of Magic. But during the first spell she tried, the wand blew up on her, causing her severe injury that required a four-week stay in St. Mungo's Hospital for Magical Maladies and Injuries. Her hospital bill was approximately $800,000.00.

On April 29, 2014, Pansy filed a products liability action against Olivanders in Massachusetts State Court. She served Olivanders with process the same day. On May 14, 2014, Olivanders moved to dismiss, claiming that the lawsuit was barred by the Massachusetts statute of limitations. The Massachusetts State Court granted the motion to dismiss on July 7, 2014 and dismissed the case.

On August 5, 2014, Pansy sued Olivanders (the company, not Mr. Olivander) in a products liability action in the United States District Court for the District of New Hampshire. She served Olivanders with process the same day by delivering a copy of the lawsuit to Olivanders' Boston headquarters (hand-delivered to Mr. Olivander himself) and by mailing a copy to Olivanders' Boston address. Filed simultaneously with the federal court complaint was a motion by Pansy to transfer the case to the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts. On August 13, 2014, before Olivanders had filed anything in the federal court suit, the federal court issued a short order as follows: "In the interests of justice, and given that the wand was manufactured in Boston, this case is transferred to the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts." The file was transferred to the U.S. federal court in Boston the same day.

On August 22, Olivanders filed a motion to dismiss or, in the alternative, for summary judgment. In the motion, Olivanders made three arguments: (1) that this lawsuit, too, should be dismissed as barred by limitations; (2) that the doctrine of claim preclusion should compel dismissal of this lawsuit; and (3) that the doctrine of issue preclusion applied, under which the case has to be dismissed because Pansy had already litigated the issue of whether the lawsuit was filed too late, and she could not litigate it again.

You are a law clerk for the Boston federal judge. The judge asks you for a memo addressing two things:

<!-- page: 4 -->

First, are any of Olivanders' three arguments well-founded?

Second, regardless of what the law actually is, *should* the law be such that this case be dismissed on any of the three grounds Olivanders argues? Note: this is a "should" question. I am interested in your views about what the law should be given the policies and principles we have discussed in class.

## 2014-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-03-bedell-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3: Model Answer

## Question 1

At least in the absence of any possible federal SOL, SOL is substantive for VCOL purposes (*York*), so we will use state law; the question is which state.

If NH were a happy court, you will use NH's laws (including procedural rules). Is NH a happy court? Venue is likely appropriate in NH because that is where the accident took place (substantial event). However, NH is unlikely to have IPJ over Olivanders because there are no minimum contacts. In federal court, 4(k)(1)(a), NH long-arm goes to the limits of the Constitution, so issue is specific IPJ under the DPC. Olivanders would argue that it did not purposefully avail itself of NH's laws or otherwise do business in the state (*McGee*); this was an unintentional tort where the liability was not foreseeable (*WWVolkswagon*), and because Olivanders used a distributor, it is unlikely to be liable (*Nicastro*). However, Pansy Parkinson would argue that Olivander could foresee being haled into court because Mr. Olivander encouraged Luna Lovegood to sell there and thus sought to access the NH market. Olivanders seems likely to win on IPJ, in which case the shorter MA SOL would apply and the suit would be dismissed. However, if NH could exercise IPJ, NH law would apply, and Parkinson would win the SOL question.

For claim preclusion (only if case lives under NH SOL): This is clearly the same claim and the same parties, so it's a question of whether the dismissal is on the merits. NH would have to give at least as much preclusive effect as MA, but in MA, an SOL dismissal is not on the merits. The case could proceed. However, under *Hart*, a state may be able to give more preclusive effect, in which case NH could use its own policy and dismiss.

For issue preclusion (again, only if under NH law), Olivanders's claim would fail because though the issue of whether the claim was filed too late under MA law was litigated, the issue of whether the claim was filed too late under NH law has not been litigated. Because the laws have different substantive provisions, it is not the same issue, and there is no issue preclusion.

## Question 2

This case should be dismissed. Parkinson had the opportunity to file this case anywhere she wanted, but on her first try she irresponsibly picked a court where she was not able to make a valid claim. Olivanders had to hire lawyers and go to court to respond to this poorly-planned lawsuit, and it is unfair to him that he should then have to do the same thing again after Parkinson realized her mistake. This is also a clear example of forum shopping, which is something we have seen the courts try to discourage in other contexts (e.g., *Erie*). Finally, this is an inefficient use of judicial resources, and by allowing the second lawsuit to continue, the court removes the incentive for Parkinson to get her lawsuit right the first time around.

Moreover, this case raises problematic federalism concerns, as the first case was dismissed in state court, but could now potentially be heard in federal court right down the road. Moreover, the potential for bizarre combinations of different states' laws means

<!-- page: 2 -->

that it's possible the court could apply Massachusetts SOL, but NH "on the merits" rules, leading to non-sensical legal combinations that no legislature actually would have enacted.

However, though the result in this particular case seems to make little sense, it would be difficult to craft a rule that properly resolves the problem. Many of the principles and theories underlying the analysis make sense as applied in most cases and further values we want to promote. For example, we do want a state to respect another state's laws by recognizing that other state's judgments on preclusive effect; we also want to ensure that if a case is transferred for convenience purposes, the plaintiff still has the opportunity to use the laws of the jurisdiction where he originally filed. If rules are sensible the vast majority of the time, it doesn't necessarily serve larger goals of efficiency, fairness, and proper power distribution to rewrite those rules completely just because they produce strange results at the margins. The only way to "fix" a situation like we have here may be to give judges latitude to decide cases "against" the rules where the system is so clearly being abused — but even this introduction of discretion raises questions about equal application of laws and foreseeability. So while an outcome that allows this case to continue may initially seem like a perversion of justice, it may in fact be a small price to pay for a generally more consistent and predictable system.

## 2014-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-03-bruland-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3: Model Answer

## Question 1

### Statute of Limitations

The MA federal court will use the Greiner Happy Court rule to decide which state's (MA's or NH's) choice of law rules apply — and thus, which statute of limitations governs the case. Because the injury occurred in NH, venue was proper in NH under § 1391(b)(2). Moreover, NH's long arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution, so there was statutory authorization for IPJ under Rule 4(k)(1)(a). Minimum contacts, however, is a harder question.

Olivanders could argue that it had insufficient contacts with NH because it did not purposely avail itself of NH's laws or of the right to do business in the state (*McGee*), working instead through an independent distributor (*McIntyre*) who was contractually obligated to make her own decisions. Thus, there was no specific link to NH and liability there was not foreseeable (*World-Wide Volkswagen*). Finally, because the distributor was given only a handful of wands at a time — with identifying stickers that would almost certainly be removed — Olivanders could argue that there was a mere trickle of (unbranded) commerce flowing into NH.

Parkinson, on the other hand, could point to Olivanders's encouragement to sell in NH as evidence of purposeful availment and of the foreseeability of being sued in NH. In addition, Olivanders might have foreseen that the distributor would sell in NH from the private investigator's report. Finally, Parkinson could also argue that NH should provide the choice of law because it has an interest in protecting its citizens (as expressed in its long limitations period).

If the MA federal court decides that there wasn't IPJ in NH, MA choice of law rules will apply, including MA's statute of limitations. Thus, Olivanders's motion will be granted and Parkinson's case will be dismissed as falling outside the limitations period. If, by contrast, there was IPJ in NH, the NH court was happy, so NH choice of law rules (including NH's statute of limitations) will govern and Parkinson's lawsuit can proceed.

### Claim Preclusion

If Parkinson's lawsuit proceeds under NH law, it will involve the same claim and the same parties as the first (dismissed) lawsuit. Because the MA court dismissed Parkinson's first lawsuit on statute of limitations grounds — which did not constitute dismissal on the merits — Parkinson's second lawsuit will probably not be claim precluded. It's possible, of course, that a NH court (or, in Parkinson's case, a MA federal court applying NH law) might give the dismissal more than the minimum preclusive effect (*Hart*). However, given NH's generous statute of limitations (three times the length of MA's!) it seems unlikely that NH would give MA's dismissal additional preclusive effect on policy grounds.

### Issue Preclusion

If Parkinson's lawsuit proceeds under NH limitations law, it will not be issue precluded. Although the issue of whether Parkinson filed her lawsuit too late under MA law has already been litigated,

<!-- page: 2 -->

NH has a different statute of limitations, so Parkinson is not litigating the same issue. Thus, Olivanders's issue preclusion argument is ill-founded.

## Question 2

Something seems wrong about the fact that, after neglecting to bring her case for two years and then filing in a court where she was time-barred, Parkinson can simply re-file in NH federal court and then transfer to the MA federal court right across the street from the state court that had originally dismissed her case. Parkinson is clearly forum-shopping, and both her failure to bring the case sooner and her poor choice of original forum hardly suggest that she's taking the litigation seriously. Allowing plaintiffs like Parkinson to re-file and proceed gives them no incentive to stay on top of their cases and file correctly the first time around. It also drains resources from defendants and courts.

However, as wrong as this particular situation appears, there are legitimate justifications for why the system works the way it does. In this case, MA decided that dismissals on statute of limitations grounds aren't on the merits and NH decided to allow plaintiffs a generous window to file their claims. Federalism argues that MA and NH should be able to experiment with different policies on these issues and to settle upon the ones that best further their citizens' interests. Federalism also demands that these policies be respected by other states and by the federal system.

Furthermore, although something seems perverse about Parkinson's being right across the street from the court that dismissed her original claim, the fact that the federal rules allow parties to transfer within the federal system generally furthers the interest of efficiency. Similarly, the MA federal court's ability to apply NH state law respects NH state policies (again, federalism) and facilitates diversity suits (although, admittedly, it is doubtful whether the justifications for diversity jurisdiction are as strong today as they were 200 years ago).

All in all, then, although there will inevitably be cases at the edges — like Parkinson's — that make us shake our heads, there are good reasons for why the system works the way it does. Additionally, it would be a little much to expect a procedural system that worked perfectly in *all* cases.

## 2014-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2014-assignment-03-reese-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3: Model Answer
**TA Model Answer**

Dear Judge:

## Issue (1)

We don't need to do a HCOL analysis because SOL is the exemplar instance of procedural for the broader HCOL analysis. Also no Erie; under *York*, state SOLs apply, at least when there is no competing federal SOL.

Whether to grant Olivanders' request to dismiss the lawsuit as barred by statute of limitations depends on how we come out on a Greiner Happy Court Rule analysis of the original federal court filing in NH (which has diversity jurisdiction over claim between MA-citizen Olivanders and NH-domiciled Pansy Parkinson). Venue is proper in NH because the major event giving rise to it — the explosion — occurred in New Hampshire. IPJ is slightly more tricky because it depends on if NH's long arm statute (the limits of the constitution, i.e., Minimum Contacts — *International Shoe*) can pull Olivanders into NH state court, and therefore federal court since federal courts use the IPJ rule of the state courts they sit in, Rule 4(k)(1)(A). The strongest argument is that Olivanders purposely availed himself of the NH market by encouraging Luna Lovegood to sell in NH. If NH/NH-District Court has IPJ over Olivanders, then the transferor court was a happy court, and this should be a change only in courtroom so the motion to dismiss should be denied because Pansy filed within the 6 year SOL period for NH, which applies under *Ferens*. If, however there was no IPJ, the motion should be granted because the 2 year SOL period for MA has expired.

## Issue (2)

Generally for intersystem preclusion, the second court must apply at least as much preclusive effect as the rendering court. However, it's somewhat of an open question after *Hart* if the subsequent court can apply more. The MA dismissal was 'not on the merits for claim preclusion.' So if you decide to go with the floor rule, the MA dismissal would not preclude because though this is the same claim with the same parties, the final judgment was 'not on the merits for claim preclusion,' and so would not be preclusive in MA; a policy choice explicitly made by the MA legislature when they crafted the 'limitations system act.'

## Issue (3)

If NH was a happy court then the issue of complying with the NH statute of limitations scheme has never been litigated, because NH's longer statute of limitations is a different issue. If NH is not a happy court, then we are applying the SOL of MA, which was an issue already litigated, clearly necessary to the dismissal decision between the exact same parties.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## On the "Should"

It does seem that allowing this lawsuit to go forward confounds many of the policy intentions that MA and NH had in mind by combining their SOL and preclusive effects in one bill: a happy court transfer that allows for the plaintiff to be back in MA, using the longer SOL of NH, but still rely on the lack of preclusive effect from the initial MA dismissal. It seems that the intent of the MA statute is to aggressively keep litigants from sleeping on their rights, without punishing them too much in later more lenient forums. This then might be an example of that working out just fine. Furthermore, with regards to other hypothetical plaintiffs, none could be stuck with the short end of a reverse scenario of filings/transfers that created a preclusive NH dismissal with a shorter MA SOL because the NH initial lawsuit wouldn't have been dismissed as beyond their SOL. Though it seems to confound policy intents to bundle, I don't see much practical problem with how this would play out.

This also points to questions about preclusion and SOL generally. As a policy decision it seems reasonable to deter parties who don't look up the SOL and file anyway wasting everyone's time, by not letting them just keep refiling. So maybe this preclusive effect leeway and the SOL state policy gaming more broadly is ripe for giving more preclusive effect to deter such frivolous lawsuits. On the contrary, people who file outside of SOL aren't using this filing to test claims, or game the system — they are usually just uninformed, and maybe our system should be more forgiving of that.

Finally, this raises some federalism issues, since at the end of the day we have a MA federal district court that is going to be allowing a case to go forward (on the same legal claims), that was dismissed by the MA state court that is probably just across the street. If the federal district court system of sitting in diversity and applying state laws allows for some form of gaming that enables plaintiffs to circumvent a state court decision by forum shopping/swapping, that might be a problem. However, I also think that part of the point of having so many different overlapping court systems is to let people make mistakes, or find the system that works for them so they can litigate their actual claims for relief — like a million dollar hospital bill.

**Professor Jim Greiner**

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
