This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Assignment

For the purposes of this assignment, assume the following.

New Jersey uses the first restatement for horizontal choice of law. Both New York and New Jersey have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the federal constitution. Both states have declaratory judgment statutes identical to the federal declaratory judgment statute. New Jersey substantive (in both the horizontal and vertical choice of law senses) tort law prohibits punitive damages for negligence actions. New York substantive (in both the horizontal and vertical choice of law senses) tort law allows unlimited punitive damages for negligence actions. Both New York and New Jersey have four-year limitations periods for negligence lawsuits.

In the tort law in both New York and New Jersey, there is a doctrine called "negligence per se." Roughly speaking (and sufficient for the purposes of this problem), the doctrine of negligence per se states that an actor is negligent if, without excuse, the actor violates a statute or a regulation that is designed to protect against the type of harm that the actor's conduct causes, and if the victim of the harm is within the class of persons the statute or regulation is designed to protect.[^1]

Note that negligence is a state law cause of action, but the statute or regulation giving rise to a negligence per se argument could be state or federal.

New Jersey has only one federal district, the United States Federal District Court for the District of New Jersey. New York has four federal districts; Manhattan is located in the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In 2012, the United States Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (the "CFPB"), a federal agency with the power to regulate financial products and transactions involving consumers (but not to create a federal cause of action), promulgated a regulation entitled "Credit Card Default Rates: Duty to Warn." In relevant part, the regulation specified as follows:

> **Credit Card Sold Over the Internet**
>
> § 5633: Any financial institution offering credit cards on an Internet website shall at all times include the following information, in at least 13-point typescript and in a clearly visible location, on the webpage in which such offer shall be contained:
>
> (a) The credit limit of the credit card offer; and
>
> (b) The interest rate or rates offered; and
>
> . . . [Other requirements not relevant to this problem]
>
> (h) A statement of the percentage of previous customers who have credit card accounts with the financial institution and whose accounts the financial institution has declared to be in default.

Accompanying the regulation was the following CFPB commentary:

> *The purpose of subpart (h) of section 5633 is to protect consumers. Potential credit card customers should know whether the financial institution from which they are contemplating purchasing credit card services has a high rate of consumer default. Consumers might be able to use this information to evaluate whether the institution's offers seem "too good to be true" or are being marketed to persons who will, in truth, unable to meet the terms of the credit agreements.*

Neither Congress nor the CFPB created a federal private cause of action by which a plaintiff might enforce section 5633.

LupinBank, Inc. is incorporated in New York. 90% of its employees, including all of its customer service representatives, work in a skyscraper in the city of Trenton, New Jersey. Its Board, Chief Executive Officer, and upper level management work in its office in Manhattan, New York City, and the bank's corporate strategy, financial strategy, and operational decisions are made from its Manhattan office.

<!-- page: 4 -->

LupinBank offers credit cards, including its signature "Wolf Card," over the Internet. On its website, LupinBank says, "We do business with anyone anywhere. The WolfCard is your best bet for financial success and independence. Get one today! Click HERE for your card!".

A customer clicking on the relevant location is taken to a webpage that says, "LupinCard: Make yourself into a wolf! Bad credit history? No problem! We work with all types!" Farther down the webpage appears the following information, in 13-point type:

> **CFPB Required Disclosures**
>
> LupinBank provides the following information in compliance with CFPB Regulation 5633
>
> (a) WolfCard credit limit: $2000 initial offer, may be increased after a review of credit history.
>
> (b) Interest rate: Super-Low-Low-Low 75% APR
>
> [Other information]
>
> (h) Percentage of customers in default: click HERE

You are a lawyer in LupinBank's general counsel's office. On January 14, 2014, one of the bank's credit officers walks into your cubicle in the New Jersey high-rise and shows you the following letter:

<!-- page: 5 -->

> **Law Offices of Creevey & Creevey, LLC**
>
> January 6, 2014
>
> **Demand Letter**
>
> LupinBank:
>
> This letter communicates the demand on behalf of our client that you pay her the sum of $80,000.00 to compensate her for the damages the negligence of your company caused her. The relevant facts are as follows:
>
> On December 12, 2012, our client, Penelope Clearwater, age 22, while at her college dorm room in Manhattan, NY, visited the LupinBank website. From the papers LupinBank subsequently sent her, we can tell that her visit occurred from 3:10–3:30 pm Eastern time. She clicked to the WolfCard webpage and reviewed the terms. Upon seeing the "CFPB Required Disclosures," she went to the portion dealing with subsection (h), and clicked on the "HERE" link. She was taken to a page that said, "This page under maintenance. Please check back soon." From looking at your regulatory filings, we can tell that had the webpage contained updated and accurate information on consumer default, Ms. Clearwater would have discovered that approximately 62% of WolfCard credit card customers end up in default on their cards. Had she known about this astronomically high rate of default, she would not have (as she subsequently did) obtained a WolfCard.
>
> Your failure to provide this disclosure violates CFPB regulation 5633(h) and therefore constitutes negligence per se. As a result of your negligence, Ms. Clearwater did obtain a WolfCard. Subsequently, she was unable to pay for the charges (particularly given the exorbitant 75% APR). Her college bank account in Manhattan was subsequently seized by a debt collector, and she had to ask her parents (who live with Ms. Clearwater in her Jersey City, NJ home) to ask for financial support to finish her semester. She suffered actual financial losses and accompanying emotional distress in the amount of $20,000.00, and if forced to sue you, we will seek punitive damages in the amount of $60,000.00.
>
> Please remit a check in the amount of $80,000.00 to my client's home address, #4 Privet Drive, Jersey City, NJ, 00713.

Through a phone call to Creevey and Creevey, you verify that the only cause of action that Clearwater contemplates asserting against your client is a negligence per se theory based on the violation of CFPB § 5633(h).

Through further investigation, you discover that LupinBank's website programmers are located in the basement of a Manhattan building. On December 12, 2012, from approximately 2:45–4 pm, the programmers were doing maintenance on the portion of the LupinBank website dealing with CFPB-mandated disclosures, and they had taken the subsection (h) portion of the site down. When the credit card officer asks you whether you think that there could be liability here, you reply, "I'm not sure. That's something a court would have to decide."

<!-- page: 6 -->

In fact, you'd like to get a court to decide it. You'd rather not wait around to be sued. You believe that federal courts are pro-big-bank, so you'd like to litigate in federal court if possible.

## Question

In what court or courts could LupinBank initiate litigation? What would be the nature of the lawsuit? Why is this court, or why are these courts, able to hear the suit LupinBank would file? What court or courts would be best from LupinBank's point of view? Discuss.

[^1]: Some jurisdictions may limit the application of the doctrine of "negligence per se" to cases involving physical harm. Assume that no such limit applies for the purposes of this exam. So, a plaintiff can assert a negligence per se cause of action alleging only monetary harm.

## 2015-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-01-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

LupinBank will try to get a declaratory judgment that it was not negligent. LupinBank can pursue its claims in state and federal courts in New York and New Jersey.

A federal court will have SMJ over a declaratory judgment lawsuit only if that court would have SMJ over the as-yet-unfiled suit they seek to preempt (Greiner Imaginary Lawsuit Rule).

**SMJ:** LupinBank wants to be in federal court but likely lacks Federal Question Jurisdiction (as CFPB's regulation is not meant to create a federal cause of action). LupinBank could argue that there's a federal issue embedded in a state law cause of action because the regulation relates to federal interests (*Smith*/*Grable*), but that would be a tough sell given the volume of negligence claims and the potential consequences of letting them into federal court. Without FQJ, LupinBank must turn to diversity jurisdiction to get a declaratory judgment in federal court on Penelope Clearwater's presumptive state law negligence claims. Diversity jurisdiction requires complete diversity (all plaintiffs citizens of different states from all defendants) and sufficient amount-in-controversy (§ 1332).

Clearwater is domiciled in NJ (her permanent address is in NJ, she lives there, and she hasn't declared intent to stay anywhere else permanently) (*Mas*). LupinBank is a citizen of NY because NY is its principal place of business (according to the nerve center test (*Hertz*)) and because it is incorporated there. As the parties are citizens of different states, they satisfy complete diversity.

The next test is AIC: Clearwater wants $20k in compensatory damages and $60k in punitive damages. New Jersey does not permit punitive damages, whereas New York permits unlimited punitive damages. See below re which state's law applies.

**IPJ:** As Clearwater is a domiciliary of NJ, both state and federal courts in NJ will be able to exercise jurisdiction over her (Rule 4(k)(1)(A)). There would likely only be general IPJ over Penelope in NY if she were tagged there (*Burnham*). More likely, NY courts could assert specific IPJ in New York given that a substantial portion of the relevant events occurred there and because Penelope established minimum contacts with the state and the tort arose from those contacts (*Int'l Shoe*). By attending school there and doing business with a NY corporation, Penelope also availed herself of the laws of New York state (*McGee*).

**Venue:** In New Jersey, venue is laid under § 1391(b)(1) because Penelope Clearwater is the defendant (in the declaratory judgment action) and is domiciled there. In New York, venue is laid under § 1391(b)(2) because a substantial portion of the events occurred there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Keeping all of that in mind, the options:

**NJ State Ct:** Though the suit would be viable (IPJ over Penelope Clearwater, SMJ because negligence per se is a state cause of action, venue properly laid), LupinBank prefers federal court.

**NJ Fed Ct:** NJ is a First Restatement state, so it applies the substantive law of NY, the place where the injury was felt (where Penelope signed up for the card and where her bank was). Because NY law applies, Penelope CAN seek punitive damages, meaning she meets the AIC requirement. This option is a solid one for LupinBank, though they might be concerned about NJ Fed Ct offering a slight home-state advantage to Penelope.

**NY State Ct:** Though the suit would be viable (IPJ over Penelope Clearwater because the harm was felt in New York, SMJ because negligence per se is a state cause of action, venue properly laid), LupinBank prefers federal court.

**NY Fed Ct:** NY is a Second Restatement state, so it applies the laws of whatever state has the greatest interest in the issue at hand. If the court determined New Jersey to have the greatest stake in damages, it would bar punitive damages, meaning the AIC requirement wouldn't be met. If the court applied NY damages law, AIC would be met and it could issue a judgment. Given that the center of the parties' relationship appears to be in NY, and that the injury was felt there, it is unlikely the court would apply NJ substantive law.

## 2015-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-01-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

LupinBank should sue for a declaratory judgment that it is not liable for negligence per se. Lupin could possibly sue in state and federal courts in NY and NJ. NY and NJ both have declaratory judgment statutes, and we can use § 2201 as well. Lupin wants to sue in a court where there's a chance NJ negligence law will be applied, which would eliminate its liability for punitive damages. That court is most likely NY state court. (If it sues in a NY federal court, and the court applies NJ law, the court might dismiss for lack of SMJ. See below.)

**IPJ:** Penelope is only in NY to attend college and it doesn't seem like she has any expressed intention to stay, so it's likely she's still a NJ domiciliary. Therefore, NJ state court has general jurisdiction over Penelope. And with Rule 4(k)(1)(A), NJ federal courts have general jurisdiction over her as well.

NY courts likely have specific jurisdiction over Penelope for this transaction or occurrence. It's likely an NY court will find Penelope has substantial contacts with NY since she purposefully availed herself of NY protections. She (1) visited Lupin's website, (2) maintained a NY bank account, and (3) obtained an NY credit card. Alternatively, Lupin could tag Penelope in NY. Either way, if NY state court has jurisdiction over Penelope, NY federal courts do as well through Rule 4(k)(1)(A) and NY's long-arm statute.

**SMJ:** We use the Imaginary Lawsuit Rule to determine federal arising-under SMJ. Although negligence is a state cause of action, because Penelope's claim depends on an interpretation of federal law (CFPB regulation § 5633), there may be federal question jurisdiction in NY and NJ federal courts (*Smith*/*Grable*). The commentary to § 5633 articulates a federal interest in protecting consumers. However, FQJ on this basis may upset the balance of labor between state and federal courts. Because the violation of § 5633 is fact-bound, and adjudication would open the door to a flood of negligence per se suits in federal courts (FQJ any time there is a violation of a federal statute or regulation in a suit by a person within the zone of protection), a federal court may hold that the *Smith*/*Grable* exception does not apply.

So SMJ in NY and NJ federal courts would likely need to be based on diversity. Penelope is a NJ citizen. NY is Lupin's nerve center, so even though 90% of its employees work in NJ, it's a NY citizen. *Hertz*. Federal courts in both states would apply state law regarding punitive damages, since they're substantive (*Erie*). But the horizontal choice-of-law (HCoL) between NY and NJ determines whether Lupin can meet the AIC requirement for diversity: AIC depends on whether the $60k in punitive damages is available to count as part of § 1332's $75k requirement. For vertical choice-of-law (VCoL), punitive damages are substantive. That means that in NJ, *lex loci* applies since it's a First Restatement state. Since the tort happened in NY, NJ state and federal courts would apply NY law and allow unlimited punitive damages. This means NJ federal court has SMJ. This also means NJ courts are a bad option for Lupin.

<!-- page: 2 -->

An NY suit may be a better option for Lupin. We know NY is a Second Restatement state (*Babcock*), and as such NY would consider a number of factors to inform its HCoL analysis. Some of the most relevant factors include: (1) the place of the tort; (2) the forum's interest in governing conduct in state territory; (3) protection of state residents; (4) convenience and efficiency of litigation; and (5) the locus of the parties' relationship. It seems like the balance of these factors weighs in favor of applying NY law and finding SMJ in NY federal court. But Lupin would argue that NJ law should apply, perhaps because NY doesn't have a strong interest in protecting a NJ citizen. If the analysis led to the application of NJ law, AIC would not be met and a NY federal court could not hear this case in diversity (there's no AIC requirement for FQJ). Since at least there's a shot at barring punitive damages here, Lupin would prefer to bring suit in a NY court, probably a NY state court.

**Venue:** Lupin can likely lay venue in both NY and NJ federal courts. Since Penelope is a NJ domiciliary, venue is properly laid there through § 1391(b)(1). Venue is also probably proper in Manhattan federal court through § 1391(b)(2) since a substantial portion of the transaction or occurrence occurred there.

## 2015-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-01-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

LB will seek a declaratory judgment that it was not negligent per se. There are four courts where this judgment could possibly be sought: NY State, NY Fed, NJ State, and NJ Fed Ct.

## NJ State

SMJ and venue are settled because it is a state court. There is likely general IPJ over PC because she is still domiciled in NJ. Though she is attending school in NY her domicile has not changed because there is a definite leaving point, i.e. graduation (*Mas*).

## NY State

As above, SMJ and venue are settled. There is likely not general IPJ over PC unless she is tagged in NY (*Burnham* plurality). More likely, by getting a bank account in NY and getting a credit card with a company that has its principal place of business and is incorporated in NY, PC purposefully availed herself of the laws and protections of NY (*McGee*) and because the alleged tort occurred in NY (*Griffis*) there are likely minimum contacts (*Int'l Shoe*). NY's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the constitution. Therefore, likely IPJ over PC.

## NJ Fed

IPJ is established as stated in the NJ State analysis via Rule 4(k)(1)(A). For SMJ, this is a state cause of action as § 2201 does not create a federal cause of action (Coercive Action Rule). Because CFPB promulgated the regulation that was violated there is possibly an embedded federal issue (*Smith*/*Grable*). Consumer protection, especially of especially vulnerable consumers, could certainly touch on serious federal interests, especially seeing as Congress was so concerned that it created an agency to promulgate regulations. But it is also likely that allowing everyday negligence claims to come into federal court could very well shift the balance of the federal-state caseload, sweeping many cases from state courts to federal courts, even where it is limited to credit cards sold over the internet. Overall, it is unlikely that a court would find an embedded federal issue here, but it is possible.

Even if there is no *Smith*/*Grable* exception there may be diversity jurisdiction. As established above, PC is almost surely still domiciled in NJ and LB is a citizen of NY because its nerve center is in NY (*Hertz*), and is incorporated in NY. This establishes complete diversity. The federal court, sitting in diversity, would apply the HCoL rules of the state where it sits (*Klaxon*). Applying the First Restatement, the court would locate PC's monetary injury in NY. The court would then apply the substantive law of NY, which would allow punitive damages and therefore the AIC would be met (no conflict with federal law so no *Erie* analysis). It is possible that a court could find that the injury was in NJ if it considered the fact that the parents were harmed as well, or that the injury occurred where the programmers of the website were and not where the victim was, but this seems tenuous at best.

Venue is laid under § 1391(b)(1) because PC is the defendant and is domiciled in NJ.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## NY Fed

IPJ over PC is established as analyzed in the NY State section via Rule 4(k)(1)(A).

Venue will likely be properly laid under § 1391(b)(2) because the application was filed in NY, the injury occurred there, and in short, a substantial part of the events took place in NY.

SMJ can get tricky. The *Smith*/*Grable* analysis is the same as above. The only difference is HCoL. NY uses the Second Restatement (*Babcock*) and will give the most weight to where the injury occurred but balance that against NY's interests in governing conduct within its territory, NJ's interest in protecting its citizens, and convenience (location of witnesses, evidence, documents, etc.). Because "where is the injury" carries the most weight, the court will likely apply NY law, allowing punitive damages (no conflict with federal law so no *Erie* analysis). There is an unlikely scenario in which NY could determine that NJ has such a pressing interest that NJ law should be applied. In that case punitive damages would be prohibited and SMJ would fail — the case would be AIC-deficient.

Finally, LB will have a tough decision to make about where it wants to file. All four courts will likely apply NY substantive law and will thus allow punitive damages. If federal courts really are more pro-big bank, then the best would be NJ Fed Ct. PC cannot dispute IPJ there. The pro-big-bank NY Fed Ct gives LB the best chance of getting the court to apply NJ substantive law, and venue will almost surely be laid properly.

- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.

For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Assignment

An issue that sometimes arises in the context of limitations periods is when the limitations clock begins to "accrue" or tick. In other words, suppose that a statute of limitations for a certain kind of tort is \_\_ years. At what point in time does the \_\_-year period begin to run? Broadly speaking, there are two general approaches to this question: the "misconduct rule" and the "discovery rule."

- If the "misconduct" rule applies, the applicable statute of limitations begins to run on the date that the act or omission giving rise to the claim occurs, assuming of course that some harm eventually results (or else there would be no tort). In other words, under the "misconduct rule," the limitations period begins to run on the date that the defendant commits the relevant misconduct, meaning the date upon which the act or omission occurs, not when the plaintiff discovers the fact that an injury/harm has occurred.

- If the "discovery rule" applies, in contrast, the limitations period begins to run instead on the date that the claimant first knows about, or first should have known about, the act or omission giving rise to the claim. In other words, under the "discovery rule," the limitations period begins to run on the date that the plaintiff first discovers, or should have discovered, that the defendant committed some kind of misconduct. This might be when some hidden injury or disease first manifests itself.

To illustrate the difference between the "misconduct rule" and the "discovery rule": Suppose a patient visits the doctor on January 2, 1996. The doctor tells the patient that she is healthy. In fact, a non-negligent doctor would have diagnosed the patient with a serious illness. The patient does not find out that she in fact has been suffering all along from a serious illness until she visits a second doctor on May 4, 1999. Under the "misconduct rule," the applicable limitations period began to run on January 2, 1996. Under the "discovery rule," the applicable limitations period might not begin to run until May 4, 1999.

Federal courts, as a matter of federal common law, have long used the discovery rule. The state of Maine uses the discovery rule. The state of Nebraska uses the misconduct rule.

<!-- page: 3 -->

The Nebraska limitations period for suits alleging negligent driving of a motor vehicle is two years. The Maine limitations period for suits alleging negligent driving of a motor vehicle is three years. Both states have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the U.S. Constitution.

Both Nebraska and Maine have rules of civil procedure identical to the federal rules, and their state high courts have handed down opinions interpreting their state rules as requiring plaintiffs' complaints to meet the plausibility pleading test identified in recent United States Supreme Court case law. Under both states' laws, the elements of the tort of negligence are that the defendant owed a duty of due care to the plaintiff, that the defendant breached that duty by failing to act with due care, that the breach caused injury the plaintiff, and that the plaintiff suffered injury.

In 2011, Congress passed the "Federal Limitations Period Act of 2011" (the "FLPA"). In relevant part, the FLPA provides as follows:

> In any action in a federal court in which the plaintiff alleges damages resulting from the negligent operation of a motor vehicle, the applicable limitations period shall be one year.

On March 1, 2014, Ginny Weasley and Harry Potter, both residents and domiciliaries of Nebraska, left their home in Omaha on a driving trip to Portland, Maine. Both were beer fanatics, and they hoped to sample some of the state of Maine's local brews. When they arrived in Portland, they did in fact sample, and sample somewhat liberally. On March 15, 2014, Ginny and Harry attended a local beer festival. On the way back to their hotel, outside 12 Grimmauld Place, in Portland, with Ginny driving, their car hit Aberforth Dumbledore, a Maine domiciliary, who was crossing the street with his herd of goats. Ginny and Harry stumbled out of their car to see if Aberforth was hurt. At the time, Aberforth reported that he was not hurt, but Ginny and Harry gave Aberforth their contact information in case things turned out badly. And they clarified with Aberforth that Ginny was driving. When they got back to their hotel, however, Ginny and Harry resolved to have nothing to do with Aberforth if he contacted them.

In the following weeks, Aberforth felt no discomfort. In late April of 2015, however, Aberforth visited his personal physician. The physician noticed an unusual swelling on Aberforth's left hip. Subsequent examination revealed that Aberforth had a life-threatening degeneration of his hip vasculature. The physician said that the degeneration was mostly likely caused by trauma to the area, such as might occur in a car accident. Surgery was immediately performed, and Aberforth racked up $150,000 in medical, physical therapy, and medication bills.

After unsuccessful attempts to contact Ginny, on August 12, 2015, Aberforth sued her in the United States District Court for the District of Nebraska. Aberforth's complaint was as follows:

[Case caption omitted]

<!-- page: 4 -->

**Complaint for Negligence**

1. Defendant Ginevra Weasley is a citizen of the State of Nebraska. Plaintiff Aberforth Dumbledore is a citizen of the state of Maine. The amount in controversy, without interest and costs, exceeds the sum or value specified by 28 U.S.C. § 1332.
2. On March 15, 2014, on the street outside 12 Grimmauld Place, Portland, ME, the defendant negligently drove a car against the plaintiff.
3. As a result, the plaintiff was physically injured, lost wages or income, suffered physical and mental pain, and incurred medical expenses of $150,000.

Therefore, the plaintiff demands judgment against the defendant for $150,000, plus costs.

August 12, 2015 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; [Signature]

Aberforth arranged service on Ginny by hand in Omaha. Immediately after service, before Ginny had time to respond to the complaint, Aberforth moved to transfer the case to the United States District Court for the District of Maine. The Nebraska federal court granted the motion immediately, and the case was transferred to Maine District Court.

Filing in Maine District Court, Ginny moved to dismiss the complaint on the grounds that it was filed outside the applicable limitations period and that it failed to state a claim.

## Questions

**Question 1:** You are the law clerk for the Maine District Court judge, who asks for your recommendation regarding whether the complaint should be dismissed. What recommendation do you make? Explain. (Suggested approximately 550 words)

**Question 2:** In your opinion, do you think that the law should be such that the complaint above SHOULD be dismissed on any of the possible grounds you identified in your response to question 1? Why or why not? (Suggested approximately 400 words)

## 2015-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-02-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

**(a)**

Ginny would not prevail for lack of SMJ, IPJ, venue, or service of process: Nebraska District Court had SMJ because complete diversity existed (Ginny = Nebraska, Aberforth = Maine) and AIC was sufficient ($150,000). General IPJ existed because Ginny is a domiciliary of Nebraska. Venue similarly existed under § 1391(b)(1) (resident clause). Ginny was served personally. FRCP 4(e)(2)(A).

Aberforth could transfer the claim to Maine District Court because he would have been able to originally bring suit there. For IPJ, Maine's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution. The Maine federal court can piggyback on that statute. FRCP 4(k)(1)(a). IPJ will likely exist in Maine because Ginny purposely availed herself of Maine's laws and had minimum contacts. *International Shoe*. Maine was Ginny's destination. She stayed there overnight to attend a local beer festival. The events giving rise to a lawsuit occurred there. *Hess*. *Cf. WW Volkswagen*. Venue in Maine will exist because Ginny hit Aberforth with her car there. § 1391(b)(2).

**(b)**

Ginny would prevail on a 12(b)(6) motion: Before *Twiqbal*, Aberforth's complaint would have stated a viable claim. It includes (1) grounds for jurisdiction; (2) a short and plain statement showing that he is entitled to relief (that Ginny "negligently drove a car against the plaintiff"); and (3) a demand for relief ($150,000), in accordance with Rule 8(a) and Form 11. However, *Twiqbal* requires a claimant to allege material and essential facts that show the claim is plausible on its face (e.g., that Ginny ran a red light or was not paying attention to the road, i.e., breach of duty). If the district court were to cross out the conclusory statements (i.e., that Ginny was negligent) in Aberforth's complaint, it would leave nothing but the place and date of the accident. The complaint therefore does not state a claim upon which relief can be granted and should be dismissed.

**(c)**

Ginny would prevail on limitations grounds: Because Nebraska court was a happy court (IPJ and venue existed), Nebraska's choice-of-law rules apply. There are two *Erie* issues: whether the federal discovery or the Nebraska misconduct rule should apply and whether the federal one-year limitations (FLPA) or the Nebraska two-year limitations should apply.

*Discovery/Misconduct:* York — if FLPA applies, the outcome would be different and state law should apply; if Nebraska limitation applies, the outcome would be the same and federal law should apply. *Hanna* — common law rule, look at the twin aims of *Erie*; applying the federal discovery rule could lead to inequitable administration and forum-shopping because it would provide a more lenient rule for out-of-state plaintiffs (although on these facts it doesn't matter, could imagine facts on which it would; e.g., if Aberforth had not discovered his injury until April 2016 he could not have sued Ginny in Nebraska state court, but could in federal); state law should apply. *Harlan* — would alter pre-

<!-- page: 2 -->

litigation conduct; Aberforth might have waited to be examined because of the discovery rule.

*Limitations:* York — if misconduct rule applies, the outcome would be different and state law should apply; if discovery rule applies, the outcome would be the same and FLPA should apply. *Hanna* — FLPA is an applicable federal statute which is valid under the Constitution pursuant to Congress's Art. III power to create the lower federal courts; FLPA should apply. *Harlan* — no primary conduct effect; FLPA should apply.

The court should apply FLPA because Congress has passed a federal statute on the question and the state misconduct rule because applying the federal discovery rule would violate the twin aims of *Erie*. Aberforth's lawsuit should therefore be dismissed as outside the limitations period. However, if the court decides to apply either Nebraska's limitations period or the federal discovery rule, then Aberforth's lawsuit would not be dismissed on this basis.

---

## Question 2

**(a)**

Aberforth is a simple goat-herder, with no specialization in the law and possibly no lawyer. His complaint accords with the plain language of the federal rules (Form 11 and Rule 84) and should be honored. Moreover, it is unfair to deny him the opportunity for discovery, which might reveal that Ginny was drunk or not wearing her glasses when she hit Aberforth, facts he could not otherwise know. On the other hand, *Twiqbal* was designed to weed out meritless litigation, and perhaps Aberforth has no valid reason to believe that Ginny was driving negligently when she hit him. However, in a simple negligence suit, we aren't really worried about the expense of discovery. Therefore, his complaint should have been allowed to proceed.

**(b)**

The double-*Erie* issue in this case creates a hybrid law that frustrates the schemes for which the laws were originally created. If the Nebraska legislature had known that a one-year limitations rule would apply with its misconduct rule, it might have opted for the discovery rule instead. If Congress had known that a misconduct rule would apply with its one-year statute, it might have made the limitations period longer. It would be better policy for a court to evaluate the state and federal schemes as a package, rather than two separate *Erie* issues, thereby preserving the intent of one legislature, rather than frustrating both. Under either scheme applied as a whole, Aberforth's claim would not be dismissed.

Because Congress passed a statute for this particular occasion, courts should honor the federal scheme. Although this frustrates *Erie*, the Constitution contemplates that out-of-state plaintiffs would be able to avail themselves of federal courts, and Congress has

<!-- page: 3 -->

chosen to exercise its authority to provide a special rule for federal plaintiffs on this occasion. Supremacy concerns would suggest that federal law should therefore win out.

## 2015-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-02-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

Yes, the court should grant the motion to dismiss. First, the complaint does not pass the plausibility pleading test articulated in recent U.S. case law (*Iqbal*). We must cross out any conclusory statements and accept only those leftover pieces of the complaint as true before deciding whether it plausibly states a claim. Here, both paragraphs 2 and 3 of the complaint are conclusory (they merely state that the defendant drove negligently and that such negligence caused plaintiff's injury). The elimination of paragraph 2 alone renders the complaint insufficient, as it only states an amount in controversy and establishes the court's SMJ over the action.

The court could also find that the complaint is time-barred, depending on which *Erie* test it adopts. The SoL argument depends upon two questions: whether a one-, two-, or three-year SoL governs, and whether the governing SoL began to run in March 2014 (misconduct) or April 2015 (discovery). Because this is a diversity case, under *Erie*, the Nebraska District Court must apply federal procedural rules, but state substantive rules. The relevant state law here is Nebraska's because the Nebraska District Court was a happy transferor (it had SMJ under diversity, PJ and venue because Ginny is domiciled in Nebraska) [*Van Dusen*], and because limitations periods are procedural for purposes of HCOL. Depending on whether the limitations period and limitations triggers are considered procedural or substantive for VCOL purposes, then, four combinations of rules are possible at the outset: 1yr + discovery; 1yr + misconduct; 2yr + discovery; 2yr + misconduct.

The holding of *York* (that limitations periods are substantive for VCOL purposes) is not dispositive here, because that case involved only federal laches (whereas here, Congress passed a 1 year statute of limitations). Under the reasoning of *York*, which is concerned with vertical uniformity of outcomes, the complaint should not be time-barred, because a Nebraska state court would allow the suit to proceed (under the state's 2 yr limitations period beginning at the time of misconduct).

Under the *Hanna* majority test, the suit might be time-barred because the court would apply the federal limitations period but the state limitations trigger. Because the federal 1 year limitations period is a statutory provision, then so long as it is constitutionally valid and applicable, it trumps the state SoL (*Stewart*). The FLPA is directly applicable, and is likely permissible under Congress' constitutional authority to create the lower federal courts. Because the discovery rule is federal common law, a *Hanna*-minded court might perform a modified outcome-determinative test (probably under the guise of the twin aims of *Erie*, which amounts to the same thing) to determine whether federal or state law ought to apply. Application of the federal and state rules here would certainly lead to different outcomes, so the outcome-determinative test would suggest using the state rule for when the limitations clock starts ticking. See below on whether this makes any sense.

Under Harlan's *Hanna* opinion, the question is whether choosing the federal over the state law would affect the primary conduct of parties outside of court, which is meant to be left to state regulation. It does not seem likely that a 1 or 2 year statute of limitations would alter a tortfeasor's degree of care or a tort victim's conduct beyond their choice of when to sue. However, it is definitely plausible that application of a discovery rule vs. a misconduct rule would impact the way the victim of a tort might respond to the kind of accident at issue here. For

<!-- page: 2 -->

example, had Dumbledore known that a statute of limitations might be triggered at the moment of misconduct, he might have scheduled a doctor's appointment immediately following the accident, instead of waiting for any symptoms to present. Under *Hanna* 2, then, we might also get the absurd result that the federal limitations period but state limitations trigger would apply.

---

## Question 2

No. The complaint should not be dismissed for failure to state a claim or for being outside the statute of limitations. First, it is almost an exact copy of Form 11, which is provided as part of the FRCP (see Rule 84) as an example of a permissible complaint for negligence. Arguably, the plausibility pleading standard articulated in recent Supreme Court case law is inconsistent with the rules. From an access to justice perspective, it seems especially important that a pro se plaintiff be able to model their papers off officially provided forms, rather than needing specialized legal knowledge to file a complaint.

Second, practically speaking, dismissal of a complaint that meets the demands of Form 11 would pose an insurmountable bar to many potential negligence plaintiffs. After all, the complaint can only allege information that a plaintiff can gather without the tools of discovery. How is a potential plaintiff like Dumbledore to gather more information about the alleged tortfeasor without access to the court system?

Third, on the SoL question, strict application of the various *Erie* tests to the two VCOL questions separately can yield the absurd result that a federal court sitting in diversity would apply the federal limitations period and state limitations trigger. This would make the suit time barred when brought as a diversity case in federal court even though both under an exclusively federal set of rules and under an exclusively state set of rules, the suit would be allowed to proceed. This result seems directly at odds with the rationales of *Erie*, as it manufactures a forum-shopping problem where there need not be one at all.

## 2015-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-02-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

To determine whether the suit is timely, we first have to determine which tolling period rule to apply. From an HCOL perspective, both Restatements characterize limitations rules as procedural, thus we apply the forum's rules. Which forum? Aberforth first sued in Nebraska, which had both IPJ and venue over Ginny, since it's her home district (*Hoffman*). Thus, Nebraska is a 'happy court' and its rules follow the transfer (*Ferens*). Thus, we apply the misconduct rule, if applying state law. This raises an *Erie* issue, since federal common law uses the discovery rule.

Under the *York* test, this choice might affect the outcome (depending on the limitations period, as per below), and thus we apply state law. Applying *Hanna* majority, the discovery rule is common law. *Hanna* suggests we should look at the twin aims of *Erie* (to prevent forum-shopping, and inequitable administration of the laws). Applying the federal rule here would lead to both, since the federal rule is different from the state rule, and the plaintiff can choose a forum based on which rule he wants. Similarly, an out-of-state defendant might have to face a different rule in a diversity action than an in-state defendant. Thus, we would apply state law. Applying Harlan, however, we would use federal law, since it seems unlikely that the tolling period would affect pre-litigation behavior. It has nothing to do with the actual standards of conduct for negligence, but only deals with when the limitations period starts to tick. Because the tests point in different directions, we can't know for sure which rule will apply. If the discovery rule applies, then Ginny's motion to dismiss on limitations grounds should be dismissed regardless of the length of the limitations period. So I'll assume for now that the Nebraska misconduct rule applies.

The next determination is the length of the limitations period. For the same reasons as above, this is another *Erie* question between Nebraska (2 years), and federal law (1 year). The *York* decision on limitations periods is not controlling here, since *York* was decided in the absence of a controlling federal statute. Nonetheless, *York* still suggests we use state law, since applying federal law would cause this suit to be untimely, whereas state law would allow it to go forward. The *Hanna* test, however, leads to a different result here. Since the limitations period comes from a statute, the question is whether the rule is valid and applicable. Congress can rely on Article III to pass this statute, since it governs what happens in a federal court, and thus it is valid. It is also so specific and on-point that it's hard to argue it's not applicable to this case. Thus, *Hanna* suggests we apply federal law. Harlan, like above, suggests we apply federal law since the limitations period does not affect primary conduct.

If we apply the federal period, this suit is out of time, since the case was filed more than a year after the misconduct occurred. If we apply the state period, the suit is timely. If we believe that the *Hanna* majority is controlling, then the suit is out of time. (But see below.)

<!-- page: 2 -->

In addition, Ginny can also get the suit dismissed under FRCP 12(b)(6) on the grounds that it fails to state a claim. Under the *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* standards, the complaint needs to allege enough facts to raise a reasonable expectation that discovery will reveal evidence, and the plaintiff must allege a plausible claim for relief. The complaint merely conclusively states that the "defendant negligently drove a car against the plaintiff," without alleging the elements of the claim of negligence, or any facts suggesting Ginny's actions meet these elements. Taking out this conclusory statement (*Bell Atlantic*) leaves the complaint failing to state a cause of action. Thus, Ginny's motion to dismiss should be granted. (But again, see below.)

---

## Question 2

I do not think the complaint should be dismissed on these grounds, because the law as applied to this case results in unfair outcomes.

Limitations periods in general serve efficiency and fairness purposes, by incentivizing plaintiffs to bring cases early, while the evidence is still fresh. In this case, however, applying state law to one aspect of the limitations period, and federal law to another, as the *Hanna* majority arguably requires, doesn't make any sense. Applying all of Nebraska law, or all of federal law, to this case would result in the case being timely. It seems ridiculous to expect plaintiffs to file within a year of misconduct happening, when it might take them a while (as in this case) to realize that an injury occurred. This is especially ridiculous when proof of injury is a required element of a tort claim — the plaintiff cannot file a negligence suit pre-emptively, in the expectation that they may be injured.

In addition, applying the *Twiqbal* standards to this case seems unnecessary. In *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal*, the defendant was facing a costly, time-consuming, and (in the *Iqbal* case), potentially national-security-related discovery process, if the claim was allowed to proceed. In these situations, it made sense to require a higher standard of plausibility before proceeding to discovery. It makes less sense in this case, where the claim is fairly simple, discovery is unlikely to be very extensive, and the plaintiff cannot possibly know, without discovery, whether Ginny was drinking (and therefore breached the standard of care etc.). In addition, this complaint mirrors Form 11, which FRCP 84 says is sufficient under the rules.
