## 2015-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 (graded)
**Civil Procedure 2, 2015**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** November 13, 2015
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Thursday, November 19, 2015
**Word limit:** 950 words
**Time limit:** 3.5 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment several days before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded. See the syllabus for an explanation of the grades to be assigned.
12. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Assignment

**First:** In 2009, in response to the financial crisis that led to the Great Recession, Congress passed the Deceptive Investment Practices Act of 2009 (the "DIPA"). As relevant to this question, the DIPA provided as follows.

> Any business or individual who takes out a loan from a financial institution or a bank because of, in whole or in part, of a false or misleading statement or omission (orally or in writing) by an officer or official or agent of such bank or financial institution, may sue the bank or financial institution for a statutory penalty of $10,000 (no more, no less) for each such loan taken out. The right to relief in this statute is in addition to, and not instead of, any other relief that such business or individual might have in law or equity. Any suit pursuant to this section shall be brought within eight years of the allegedly false or misleading statement or omission.

**Second:** You may assume for the purposes of this Assignment that if a bank officer makes a false or misleading statement or omission while offering a loan to a potential borrower, and that the statement or omission caused the borrower to take out the loan, the borrower may sue the bank on the theory that the loan contract should be rescinded (meaning that the contract should be declared null and void). This legal theory would be called fraud in the inducement to contract. This is a state law cause of action. Every state has some version of this cause of action. Assume that all states have limitations periods of at least six years on this cause of action.

**Third:** For the purposes of this assignment, the preclusion laws of Delaware, Idaho, and South Carolina are all the same as federal common law.

In March of 2011, Peter Pettigrew, a domiciliary of Idaho, represented by the law firm of Antioch, Cadmus, and Ignotus, sued Gringotts Bank, a Delaware Corporation with its principal place of business in Oklahoma City, Oklahoma. Pettigrew filed his lawsuit in the United States District Court for the District of Delaware. Pettigrew alleged that in 2010 and early 2011, he had taken out 10 loans of varying amounts each to finance a business he was attempting to start up in his home town of Boise. Each time he took out a loan, Pettigrew alleged, the loan contract stated that the annual interest rate would fluctuate monthly, that is, that the rate would be the Knut rate plus 5%. The Knut rate, which is based on a complex calculation of foreign currency valuations, is known to fluctuate wildly and unpredictably. According to Pettigrew's complaint, each time he took out a loan, the Gringotts loan officer in Boise stated words to the effect of, "Don't worry about the Knut rate. We've never charged a customer more than 12% per annum, and we won't charge you any more than that, either." Pettigrew alleged that in fact, however, just after he took out his last loan, the Knut rate spiked to 25%, and Pettigrew began receiving monthly demands from Gringotts for payments based on an annual interest rate of 30%. Pettigrew further alleged that from late 2009 to early 2011, Gringotts had instructed its loan officers to provide similar oral assurances about the Knut rate and Gringotts' interest rate practices to all would-be Gringotts borrowers. Accordingly, Pettigrew sought to represent a class of all persons who took out loans based on the Knut rate from Gringotts Bank from late 2009 to early 2011. Pettigrew requested $100,000 in statutory penalties for himself and, for each member of the class, $10,000 in statutory penalties for each loan Gringotts made based on the Knut rate. No other relief was requested.

In March of 2012, after discovery (and the collapse of settlement discussions), the district court certified the class under Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(b)(3). Discovery had demonstrated that there were about 25,000 members of the class across the country, and a total of around 40,000 loans had been taken out with the Knut interest rate clause. After appropriate notice was given, no class member opted out.

<!-- page: 3 -->

The parties agreed to try the case to the court. A three-week trial was held in April of 2014, and in July of 2014, the Honorable Severus Snape issued findings of fact and conclusions of law holding for Gringotts. Judge Snape found that Pettigrew had proved that in his individual case, the Gringotts loan officer had made the oral statement regarding a 12% interest rate ceiling, and that this statement was false. But Judge Snape found that given his financial situation in 2009 to 2011, Pettigrew would have taken out the loans had no such statements been made. Thus, Pettigrew failed to prove that the false and misleading statements caused Pettigrew to take out the loans, as DIPA required him to prove, and so his individual DIPA claim failed. Further, Judge Snape found that Pettigrew had failed to prove that Gringotts had a bankwide policy of having its loan officers make oral statements regarding a 12% interest rate maximum, so the classwide DIPA failed.

Pettigrew filed a notice of appeal, and the appeal is still pending.

In August of 2014, two new lawsuits were filed.

**First,** Gringotts sued Pettigrew in Idaho state court, alleging that Pettigrew had in early 2011 stopped making payments on his Knut-rate loans and demanding damages in the amount of the unpaid payments. Pettigrew answered and counterclaimed. In his counterclaim, Pettigrew stated only a claim under Idaho state law for fraud in the inducement (see above). His argument was that due to the false and misleading statements made to him when he took out his Knut-rate loans, which made him take out his Knut rate loans, his loan contracts with Gringotts should be declared null and void (*i.e.*, rescinded), and thereby making him liable only to repay the money he actually borrowed (under a restitution theory) but not any interest. In his answer, Pettigrew similarly contended that the misleading statements to him were a defense to Gringotts' collections lawsuit, because without those statements, he would not have taken out the loans. Gringotts moved for summary judgment on Pettigrew's counterclaim, citing preclusion principles. Pettigrew moved for summary judgment on Gringotts' loan collection lawsuit, citing preclusion principles. Assume that Idaho rules of civil procedure are identical to the federal rules.

**Second,** Gringotts sued Ludo Bagman, a South Carolina domiciliary, in South Carolina state court. Gringotts' complaint alleged that Bagman had taken out three loans from Gringotts branch in Columbia (SC), each based on a Knut rate contract, in the total amount of $50,000 from Gringotts in 2010, and that while he had paid each month the amount that would have been due had the annual interest rate been 12%, he had not paid the amount that was due under the written contract (especially after the Knut rate spiked). Bagman counterclaimed, alleging two legal theories. First, he alleged a DIPA theory. That is, he alleged that when he took out his Knut rate loans, the Gringotts bank officer in Columbia had orally assured him that Gringotts would never charge more than 12% interest, and that these assurances had caused him to take out the loans. On that basis, he sought $30,000 in statutory penalties under the DIPA. Second, Bagman alleged a state law fraud-in-the-inducement theory. That is, he alleged that because the false oral assurances had caused him to take out his Knut rate loans, his loan contracts with Gringotts should be declared null and void (*i.e.*, rescinded), thereby making him liable only to repay the money he actually borrowed (under a restitution theory) but not any interest. Gringotts moved for summary judgment on both of Bagman's counterclaims, citing preclusion principles. Assume that South Carolina's rules of civil procedure are identical to federal court rules.

You are a civil procedure professor writing a blog post about Gringotts' suits against Pettigrew and Bagman and the resulting counterclaims. You decide to address these two questions:

<!-- page: 4 -->

## Questions

1. Do you think that the court will grant Gringotts' summary judgment motion in the Idaho state court case? Do you think that the court will grant Pettigrew's summary judgment motion in the Idaho state court case? Do you think that the court will grant Gringotts' summary judgment motion in the South Carolina state court case? Does the rule in *Marresse* help here? What about Fed. R. Civ. P. 13? Would your answer to any of these questions change if you discovered, in the end of the April, 2014 trial, that the transcript showed that Judge Snape made the following comment: "The plaintiffs' trial strategy in this case made no sense. When this case is over, the unnamed class members in this class might want to consider suing Antioch, Cadmus, and Ignotus" for malpractice.

2. What should the law be in this area? Should the law be such that any of these motions should be granted? On what grounds? Would your answer to any of these questions change if you discovered, in the end of the April, 2014 trial, that the transcript showed that Judge Snape made the following comment: "The plaintiffs' trial strategy in this case made no sense. When this case is over, the unnamed class members in this class might want to consider suing Antioch, Cadmus, and Ignotus" for malpractice.

## 2015-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-03-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

The only judgment that has been rendered is that of the District of Delaware ("DoD"). All subsequent litigation occurs in state courts. We are therefore confronted with questions of intersystem preclusion where the rendering court was federal and the subsequent courts state. *Semtek* is inapposite because DoD was not exercising its diversity jurisdiction. The subsequent state courts must afford DoD's judgment at least as much preclusive effect as would DoD. *Hart*. However, because those courts are in states the preclusion laws of which are identical to federal common law, they will afford DoD's judgment precisely as much preclusive effect as would DoD.

The parties are identical throughout (Bagman was an unnamed class member because no one opted out).

### Gringotts — Pettigrew

Gringotts will almost surely estop Pettigrew from relitigating causation. DoD's judgment addressed the very issue of causation Pettigrew's counterclaim raises. Causation for Pettigrew's individual case was actually litigated during the three-week trial, and Snape's conclusion that causation was wanting was necessary to the judgment because it was the reason Snape found against Pettigrew.

Gringotts would likely claim preclude Pettigrew, too. DoD's final judgment resolved a claim arising from the same transaction or occurrence ("T/O") from which Pettigrew's fraud-in-the-inducement ("inducement") claim arises. Furthermore, DoD litigated the factual merits of Pettigrew's claim because DIPA and inducement are almost identical.

Pettigrew's motion for summary judgment ("MSJ") is based only on claim preclusion because the issue of missed payments was not litigated in DoD. Whether Gringotts is claim precluded depends on whether its loan collection claim was compulsory under Rule 13(a) because Pettigrew was the named plaintiff in the class

<!-- page: 2 -->

action. That question turns on whether the claims arise from the same T/O, a difficult question here. Indeed, although whether Gringotts misled Pettigrew into assuming the loans involves evidence different from whether Pettigrew missed a payment, one could view the entire loan process as one transaction, in which case Gringotts' counterclaim would be compulsory.

### Gringotts — Bagman

Gringotts is unlikely to estop Bagman from relitigating causation because whether Gringotts' caused *Bagman* to assume a loan was not actually litigated in DoD.

However, Gringotts will likely claim preclude both of Bagman's counterclaims. Bagman's DIPA counterclaim is claim precluded because DoD's final judgment resolved the merits of that very claim. Bagman's inducement claim will also likely be claim precluded. Indeed, Bagman's inducement claim arises from the same T/O as his DIPA claim. Moreover, although DoD did not litigate the factual merits of Bagman's inducement claim, the claim is unquestionably the type we would want Bagman to litigate alongside his DIPA claim.

---

## Question 2

It seems unjust to preclude Bagman's DIPA claim with a judgment that denied relief to the class because there was no bank-wide policy of misleading prospective investors. After all, Gringotts might have misled Bagman. It also seems unjust to preclude his inducement claim if he could not have litigated it in the class action. Accordingly, Gringotts' MSJ should be denied because, for reasons I will explain, Bagman likely could not have litigated his individual claims in the class action.

Bagman likely could not have litigated his individual claims in the class action. An unnamed class member cannot litigate a claim the class doesn't assert unless the unnamed class member intervenes. But Bagman almost surely cannot intervene under Rule 24(a) because his inducement claim does not relate "the subject of the [class] action" and because Pettigrew "adequately represent[ed]" his DIPA claim (adequate representation is a prerequisite to class certification under Rule 23(a)(4)). Nor can I imagine a court granting intervention under Rule 24(b). Furthermore, Bagman's

<!-- page: 3 -->

inducement claim would likely violate Rules 23(a)(2) (commonality) and 23(a)(3) (typicality) because his inducement claim would not be representative of and would involve a question of law different from the class. Bagman's inducement claim would also likely violate Rule 23(b)(3) (predominance).

If Bagman's plight is unconvincing, consider this example. Suppose an unnamed class member participates in a class action under Title VII against an employer for race discrimination in its *hiring* practices. The court finds otherwise and denies relief to the class. An unnamed class member files suit against the employer under 42 U.S.C. § 1981, alleging she was not *promoted* because of her race. If claim preclusion bars Bagman's inducement claim, then it would almost surely bar the § 1981 lawsuit. *But see Cooper*, 467 U.S. 867 (1984). I find that result untenable.

I therefore propose the following rule, which limits claim preclusion's breadth in the class action context:

**IF** claim preclusion is
1. based on a judgment *rendered in a class action*;
2. asserted against the claim of a party *all the members of whom were unnamed class members* in the class action; and
3. *the named plaintiffs* in the class action *did not litigate the claim* against which claim preclusion is asserted;

**THEN** the claim against which claim preclusion is asserted is the "same" as the claim that was litigated in the class action only if the members of the party whose against which claim preclusion is asserted could have asserted their claim as intervenors in the class action.

Neither Bagman nor my unnamed class member would be precluded under this rule.

The unnamed class members should consider suing Antioch for malpractice because proving causation for this class is extremely difficult, and failure to do so has extinguished their inducement and DIPA claims (the latter of which would be more likely to succeed if litigated by individual plaintiffs).

## 2015-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-03-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

In the Idaho state court case (L2), the court will likely grant Gringotts' summary judgment motion. Pettigrew's counterclaim is claim-precluded by the 2011 lawsuit (L1): it involves the same claim (same "transaction," i.e. Pettigrew's loans from Gringotts); it involves the same parties; and Delaware federal court rendered a final judgment "on the merits." That Pettigrew is now asserting a theory under state law — not under DIPA — doesn't mean that Pettigrew is now asserting a different "claim." Following *Semtek*, Idaho state court would probably grant as much preclusive effect as Delaware fedcourt would — which likely means federal common law because Delaware fedcourt had "arising under" jurisdiction over the DIPA claim. However, none of this matters here where there are no differences in preclusion law.

The court is unlikely to grant Pettigrew's SJ motion. Pettigrew could cite claim preclusion, arguing that because Gringotts' claim in L2 relates to the same "transaction" (Pettigrew's loans) as in L1, it was a compulsory counterclaim (Rule 13) that Gringotts cannot raise now after it failed to do so in L1. However, the court might define the "transaction" narrowly, finding that Gringotts is precluded from suing for unpaid payments due *before* L1, but not payments due thereafter (as the *Jones* court might have, absent an acceleration clause). Alternatively, Pettigrew could cite issue preclusion, arguing that because the court in L1 found that Gringotts made false statements to him, this issue shouldn't be relitigated. However, this finding is not preclusive because it wasn't "necessary to the decision" in L1. The court found that Pettigrew would've taken out the loans even if "no such statements had been made." It would've reached its decision (in favor of Gringotts) regardless of whether the statements were made.

In the South Carolina state court case (L3), the court will likely grant Gringotts' SJ motion. Bagman's counterclaim is precluded by the class-wide claim in L1: it involves the same claim (same transaction, i.e. all Gringotts Knut-rate loans between 2009 and 2011, including Bagman's); it involves the same parties since Bagman was a member of the class in L1 (no members opted out); and Delaware fedcourt rendered a final judgment "on the merits." Again, SC state court would probably grant as much preclusive effect as Delaware fedcourt would under *Semtek*.

*Marrese*, however, might cut in the other direction. In *Marrese*, the Supreme Court refused to find claim preclusion where the plaintiffs were unable in their first claim to assert a federal anti-trust theory that formed the basis of their second claim — even though both claims involved the same facts. Here, similarly, Bagman was unable to assert his state-law theory in L1, where he was a member of a class asserting a class-wide DIPA theory. As such, the SC court might find that Bagman's counterclaim — or at least, his still-unasserted state-law theory — shouldn't be precluded.

If so, Gringotts might still cite issue preclusion, arguing that the court's finding in L1 — that there was no "bankwide policy" of making false statements — precludes Bagman's DIPA theory. However, this finding doesn't concern the "same issue." The issue in L2 is whether Gringotts made false statements to Bagman on an individual basis, not as a matter of bankwide policy. This specific issue wasn't "actually litigated" in L1 and is therefore not precluded.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Question 2

The law *should* preclude Pettigrew's counterclaim in L2. Pettigrew had the opportunity to fully litigate the same claim and issues in L1. Notably, the issues that were crucial to his state-law theory — false statements and causation — were specifically litigated and ruled upon in L1. This isn't a situation like in *Marrese*. Pettigrew had his "day in court." Allowing him to relitigate would run counter to the efficiency principles at the core of preclusion law.

Whether the law should preclude Gringotts' claim in L2 is a closer issue. Allowing Gringotts to bring this claim when it could've raised it as a counterclaim in L1 invites an inefficient result, i.e. repeated litigation over the same loans. However, if we want to preserve the liquidity of debt, it makes sense to allow lending institutions to sue for each unpaid payment separately.

Meanwhile, the law should *not* preclude Bagman's counterclaims in L3. Unlike Pettigrew, Bagman didn't have a meaningful opportunity to litigate the issues that might've afforded him recovery under state law. In L1, the court ruled on whether Gringotts made false statements causing *Pettigrew* to take out loans; it didn't consider Bagman's individual circumstances. What's troubling is that, although Bagman was technically a party to L1 — as a class member — he didn't have the kind of meaningful involvement that should properly be guaranteed by the "same parties" requirement in claim preclusion. Like the plaintiffs in *Marrese*, Bagman didn't have a chance to assert his individual state-law theory in L1.

Judge Snape's comment underscores the issue. The implication is that counsel disserved the unnamed members of the class — who, like Bagman, might've had viable state-law claims as individuals — when it proceeded on its class-wide DIPA claim and lost, binding all members to an adverse judgment and precluding them from recovering under state law. This is problematic where, as here, class members are entitled to recovery under diverse theories. We might argue that such classes should never have been certified — under the rigorous commonality requirement in *Wal-Mart* — but assuming that some such classes might still squeeze past Rule 23, we should refrain from unfairly wielding preclusion against them.

## 2015-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2015-assignment-03-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

L1's rendering court was a Delaware federal court exercising AU jurisdiction. The L2 and L3 subsequent courts must give at least as much preclusive effect to L1's judgment as the rendering court would. It's not clear which jurisdiction's preclusion law applies with a rendering AU federal court and state subsequent courts, but *Parklane Hosiery*'s rule might apply.

### A. Gringotts's L2 MSJ

Gringotts's MSJ in L2 will be granted on CP grounds. Pettigrew's L2 counterclaim is the same claim as that of L1, because both relate to the same transaction/occurrence and the same evidence would be used to prove both. Snape's judgment was final, because no elements of the claim are pending/undecided and ongoing appeals don't influence the judgment's finality. The judgment is on the merits, because Snape decided that an element of the case wasn't satisfied. Finally, L2 meets the *Gonzalez* requirements, because they're literally the same parties.

Gringotts's MSJ will also succeed on IP grounds. The narrowly interpreted issue is whether the false statements caused Pettigrew to take out the loans. This same issue was actually litigated in L1. To decide whether to award statutory penalties in Pettigrew's individual DIPA claim, Snape needed to determine that Pettigrew didn't prove the causation element. Thus, Snape's causation finding was necessary to the decision. The parties satisfy strict mutuality — which is more than Delaware or FCL preclusion law requires — so IP applies.

### B. Pettigrew's L2 MSJ

Pettigrew's MSJ in L2 will be claim precluded under FRCP 13(a). He can argue that, because L2 has a "close and logical relationship" to L1, the claims should have been tried with the same evidence. *Heyward-Robinson*. Furthermore, there would have been no issue with IPJ

<!-- page: 2 -->

under FRCP 13(a)(1)(B), because Pettigrew filed L1 in Delaware federal court. Under FRCP 13(a), Gringotts was obligated to raise the loan collection claim as a compulsory counterclaim in L1, and its failure to raise the claim in the Delaware litigation claim precludes it from doing so now.

Pettigrew's L2 MSJ will fail on IP grounds. Showing that the loan contracts were based on Gringotts's false/misleading statements would be a complete defense to the collection suit, so Pettigrew wants to issue preclude that finding from L1. Although the false/misleading statements issue was raised and actually litigated in L1, it wasn't necessary to Snape's decision in Pettigrew's individual case. His decision was based on Pettigrew's failure to prove causation, so Snape's finding that Pettigrew proved Gringotts made false/misleading statements to him was gratuitous. *Rios*.

### C. Gringotts's L3 MSJ

Gringotts's MSJ will be denied on both CP and IP grounds.

Under the CP analysis, Snape's decision was final and on the merits (see above). Gringotts can argue that Bagman's claim is part of the same very broad transaction/occurrence as was litigated in the L1 class action (meaning the bank's granting of loans nationwide based on the Knut rate from 2009 to 2011). While litigants can interpret "same claim" broadly and manipulate its definition to achieve policy ends, this seems to be going too far, however. Regardless, Gringotts probably cannot show that the L1 class and Bagman are the same party. Privity typically applies to parties in contractual relationships, so under current doctrine, it's unlikely that an unnamed class member would be found to be in privity with a class. The *Marrese* rule (can't preclude someone out of a claim if that person couldn't have brought the claim in L1 for SMJ reasons) doesn't affect the analysis, because CP doesn't apply anyway.

<!-- page: 3 -->

The IP analysis is brief. In the L1 class action, the issue was whether Gringotts had a bankwide policy of misleading customers about the interest rate. Bagman needs to prove that false/misleading statements caused him to take out his loans, which is a different issue from the policy litigated in L1. Thus, no IP applies.

Snape's statement in the record would apply most clearly to Gringotts's MSJ in L3. However, because neither CP nor IP applies, it doesn't change my analysis.

---

## Q2

I agree with the law as it stands in Parts A and B. To promote judicial economy and efficiency, CP should apply in Part C, as well.

The goals of class actions and preclusion principles are similar: dispose of duplicative legal claims to decrease unnecessary litigation. When L1 is a 23(b)(3) class action — from which potential class members can opt out to preserve future legal claims — it makes sense to apply CP in L2, if a class member tries to sue the same defendant. By choosing to go forward with the class, class members are implicitly giving up their rights to pursue their individual claims. For the law to reflect these policies, "same claim" would have to be interpreted as broadly as possible and the privity doctrine would need to be expanded. An argument for holding class members to be in privity is that by choosing not to opt out, they're entering the equivalent of a "contractual" relationship with the class as a whole.

Judicial efficiency must be balanced against fairness to class members. Snape's statement causes concern, because it shows that class counsel's incompetency in L1 could prevent unnamed class members from ever vindicating their rights. Additionally, joining claims under multiple states' laws can destroy commonality or superiority, which disincentivizes looking out

<!-- page: 4 -->

for individual class members. *Shutts*; *Amchem*. However, FRCP 23's protections for class members outweigh these dangers. Notice and the opt-out are two important safeguards. By providing substantial checks on class counsel's qualifications, FRCP 23(g) is another significant protection. Finally, *Wal-Mart*'s high commonality bar is an additional check on potentially unfair classes.

Because strong protections exist for class members and efficiency is best preserved by dismissing duplicative lawsuits, CP should apply when L1 is a 23(b)(3) class action.
